Introductory Chemistry covers the prescribed course for high school students who intend to enter college with credit for a year of high school chemistry. Its distinctive features are that in it our newer knowledge of the nature and structure of matter is applied in explanation of the facts of chemistry, and our newer knowledge of educational principles and methods is applied in the organization and presentation of the material. 

World Book Company believes that the volume will be a noteworthy addition to its list of high school science texts and that it effectively carries out the motto of the house, "Books that apply the world's knowledge to the world's needs"

PREFACE 

PART I of this text contains only those fundamentals of the science that are prescribed in practically all outlined high school chemistry courses and by the College Entrance Examination Board. It includes the material that a student entering college with credit for a year of high school chemistry is supposed by his college instructors to know. Part II consists of topics from which selection may be made for the remainder of the year's work if these topics are preferred to others chosen because of more local application or for other reasons. 

This arrangement furnishes a brief required course, but it also gives more freedom in the selection of material for study than does a course that is all rigidly outlined or a text that occupies the entire year; and it is believed that this attempt to give some flexibility to the work in chemistry is in accord with modern educational practice in other fields. To achieve at the same time individual freedom and the uniformity demanded by a closeknit civilization is difficult, and a wise compromise is usually the best solution when we are confronted with a problem growing out of this fact. In this case, as in others, the liberty that is granted carries with it responsibilities, and the teacher and pupils must decide what topics not included in Part I will be studied and also when each of these topics will be brought in. Obviously all the required material may be completed before any of the additional topics are studied, or a chosen optional topic may be considered in connection with related materials in Part I. The topics treated in Part II are supposed to be included in a college course in chemistry, regardless of whether they have been taught in high school, and students expecting to enter college and pursue the study of chemistry further should make sure of a thorough mastery of Part I. 

In the explanation of chemical properties and reactions in terms of electrons, the text goes somewhat beyond some of those to iii

iv 

Preface which we have been accustomed, but perhaps not beyond current teaching practice in many of our high schools. To the author the introduction of these new ideas in an elementary course seems justified because they are extraordinarily provocative of interest, because they give to chemistry a concreteness and meaning that without them it lacks, and because during the lives of present-day high school students chemical investigations and discussions of chemical matters promise to be largely in terms of electricity. It is well, moreover, to remember that the electron was discovered before those now in our chemistry classes and many of the teachers of these classes were born, and that a chemistry of the indivisible atom is now not only quite incomplete but even discordant with present knowledge. There is inevitably a lag between the discovery of new facts and principles in science and the incorporation of those facts and principles into courses of instruction, but it appears to the author that in the application of our presentday knowledge of the structure of matter and in the teaching of general chemistry the lag has been unusually great. When books on popular science, and even the daily newspapers, are filled with material relating to the electron, surely our students in science should receive a training that will enable them to read this matter understandingly. And when all the laws of chemical combination are traceable to the structure of the atom, how can we maintain that we are imparting a knowledge of chemical principles if we neglect the most fundamental principle of all? 

This latter point is one which we believe should be further emphasized. If the structure of matter were but one more rather difficult and isolated topic to be added to an already crowded syllabus, the objectors could make a very good case for themselves. But we are not pleading for a mere addition to the course; we advocate a revised treatment of chemistry throughout in terms of modern thought. The teacher who attacks the subject from this angle will soon discover that the initial outlay in time and effort necessary to impart an under-

Preface standing of the essentials of atomic structure will pay for itself many times over in a more thorough understanding and a better organization of chemical subject matter. Once mastered, the electronic concept dispels many dark mysteries and relates many hitherto disconnected phenomena. It raises chemistry from a semi-empirical to a truly scientific basis. 

Whatever of unconventionality there may be in this text from the point of view of method is due to attempts to introduce certain educational principles and methods that have been worked out and are being applied with success in many fields of elementary and secondary education. The idea that interest must precede attention and learning, the principle of self-activity and the supplying of the pupil with material that allows him in large part to complete his work without dependence on or interference from the teacher, the individual method of instruction, the Dalton Plan, the project method and project philosophy, and the unit-study idea of organizing material have worked a mighty change in our elementary and certain fields of high school instruction, and to the author it seems that they must have a great effect upon all our teaching. It is not possible to enter here upon a discussion of educational theory; but in accordance with the project and unit-study ideas the material in this text is broken into definite short units and in as far as it has been possible to do so, the material has been arranged so that the student can do his own work and his own thinking. To this end experiments and text have been built into one, while to stimulate and guide the pupil in his thinking, all through the sections numerous questions and equations to be completed have been inserted where they immediately apply. A minimum number of questions and mathematical problems have been provided at the ends of the sections. It is expected that this phase of the work will be developed in accordance with the needs and capacities of the students and the desire of the instructor in charge of the course. 

References have been provided through both Part I and Part II.

vi 

Preface 

These are listed at the ends of sections in order to make them timely. To interest a student in outside reading is often no small task, but it is an accomplishment that pays heavy interest. 

This outside reading is supposed to help show the application of chemistry to life and the service of chemistry to the country. 

No attempt has been made to mold all the material into any one form. In some units it has been possible to make the approach through familiar substances; often the inductive method has been employed and the student left to answer most of his questions from his experiments. Always, however, it has been kept in mind that method must not interfere with learning, and in one way or another a full course in chemistry has been supplied. The author believes that any departures the present text represents from usual methods are of degree rather than of kind and are but the application in the teaching of chemistry of educational principles that are acknowledged to be well established. 

Briefly, the aim has been to have the student learn chemistry by his own work rather than by having it fed to him by another this on the principle that no one gets into condition for athletic competition by watching others, however well they may perform. 

The experiments are included in the text for the simple reason that this is the only natural way to study an experimental science and because all experience has proved that, where this is not done, many of the students fail to correlate their laboratory and their theoretical work.

Of the value of notebook work there is much discussion, and there is much difference of opinion as to how much of it is advisable. In a few schools nothing more than would be represented by filling in the blanks left in this text is required. In other institutions the notebook work is a prominent feature of the course. In some schools there has been a tendency toward the requiring of only short records because of the great tax laid on the time and energy of both pupils and teachers by an elaborate and carefully corrected system of notes. The form of this text

Preface vii lends itself to a brief system of recording the results of experimental work in the text itself, and certain "work book" features that have proved themselves in the teaching of other subjects have been included. Moreover, for use when the pupil does not own his book and is not at liberty to write in it, a low-priced record book containing space for answers to the questions on the experiments and practically all of the blank tables and uncompleted equations has been provided. But if in the judgment of the teacher it is desirable to do so, there is no reason why the pupil should not keep his records in a blank notebook provided by the school or pupil and in any form that the teacher approves. 

Finally, the author conceives of a text not as an independent instrument of instruction, but as one of the parts of the teaching mechanism. He believes that, generally speaking, it is better for the text to deal with principles rather than with applications, because many of the applications that are most important to the members of a given class are local in time and space and because it seems unfair to the classroom instructor to rob him of his opportunity for creating interest and enthusiasm by pointing out from day to day the applications of the material that he is teaching. General principles are emphasized throughout the text.

There has been no attempt to make a showy text or the most interesting text possible, but simply one that will enable the instructor to give as interesting a course as possible, — a text that does its fair share of the drilling and plodding and leaves to the instructor a fair share of the easier and more attractive work. 

A list of the materials and apparatus used in the exercises and experiments throughout the book will be found on pages 601 ff.

TO THE STUDENT 

"LIFE is short and art is long," wrote the physician Hippocrates, over two thousand years ago, and every one engaged in worthwhile effort finds the time at his disposal all too brief. Hence, both in school and after school years are over, you ought to know whether a thing is worth while before spending time and labor on it; and before beginning the study of chemistry you should understand why you are doing it. Why is chemistry taught in high schools and colleges? Why should you spend your time studying it?

Early chemistry was called alchemy, and this term traces back to a Greek word which means to make an infusion or boil. At first, the work of alchemists, in accordance with their title, was chiefly boiling and making extractions of plants for medicines. 

But these early chemists saw changes in matter going on all about them; they took it for granted that one substance could be changed into another, and impelled either by scientific curiosity or the hope of making some wonderful discovery (particularly the discovery of a way to change more common materials into gold), many of them spent long hours in experimenting. For centuries this experimentation lacked guiding principles, but finally, a little more than a hundred years ago, the theory was developed that all matter is built up of small particles called atoms, that there are only a few different kinds of atoms, and that all the many substances found in nature are made by joining together these atoms in different combinations. 

Since the development of this theory chemistry has progressed by leaps and bounds. Now the composition of most of the substances found in nature has been learned; for reasons of economy, many of these substances are now made in laboratories; and literally thousands of substances never before known to man, many of them of the highest value, have been produced. Without this advance in chemistry modern civilization could not have xi

xii 

To the Student come into existence, and the demands of modern civilization make necessary. the employment of an army of men in chemical industries over the whole civilized world. Recently it has been discovered that the atoms with which chemists work are composed of electricity, and there is every reason to believe that, important as chemistry is at the present time, it will be much more important in the years to come.

The course in chemistry that you are now beginning will require one fourth of your time for a school year, or perhaps nine or ten weeks of your full working time. Some of the advantages you will gain in return for the work which you put into the course, granting that it is successfully carried through, are: 

A general understanding of one of the most interesting and rapidly growing fields of science; 

Some appreciation of the place which science in general and chemical science in particular hold in modern life; 

A partial understanding of how scientific discoveries are made and of how scientific investigators think and work; 

A slight acquaintance with and appreciation of a few of the great men who have labored in the field of chemistry; 

An ability ofttimes to use both the substances found in nature and those made in laboratories more advantageously than persons who are ignorant of chemistry can use them; 

An opportunity to work and study for a short time in a field in which you may find an interesting life career. 

Reading some of the books and articles referred to on the next two pages, and at the ends of the sections throughout the book, will help in developing an understanding and appreciation of what the science of chemistry is, the great importance of this science in our complex industrial age, and the relation of chemistry and of chemical industries to the progress and safety of our country.

PART ONE: MINIMUM ESSENTIALS OF HIGH SCHOOL CHEMISTRY

INTRODUCTORY CHEMISTRY INTRODUCTORY 

THE laboratory work is a very vital part of the chemistry course, since one can never become a real chemist unless he masters the technique necessary for experimental work. A few general suggestions as to the use of apparatus will be given in this introduction, but most of the laboratory technique must be acquired as the occasions for its application arise. 

Exercise 1* 

OBJECT. To become acquainted with the apparatus found in the locker.

Place all the apparatus found in the locker and drawer upon the desk. See that each article is clean and dry and then stow it away in an orderly condition, checking each piece, as placed in the desk, upon the apparatus list.

Take the Bunsen burner apart by unscrewing the tube from the base, and examine the different parts. Note the small opening through which the gas issues. Great care must be taken not to drop liquids or semi-solids in the top of the burner. Put the burner together again. The two holes at the bottom opposite each other are for the entrance of air, which mixes with the gas. 

Attach the Bunsen burner to a gas connection with a piece of rubber tubing. Close the air holes at the base, and light. Now open the air holes slowly. 

What is the effect upon the flame?

Close the air holes again, and hold a porcelain dish in the flame. 

What is deposited upon the dish?

When using the burner, regulate the gas and air supply so as to get a noiseless, non-luminous flame. 

Often the flame will "strike back" where the air enters. is entering the tube. i.e., begin to burn at the base

POINTS ABOUT HANDLING APPARATUS 

1. Do not hurry. Handle all glassware carefully, and see that all apparatus has a secure resting place. 

Do not set apparatus near the edge of the table and reach over it while working. 

2. Do not use great pressure in forcing a stopper into a flask, as it is likely to break out the side of the neck. 

3. Take care that all glass tubing is fire polished, as explained in Exercise 2. 

4. Before attempting to push thistle tubes or any other glass tubing through a stopper, moisten the tube with water or put a little soap on it. This will make it slip through much more easily. 

5. Stoppers should not be placed on the table when pouring from a bottle. Most acid bottles have stoppers of such a type that they may be held between the second and third fingers, as shown in Figures 1 and 2. The thumb and forefinger are then free for grasping the bottle. 

6. A liquid may be poured from a beaker by placing a stirring rod against the lip of the beaker and allowing the liquid to flow down the rod. This keeps the liquid from running down the side

Introductory 

3 of the beaker. The same method may be followed in pouring a liquid from a bottle.

Exercise 2 OBJECT. To learn how to cut and bend glass tubing. 

1. Glass cutting. Lay a piece of glass tubing about 24 inches long on the desk and make a scratch about 8 inches from one end with one forward stroke of the file. Grasp the tube in both hands, and hold the thumbs together behind the scratch; now, by pushing gently with the thumbs while at the same time pulling with the hands, the tube can be broken at the desired point. The sharp ends should be smoothed by rotating them slowly in the hottest part of the Bunsen flame until a yellow color is distinctly seen or until the end becomes redhot. This is called "fire polishing.' وو 

2. Glass bending. Place the wing top on the Bunsen burner. The flattened Bunsen flame should be about 2 inches wide for ordinary bends, and the ring at the bottom should be so adjusted that the flame will be slightly yellow.

Take the shorter piece of tubing with both hands, and rotate it in the flame so that about 2 to 3 inches of the middle portion will be heated equally on all sides. When the glass has become rather soft, take it from the flame, and without delay, but not with too great haste, bend it to form a right angle. It is convenient to have at hand a block of wood or some other right-angled object to assist the eye in making an exact right angle. It is desirable but not always necessary to anneal the bent part of the tube. This is done by holding it in the yellow part of the flame until it becomes coated with soot. After it has cooled, this soot should be wiped off. 

Make a bend in the longer piece of tubing about 3 inches from one end. 

Make other bends until they are approved by the instructor. right wrong

Save the pieces of glass tubing FIG. 3. The pointed effect is obtained by heatfor future use. ing a piece of tubing as for a bend, drawing it somewhat apart, and then cutting it in two. 

Always remove the glass from the flame before bending. If this is not done, the glass will be too fluid as the bend is made. In consequence, the walls at the bend may be thin and the product easily broken at this point.

Introductory Chemistry METRIC SYSTEM 

The United States and Great Britain are practically the only civilized nations which have not adopted the metric system of weights and measures, and even in these two countries the system is used almost exclusively by scientists. Because, like our money system, it is founded on a basis of 10, it is sometimes termed the "decimal system." Each unit is 10 times as large as the next lower unit.

Linear measurements. The meter is taken as the unit of length. It is equal to 39.37 inches. The divisors and multiples of the meter used in the metric system are as follows: 

10 millimeters (mm.)

IO centimeters 

10 decimeters 

10 meters 

10 dekameters 

10 hektometers = 1 centimeter (cm.) = 1 decimeter (dm.) = 1 meter (m.)

I dekameter (Dm.) 

= 1 hektometer (Hm.) = 1 kilometer (Km.)

The centimeter and the millimeter are the two measurements of length which are used most by the chemists. If the length of the meter in terms of inches is kept in mind, the length of any other unit of the metric system is at once at hand because of the decimal form of the metric system; thus: 

I meter 

39.37 inches 

I decimeter 3-937 inches 

I centimeter 

0.3937 inch 

I millimeter 

0.03937 inch

Think of the meter as about a yard and the centimeter as nearly of an inch.

Measurement of volume. The unit of volume in the metric system is the volume of a cube the edge of which has a length of one centimeter. Thus we measure the capacity of a flask in

Introductory 

5 cubic centimeters (cc.). Become familiar with the following units of volume: 

1000 CC. = 1 liter 

I liter 1.06 quarts 

Measurement of weight. The standard metric unit of weight is the kilogram. This is approximately the weight of 1 liter of pure water at the temperature of greatest density (4° C.). By referring to the table of length, if necessary, complete the following table of weights. 

10 milligrams (mg.) = I centigram (cg.) 

Remember that I kilogram = 2.20 lb.

The gram, of course, is 1000 of 2.20 lb., but a better idea of the weight of a gram will be obtained from the next exercise.

B. Measurement of volume. 1. Measure in a 50 cc. graduate 10 cc. of water, and pour it into a test tube. Make a cross-section drawing of the test tube and its contents. 

2. Pour out the water and then place what you estimate to be 10 CC. of water in a beaker. Verify by measuring the amount, and find the per cent of error. In a similar way estimate and verify 2 cc., 5 CC., and 50 cc.

C. Measurement of weight. Place a filter paper on each arm of a trip balance. On the right-hand pan place a 10-gram weight. Balance with sand. Note with the eye and hand the amount of sand, and remove it. Now estimate 10 grams, and then weigh it to centigrams.

Density. The density of a substance is its weight per unit of volume. Any unit of volume or unit of weight may be taken, but in each case it must be specified. In the English system it is

Introductory 

7 generally given as the number of pounds per cubic foot. The density of water is 62.4 pounds per cubic foot. In the metric system the density is given as the weight in grams per cubic centimeter.

Using the cubic centimeter for the unit volume, devise a way for finding the density of water. Explain briefly your method, and give results.

Specific gravity. The specific gravity of a substance is the weight of the substance as compared with the weight of an equal volume of water under the same conditions. If 10 cc. of mercury were taken, it would be found to weigh 13.6 times as much as an equal volume (10 cc.) of water, and hence we should say that the specific gravity of mercury was 13.6. Specific gravity is an abstract number, while density is a concrete number. 

Devise and carry out a plan for finding the specific gravity of sand.

THE USE OF THE BALANCE 

A chemical balance is a delicate piece of apparatus, and it must be handled with care. The following directions need not be studied now, but should be given careful attention before an experiment calling for the use of the balance is begun : 

Precautions. 

1. Never put the substance to be weighed directly on the pan. 

First weigh a watch glass or small beaker or some other suitable

Introductory Chemistry container and place the material to be weighed in this. If chemicals are allowed to come into contact with the pan, it will be corroded and the balance of the instrument destroyed. 

2. Keep the balance pans, the case, and the weights very clean. 

3. The weights should never be handled with the hands but with the tweezers provided with the weights. If the weights are corroded, they will of course become lighter or heavier. 

4. Except during an actual weighing, lift the beam from its bearings. This is to protect the knife edges of the bearings, which are easily dulled. 

5. The weights should never be put in any place except in the pan or in the box. 

6. Never weigh an object until it has been cooled to room temperature. Heat causes expansion of the arms of the balance and sets up air currents about the pans. 

The process of weighing. 

1. Lower the beam by turning the thumbscrew. If the vibration does not carry the pointer to equal distances on each side of the zero mark, let the instructor adjust it. Watch carefully while the adjustment is made, so that next time you may be able to do it for yourself. 

2. Place the object to be weighed on the left-hand pan and the weight which is thought to be sufficient on the right-hand pan. Now release the beam, and note which way the pointer moves. By using the larger weights first, and when one proves too heavy trying the next smaller, much time will be saved. 

3. Get the weight by adding up the weights as noted by the empty spaces in the box. Verify your figures by adding the weights as they are taken from the scale and put back into the box. 

4. Record your weights directly in your book.

UNIT ONE WATER 

WATER in liquid form is so very abundant and widely distributed in nature that it covers three-fourths of the globe to an average depth of a mile, forms countless inland lakes and streams, and is present in large amounts in the soils and rocks of the earth's crust. Furthermore, wood and many other solid materials contain water, and all living matter is composed in large part of it. 

A pound of beefsteak is almost three-fourths water, vegetables like celery and lettuce are more than nine-tenths water, and even such a comparatively dry food as bread is 35 per cent water. The atmosphere, also, holds water in gaseous form, and in the colder parts of the earth the land areas are buried deep under it in solid form, while in the winter season billions of tons of it fall over the temperate zones as snow. 

Our work in chemistry proper will begin with a study of water. 

This seems to be a very simple substance, and because it is so familiar to us, we do not think of it as having unusual properties; yet the chemist views it as one of the most remarkable substances known. Just why this familiar substance is so interesting to the chemist will become evident as our study of it proceeds. 

II

SECTION ONE OCCURRENCE AND PHYSICAL PROPERTIES OF WATER 

A GREAT philosopher has said that "the scientific method is the only method by which the human mind reaches truth." Just what is the "scientific method"? The important element in it is the testing out by observation and experiment of theories and statements of supposed facts to see if they are true. Since an understanding and mastery of the method of science are among the chief benefits that should come from taking up a course in science, we shall, in so far as is possible, use this method in our study of chemistry and we shall begin the use of it at once. 

On the last page it was stated that many foods are in large part composed of water. In our first experiment we shall test the correctness of this statement as applied to milk. Experiment 1 OBJECT. To find the percentage of water in milk.

Water from crystals. Salt that has been dissolved in water can be recovered by evaporating the water. The salt is deposited in the form of small cubical crystals, as may be seen by examining it with a magnifying glass. In a similar manner, crystals of many other substances are formed from solutions, those of each substance having a shape characteristic of themselves. Is a crystal built entirely of the material in solution in the water, or is water used in forming it? We shall now test a number of different kinds of crystals to see if water can be driven off from them. Crystal surfaces, like other surfaces, adsorb and hold films of water, but the amounts of moisture coming from this source are so slight that they may be disregarded in our experiment.

Experiment 2 OBJECT. To determine whether crystals contain water.

In a later section we shall learn more of the formation and structure of crystals and how water is used in the construction of many of them. Here it is sufficient to note that water can be driven off from many kinds of crystals, and that when water enters into the building of a crystal, it does so in a definite proportion by weight. 

Efflorescence and deliquescence. It was noted above that crystals adsorb water from the air and hold it on their surfaces. Some crystals do this to such an extent that they dissolve in the water that they thus gather. 

Such substances are called deliquescent. Other crystals lose water at ordinary temperatures just as those we used in our experiment lost 

FIG. 5.

Introductory Chemistry it on heating. These become powdery when exposed to the air and are called efflorescent. 

Magnesium chloride, zinc chloride, and calcium chloride are examples of deliquescent substances; copper sulfate and sodium sulfate, of efflorescent substances. By exposing a little calcium chloride and a clean crystal of sodium sulfate to the air, deliquescence and efflorescence may be observed.

The forms of water. Not only is water abundant and widely distributed in nature, but it occurs as a solid, a liquid, and a gas and easily passes from one of these forms to the other. Because of this latter fact, water vapor rises from rivers, lakes, and seas, and later may be condensed as rain or snow. Water is thus constantly sprayed over the land areas and drained back to the oceans, making life possible on the land and giving the water an opportunity to exert its chemical and physical powers on the soil and rocks. The amount of water vapor in the air (an average of 

1.2 per cent at the surface of the earth) is insignificant as compared with the amount existing in liquid and in solid form, and insignificant also in comparison with the great effects that it produces. 

In passing from one form to another, water takes up and releases heat, and great climatic effects are produced through the freezing, melting, evaporation, and condensation of water in nature. How important is the fact that water freezes and evaporates within a comparatively narrow temperature range may be understood by imagining what would be the effect if all the water on the earth should collect in the lakes and seas and there remain in liquid form, or if it should all pass permanently into a gaseous or a solid state. Without a change in form, the wide distribution of water and much of its climatic influence and geological work would be impossible.

Physical properties. Every person has certain characteristics by which he may be told from every other person. In the same manner, every substance has certain peculiarities by which it may be distinguished from every other substance. Such peculi-

Occurrence and Physical Properties of Water 

17 arities or qualities as its color, odor, taste, solubility, hardness, density, boiling point, and freezing point are known as the physical properties of the substance. 

The use that we make of a substance is often determined by its physical properties. For example, we have steel tools, because steel is hard and tough; boilers are covered with asbestos, because asbestos retains heat and does not take fire; tires are made of rubber, because rubber is tough and resilient. By certain processes for example, the vulcanization of rubber, the tanning of leather, or the firing of clay — the properties of a substance may be changed so that it is adapted to new uses. Many substances with properties different from any of those in nature for example, glass, brass, solder, various kinds of steel, bakelite are made of mixtures of other substances subjected to certain treatments.

Physical properties of water. water are familiar to every one; found its density (page 7). The freezing point of water is 32° 

Fahrenheit and the boiling point 212° Fahrenheit. On the centigrade thermometer, which is the one commonly used in scientific work, the freezing point of water is o° and the boiling point is 100°. Hereafter in this text, unless otherwise indicated, all temperatures will be given in terms of the centigrade scale. 

On the Fahrenheit thermometer there are 180 divisions between the freezing point and boiling point of water. On the centigrade scale there are 100 divisions between the same points. Therefore, 100 divisions on the centigrade scale are equivalent to 180 divisions. on the Fahrenheit scale; hence one division centigrade is equivalent 180 to 100 (or 2) divisions Fahrenheit.

From these facts it is evident that if we are to change from centigrade to Fahrenheit, it is only necessary to multiply by and add 32°. 5

The heat-absorbing capacity of water. To raise a given quantity of water 1° in temperature requires more heat than is required to raise the temperature of an equal mass of any other substance a like amount. For example, the heat that will raise the temperature of a kilogram of water 1° centigrade will raise that of a kilogram of iron 9°, or that of a kilogram of lead or mercury 30°. This property of absorbing so large an amount of heat and giving it off again in cooling explains the effectiveness of water in cooling gasoline engines and as the circulating medium in heating systems. 

Its heat-absorbing capacity, in conjunction with the heat intake and outgo attending its changes of form, causes water to act in nature as a temperature regulator on a gigantic scale. When its temperature is raised, when it is evaporated, or when it melts, it takes up heat. When it is cooled, when water vapor is condensed, and when water is changed to ice, heat is given off. Thus, through the effects of water, warm regions and warm seasons are made cooler and cold regions and cold seasons are made warmer. 

The unusual expansion of water. Most substances expand when heated and contract when cooled. Water follows this

Occurrence and Physical Properties of Water 

19 general law, except between 4° and the freezing point. Between these temperatures it does just the reverse; that is, when water is cooled, it contracts until it reaches 4° ; then it expands regularly until it reaches o°, when it changes into ice. 

The surface of a placid lake or pond on a cool day in autumn shows no movement of the water, but in reality the cooler surface layer, because it is denser (or, as we generally say, “heavier "), is continually sinking and being replaced by the lighter, warmer water below. This circulation continues until the upper layer reaches 4º, when, through its expansion, it becomes lighter than the warmer water beneath. It therefore remains on the surface and forms a blanket, which helps protect the deeper water from loss of heat. If the cooling continues and the surface layer becomes ice, the water below is even better protected from freezing. If the interchange between the surface and the deeper layers continued as the temperature fell from 4° to o°, the temperature of the whole body of water would finally be reduced to o°, and when freezing began it would proceed downward much more rapidly.

If ice were heavier than water and sank as it formed, bodies of water where the winters are severe would freeze solidly from bottom to top. The summer heat would not melt the ice thus formed, and the higher forms of aquatic life would be destroyed. 

Great changes in climate would also follow, and much of what is now the temperate zone would be a region of arctic cold. 

The expansion of water on freezing. Water not only increases in volume as it nears the freezing point, but it expands very markedly (about one tenth of its volume) when it changes into ice; that is, 100 cc. of water make about 110 cc. of ice. One has only to think of the destructive effects of ice formed in vessels and pipes to realize that, in freezing, water expands with great force. 

Because of this expansion of water when it changes to ice, frost is one of the chief factors in the splitting of rocks and the formation of soils.

Introductory Chemistry 

Our study of water has now led us far enough to demonstrate that we are dealing with a most remarkable substance. Because of its abundance and easy change of form, water is of practically universal distribution over the face of the earth. It enters into the composition of substances as diverse as protoplasm and crystals, and because of certain unusual physical properties, it has a great climatic influence and produces great geological changes.

SECTION TWO DISPERSIONS IN WATER 

SUPPOSE that we have three vessels of water and stir into the first a little sugar, into the second some gelatin or raw white of egg, and into the third a quantity of fine sand. In each of the vessels the material becomes distributed throughout the water in fine particles, and the content of each vessel may be spoken of as a dispersion of the material in water. 

It is evident, however, that there is a difference in these dispersions. The sugar separates into such fine particles that it disappears entirely, leaving the water quite clear. It goes into solution in the water.

The gelatin or white-of-egg particles are large enough to interfere with the passage of light and so give a cloudiness to the water, and they remain distributed throughout the water indefinitely. 

Such an arrangement of material in a liquid is called a colloidal dispersion, or suspensoid (page 571).

The sand particles are larger, and in time they settle out if the water is left undisturbed. Such particles in a liquid are said to be in suspension.

The fundamental difference in these different kinds of dispersions is in the size of the particles of the dispersed substance, colloidal particles being intermediate in size between those of solutions and suspensions. We shall now make a brief study of solutions, suspensions, and colloidal dispersions, and of water as a medium in which dispersions are formed. 

Solutions, solvents, and solutes. When one substance is dissolved in another, a solution is formed. The liquid in which the other substance is dissolved is a solvent. The substance going into solution is a solute. 

Every substance is supposed to be composed of very fine particles called molecules, ultimate units which cannot be divided without destroying the nature of the substance (page 38). 22

In a solution the molecules of the solute are supposed to separate and distribute themselves evenly throughout the water.* 

A solution is, therefore, a "molecular dispersion," which means that the material of the solute keeps separating until in the solution no particles larger than molecules exist. 

Water as a solvent. One of the most important properties of water is its power to dissolve other substances. A study of water and of solutions may, therefore, be very appropriately carried on together. In general the conclusions that can be drawn concerning solutions from our experiments with water are applicable when other solvents are used and whether the solute is in solid, liquid, or gaseous form.

Such liquids as kerosene and water, which do not mix, are called non-miscible. Would you say that alcohol and water are miscible or non-miscible?

Process of solution. If a piece of some soluble substance is dropped into a vessel of water, it will begin to go into solution. 

It has been shown, however, that no sooner are the molecules in solution than some of them begin to be deposited again. Some of the molecules pass off from the solid, while at the same time others are going out of solution and settling on the surface of the solid again.

At first the molecules go into solution more rapidly than they are deposited, but if enough solid matter is present, the solution will finally reach a concentration when the rates of solution and of deposition will be just equal. When this condition exists, the solution is known as a saturated solution. Preceding this condition, there existed an unsaturated solution. If the rate of deposition should exceed the rate of solution, we should have what is known as a supersaturated solution. 

The term supersaturated is used also in a different sense, which will be understood from the following: Suppose a saturated solution, of alum is made in boiling water and allowed to cool. If the solution is not disturbed, the alum is not precipitated; but if a crystal of alum is introduced into the cooled solution, precipitation on the crystal at once takes place.

Before the introduction of the crystal, the solution contains more alum than would go into solution at that temperature — more than

might be considered a normal amount. Hence, it is said to be supersaturated. In the stricter scientific sense, however, the solution is not supersaturated as long as all the alum can be held in solution, and it becomes supersaturated only after the introduction of the crystal changes the conditions so that some of the molecules must be precipitated. A supersaturated solution may, therefore, be defined as one in which the rate of deposition exceeds the rate of solution. 

Solubility of different substances in water. We have seen that solids, liquids, and gases may be dissolved in water. We shall now find whether a given amount of water dissolves equal amounts of different solutes.

Solubility affected by the solvent. From our studies it is now evident that two factors in determining the solubility of a substance are the nature of the solute and the temperature. A third factor is the nature of the solvent. What we learned in Experiment 5 in regard to the solubility of sodium chloride or sodium sulfate in water gives us no hint as to the solubility of these substances in any other substance, such as alcohol or gasoline. 

Every solvent has its own characteristic solvent powers. Water is the most general solvent; alcohol probably stands second on the list.

Effects in nature of the solvent powers of water. Because of the great solvent power of water, such a thing as pure water does not exist in nature. Sea water not only contains great quantities of materials like salt, limestone, and potash, but also smaller amounts of gold, silver, iron, and other substances. Even rain water, which might be supposed to be pure, contains dust particles and dissolved gases which it takes up from the air as it descends. 

The solvent action of water on soil and rocks is most important in nature. In many rocks certain substances are carried away in solution, causing the rocks to crumble into soil. Limestone is dissolved entirely by water, and limestone formations are thus removed and carried to the sea. As the water passes over and through the soil and rocks, nearly everything with which it comes in contact is dissolved and picked up in greater or less amount. 

For this reason all our natural waters carry many different substances in solution, and it is largely because of the solvent powers of water that it is so effective in molding the earth into new forms. 

Freeing water from materials dissolved in it. The scientist in his work constantly requires water that is approximately pure,

and for various purposes in industry and the arts water that has been wholly or in part freed from dissolved substances is needed. 

Some of the methods commonly employed in the purification of water will be demonstrated in the next experiment.

C. Liquids.

Clean the distilling flask and add about 100 cc. of water and 10 CC. of ethyl alcohol. Ethyl alcohol boils at 78.3°, and we know that water boils at 100°. Which will therefore distill off first?

Verify your conclusion by carrying out the experiment. The distinctive odor of the alcohol, or the fact that it will burn, will help you in testing for it.

In general, what kind of liquids can be separated from water by the method used in your last experiment?

As a rule, solids, gases, or liquids that are dissolved in water may be separated from it in the same way that copper sulfate, ammonia, and alcohol were separated in the preceding experiment.

Suspensions and colloids in water. Turbidity in water is due to particles either suspended in the water or in colloidal dispersion in it. In natural waters these particles are usually sand, clay, or disintegrating plant or animal tissues. Clay often gives a red turbidity because of the iron which it contains. Water draining through swampy or forested soil usually has a yellowish or brownish color because of the organic material carried in

FIG. 8. A beam of light passing simultaneously through a bottle of distilled water (left) and a bottle containing a colloidal dispersion (right). The beam does not show in the water, while in the colloidal dispersion it does. The reason for this is that the colloidal particles reflect the light. This phenomenon is known as the Tyndall effect (see page 583). colloidal suspension in it. If this water passes through the earth, the colloidal material is filtered out, and for this reason nearly all spring and well waters are colorless. Running water, however, carries far more material in suspension and in a colloidal state than it does in solution, and considering water as a geologic agent, its property of carrying matter in suspension and as colloids must be regarded as a quality of great importance. 

Industrially, in agriculture, and in the organic world, aqueous suspensions and colloids are of very great importance. Living protoplasm is itself a colloid, and many of the processes carried on in living plants and animals are best explained in terms of colloidal chemistry. The blood, like milk, is a combined solution, suspension, and colloid; soils contain much colloidal material; and soap is a colloid dispersed in water. Colloidal chemistry is becoming of great importance in practical and industrial life (page 588), and water is of great importance because many colloids are formed in it.

Introductory Chemistry 

Purification of water for drinking purposes. It is not always necessary to free water from the dissolved matter to make it fit to drink. In fact, many dissolved substances add to the value of water for drinking purposes. It is because of the suspended and colloidal matter which makes waters muddy and cloudy and because of the disease germs which float in the water like colloidal particles that the purification of public water supplies is necessary. 

The distillation of water, as was done in Experiment 6, would remove practically all impurities, but this method is impractical on a large scale. In the purification of most city water supplies at present the so-called mechanical system of filtration is used. If the water is turbid, it is usually first run into settling basins and allowed to stand until the suspended matter settles out. Often the water is treated with such chemicals as iron sulfate or aluminum sulfate. These form a sticky gelatinous colloid which enmeshes the solid particles in the water and which upon settling carries down with it much of the other matter, including many of the bacteria. The water is then run through filters of sand and gravel to remove the colloidal materials and the bacteria, and usually after this it is treated with chlorine, or in some other way, to kill any disease germs that may still be in it. A few cities "soften" the water before it is distributed, but in most places effort is directed toward removing the sediment and colloidal particles and toward killing the pathogenic microorganisms that the water may contain. 

The rôle that any substance plays in nature is determined by its properties. Thus the wide distribution of water, its presence in all living matter, its importance in all life processes, its constant change of form in nature, its great solvent powers, and the almost countless colloids that are formed in it are all due to its remarkable properties. And because of these unusual properties, water differs markedly from any other substance with which we are acquainted. In the next sections we shall study the composi-

tion of water and some of the chemical reactions in which it takes part.

SECTION THREE COMPOSITION OF WATER 

In the last sections some of the more familiar properties of water were considered. In this section we shall begin a more technical study of this substance. First, we shall determine the composition of water by volume. Then in the next section we shall turn to the closely allied subject of the chemical properties of water.

Composition of water by volume. Until comparatively recently, it was not known that water could be broken into simpler substances. It could be changed from a liquid to a solid state or turned into steam and condensed again, but in these changes in form it did not lose its identity as water or become altered in its chemical behavior. In the latter part of the eighteenth century, however, it was proved that water was composed of two substances, and at the beginning of the nineteenth century an experiment with the electric current was devised by which its composition is easily determined. 

Experiment 7 OBJECT. To find the composition of water. 

Set up the electrolysis apparatus as shown in Figure 9. Prepare 

500 cc. of acidified water by adding 25 cc. of sulfuric acid to 475 cc. of water. Pour it into the apparatus, filling the test tubes with the same solution, and placing them over the two pieces of platinum, a and b.

Connect the two platinum wires found at the bottom, c and d, with a battery of three dry cells in series. A direct current from a 

110-volt line may be used, provided an 80-100-volt lamp is placed in the circuit between the source of current and the apparatus. 

As soon as the circuit has been closed, note the bubbles that collect on the pieces of platinum and then make their way to the top of the tubes. While some of the gas is being generated, determine the negative and the positive poles of the battery. (The compass may be used for this purpose. Consult the instructor.) The electrode connected 34

with the positive pole of the battery is called the anode, and that connected with the negative pole, the cathode. ་་་ང་ས་ང་ང་ང་་་་་་་་་་སང་་ང་་༥་

This gas is known as hydrogen.

This gas is known as oxygen.

The quantity of sulfuric acid has not changed during the experiment. 

From what must the gases have come?

The fact that water is decomposed into hydrogen and oxygen may be expressed in a brief way by the equation: water hydrogen + oxygen (The arrow sign in a chemical equation is read as "give[s]," "produce[s],” or “form[s]," and the plus sign is read as "and.") 

Compounds and elements. In the preceding experiment it was shown that water is composed of at least two substances, both entirely unlike water itself. A substance such as water, which

Introductory Chemistry consists of two or more other substances, is known as a compound. 

A substance which cannot be broken into simpler substances is known as an element. No one up to the present has succeeded in decomposing oxygen or hydrogen. Hence they are known. as elements. Ninety substances are now known that are indivisible into simpler substances, and therefore they fall under the definition of an element. Gold, iron, and copper are examples of familiar elements.

The separation of a compound into its elements or into simpler compounds is called analysis. 

Synthesis of water. Can hydrogen and oxygen be made to combine to form water? This can be effected by means of a piece of apparatus known as an eudiometer (Fig. 10). Two platinum wires are fused in the top of the eudiometer tube and allowed to project within the tube, with a small gap between their points. 

The wires are connected to a battery and induction coil, by which a spark may be made to jump between the two'platinum points. 

To perform the experiment, equal volumes of oxygen and hydrogen are introduced into the tube and a spark passed through Leeeee

FIG. 10. 

the mixture. The gases unite with an explosion to form water, the excess oxygen remaining in the tube (see next page).

If the temperature of the whole eudiometer were kept above 100° (the boiling point of water), the water thus generated would, of course, be in the form of steam. But as the experiment is usually carried out, the water produced condenses to liquid form and the amount of it in the eudiometer is insignificant. 

Therefore, a partial vacuum is formed in the tube and the mercury rushes up into it. 

It would be expected from Experiment 7 that if two parts of hydrogen and one part of oxygen were introduced into the tube, all the gas would be converted into water. This inference is correct (page 329), but if a complete vacuum were formed in the tube, the mercury would rush to the top of the tube and break it. To prevent this, an excess of oxygen is introduced, and this acts as a cushion in preventing a rush of mercury to the top of the eudiometer tube. 

The building up of a compound out of elements or out of simpler compounds is called synthesis. 

Physical changes in matter. All the changes of matter studied or mentioned in the preceding section, as the evaporation or freezing of water, or the solution of salt in water, are known as physical changes. It is apparent that the real nature of the substance is not altered by these changes. Iron, whether it be hot or cold, is the same material. Steam or ice is merely water in another form; the composition of the water is in no way affected by the change it has undergone. The salt in a salt solution is easily recovered in its original condition by evaporating the water. After undergoing a physical change a substance is still the same substance; the properties by which we identify it have not been lost.

Chemical changes. When water is decomposed by electrolysis, it loses entirely its original characteristics. It disappears, and two gases, neither of which has the slightest resemblance to water, appear in its place. When a substance is changed and one or more new substances with entirely different characteristics ap-

Introductory Chemistry pear in its place, it is said to have undergone a chemical change. 

The digestion of food, the rusting of iron, the corrosion of metals by acids, and the decay of wood are examples of chemical changes.

The atomic and molecular theories. Scientists believe not only that matter is composed of molecules, but also that molecules are composed of still smaller particles called atoms. The atoms of each element are all supposed to be practically alike, but to differ in their properties from the atoms of every other element; therefore, there are as many different kinds of atoms as there are elements, probably ninety-two in all (page 355). 

In the same way the molecules of any given substance are all supposed to be alike, and different from the molecules of every other substance. Just as every atom is like every other atom of its own kind and different from every atom not of its own kind, so every molecule is like every other molecule of its own kind and different from molecules of every other kind. 

In terms of these theories, let us now define the terms element and compound.

An element is a substance in which all the atoms are of one kind. 

Its molecules are either built up by the union of atoms of the same kind (page 331), or, as in the case of the inert gases (e.g., helium, krypton, page 597) and most metals, the molecule consists of a single atom.

A compound is a substance in which there are atoms of different kinds. Each of its molecules is built of two or more kinds of atoms.

Physical and chemical changes are also easily explained in terms of the atomic and molecular theories. In a physical change the molecules pass through intact, but in a chemical change the molecules are broken up and the atoms built up into new kinds of molecules. Hence, after a physical change a substance retains its properties, but after a chemical change the properties of the original substance are lost. The water particles that pass off in

International

FIG. II. Sir J. J. Thomson, of Cambridge 

University, England. In 1897 he discovered the electron, the most common thing in the universe. By this discovery he inaugurated a new era in physics and chemistry. evaporation are too small to be seen, but the molecules remain entire and each retains the properties of the mass. But when water is decomposed by the electric current, its properties disappear, because the molecules are broken up and the water (as water) ceases to exist.

The atom is a small particle of matter that passes through chemical changes intact. It is the chemical unit of matter.

The molecule is the smallest particle of a substance that can exist and still retain the properties of the substance. It is the physical unit of matter. The electrical theory of matter. Why do atoms unite to form molecules, and in just what does the difference in different kinds of atoms lie? The evidence for the theories that have been built up to answer these questions cannot be presented here, but more than a century ago the fact that water and other compounds can be broken up by electricity suggested to the chemists of that time that the forces attracting atoms to each other are electrical. Now scientists believe not only that this is true, but also that atoms are made up of unit charges of positive and negative electricity. The positive charges are called protons. The negative charges are called electrons. The weight of a proton is more than eighteen hundred times that of an electron, but the electrical force exerted by one proton is exactly equal to that exerted by one electron. Hence, elec-

Introductory Chemistry tit tit 持​莪 ​+I+ ++ Oxygen 1 trically a proton and an electron will just balance, or neutralize, each other. +1+ +1++ ++ ++ +1+1+ :+I+ +1. Hydrogen

Helium 

Nitrogen

FIG. 12. Diagrammatic representation of the structure of certain atoms. (See page 351.) 

The hydrogen atom is supposed to have one proton for a nucleus, and outside of this one electron.

The helium atom, which in lightness is next to the hydrogen atom, has a nucleus of four protons and two electrons, and to balance the excess positive charges of the nucleus, two outside electrons.

The nitrogen atom nucleus contains fourteen protons (the protons of three helium nuclei and two additional ones) and seven electrons. Outside the nucleus are two electrons as in helium, and outside these again, in a second "concentric shell," are five more electrons. 

The oxygen atom nucleus contains four helium nuclei (sixteen protons), and outside electrons as indicated above.

An atom is supposed to be built of these unit charges of electricity. In the center is a nucleus, which contains more protons than electrons, and which is, therefore, positively charged. 

Around the nucleus and outside of it are placed enough electrons to balance the excess positive charges of the nucleus. The number and arrangement of the protons and electrons differ in atoms of different kinds, as the accompanying diagrams indicate. 

It should be understood, however, that these are merely diagrams, and that an atom is supposed to occupy space in three dimensions and not have its particles lying in one plane. zone.

The atoms of hydrogen, helium, and oxygen are all neutral electrically, but they differ widely in their chemical properties, and these differences are determined by the electrons of the outer The helium atom with its pair of electrons is stable chemically. Under any ordinary conditions it does not unite with any other atom, and helium gas is therefore inert - does not burn, explode, or unite chemically with other substances. The hydrogen atom, on the other hand, with its lone electron, is chemically

4I unstable or unbalanced. It unites with atoms of many different kinds, including its own kind. 

Oxygen belongs to a group of elements that have in their atoms two electrons outside the nucleus, as in the helium atom. Then outside of these again is a second electron layer which seems to require eight electrons to complete it. The atoms of these elements become stable only if they can lose enough electrons to get rid of this layer entirely, or if they can gain enough electrons to complete it. The oxygen atom has six electrons in this layer and requires two additional ones to complete its octet. When a water molecule is formed, it gains these electrons by taking one from each of two atoms of hydrogen. 

When this has been done, the oxygen atom has its full quota of eight electrons and is chemically satisfied. But it has two excess negative charges of electricity, and each of the hydrogen atoms has one excess positive charge. Hence, the oxygen and hydrogen atoms attract each other and hold together as a water molecule. 

At first glance it seems strange that the union of two gases, oxygen and hydrogen, should give rise to a substance so very different from either of them. Naturally we should expect a compound to have properties resembling or intermediate between those of the elements from which it is formed. It does not work out this way, however; so the chemist accepted this fact and proceeded with his investigations in the light of it. 

This method of thinking the acceptance of an external world uninfluenced by what any one thinks of it is in direct contrast to that of the idealistic philosophers (and those naturally of their way of thinking), who hold that the world of reality is in the mind and that believing something causes it to be true. Whatever may be said of the relative merits of the two schools of thought, it remains true that for some reason the scientists are able constantly to push their subject forward, while idealistic philosophy is supposed to have culminated in Plato, who died in 347 B.C.

SECTION FOUR THE CHEMICAL PROPERTIES OF WATER 

By the "chemical properties" of a substance is meant the way it reacts chemically - what happens in its atoms and molecules when brought into contact with other substances and under various physical conditions. Some easily performed experiments will show some of the more important chemical properties of water.

Experiment 8 

OBJECT. To investigate the interaction of water with sodium, potassium, and calcium.

CAUTION. Follow directions carefully, for the action of sodium or potassium with water may be very violent. 

A. Pour about 10 cc. of water into an evaporating dish. Then with a piece of filter paper remove completely the adhering oil from a freshly cut piece of sodium about the size of a pea. Grasp the piece of sodium with dry forceps, and, holding it at arm's length, place the sodium on the water. Result?

This residue is sodium hydroxide.

When burned in air, sulfur forms sulfur dioxide and phosphorus forms phosphorus pentoxide. These two oxides when dissolved in water form sulfurous acid and phosphoric acid, respectively. Write word equations for the formation of these two acids: From the above experiment it is seen that oxides unite with water to form both bases and acids. 

Anhydrides. Oxides which unite with water to form bases or acids are known as anhydrides. Those that form bases are known as basic anhydrides, and those that form acids, as acid anhydrides. 

The elements whose oxides form bases when placed in water are classed as metals; those which form acids are classed as nonmetals.* Which of the following elements are non-metals: sodium, potassium, calcium, sulfur, phosphorus? 

The oxides of some elements, as copper and iron, do not react with water at all and so form neither acids nor bases when placed with water. These elements must be classed as metals or non-metals, on account of properties other than the acidic or basic reaction of the anhydride. 

The stability of water. We have seen that water, which was considered by all the ancients and by the alchemists to be an elementary substance, is in reality a compound. The reason why it was regarded as an element for so long is that chemically it is a very stable substance; the oxygen and hydrogen atoms that compose its molecules have a strong affinity for each other and are not easily separated. It is possible to decompose water by great heat as well as by the electric current; yet at 2000° the decomposition is only 1.8 per cent, and as the temperature falls, the elements recombine to form water. The stability of water * A few elements are on the border line between metals and non-metals, but this classification is sufficiently accurate for our present purposes.

Introductory Chemistry is an important chemical property; for because of this property water is able to retain its identity under conditions where a more loosely built compound would be broken up, and it is because of its stability that water is so abundant on the earth.

SECTION FIVE OXYGEN 

By our work thus far we have proved that water is composed of two elementary substances, oxygen and hydrogen. Now we shall study these elements, beginning with the more familiar one, oxygen.

In the study of an element or a compound, chemists are accustomed to consider its occurrence, preparation, properties, and uses, in the order named. It is evident that the order in which the study proceeds is, in theory at least, a logical one; for a substance cannot be prepared for study until we know where it is found, its properties cannot be investigated until it is prepared in sufficient quantities for such study, and as its uses are all based upon its physical and chemical properties, these uses can be determined only after its properties are known. 

Of course some substances are found in a form sufficiently pure for them to be studied without special preparation, and we already have sufficient knowledge of the properties of great numbers of substances to put them to use without assistance from the chemist. But the investigation of rarer elements and compounds often follows the order outlined above, and this order of study helps the beginner in the classification of his knowledge and in the comparison of any given element or compound with others. 

Occurrence of oxygen. The interior of the earth is supposed to be composed chiefly of metals which, when the earth was in a heated condition, settled toward the center because of their weight; but in the part of the earth that we know, oxygen is the most abundant element. Taken together, the crust of the earth, the water on the earth's surface, and the atmosphere are about one-half oxygen. In the air the oxygen is in a free state; elsewhere it is found combined with other elements. Or, stating the same facts differently, oxygen is found in the air as an element and elsewhere as a compound. The oxygen in the crust of the 49

Introductory Chemistry earth is found chiefly in compounds of calcium and silicon. In a former experiment (page 34) it was found that oxygen makes up one third of water by volume. In the organic world, too, oxygen is very prominent, making up with carbon, hydrogen, and nitrothe "Big Four " that enter into the composition of all plants and animals. gen Preparation of oxygen. There are various ways of preparing oxygen. The particular method to be employed must be determined by cost, convenience, and the purity of the product required. If very pure oxygen is desired, it may be necessary to use complicated apparatus, and when only small quantities are needed, the expense of manufacture may not have weight; but if quantity rather than absolute purity is important, as in industrial work, the cost of producing it is of prime importance. 

Manufacturers are continually asking that chemists devise methods for providing them with large amounts of the chemicals that they need, at low cost, and several methods of producing oxygen for commercial use have been worked out. 

In the most commonly employed of these methods, oxygen is secured from liquid air. Air is principally a mixture of nitrogen and oxygen, and it can be liquefied by pressure and intense cold. 

Liquid nitrogen boils at 195.5°, while liquid oxygen boils at 

- 182.7°; hence, when liquid air is heated, the nitrogen passes off first. By repeated fractional distillations of the liquid air (see Experiment 6, page 29), oxygen is obtained fairly pure. It is stored, under pressure, in large iron cylinders. 

Another commercial method which has some promise of importance in the future is that known as the "Electrolab" process. 

By this method oxygen is secured by the decomposition of water with the electric current. Hydrogen as well as oxygen is produced by this process, as we might suppose upon recalling Experiment 7. 

In the laboratory, oxygen is usually prepared by heating some compound that contains it, as lead dioxide, barium dioxide, potassium chlorate, or mercuric oxide. The purpose of the next

Oxygen experiment will be to determine which of several compounds or combinations of compounds it is best to use in the laboratory preparation of oxygen.

Experiment 10 

OBJECT. To determine the best materials to use in the laboratory preparation of oxygen.

mercuric oxide · B. Repeat A, using silicon dioxide, manganese dioxide, lead dioxide, barium dioxide, and sodium nitrate. Which of these give off oxygen?

C. Place a little sodium chlorate or potassium chlorate in a softglass test tube, clamp it in a vertical position, and melt the substance at the lowest possible temperature. 

Test for the evolution of oxygen. As soon as the solid material has completely melted, throw into the molten mass a pinch of powdered manganese dioxide, keeping the face away from the mouth of the test tube. Result?

If the residue were analyzed, it would be found that the manganese dioxide had not changed, but that the sodium 

FIG. 15.

Introductory Chemistry or potassium chlorate had lost its oxygen. The manganese dioxide causes the oxygen to be liberated more regularly and at a lower temperature from the chlorate, but after the experiment the same amount of manganese dioxide is found as at the beginning. 

A substance like manganese dioxide, which affects the speed of a chemical reaction without being affected itself, is said to act as a catalytic agent, or catalyzer. The process is called catalysis. 

It will be seen later how water, finely divided metals, enzymes, and many other substances act as catalyzers. 

Some catalyzers, like manganese dioxide, accelerate chemical reactions, while others retard them. It is not difficult to find substances that will act as catalytic agents, but it is very difficult to explain just what rôle they play. Perhaps as reasonable an explanation as any yet offered of the action of an accelerating catalyzer is that it first forms a compound with one of the reacting substances present, and this compound then reacts with the other substance in a way that allows the catalyzer to return to the free state.

Method of testing for the solubility of gases. The general method of determining the solubility of a gas in a liquid is to turn a vessel of the gas mouth down over the liquid. If the gas goes into solution, the liquid rises in the vessel. This method is not delicate enough to measure slight solubilities, but it is the only one that can be conveniently employed in elementary work.

Write a word equation for what has taken place: Combustion. The reactions observed in the last experiment are all examples of combustion. This is defined as a chemical action through which heat and light are evolved. Combustion may be due to the action of elements other than oxygen, but in all the cases that we commonly see, oxygen is involved. It is customary to describe gases as supporters or non-supporters of combustion. Oxygen, of course, is classed as a supporter of combustion.

Oxidation. When oxygen unites with another substance, the process is known as oxidation, and the substance that unites with the oxygen is said to be oxidized. In terms of the electrical theory of matter, oxidation consists of the transfer of electrons to the oxygen atoms from the atoms of the substance oxidized (page 40). From the point of view of the substance oxidized, oxidation is the losing of electrons, and a substance that has been oxidized may be said to have been deëlectronized.

Introductory Chemistry 

When the electrons pass to the oxygen atoms heat is liberated, and often in oxidative processes the heat is so intense that light also is evolved. When this occurs, we have combustion, or ordinary burning. On the other hand, oxidation may take place very slowly, as in the rusting of metals or in the decay of wood and other substances. The temperature may not be appreciably raised during the process, because the heat escapes about as fast as it is liberated. However, the same amount of heat is evolved by the oxidation of a given amount of a substance, regardless of the rate at which the oxidation takes place. A log of wood gives off exactly the same amount of heat when it decays in the forest as when it is burned in a stove. 

A good example of slow oxidation is the union of oxygen with certain oils. The skin that forms over the top of linseed oil which has been left exposed to the air is simply an oxidized layer of the oil. Oil paints are made by adding covering substances and pigments to linseed oil or a similar oil, and it is the oxidized layer of oil, corresponding to the leathery skin on the surface of an exposed vessel of oil, that holds the color to a painted surface. 

The paint dries, not by evaporation, but by oxidation of the oil. 

The driers that are sometimes added to paint are catalyzers that hasten the oxidizing of the oil. 

Spontaneous combustion. If linseed oil or oils of a similar nature are left on rags which are thrown in a pile, heat is evolved from the oxidation of the oil. Rags are poor conductors of heat, and as there is no free circulation of air through the mass, the heat accumulates until the temperature of the interior of the pile may reach the ignition point; then the whole pile bursts into flame. 

The starting of a fire in this way is known as spontaneous combustion.
