The "Founder of Modern Chem istry," Lavoisier (1743-1794). He proved the true nature of oxidation and respiration, demonstrated the indestructibility of matter, introduced quantitative methods into chemical experimentation, and systematized the naming and grouping of chemical compounds. When he was beheaded, the philosopher Lagrange exclaimed: "A moment only was required for the fall of this head and one hundred years may not be sufficient to produce another like it."

Spontaneous combustion may occur wherever easily oxidized material is kept where the heat accumulates. A pile of rags soaked in paint and turpentine may easily become heated to the kindling point (i.e., the temperature when combustion begins), and stored hay and grain sometimes heat until spontaneous combustion occurs. Soft coal in the bunkers of ships, or even when in large piles in the open air, is likely to develop enough heat to ignite itself unless care is taken to prevent this. 

Lavoisier's experiments. The subject of oxidation should not be passed without paying tribute to Lavoisier, the famous French

Introductory Chemistry chemist of the eighteenth century. He was the first to explain ordinary burning as the combining of a substance with oxygen. 

This was about the year 1786. Up to this date it had been generally accepted that when substances burned they lost an unknown "phlogiston," and thus became lighter; and that it was this phlogiston, burning as it passed off, that caused the flame. 

Lavoisier proved, however, by actual weighing, that sulfur, phosphorus, and certain other substances when heated in air became heavier. Through his experiments with mercury, he was able to arrive at the true explanation of combustion. 

In one of Lavoisier's experiments a small quantity of mercury was heated for several days in a flask, at a temperature just below its boiling point. At the end of this time a red powder, mercuric oxide, which weighed more than the original mercury, was present in the flask. Heating this powder at a temperature above the boiling point of mercury (instead of below it, as in the first part of his experiment), Lavoisier obtained a small quantity of free mercury and a gas that supported combustion much better than air. He therefore concluded that when the mercury was heated at the lower temperature, it united with some part of the air to form the red powder. Then, on the application of greater heat to this red powder, the process was reversed: the red powder was broken up into mercury and a gas which supported combustion. 

This gas which united with the mercury presumably came from the air. Lavoisier called this gas oxygen, and asserted that whenever burning takes place there is a uniting of oxygen with some other substance. The red powder he called mercuric oxide, on the theory that it was composed of mercury and oxygen. 

Carrying his experiments further, Lavoisier showed that oxidation goes on within living organisms; that the food we eat combines with oxygen within the body, just as other substances do outside the body. Thus he explained the source of the body's warmth and of the energy which keeps in operation all those vital processes which collectively we know as "life.”

Oxygen By these experiments Lavoisier freed chemistry from certain false theories that had retarded its progress, and initiated a period of rapid chemical advancement. His method of submitting chemical problems to trial by the balance put a new spirit into the science. He is called the "Father of Modern Chemistry," and is held in almost as much veneration among physiologists as among chemists. He was condemned to death with twentysix fellow scientists by the Revolutionary Tribunal and was guillotined in 1794. A petition in his favor addressed to the 

President of the Tribunal is said to have received the reply, “The 

Republic has no need of savants."

The oxygen cycle in nature. By the use of oxygen in respiration, plants and animals oxidize food and thus obtain energy for their life processes. In the oxidation, the carbon of the food is converted to carbon dioxide and released to the air. This process continually removes oxygen as an element from the atmosphere and returns it as a compound, and it would seem that in time much of the free oxygen of the air would be used up. This would be the case if it were not for a process of food manufacture (photosynthesis) that goes on in green plants. In this process carbon dioxide is removed from the air and the carbon is used in the manufacture of food, while the oxygen is released to the atmosphere. In this way these two vital processes act reciprocally, as indicated below, to keep the oxygen content of the air O2 

Photosynthesis 

Respiration _CO2- approximately constant. When the food is oxidized in respiration, the transfer of electrons to the oxygen energy that enables plants and animals to live. atoms yields the 

The energy that causes the electrons to leave the oxygen atoms and pass back to the carbon comes to the green plants in the light of the sun. 

Uses of oxygen. Oxygen is used in many ways, two of which will be mentioned.

Introductory Chemistry 

FIG. 18. Scrapping a battleship by the aid of the oxy-acetylene flame. 

U.S. Navy 

Oxygen is used when it is desired to increase the oxygen supply in the lungs, as in pneumonia, in cases of collapse under surgical operations, in mountain climbing and flying at great heights, and in submarines. Oxygen helmets are used by firemen and in emergency work in mines and manufacturing plants. 

A second most important use of oxygen is in industrial processes where great heat is required. Hydrogen mixed with oxygen burns with an exceedingly hot flame, and oxygen and acetylene make one of the hottest flames known. These flames are used industrially for melting refractory material, for welding, for cutting steel, and for other similar work. With such a flame a hole can quickly be made through a plate of hardened steel, and a girder can be cut with it in a fraction of the time required by any other method.

Ozone. When an electric spark is discharged through oxygen,. the oxygen is changed into another form, which is known as

Oxygen ozone. Under ordinary conditions ozone is a gas having a pale-blue color and a penetrating odor. As a liquid it boils at -119. At normal atmospheric pressure and a temperature of 12°, 100 volumes of water dissolve 50 volumes of ozone. On heating ozone to 271°, it may be changed to oxygen, 2 volumes of ozone yielding 3 volumes of oxygen. 

It is supposed to differ from oxygen in that a molecule of oxygen contains 2 atoms of oxygen and a molecule of ozone 3 atoms.

He also discovered how, by the use of a metal catalyst, ammonia can be synthesized from hydrogen and nitrogen, the principle underlying the Haber process of nitrogen fixation (page tricity, and if it were formed in the air, it would quickly change back to oxygen. When it is decomposed into oxygen, a great amount of heat is released, and it is a much more active oxidizing agent than oxygen. Silver, which is not affected by oxygen, may be oxidized with ozone, and bacteria and other organic matter in water may be entirely burned up with it, leaving the water pure and colorless. Introductory Chemistry preparation of certain dyes, in bleaching oils and waxes, and in other ways.

As we review the work on oxygen, we note that it makes up more than one half of the part of the world that we know, and that it is of practically universal distribution. These facts alone entitle it to special consideration. It is the "active element " entering into more chemical reactions than any other element, which is a second point of distinction. Further, in the chemical reactions in which oxygen takes part, large amounts of energy are transformed, and from the energy released by the oxidative processes man secures heat and power. Finally, the energy of living organisms is derived from the deëlectronizing processes brought about by oxygen within the cells; in its energy aspects, life itself is built about oxygen. When we consider all these facts, we must decide that oxygen, like water, is a very remarkable substance.

SECTION SIX HYDROGEN 

ANIMALS and persons seek out others of their own kind for association, but in the electrical world it is the unlike that attracts. 

Since we have learned that the forces active in chemistry are electrical, we might expect that elements in the combinations that they make would follow the electrical rather than the biological rule, and that hydrogen would prove to be very different from the oxygen with which it is found combined in so great amounts in the water of the earth. Our studies of hydrogen will show whether this inference is correct. 

Occurrence of hydrogen. Hydrogen escapes in the free state from certain volcanoes; it is found in the gas from certain natural-gas wells; it is present in minute amounts in the lower air; and it is supposed to be the chief constituent of the upper atmosphere. The total amounts thus found in the free state are insignificant (the upper air is very rare), but combined with other elements hydrogen is very common. As has been seen, it forms two thirds of water by volume. Combined with carbon, it makes up a large class of compounds known as hydrocarbons, which are the principal constituents of illuminating gas and petroleum. 

In combination with oxygen and carbon it forms the fats and oils found in plants and animals and the great class of organic compounds (carbohydrates) which includes the sugars, starches, cellulose, gums, wood, and alcohols. It is also a constituent of the living material of the cell. Furthermore, as will be seen later, hydrogen is an essential constituent of the two classes of compounds known as acids and bases. 

Preparation. We have already obtained hydrogen from water by two different methods. What are they?

By a third method hydrogen is prepared through the action of certain metals on dilute acids. The reaction of metals like 64

Hydrogen 

65 sodium and potassium, which liberate hydrogen from water at ordinary temperatures, is too vigorous for safety when they come into contact with acids. Other metals, like gold or platinum, may fail to release the hydrogen of the acids at all. In the next experiment we shall test some metals to determine which is best for use in the preparation of hydrogen. 

Experiment 14 

OBJECT. To determine the best metal to use for a reaction with dilute acids in the preparation of hydrogen; and to determine the source of the hydrogen. 

What is the test for hydrogen?

A. Place in separate test tubes a few small pieces of each of the following metals: tin, iron, zinc, copper, lead, aluminum, and magnesium. Place 25 cc. of water in a graduated cylinder, and add 

25 cc. of concentrated hydrochloric acid. Add 5 cc. or 6 cc. of the solution to each test tube containing the metal, applying the hydrogen test to each tube immediately after the acid has been added. 

If there is no action, heat the test tube gently. If heating induces bubbling, remember that this may be caused by steam or some gas dissolved in the acid. The only true evidence of hydrogen liberation is the continued appearance of gas bubbles after artificial heating is discontinued; the fact that an explosion does not follow the bringing of a light to the mouth of the test tube is not conclusive evidence that no hydrogen is generated. Hydrogen is not explosive when mixed with oxygen (air) unless the proportions of the gases are within certain limits.

Place 40 cc. of water in a beaker, and add 7 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid. (Always pour the acid into the water.) Repeat the first part of the experiment, using the prepared sulfuric acid (H₂SO4) instead of the hydrochloric acid (HCl).

The reaction by which the hydrogen is released is termed a displacement reaction. Explain how the term "displacement" fittingly describes the reaction. 

What happens in a displacement reaction. Leaving out of the account the ionization, or separation into ions, of a part of the molecules (page 163), a displacement reaction can be explained in terms of electrons briefly as follows: 

When a molecule of hydrogen chloride, HCl, is formed, an

Hydrogen 

67 electron passes from the hydrogen atom to the chlorine atom. 

The chlorine thus receives an additional electron (a unit particle of negative electricity represented by e in the diagram) and Ho C1°) €(-). H+ Clo H+Cl H+Cl- FIG. 20. Diagram indicating the successive steps in the formation of a molecule of hydrogen chloride. becomes negatively charged; the hydrogen loses an electron and is left positively charged. Then the chlorine and hydrogen (as bodies with opposite electrical charges always do) attract each other and hold together as a molecule of HCl. Graphically the formation of the molecule may be represented as in 

Figure 20. ​Ho Ho H+C1- (H+C1-) C1- (Na+ C1- E(-) (Na+) 

(Nat 

(Na 

FIG. 21. Diagram illustrating displacement. 

The charged hydrogen atom has a greater attraction for an electron than the atoms of many metals. Consequently, when a metal atom of this kind approaches the hydrogen, an electron passes from the metal to the hydrogen. This makes the hydrogen neutral, so it is no longer attracted by the chlorine. At the same time, the metal becomes positive and is attracted, and the metal takes its place. If we suppose sodium (symbol, Na) to be the metal used and indicate electrical neutrality by zero, we can represent the reaction as in Figure 21. 

In the displacement reactions we have observed in our experiments, positively charged hydrogen takes electrons from the

Introductory Chemistry metal and goes free, leaving the metal in its place.

Introductory Chemistry 

The occlusion of gases. Platinum, palladium, and some other metals have the property of absorbing (or, one might say, of dissolving) large volumes of hydrogen. This absorption of gases by metals is known as occlusion. In the process of absorption large amounts of heat are evolved, and if oxygen is present, the hydrogen ignites. The occluding action of such metals as platinum and iridium is put to use in making self-lighting gas burners.

Does hydrogen support combustion? 

Write a word equation for the burning of hydrogen: Why was there an explosion when a lighted taper was brought to a mixture of oxygen (air) and hydrogen?

Reduction. When oxygen is taken from one substance by another substance, the process is known as reduction. A substance like hydrogen, that will take oxygen from something else and thus cause reduction, is known as a reducing agent. While the hydrogen was reducing the copper oxide to copper, the hydrogen itself was being oxidized to water. Thus it is plain that the reduction was accompanied by oxidation. The two processes are opposites, but they always accompany each other, the reduced substance having oxygen taken away from it, as the oxidized substance has oxygen added to it.

Introductory Chemistry 

Write a word equation to illustrate the action of hydrogen as a reducing agent : Since reduction is the opposite of oxidation, reduction from the point of view of the reduced substance is the gaining of electrons, or of being electronized. Thus in the last experiment the copper regains its electrons and is set free, while the hydrogen loses its electrons and unites with the oxygen. 

Explain in terms of atoms and electrons exactly what takes place when copper oxide is reduced by hydrogen. 

Uses of hydrogen. Formerly hydrogen was extensively used for filling balloons. It is the lightest substance known, and the 

United States Weather Bureau has sent hydrogen-filled balloons to a height of 39 km. But because hydrogen has the greatest tendency to diffuse of any gas (the lighter a gas, the higher its rate of diffusion), it is difficult to prevent rapid loss of it from a balloon, and other gases are now often used instead in aëronautic work. The combustibility of hydrogen is also a very objectionable quality in a gas used in dirigibles and manned balloons.

Hydrogen burns with a very hot but colorless flame. Hence it is of great value for heating purposes, but of little value for lighting unless "enriched "with other gases. The intense heat of the hydrogen-oxygen flame was mentioned on page 60. 

A third use of hydrogen with which the public is less familiar is in the preparation of certain oils for special use, or for food. 

When hydrogen is passed into the oils in the presence of metals (e.g., nickel) in a finely divided condition, the hydrogen enters into combination with the oil. In some cases this results in deodorizing bad-smelling oils, while in other cases an edible product results. Some of the substitutes for lard are made in this way, and "nut butters" are made by treating coconut oil with

Hydrogen 

73 hydrogen, thus securing a solid product. The finely divided metal acts as a catalytic agent which hastens the reaction between the hydrogen and the oils.

From our study of hydrogen we have now learned that it is the lightest and most highly diffusible of all gases. We have seen that it occurs widely in compounds, but because of the ease with which it unites with oxygen, little of it is found in the elementary state. It is present in all acids and is released from acids by some metals, as our experimental work has shown. 

Because of its affinity for oxygen it is a powerful reducing agent, and when it unites with oxygen it gives one of the hottest flames known. It is used to fill balloons, in industrial work where great heat is required, and in the "hydrogenation" of oils. 

One property possessed by hydrogen may in time have a highly important effect on conditions on the earth. Because of its lightness and its high diffusibility some scientists believe that hydrogen rises so far from the earth that it passes beyond the power of gravity to hold it and is lost into space. This of course decreases the world's water supply, for there is a great excess of oxygen over hydrogen on the earth and the amount of water is limited by the hydrogen available to form it. The moon is a desiccated body with bare seas and no enveloping clouds. In time the earth may become like it in respect to its water supply. 

Water as a promoter of chemical reactions. One important property of water is better appreciated after the action of a catalyst has been observed and a number of chemical reactions have been studied; so the discussion of it has been postponed until this time.

In many chemical reactions, water seems to act as a catalyst. 

Iron rusts more readily if it is wet. The gasoline in an automobile engine burns better and the motor gives more power on damp days than on dry days; steam can be kept up in the boilers of sawmills with green oak slabs, when firing with dry wood fails to

Introductory Chemistry furnish sufficient power; and water from a hose directed on a very hot fire causes it to burn more fiercely. Moreover, chemists have found that hydrogen and chlorine, which when heated in ordinary air unite very rapidly, will not unite at all if water is not present. Because of these and many other facts that are known to chemists, it is believed by some that without water there is no oxidation, and it has even been claimed that in the absence of water no chemical reactions of any kind take place. 

The chemical processes in living organisms are carried on by enzymes. These are catalyzers, but they are catalyzers that without water are absolutely without chemical effect. All the building-up processes in plants, by which the food supply of the world is manufactured and countless substances like wood and cellulose are formed, the processes of growth in both plants and animals, the digestive processes of animals, the oxidative processes that supply living organisms with energy, and in so far as we know all the other chemical reactions in living organisms take place only in the presence of water. Bread does not mold nor do foods decay without water. Without it all growth and life in plants and animals are at an end. As we have noted in a previous section (page 31), protoplasm is a colloidal dispersion in water, and water may very correctly be referred to as the medium in which life is carried on. 

Still a third way by which water promotes chemical activity is by carrying substances into solution. In this finely divided condition large numbers of the atoms and molecules are able to come into contact with one another, and because of this fact the speed of many chemical reactions is accelerated. This subject will be discussed later and is mentioned here only because some knowledge of it is necessary for an understanding of the real importance of water in the chemical world. 

Hydrogen peroxide. Besides water there is another compound of hydrogen and oxygen. This is hydrogen peroxide, H2O2. It is a clear, syrupy liquid, and a three per cent solution of it is com-

Hydrogen 

75 monly sold in drug stores. It breaks up easily into water and oxygen, and since light hastens the reaction it is kept in dark bottles. The reaction in the decomposition is: H₂O2 H₂O + O 

Hydrogen peroxide Water Oxygen 

In ordinary oxygen the atoms are united in pairs to form molecules, but in the above reaction (as in the decomposition of ozone) single oxygen atoms are released. Two of these atoms will unite together to make an oxygen molecule, but until they do this the unpaired atoms are exceedingly active. 

Hydrogen peroxide is used in bleaching hair, silk, feathers, and other delicate materials. It produces its bleaching effect by releasing atomic oxygen which unites with (oxidizes) the coloring matter in the material.

Hydrogen peroxide is used also to oxidize the pus and kill the germs in infected wounds. It is not advisable, however, to treat a clean wound with it, for the nascent (newborn) oxygen is destructive to the tissues. Mercurochrome and iodine are much better disinfectants for general use on cuts and wounds.

UNIT TWO SOME PRINCIPLES AND METHODS OF CHEMISTRY 

UNDER a given set of conditions an element or a compound always reacts in the same way. What the chemist finds to be true of a substance at one time or place is true of that substance at any time and place if the conditions are the same. 

This uniformity in the behavior of matter makes it possible for the chemist to bring about the reactions that he desires and so turn his knowledge to practical use. It enables him to collect a mass of dependable facts concerning the substances that he knows; to group and classify these facts with assurance; to formulate general statements (the "laws" of chemistry) about how matter acts; and to use the imagination constructively in formulating theories for the explanation of the known facts of the subject. It also makes it possible for him to employ mathematical calculations in his work, which could not be done if the chemical properties of substances were inconstant. 

In our next studies we shall become familiar with some of the more general ideas that chemists hold concerning matter; with certain of the facts on which these ideas are based and some of the deductions that must follow if these ideas are accepted; and with the symbols, equations, and mathematical calculations that chemists employ in their work. All these are a part of a first course in chemistry; for an education in any science implies an understanding of its theories, facts, and methods of work. 77

SECTION ONE IMPORTANT LAWS OF CHEMISTRY 

In our experiments we have found that in chemical changes the original substances entering into the reactions disappear and that new substances with new properties appear. For example, when water is decomposed into hydrogen and oxygen the water vanishes and substances totally unlike water are given off. After oxygen has united with sulfur, both the oxygen and the sulfur have disappeared and a new substance is present. 

In a chemical transformation is matter created or destroyed? 

Or do the atoms of which the substances are composed merely change from one combination to another, and all reappear in the new substances that are formed?

Law of conservation of mass. The work of Lavoisier and the work which he inspired helped to establish one of the important laws of chemistry. This law may be stated thus: In a chemical reaction there is no gain or loss of weight. The same idea may be expressed in another way by saying that in chemical changes matter is neither created nor destroyed, but merely changed to other forms. The exact amount of material in the original substances entering into a chemical reaction is always found in the new substances that are formed. This law is called the law of conservation of mass, or the law of conservation of matter. 

The law of conservation of mass is in accordance with the theories we have given as to the structure of matter, since all the electrons and atoms present before a chemical change are supposed to be still existent after the reaction. In a chemical reaction the atoms change partners, but in the change in combinations there is no increase or decrease in the number or weight of the atoms involved (page 38). 

Law of definite proportions. Does a definite amount of a given substance always unite with the same amount of a second substance in forming a given chemical compound? Or, stating 78

Two Great Laws of Chemistry 

79 the question differently, does a chemical compound always contain the elements of which it is composed in exactly the same proportions? Is water, for example, always composed of the same relative amounts of oxygen and hydrogen, measuring these gases by weight? The work of Lavoisier helped to answer these questions also and led to the statement of the law of definite proportions by weight.

Every chemical compound has a definite composition by weight, or a compound always has the same composition. 

According to this law, we may expect to get definite amounts by weight of each of the elements that compose a compound when given amounts of the compounds are decomposed; or, when elements unite to form a given weight of a compound we may expect a definite weight of each element to enter into the compound. For example, when carbon is burned, 12 grams of it always unite with 32 grams of oxygen and (according to the law of conservation of mass) 44 grams of carbon dioxide are formed. We might represent this reaction thus: carbon + oxygen (12 grams) (32 grams) carbon dioxide (44 grams) The law of definite proportions follows as a corollary if the atomic and molecular hypotheses are accepted. If every molecule of a compound has the same number and kinds of atoms in it, we should expect the proportionate weights of the elements in the compound to be always the same. 

When we reach a clear-cut conclusion to which no exceptions can be found concerning anything in realm of nature, we announce the conclusion and call it a law. Hence, the so-called "laws of nature" are not laws at all in the sense in which we ordinarily use the term "law," but are merely statements of what we have discovered to be true about the physical universe. Two of the most important laws of chemistry have been given above. They are general conclusions concerning the behavior of substances in chemical reactions, based on a great number of observed facts.

Introductory Chemistry 

Three other important laws of chemistry. Three other laws that are important in physics and in the chemical study of gases will be given here. The explanations, proofs, and applications of these laws will be found in the first unit of Part Two of this text. 

I. When the pressure remains constant, the volume of a gas varies directly as the absolute temperature. This is Charles' Law. (See pages 317-320.) 

2. The volume of a gas varies inversely as the pressure, provided the temperature remains the same. This is Boyle's Law. (See pages 321–323.) 

3. When in chemical reactions gases combine and form other gases, the volumes of the gases used and produced always bear a ratio to each other that can be expressed in small whole numbers. This is Gay-Lussac's Law of Gas Volumes. (See pages 329-330.)

Avogadro's Hypothesis. This hypothesis is stated thus: 

Equal volumes of all gases under the same conditions of temperature and pressure contain the same number of molecules. (See pages 330-331.)

The laws that we have given in this section are statements of facts that scientists have observed. Dalton's Atomic Hypothesis and Avogadro's Hypothesis are important theories that have been put forward to explain these facts.

SECTION TWO SYMBOLS AND FORMULAS 

WHEN We have occasion to write the name of anything again and again, we usually fall into the habit of abbreviating it. 

Chemists are like other persons in having this trait, and standardized abbreviations have been worked out that are understood and used by the entire chemical world. These will now be discussed.

CHEMICAL SYMBOLS 

H is used for hydrogen and O for oxygen. These are known as the symbols of these elements. For many elements the first letter of the name is used: C for carbon, S for sulfur, I for iodine. 

But the names of a number of elements may begin with the same letter; so two letters of the name are sometimes used. Co for cobalt, Ca for calcium, Cl for chlorine, Cd for cadmium, and Zn for zinc are examples of such symbols. For still other elements the symbols are formed by abbreviations of the Latin name, as 

Fe for iron (Latin: ferrum, iron), Cu for copper (Latin: cuprum, copper), and Na for sodium (Latin: natrium, sodium). In chemistry, symbols are abbreviations for the names of elements. 

To the chemist the symbol not only stands for the name of an element, but also signifies one atom of the element and a weight of it equal to its atomic weight. O stands for oxygen, one atom of oxygen, and 16 parts by weight of oxygen.

FORMULAS 

NaCl is the formula for common salt. The formula means that in a molecule of salt there is one atom of sodium and one of chlorine. H2O is the formula for water. It means that in a molecule of water there are two atoms of hydrogen and one of oxygen. H2SO4 is the formula for sulfuric acid. It means that 81

Introductory Chemistry in a molecule of this acid there are two atoms of hydrogen, one of sulfur, and four of oxygen. A formula is a group of symbols used to represent the composition of a molecule. 

Subscripts and coefficients of formulas. When more than one atom of an element is found in a molecule, the number is denoted by a subscript. When no subscript is expressed, a subscript of one is understood. In chemical equations the coefficients of the formulas indicate the number of molecules taking part in the reaction. 3KCIO, means three molecules of potassium chlorate with three atoms of oxygen in each molecule. 5H₂O means five molecules of water. How many atoms of hydrogen in 5H₂O?

Radicals. A radical in chemistry is a group of atoms which acts like a unit in chemical reactions. The whole group goes through the reaction without breaking up, the group in this respect acting much like a single atom. The sulfate (SO4), the nitrate (NO3), and the ammonium (NH4) and hydroxide (OH) groups are examples of radicals.

When a molecule contains more than one of any given radical, the radical is enclosed in parentheses and a subscript is used; e.g., Pb(NO3)2; Ca(OH)2. Sometimes parentheses are used to indicate that a number of atoms in a molecule are grouped as a radical, as (NH4) Cl; Ca(CO3).

Atomic and molecular weights. By atomic weight is meant the weight of an atom relative to the weight of an atom of oxygen. 

The oxygen atom is assigned a weight of 16, and the atomic weights of other elements (see inside of back cover) are determined in terms of this standard. Hydrogen is one sixteenth as heavy as oxygen and is assigned a weight of 1; sulfur is twice as heavy and has a weight of 32.

By molecular weight is meant the weight of a molecule measured in terms of the atomic weight of oxygen. By evidence that is in accord with Gay-Lussac's Law of Gas Volumes, it has been proved that the molecules of most gaseous elements each

Symbols and Formulas 

83 contain two atoms; so the molecular weight of oxygen is 32 (page 

331). The acceptance of Avogadro's Hypothesis has made it easy to compare the molecular weights of gases (pages 335 and 337), and it has been found that the weight in grams of 22.4 liters of any gas is equal to its molecular weight (page 339). 

Calculating the percentage composition from the formula. To calculate the percentage composition of a compound from its formula, the atomic weight (pages 334-335, 340, and inside of back cover) of each element in the compound must be known. 

Then proceed as follows: 

1. Find the parts by weight of each element by multiplying its atomic weight by the number of atoms found in the formula. 

2. Add the products found in 1. 

3. Divide each product found in 1 by the sum found in 2 and multiply each quotient by 100; the result will be the respective percentage of each element in the compound. 

Applying these steps to the finding of the percentage composition of sulfuric acid, H2SO4, we have: 

IX 2 = (1) 32 X 1 = 16 X 4 (2) 

2 parts by weight of hydrogen 32 parts by weight of sulfur 

64 parts by weight of oxygen 98

Determination of formulas. To determine the formula of water, we must find the number of hydrogen atoms compared to the number of oxygen atoms in the water molecule. From our previous work and our calculations above we know that water consists of 11 per cent hydrogen and 883 per cent oxygen. The hydrogen is composed of atoms having a weight of 1; the oxygen of atoms with a weight of 16. 

Hence, if we divide the percentages of hydrogen and oxygen in water by the atomic weights of these elements, we get the relative number of atoms of each element in water: 

II ÷ I = II.II 

888 ÷ 16 = 5.55

Therefore, there are 11.11 atoms of hydrogen in water for every 

5.55 atoms of oxygen, or 2 atoms of hydrogen to one of oxygen. 

Thus H2O is the formula for water derived by this method. 

From the above it follows that the formula for any compound may be found in the following manner : 

1. Determine the percentage by weight of each element in the compound by careful analysis.

3. Divide each quotient obtained in 2 by the smallest of these quotients (greatest common divisor).

By careful analysis the percentage by weight of each element in the compound is found to be: Potassium. Chlorine Oxygen 31.90% 28.92% 39.16%

SECTION THREE CHEMICAL NOMENCLATURE; VALENCE 

CHEMICAL Compounds have not all been named according to an exact system, but in so far as it has been possible to have them do so, most of these names show the elements in a compound and also the proportion of each element in the compound. 

An understanding of the rules followed in naming compounds is therefore a great aid in the study of chemistry, and a brief discussion of them will be introduced at this time. 

Names of compounds containing two elements. The names and formulas of the compounds listed below will suggest some of the facts that it is important to know concerning compounds of this class: 

Sodium chloride. 

Potassium chloride . 

Ferrous chloride. 

Ferric chloride

NaCl 

Mercurous oxide

KCI Mercuric oxide Hg2O HgO FeCl2 Carbon monoxide CO FeCl3 Carbon dioxide CO2 On examining this list, the following points are evident : 

I. The names of the two elements have been combined to form the name of the compound. 

2. The name of a metal, if present, is always placed first. 

3. The second word in each name ends in ide. 4. If there are two compounds of the same two elements, they are distinguished by a different ending in the first word of the name or by prefixing a syllable to the last word.

In the list of names above it is seen that FeCl2 is called ferous chloride and FeCl3 is called ferric chloride. In general, the ic ending is given to the compound containing more of the second element. Thus the molecule of ferric chloride contains three atoms of chlorine to one of iron, while the molecule of ferrous chloride contains two atoms of chlorine to one of iron. 

The prefixes mon (one), di (two), and tri (three) used in con- 87

Introductory Chemistry nection with the second word in the name of the compound also indicate different ratios of the elements in the compound. The carbon monoxide molecule contains one atom of oxygen, and the carbon dioxide molecule two. 

Names of compounds containing radicals. A radical has been defined as a group of elements that generally act as a unit in a chemical reaction (page 82). These groups have special names. 

The (OH) group is known as the hydroxyl radical or group; (NH) as the ammonium radical; (SO4) as the sulfate radical; (SO3) as the sulfite radical; (NO3) as the nitrate radical; (NO2) as the nitrite radical. The name and formula of a compound containing each of these radicals follow:

Cu(SO4) Cu(SO3) Pb(NO3)2 Pb(NO2)2

The name of the radical always enters into the name of the compound of which the radical is a part. A substance that contains the (SO4) group is a sulfate, and one that contains the (NO3) group is a nitrate. It will be noted that the compounds with names ending in ate contain more oxygen than those with names ending in ite. These compounds are common among salts. The relation of ate and ite compounds to corresponding acids is discussed later (page 157). 

Compounds containing three elements. No definite rule can be given for the terminations of names of compounds containing three elements. If the names of all three elements are used in making up the name of the compound, this name may end in ide, as sodium aluminum fluoride, NaзAlF6. If a compound contains three elements, one of which is oxygen, the word oxygen " is often omitted from the name, and usually the

VALENCE 

Valence is the combining capacity of an atom or a radical the capacity to hold other atoms and radicals in combination. 

This is measured in terms of the capacity to combine with the hydrogen atom or its equivalent. From our previous studies we have seen that:

I atom of chlorine combines with 1 atom of hydrogen to form HCI I atom of oxygen combines with 2 atoms of hydrogen to form H₂O I atom of nitrogen combines with 3 atoms of hydrogen to form NH3 From the above it is seen that the valence of chlorine, oxygen, and nitrogen are 1, 2, and 3, respectively; or they are univalent, bivalent, and trivalent. Carbon unites with four atoms of hydrogen, and it is therefore quadrivalent. A nitrate radical is univalent, as is proved by its combining with one atom of hydrogen to form HNO3. The valence of the sulfate group is two, as is shown by its union with two atoms of hydrogen in H2SO4. 

The valence of a substance that forms no compounds with hydrogen is found by comparing its combining capacity with that of substances that do unite with hydrogen; for any element or

Introductory Chemistry radical that unites with hydrogen becomes, so to speak, standardized and can be used for measuring the valence of other substances. One chlorine atom combines with one sodium atom to form NaCl. We conclude that the valence of sodium is one, because in combining with chlorine it shows a valence equal to that of chlorine, which we know to be one. 

Similarly, we reason that the ammonium radical, NH4, must have a valence of one, since it combines with one atom of chlorine to form (NH) Cl. Likewise we deduce from the formulas 

CaO and CaSO4 that calcium has a valence of two, for both the element O and the radical SO4, when combined with hydrogen, have a valence of two. The compound Al2O3 shows that aluminum has a valence of three; for the three oxygen atoms have a total valence of six, which is shared by two aluminum atoms. 

The explanation of valence. In terms of the electrical theory of matter, valence is explained as follows: The valence of an atom depends on its power to gain or lose electrons. The hydrogen atom easily loses one electron. It then has one excess positive charge of electricity and is said to have a positive valence of The oxygen atom takes up two electrons and then has an excess of two negative charges. Hence, it has a negative valence of two the power to attract and hold two atoms (e.g., hydrogen atoms) each of which has a positive valence of one, or a single atom (e.g., a calcium atom) that has a positive valence of two. one.

This conception of valence divides elements and radicals into those with positive and those with negative valence. Hydrogen and, generally speaking, the metals lose electrons and form the positive group. The non-metals take on electrons and are the negative group. There are some elements, however (e.g., phosphorus), the atoms of which can reach chemical stability either by losing or taking on electrons. These may exhibit either positive or negative valence and may exhibit the properties either of metals or non-metals in their chemical reaction.

Chemical Nomenclature; Valence 

91 Would you expect combinations between elements having the same kind of valence (positive or negative), or between elements of opposite groups?

Does an electrically neutral atom have valence, or does an atom acquire valence only after it has gained or lost an electron?

I atom Al liberates Some elements have more than one valence. In carbon monoxide, CO, carbon has a valence of two, while in carbon dioxide, CO2, it has a valence of four. Introductory Chemistry 

Energy changes in chemical reactions. Energy is defined as the capacity to do work. It exists in the form of motion, heat, light, and electrical energy. We cannot create or destroy energy, but it is easily changed from one form to another. The motion of a hammer is transformed to heat when it strikes a nail and makes the nail hot. Light falling on an object and being absorbed by it is transformed to heat. The energy of the electric current becomes heat in the filament of a lamp and the heat is transformed to light.

In a chemical reaction heat is either absorbed or released. In photosynthesis (page 59) the energy of the sunlight is absorbed and is stored in the molecules that are built up. Energy that is thus stored is called chemical energy. Coal and petroleum contain chemical energy stored by the plants of millions of years ago, and this energy is transformed to heat and light when we burn them.

Chemistry concerns itself chiefly with matter, and leaves energy to physics; but it is well to hold in mind that with every change in the grouping of atoms there is an accompanying storing or release of energy.

SECTION FOUR CHEMICAL EQUATIONS AND CALCULATIONS 

MATHEMATICS is the handmaiden of the natural sciences. It is at once a method of presenting facts concerning the physical universe and a system for learning new facts about this universe through deductions from the facts already known. As in all other sciences that have progressed sufficiently to allow the results of investigations to be stated in any exact way, mathematical methods are much used in chemistry. This section will be devoted to a discussion of the equations and the calculations that the chemist constantly employs. 

Writing and balancing chemical equations. Thus far we have indicated chemical reactions thus: water oxygen + hydrogen

In chemical equations symbols and formulas are used instead of words. If we substitute these for the words in the equation above, it becomes : H₂O H2 + O2 To make this equation correct and complete, however, from the chemist's point of view, it must be balanced. That is, the number and kinds of atoms on one side of the equation must be the same as on the other. The balancing of the atom account is easily effected by placing two molecules of water on the left, forming two hydrogen molecules thus: 2H2O2H2 + O2 Zinc and hydrochloric acid react to form zinc chloride, ZnCl2, and hydrogen.

Zn + HCl ZnCl2 + H₂ - → This equation too violates the law of conservation of matter by having more atoms on the right than on the left. Find a way to balance it and write it in correct form. 95

Introductory Chemistry 

Substances which are present but which do not undergo any change during the reaction are not represented in the equation. 

A catalytic agent or a medium in which the reaction takes place (e.g., water when two chemicals in solution react in it) is an example of such a substance.

Classes of chemical reactions. A convenient and everyday classification of the chemical reactions thus far studied is as follows: (1) direct combination or synthesis; (2) decomposition or analysis; (3) simple replacement or substitution; (4) double replacement. 

Working over the equations that follow will not only give familiarity with examples of each of these kinds of reactions, but will give practice in writing chemical equations and review the work already gone over.

Double replacement. A reaction of this type is illustrated in the following equation:

Ag(NO3) + NaCl 

Silver nitrate Sodium chloride

Na(NO3) + AgCl (15) 

Sodium nitrate 

Silver chloride

The silver and sodium have exchanged places; hence the term double replacement.

The above classification is not a strictly scientific one according to our present conception of the structure of matter. In terms of electrons, a classification of chemical reactions is as follows: 

1. Reactions in which there is a transfer of electrons. This group includes the first three classes given above. In all the reactions in this class, there is electronization and deëlectronization of the reacting substances. In recent years the terms oxidation and reduction have been extended to cover all these reactions, even though oxygen is not one of the reacting substances. It is confusing, however, to speak of a reaction like the union of iron and chlorine as an oxidation-reduction process,

Introductory Chemistry and we prefer to use the more descriptive terms electronization and deëlectronization. 

2. Reactions in which there is no transfer of electrons. This class includes only those reactions which are classified above under double replacements, and which differ from all other chemical reactions in that no transfer of electrons is involved.

Equations as a basis of chemical calculations. Chemical equations show the relative number of molecules of each substance involved in the reactions and the number of atoms in each molecule. Therefore, it is possible to figure out from an

equation the relative weights of the reacting substances and of the products formed, since we know the relative weights of the atoms and can find the weight of each molecule by adding the weights of the atoms in it. The way chemists make these calculations is best shown by examples. We shall first take up calculations involving weight.

Calculations involving volume only. It is easier to measure gases by volume than by weight, and the volumes of gases involved in chemical reactions can be calculated even more easily than the weights. All calculations for volume are based on 

Avogadro's Hypothesis (page 330), that equal volumes of gases under the same conditions contain the same number of molecules. 

With this assumption, suppose that we wish to know the volume of the water vapor formed when 3 liters of oxygen are exploded with an excess of hydrogen. The equation is: 2H2 + O2 = 2H₂O

It is evident that for every oxygen molecule used, two molecules of water vapor are formed; so 3 liters (vol. oxygen): x (vol. water vapor) :: 1 : 3_I x 2 x = 6

To the chemist a knowledge of chemical equations and of chemical calculations is indispensable. The industrial chemist in particular employs equations and calculations in his daily work, for by their aid he can sit in his laboratory and figure out the exact amounts of different materials that must be used to get a certain amount of product with the least possible waste. 

It is possible for him to compute also the amounts of valuable by-products that may be in the wastes and to arrive at possible profits in new lines of manufacture by figuring the amounts of materials required and of the products that should be produced. 

Like other scientists, the chemist saves himself the trouble of going through processes by using mathematics to find what the results would be if he did go through them.

UNIT THREE THE ATMOSPHERE 

ALL gases are lighter than the solid part of the earth or the water of the seas and lakes. Hence, those substances which in nature are in a gaseous condition have been collected in an enveloping layer outside the lithosphere and the hydrosphere. 

This enveloping layer is called the atmosphere. 

From observations on auroral displays, from the height at which meteors begin to glow, and from other facts it has been calculated that the atmosphere has a height of more than 200 miles. Indeed, it probably extends upward far beyond this height; hydrogen molecules are even supposed to wander off so far into space that they pass beyond the power of gravitation to recall them and are lost to earth. But although the volume of the atmosphere is very great, its mass is quite small as compared with that of the solid and the liquid portions of the earth. 

The atmosphere presents a multitude of problems for the scientific worker, and scientists from many fields have attacked these problems, each from his own point of view. The work of the meteorologist centers in the temperature and movements of the atmosphere and in the precipitation of water vapor from it. 

The physicist has for long used it in his studies of gas behavior. 

The biologist thinks of it in its relations to plant and animal life. 

The chemist concerns himself chiefly with its composition and the properties of the elements and compounds that are found in it. Our studies of the atmosphere will of course be made from the chemist's point of view. 105

SECTION ONE COMPOSITION OF THE AIR 

WE have seen that, when substances are burned in air, the same products are formed as when they are burned in pure oxygen. We may conclude, therefore, that air contains oxygen. 

But since the burning is less active in the air than in pure oxygen, there must be present in the atmosphere some gas or gases which do not support combustion. In the next experiment we shall try to find approximately the proportion of oxygen in air. 

Experiment 20 

OBJECT. To make a quantitative determination of the percentage by volume of oxygen in air.

Set up the apparatus for carrying out the experiment, as shown in 

Figure 24.

Fit the test tube, which holds about 20 cm., with a two-hole rubber stopper, as shown in position A. In one hole place a piece of glass tubing and connect it to a funnel by a rubber tube carrying a screw clamp. Cut a glass rod to fill the other hole in the stopper, but do not insert it until later in the experiment. 

The point of the experiment is to take a definite volume of air in the test tube, and let some substances in the form of a liquid enter this test tube which will absorb or react with the oxygen and thereby separate the oxygen from the other gases in the air. The liquid will continue to enter the test tube until all the oxygen is absorbed, and when this has happened the volume of the liquid in the test tube will be equal to the volume of oxygen in the air. An alkaline solution of pyrogallic acid is the substance which is usually used for absorbing the oxygen. 

Secure a 15 cm. test tube full of this solution from the instructor and keep the tube stoppered until it is ready to be used. Carbon dioxide in air. Carbon dioxide is always being given off into the air, but the amount of it in outdoor air is by volume only about three parts in ten thousand. On account of air currents its distribution is thought to be uniform up to a height of II km., but it is one of the heavy gases of the air and is

supposed to be absent from the rare upper atmosphere.

It decreases slightly in abundance from the equator to the poles.

Recalling the facts already learned in this section, devise a

ΙΙΟ 

Introductory Chemistry method by means of which it would be possible by one operation to remove from the air three of the gases thus far found in it.

Other gases in the air. If the oxygen, carbon dioxide, and water vapor are removed from air, the residue is chiefly nitrogen. 

It was long supposed that this residue was composed entirely of nitrogen, but if this were so, a liter of it should have the same density as a liter of pure nitrogen. When this fact was put to the test, however, it was found that the density of a given quantity of gas obtained by the elimination of carbon dioxide, water vapor, and oxygen from air was not the same as the density of an equal quantity of nitrogen obtained from some source other than air.

This fact was known for a number of years, but it was not until 1892 that scientists began to believe that this difference in density was due to the presence of an additional gas in the atmosphere. 

Then, in 1894, Lord Rayleigh, an English physicist, and Sir William Ramsay, a British chemist, attempted a series of experiments to prove if possible the existence of another gas in the air.

They reasoned that if the water vapor, carbon dioxide, and oxygen were removed from air and if the nitrogen were then removed, anything which was left would be a new gas. After several trials it was discovered that magnesium would remove the nitrogen. Accordingly, the first three gases were eliminated; then the magnesium was united with the nitrogen, and a gas still remained. Because its most outstanding characteristic is its inactivity, this gas received the name argon (Greek: argos, inactive). Quantitatively, it was found that argon makes up by volume about .94 per cent of the air. 

This victory inspired Ramsay and Rayleigh to continue their efforts and discover, if possible, still other atmospheric gases. 

The inactivity of argon prevented a further separation by chemical means. Hence, they resorted to a separation by fractional

III 

Kadel and Herbert FIG. 26. A chemist of the U. S. Bureau of Mines working on the recovery of helium. The natural gas containing the helium is cooled, and the constituents of the gas other than helium liquefied, leaving an impure helium. This is passed over charcoal at the temperature of liquid air, which adsorbs all the remaining gases except helium and any residuum of hydrogen. distillation of liquid air. This resulted in discovering four other rare gases; namely, helium (Greek: helios, the sun), neon (Greek: neos, new), krypton (Greek: kryptos, hidden), and xenon (Greek: xenos, foreign or strange). These four other gases combined constitute only .002 per cent of the total volume of air.

These rarer gases are all inactive. They form no compounds at all and their molecules are monatomic. The inactivity of helium, together with its low density, makes it an excellent gas for filling balloons and dirigibles. Some natural gases contain as high as 3 per cent of helium. It is separated by liquefaction and then fractional distillation of the gas. Helium has a very low boiling point (— 269°).

The atomic weight of helium is four, while the atomic weight of hydrogen is one; but since there are two atoms in a molecule of hydrogen and only one in a molecule of helium, what are the relative densities of the two gases? 

Would helium or hydrogen have the greater lifting power in balloons? Why?

Composition of the air. Including only the chief constituents of the air, the percentages by volume and by weight are: 

BY VOLUME BY WEIGHT 

Nitrogen Oxygen 75.5 23.2 Argon .94 1.3 

Carbon dioxide .03 0.04

Air a mixture and not a compound. Is air a mixture or a compound? The following are proofs that air is a mixture: 

1. Every chemical compound has a definite composition; but the composition of air varies. From the above table we can see that the proportions by weight in which the components are contained in air are not integral multiples of the atomic weights. 

2. The proportions by volume in which the gases are found in the air are not so simple as the proportions in cases of chemical combinations. 

3. The components of the air retain their own properties, and the properties of air are the mean of those of the components. 

4. The components of air may be separated by mechanical means. 

5. When nitrogen and oxygen are mixed in the ratio in which they are found in the air, no chemical action occurs, as is evidenced by the fact that there is no heat change but the mixture has the properties of ordinary air. 

Liquefaction of gases. The mention of liquid air, in connection with the isolation of the rarer gases of the atmosphere, and of the liquefaction of natural gas in the obtaining of helium,

makes appropriate a discussion of the conditions and methods by which a gas can be reduced to a liquid, Most gases (water vapor is a notable exception) require low temperature and high pressure for their liquefaction. For every gas there is a temperature above which it cannot be liquefied, no matter how great the pressure, and likewise there is a pressure which must be attained before any available temperature will bring about liquefaction. These specific temperatures and pressures are termed the critical temperature and the critical pressure. 

Manufacture of liquid air. In the apparatus for the liquefaction of air the critical pressure is produced and maintained by means of an air compressor. The critical temperature is attained by forcing the air through long, coiled, ice-cooled pipes, and then 

Research Laboratory, General Electric Co. 

FIG. 27. Liquid air.

Introductory Chemistry allowing the gas to escape from a very minute opening, called a needle valve. As it escapes from this valve, it expands suddenly. This sudden expansion causes sufficient lowering of the temperature for part of the gas to be condensed. The remaining uncondensed gas is led back to the storage tank in such a way as to cool the air which is under pressure in the pipes. This continuous process produces the liquid air. 

FIG. 28. 

A Dewar flask. 

When liquid air is allowed to evaporate, the nitrogen passes off at 

195.5°, while the oxygen does 

not appreciably evaporate until the temperature reaches 182.5°. 

This fact makes possible the production of both commercial oxygen and reasonably pure nitrogen in one operation. 

Liquid air is shipped from one place to another in a doublewalled vessel called a Dewar flask. The air in the space between the two walls of the flask is exhausted by a vacuum pump. The inner wall, within which the liquid air is held, is thus almost entirely surrounded by a vacuum, and as a vacuum is a very poor conductor of heat, the liquid air evaporates slowly. Evaporation is further prevented by silvering the flask. This bright surface reflects the external heat instead of absorbing it, as a dull surface would. The well-known thermos bottle is a modification of the Dewar flask.

The atmosphere may be considered as a mixture of those elements and compounds which under the conditions existing on earth are in a gaseous state. Oxygen is the most important of these substances. By volume it makes up about one fifth of air; but a vast amount of oxygen exists in a combined state, and it is easy to believe that in coming ages some of this oxygen might be released or a part of that which is now free might be tied up. The other chief constituent of air is nitrogen. 

Together with the inert gases, oxygen and nitrogen make up the components of the air that are present in constant propor-

tions. Carbon dioxide, water vapor, and dust make up a second class of constituents present in variable proportions. Sulfur dioxide is a representative of a third class of components which are present only accidentally.

SECTION TWO NITROGEN 

THE total amount of nitrogen on the earth is relatively small, and it is classed by the chemist as an inert substance. 

Yet it is one of the few elements that enter into the composition of protoplasm; nitrogen compounds are necessary for the life and growth of both plants and animals, and hence are important in foods and fertilizers; great classes of substances (e.g., dyes and explosives) produced in industrial and commercial chemistry are nitrogen compounds; and nitric acid is so important a reagent in chemical manufacturing that thousands of tons of it are used each year. For these reasons the chemistry of nitrogen is exceedingly important and holds in the public mind a larger place than the chemistry of any other element. 

Occurrence of nitrogen. We have seen that nitrogen occurs abundantly in the free state in the air. In the combined state it is found in a few inorganic compounds occurring in nature. 

Considerable amounts of the potassium salt, KNO3, known as saltpeter, are found in India, and there are extensive deposits of the sodium salt, NaNO,, known as Chile saltpeter, in South America.

Nitrogen is found also in great numbers of organic compounds. 

As has been noted, it is an important constituent of the living protoplasm itself and proteins are about 15 per cent nitrogen. 

Fertile soils, where organic matter is decomposing, always contain nitrogen salts, which are necessary for the growth of plants. 

As we shall see later, the removal of nitrogen from the air and its fixation in nitrogen compounds is, in nature, effected chiefly through the activities of certain groups of bacteria. 

Preparation. In the last section we learned that nitrogen can be obtained from the air by two different methods (see pages 1IO and 114); but the nitrogen obtained by either one of these methods 116

Nitrogen 117 is more or less impure because of the presence of other gases of the air. A product more nearly pure can be obtained by releasing the nitrogen from one of its compounds.

B. Chemical properties. Insert a lighted taper in a bottle of nitrogen; then answer the following questions concerning the chemical properties of nitrogen: 

1. Does nitrogen burn?

Density of gases as compared with air. If the mouth of a bottle filled with hydrogen is brought under the mouth of an inverted bottle filled with air, the hydrogen can be poured upward.

Similarly, a heavy gas can be poured downward, and in this manner the densities of light and heavy gases relative to air may be roughly determined. But when a gas has a density close to that of air, the simplest and most accurate way of comparing these densities is by comparison of the molecular weights of the gases.

Air (neglecting the minor constituents) is a mixture of nitrogen and oxygen molecules in the proportion of 4 to 1, and hence we may consider a unit composed of 4 nitrogen molecules and 1 oxygen molecule as representative of air. The weight of these 

5 molecules is (4 × 28) + 32 = 144, and their average weight is 

144 ÷ 5 28.8.

Nitrogen 119 Since air is a mixture, it is of course not correct to speak of it as having a true molecular weight, but we can still refer to the average weight of the molecules composing it. Thus we can think of the average molecular weight of air as being 29, and can compare the molecular weights of other gases with this average.

Importance and uniqueness of nitrogen. Our experiments have shown that nitrogen neither burns nor supports combustion, and from these and other similar experiments it might be supposed that it does not combine with other substances. Yet we have released it by heating sodium nitrite, and the enormous number of known compounds of this seemingly inert element shows that it can combine with other substances. Moreover, the action of dynamite and other high explosives, which are nitrogen compounds, would on first thought suggest chemical activity of a high order.

The seemingly contradictory chemical properties of nitrogen are explained by the fact that nitrogen atoms have a strong attraction for each other and a relatively weak affinity for atoms of other kinds. Hence, nitrogen as an element is inert and nitrogen compounds tend to break up easily and allow the nitrogen to return to the elementary form. This lability of the compounds give them many unusual and valuable features. Without it living protoplasm, with all the complex chemical reactions carried on in and by it, and the high explosive which acts by a sudden disintegration of its molecules, would be alike impossible. 

1. Where does nitrogen occur?

SECTION THREE ACIDS OF NITROGEN; NITRATES 

THE two most important acids of nitrogen are nitric acid, HNO3, and nitrous acid, HNO2. The latter is an unstable compound that is of little importance in chemistry as a final product. 

Nitric acid is one of the most important of all chemical reagents, and we shall make a careful study of it. 

NITRIC ACID 

Nitric acid, HNO3, is formed when organic matter decays, but it at once comes into contact with minerals in the soil that replace the hydrogen of the acid and form salts. Hence, the acid is not found in nature, although its salts (nitrates) are present in all fertile soils. 

Preparation. Nitric acid may be produced by oxidizing ammonia. Fine platinum wire gauze is used as a catalyzer. 

Since the ammonia can be formed by the union of hydrogen and atmospheric nitrogen (page 144), this process holds great possibilities. The method has been known since 1830, but it was not developed commercially until the recent war. At that time the great demand for a large amount of nitric acid, which could be produced quickly, was met by using this process. 

Complete and balance the equations: 

N₂+ H2

NH3 + O2 HNO3 + H₂O The usual laboratory method of preparation is very similar to the commercial method which has been largely employed in the past and is still being used. It consists essentially of heating a mixture of sulfuric acid and a nitrate. 121

Could you use any other nitrate except sodium nitrate in the preparation of nitric acid? 

Give reason for your answer.

When the sulfuric acid comes in contact with the nitrate in the above experiment, some nitric acid is formed. This boils at about 86°, and the sulfuric acid at about 340°; so the nitric acid is vaporized first and driven over into the condenser. This leaves the undiluted sulfuric acid in contact with the nitrate and more nitric acid is formed. This process continues until either all the nitrate or all the sulfuric acid is used up. 

Could you use any acid except sulfuric in the manufacture of nitric acid?

This method is employed not only in the preparation of nitric acid, but in the preparation of other volatile acids also.

Introductory Chemistry solutions of all acids. Taste is, of course, listed as a physical property, but the underlying cause of the characteristic taste of acids is the chemical effect of the acid on the nerves of taste, which gives a sensation of sourness. SALTS OF NITRIC ACID (NITRATES) 

Nitrates are the salts of nitric acid. Of the two nitrates occurring in nature, sodium nitrate, found in large quantities in Chile, is the most abundant; the other, potassium nitrate, is found in smaller quantities, and is thus more expensive. Calcium nitrate occurs in small quantities in the soil. 

In the preceding experiment we learned that sodium nitrate can be made from nitric acid by neutralizing the acid with sodium hydroxide.

SECTION FOUR AMMONIA 

THE Common hydrogen compound of nitrogen is the gas ammonia, NH3. When this gas is run into water, ammonia water is the product. This contains ammonia gas in solution, and ammonium hydroxide, which is formed by the chemical union of the ammonia and water. 

NH3 + H2O NH(OH) 

In nature ammonia is constantly being formed by the decay of protein materials. The popular name for ammonia water, 'spirit of hartshorn," comes down from the time when ammonia was obtained by distilling the horns of animals. 

The ammonium radical. The nitrogen atom in ammonia is carrying three hydrogen atoms. It can take on a fourth hydrogen atom, and when it does so the ammonium radical, NH4, is formed. This group of atoms has a positive valence of one, and chemically it has many of the properties of a metal. As we have seen above, it unites with the OH radical to form a hydroxide, and by combining with chlorine and the sulfate and nitrate radicals, it forms salts that are very similar to the corresponding salts of metals like sodium and potassium. Because the NH radical acts like a metal, its name has the ending ium. 

Commercial preparation of ammonia. By several methods it is possible to synthesize ammonia by bringing about the direct union of hydrogen and nitrogen. These methods will be discussed in the next section. In the future they will doubtless add greatly to our supply of this valuable compound, but at present by far the greater part of the ammonia made for commercial use is obtained as a by-product in the manufacture of coke and gas. Coal is composed of plant material which has undergone partial decomposition. Its chief constituent is carbon, but some of the nitrogen and hydrogen of the original plant sub- 133

Introductory Chemistry stances remain. By destructive distillation of coal a series of complicated reactions is effected by which a portion of the hydrogen and nitrogen are united to form ammonia. This is separated from the other gases driven off with it by "washing " (passing the gas through water). The ammonia goes into solution in the water and remains in it, while most of the other gases pass on.

This" ammoniacal liquor" is then distilled, and the fairly fine ammonia gas produced is reabsorbed in water or in an acid solution. When dilute sulfuric acid is used for this purpose, the ammonia unites with the acid to form ammonium sulfate, 

(NH4)2SO4. 

Laboratory preparation. For the laboratory preparation of ammonia we shall use ammonium sulfate which has been prepared by the commercial method described above.

Uses of ammonia. Ammonia is one of our fundamental chemicals. "Spirit of ammonia" has long been employed as a stimulant in medicine, and large quantities of ammonia water are used in the household for cleansing purposes because, like other bases, ammonium hydroxide acts on grease and, unlike other bases, any surplus removes itself from the field of cleansing by decomposition and evaporation. It is being increasingly employed in the manufacture of nitric acid (page 121), and about half a million tons of ammonium salts are used annually in the United States for fertilizers. The easy liquefaction of ammonia gas causes it to be extensively employed in the manufacture of ice and the maintenance of low temperatures in refrigerating plants.

SECTION FIVE THE FIXATION OF NITROGEN 

It is estimated that three and a half million tons of nitrogen are taken out of the soils of the United States each year by the crops grown on them, and in industrial work nitrogen compounds are entensively employed. Practically all of this nitrogen is in time returned to the air, and to keep up the supply of needed nitrogen compounds, free nitrogen must be taken from the air and fixed in combined form. Fortunately, certain soil bacteria have the power to do this, and by chemical processes we are learning to form nitrogen compounds that can be used as fertilizers and for chemical work. In this section the more important of these processes will be discussed. 

Union of oxygen and nitrogen. If an electric spark is shot through air, nitric oxide, NO, is formed: N₂+ O2 →→→ 2NO Several different forms of apparatus are used to bring about this reaction, but all of them consist essentially of a gigantic arc light through which a current of air is blown. The more completely the air comes in contact with the electric arc, the more molecules of oxygen and nitrogen will unite. 

On the other hand, if the molecules of the oxide of nitrogen remain in contact with the electric arc, they are broken up again into oxygen and nitrogen; that is, the equation goes in the opposite direction: 2NON₂+ O2 To prevent this action, the air is run through at a high speed. 

When the nitric oxide formed comes in contact with the air, it unites with another molecule of oxygen to form nitrogen peroxide, thus: 

2NO+ O2 2NO2 141

Introductory Chemistry 

Kadel and Herbert 

FIG. 38. Alfred T. Larson, who by the use of new catalysts and other improvements notably increased the efficiency of the Haber process. His work was done in the United States 

Fixed Nitrogen Research Laboratory. 

The air containing this oxide of nitrogen is then passed through towers with water trickling down them. If hot water is used, the NO2 unites with water to form nitric acid and nitric oxide. 

3NO2 + H2O — 2HNO3 + NO

The nitric oxide formed in the reaction is not lost, for it combines with oxygen to form NO2 and this with water to give HNO3. 

If cold water is employed in the process, HNO₂ is among the products, and by further reaction this is oxidized to HNO3. 

The nitric acid is easily transformed into fertilizer by converting it into calcium, potassium, sodium, or ammonium nitrates, which serve as sources of nitrogen for the plant.
