FIG. 39. Diagram of a continuous synthetic ammonia plant in operation in the United States Fixed Nitrogen Research Laboratory. 

Hydrogen is obtained by electrolysis of water, and a part of this is burned in air to remove the oxygen and thus provide a supply of nitrogen. A mixture of nitrogen and hydrogen is then forced through oil traps (one of which is filled with pulverized carbon) to remove all oil vapors from the gases; after which it passes into the purifier, which contains a cheap catalyst. This catalyst forms some ammonia (which is drawn off through the condenser and high-pressure receivers), but its main function is to remove impurities that would "poison' the more costly and more highly efficient catalyst in the converter, where the chief synthesis of the ammonia takes place. After removal of the ammonia, the unsynthesized hydrogen and nitrogen are returned to the converter and passed over the catalyst again. "9 143

Introductory Chemistry 

Not more than one per cent of NO is obtained by passing the air through the arc, and at best the process is an expensive one. 

It is employed to some extent in the mountainous sections of Norway and the Alps, where hydroelectric power can be used. 

The cyanamid process. The first step in this process is the production of calcium carbide, CaC2, from coke and limestone in the electric furnace (page 226). Then nitrogen from liquid air is passed over the white-hot carbide to produce calcium cyanamid: CaC₂+N₂- CaCN2 + C The third step is the heating of the cyanamid under steam pressure:

CaCN2 + 3H2O CaCO3 + 2NH3 

The ammonia can now be transformed into nitric acid by oxidation, as we have seen (page 121). This is done by forcing a heated mixture of ammonia and air through a finely woven platinum gauze. It requires only one five-hundredth of a second for the gases to pass through the gauze, but 85 per cent of the ammonia is converted to nitric acid.

The cyanamid process has been extensively employed in Europe, and nitrogen has been fixed in this way at Niagara Falls since 1909. During the war our government built a large cyanamid plant at Muscle Shoals, Alabama. The process is practicable only where electric current is cheap, and the Haber process, which we shall consider next, is rapidly displacing it. 

The Haber process. In the Haber process, ammonia is formed by the direct synthesis of hydrogen and nitrogen. The two gases mixed in the right proportions are heated and passed under high pressure through a tube filled with a finely divided metal catalyst. 

Uranium was originally used as a catalyst, but many other metals including iron are likewise employed. The gas, after it emerges from the tube, is passed through water to absorb the ammonia, and the uncombined portion is again passed over the catalyzer.

The Fixation of Nitrogen 

145 This process is now the most economical one for the fixing of nitrogen, its greatest cost lying in the preparation of hydrogen sufficiently pure not to "poison" the catalyzer. Moreover, it is constantly being improved, new catalyzers and different temperatures and pressures giving more efficient results. Since heat and power are required, it is coal and not electric current that is necessary for the fixation of nitrogen by this method. 

The nitride method. At the high temperatures of the electric furnace nitrogen will unite with certain metals (e.g., lithium, calcium, magnesium, aluminum) to form nitrides. From these by reaction with steam, ammonia can easily be secured. The nitride method is not economical in comparison with other methods, but it is used in France in connection with the manufacture of aluminum, because by it a purer aluminum oxide for electrolytic dissociation is secured. In the electric furnace aluminum oxide, coke, and nitrogen are heated with the following reaction: Al2O3 + 3C+N2 — 2AIN + 3CO The aluminum nitride thus secured is then treated with steam, and ammonia and aluminum hydroxide secured. Write the equation for the reaction.

Ammonia from coal. When coal is heated in the production of coke or distilled in the manufacture of gas, ammonia is one of the products that is given off. Great quantities of ammonia are secured from this source, and more can be secured as new equipment that saves the waste products is installed. It is estimated that in the coke and gas industries about 130,000 tons of nitrogen are released each year. If the American chemical industry is built up to make a demand for others of the products that go off as smoke and fumes, more of this ammonia will undoubtedly be saved. 

Nitrogen-fixing bacteria. Certain soil bacteria are able to "fix" atmospheric nitrogen. These bacteria live in the soil,

Introductory Chemistry 

FIG. 40. The roots of a clover plant, showing the nodules caused by the nitrogen-fixing bacteria. subsisting on decaying organic matter. With some of the compounds coming from this organic matter they unite atmospheric nitrogen, which gives them a supply of nitrogen compounds for their own use and also eventually increases the nitrogen content of the soil.

Some species of the nitrogen-fixing bacteria also have the power to enter the roots of legumes (e.g., peas, beans, clovers) and there live and multiply, causing a proliferation of the cells that results in a tumor-like body usually spoken of as a nodule. The bacteria use for their nourishment material drawn from the legume, but unlike a strictly parasitic invader, they perform a valuable service for the food and shelter provided for them. This service consists in building nitrogen compounds from free atmospheric nitrogen and the carbohydrates available in the root. These nitrogen compounds both the bacteria and the legumes use for growth. 

Thus, in return for a carbohydrate supply the bacteria make

The Fixation of Nitrogen 

147 available to the legume the much less abundant nitrogen compounds. And when legume and bacteria die and decay, the nitrogen that has been fixed is added to the soil supply. 

Many varieties of these nitrogen-fixing bacteria develop only in neutral or slightly alkaline soils. (Those of the soy bean are exceptional in that they thrive in acid soils.) So some soils must be sweetened by an alkali (lime is used) before most species will grow in it. In some soils the nitrogen-fixing bacteria needed for a certain crop are not found, and when such a soil has been prepared for seeding with a legume, it is advisable to inoculate it with the desired bacteria by scattering over it soil from a field on which the leguminous plant has been grown, or by treating the seed with a culture of the bacteria. Soils where legumes are flourishing always contain millions of these bacteria.

In considering the work of these bacteria, it must be borne in mind that animals and the higher plants cannot use free nitrogen; so all the higher forms of life are dependent either directly or indirectly on the nitrogen-fixing bacteria for their nitrogen intake. The plants of forests and fields (except those fertilized by artificially produced nitrates) subsist on nitrogen originally fixed by them, the ocean plants on nitrates washed down from the soil, and both land and water animals on plants that are dependent on these for their nitrogen compounds. The ammonia of coal contains nitrogen fixed by these bacteria in the distant past, and the great Chile nitrate beds are the remains of guano deposits the nitrogen of which was originally built into compounds by the bacteria of the soil. Our chief agricultural dependence for fixed nitrogen is, and must continue to be for a long time to come, these small organisms, and it is highly important that by the growing of legumes and the right soil conditions they be encouraged in their work. In the meantime, by saving the nitrogen of coal, by digging out the Chile nitrate deposits, and by artificially fixing the nitrogen of the air, we can be of some slight

Introductory Chemistry assistance to them in keeping up soil fertility, and can secure the nitrogen compounds needed in chemical and industrial work. 

The nitrifying bacteria. Another group of bacteria, called the nitrifying bacteria, cause the decay of organic matter in the soil. 

There are several varieties of these, and they work in relay fashion. One set converts the dead organic material into ammonia; a second set converts the ammonia into nitrites; and a third set, by uniting oxygen with the nitrites, converts these into nitrates. The nitrifying bacteria make the nitrogen in complex organic compounds available to the higher forms of plant life by breaking these compounds down into simple substances that can be absorbed and used. 

The Chile nitrate deposits are supposed to be the remains of great guano deposits converted into nitrates by the nitrifying bacteria and not washed into the sea because of the desert climate. Over an area two hundred miles in length and two miles wide, there is a deposit five feet deep that runs from 20 to 50 per cent sodium nitrate, and over a much wider area a lower-assaying deposit is found. As has been stated, all this nitrogen was originally taken from the atmosphere and fixed by soil bacteria, and when we mine the nitrate deposits, we are drawing on the same reserve that these small organisms have deposited for us in all fertile soils.

UNIT FOUR ACIDS, BASES, AND SALTS 

NATURE is so vast and so various that in a consideration of it the human mind would be lost in an infinity of detail if subjects of study were not grouped according to their relationships and our scientific knowledge properly classified. So important, indeed, is classification in scientific work that in dictionaries science is often defined as classified knowledge. The definition is inadequate, for science includes not only results of investigations, but also methods of work and that intellectual integrity on the part of the worker usually referred to as the "scientific spirit or the "scientific attitude of mind." Yet classification is indispensable in all sciences, and the classified facts of any science are an exceedingly important part of it. 

Thus far we have studied a few chemical elements and certain compounds. The compounds we have classified to some extent according to their composition into such groups as oxides and nitrates. Reference has also been made to three great classes of compounds — acids, bases, and salts; and some of the propperties characteristic of substances belonging to each of these groups were noted. In this unit we shall carry further our study of these compounds, giving our attention to group properties rather than to individual properties that differentiate members within a group. Since the peculiarities of these substances manifest themselves chiefly while they are in solution, our work will be in large part a study of solutions. 151

SECTION ONE GENERAL PROPERTIES OF ACIDS, BASES, AND SALTS 

ACIDS and bases are chemical opposites, and when they interact, salts are formed. Hence, in the minds and discussions of chemists, acids, bases, and salts are naturally associated. In our experiments we have already met examples of these substances and have noted some of their properties. Every one has learned something of them from his daily experiences, for vinegar, ammonia water, and salt (sodium chloride) are representatives of these three groups of compounds that are found in every household.

ACIDS 

The strong acids (hydrochloric acid, sulfuric acid, nitric acid) with which we have become familiar in the laboratory are called inorganic or mineral acids. They are among the most important reagents used by the chemist, and they are employed in industry in quantities amounting to hundreds of thousands of pounds each year. They are not found in nature in any amounts, because as soon as they are formed they react with other substances and form salts, thus losing their identity as acids.

But a second class of acids called organic acids are widely distributed in nature, and every one is familiar with some of their properties. Only acids taste sour, and the word "acid " means sour (Latin: acidus, sour). The characteristic tastes of green fruits, vinegar, sour milk, and other foods are due to acids in them. Organic acids are found in soils also and wherever animal or vegetable matter ferments or decays. 

All acids contain hydrogen, and most of them oxygen also in combination with one or more other elements. The distinguishing properties of an acid are due to the replaceable hydrogen in it. 

General properties of acids. Some of the properties of acids were discovered in previous experiments. Review this work, 152

Introductory Chemistry 

Naming of acids. The same element (for example, nitrogen, sulfur, or phosphorus) may form several acids which differ from each other in the amount of oxygen they contain. The name of the best known of these acids usually ends in ic. An example of such an acid is sulfuric acid, H2SO4. If the acid contains less oxygen, the name ends in ous, as sulfurous acid, H₂SO3. If an acid is formed which contains a still smaller proportion of oxygen, the "ous" ending is retained and the prefix hypo is added, as hyposulfurous acid, H₂SO2. Many elements form acids which contain more oxygen than the one with the name ending in "ic," and these are named by retaining the "ic " ending and prefixing per, as persulfuric acid, H2S2O8. 

Some acids contain no oxygen. Hydrochloric acid is an example of such an acid; it is composed of chlorine in union with hydrogen only. It belongs to the class of binary compounds discussed on page 87, and according to the rules for the naming of such compounds, it may very properly be called hydrogen chloride. But in aqueous solution it is the most familiar of the acids formed by chlorine, and so it is usually known as hydrochloric acid. The following series shows the different chlorine acids and illustrates the system followed in the naming of these compounds :

BASES 

Ammonia is a representative base that has been studied. 

Limewater is another common base used in the household. The lye used in soap making is another example of a base familiar to many who have lived in the country, and a strong base forms the chief ingredient of many of the cleansing powders that are

General Properties of Acids, Bases, and Salts 155 

used for removing grease from the kitchen sink. Just as hydrogen is found in all acids, so the hydroxyl group, OH, occurs in all bases. Usually this radical is found in combination with a metal, the exception being found in ammonium hydroxide, (NH4)OH, where the ammonium radical acts like a metal. 

The special properties of bases are due to the hydroxyl group, OH, which they contain. Very strong bases are called alkalies. 

General properties of bases. Just as acids have certain characteristics in common, so have bases, as the following experiment will show.

Introductory Chemistry Complete the equation: 

Base + Acid Illustrate the general reaction by a specific equation, using formulas throughout: The general properties of bases should be remembered. 

Naming of bases. The hydroxyl group, OH, is always present in bases, and these compounds are often called hydroxides. 

It is considered that in them the OH radical acts as an element, and the naming follows the rules given for the naming of binary compounds (see page 87); examples are sodium hydroxide and potassium hydroxide. The hydroxides are thus distinguished from each other by placing the names of the metal before the word "hydroxide."

The common hydroxides have had various names affixed to them by usage. Sodium hydroxide is often called sodium hydrate or caustic soda. Sometimes ammonium hydroxide is spoken of as ammonia; potassium hydroxide is often given the name caustic potash; and calcium hydroxide in aqueous solution is frequently termed limewater.

SALTS 

Sodium chloride, or common table salt, is the familiar representative of a vast class of compounds that are everywhere present in nature. Most salts have a salty or saline taste, but the members of the group are classed together more because of their origin than because of the possession of any body of common properties. All salts contain a metal (or the ammonium radical acting as a metal). 

How salts are formed.

When an acid and base react with each other, a salt is formed. For example, when HCl and NaOH interact, NaCl and H2O are formed. If H2SO4 is substituted for the HCl, the salt formed is sodium sulfate, Na2SO4.

General Properties of Acids, Bases, and Salts 157 

Salts may be formed in other ways also, as when an acid reacts with a metal (Experiment 27), by direct union of a metal with another element, or in other ways as we saw nitrates formed. 

Whenever the hydrogen of an acid is replaced by a metal (or by the ammonium radical), the product is a salt. The hydrogen is always thus replaced when an acid reacts with a base, but it may also be replaced by a metal in reactions between acids and other substances.

From the above it will be seen that salts may differ both in the metal in them and in the acid from which they were formed.

Naming of salts. A salt is named from both the acid and the metal (or radical) from which it is formed. Thus a salt of nitric acid is a nitrate, a salt of sulfuric acid is a sulfate, and a salt of hydrochloric acid is a chloride. If a salt is formed from sodium and one of the acids named above, it is called sodium nitrate, sodium sulfate, or sodium chloride. If, instead of sodium, potassium or some other metal enters into the formation of the salt, the name of this metal is used as the first part of the name of the salt.

When a salt is derived from a binary acid (e.g., hydrochloric acid), its name terminates in ide. The salts of ternary acids with names ending in ic are given names ending in ate. Salts from ternary acids with names ending in ous have names ending in ite. Nitric acid forms nitrates and sulfuric acid sulfates.

Nitrous acid forms nitrites and sulfurous acid sulfites. 

Complete the table on the following page by writing the formula and the name of the potassium salt of each acid given. 

Normal, acid, and basic salts. Salts might be expected to give a neutral reaction to litmus, since they are formed when acids and bases neutralize each other. This is not always the case. 

When all the hydrogen of an acid is replaced by a metal, we have what is called a normal salt. Such a salt is sodium sulfate, Na2SO4.

If, on the other hand, only part of the hydrogen is replaced by the metal, we have sodium acid sulfate, or sodium hydrogen sulfate, NaHSO4. This has an acid reaction because part of the hydrogen of the acid has not been replaced. 

Likewise, if a base has more than one OH radical, only part of the OH groups of the base may be replaced by the negative part of the acid in the reaction. Then we have a basic salt. An example of this is Pb(OH)NO3. 

What two possible basic salts could we have of Bi(OH)3? 

What two possible acid salts could we have of H3PO₁?

The importance of acids, bases, and salts. The lack of acids, bases, and salts would not make so great a difference in the world as the absence of oxygen or water. Yet salts compose a considerable part of the earth's crust, and they are in solution in all ground waters. Without them the number of possible compounds would be greatly limited, and the products and processes

General Properties of Acids, Bases, and Salts 159 

of industry and the life of the home would be touched at a thousand points. An idea of how important these compounds are in practical life may be gained by observing the extent of their use in the experimental work in an elementary chemistry course. 

No science is more directly practical than chemistry, and beginning courses in the subject teach the methods and the use of the reagents that are most commonly employed in industrial and commercial work.

SECTION TWO SOLUTIONS AND IONIZATION 

A CERTAIN great scientist is reported to have said that he never had a clear idea of any process in nature until he could draw a picture of what was happening. By this standard our knowledge of solutions is still far from complete, for notwithstanding all the study that has been given the subject, we can build up only a hazy mental image of what occurs when a substance is dissolved. 

We have, however, learned many facts about solutions that are highly useful in the arts and sciences, and our next studies will be to acquaint ourselves with these facts and with the theories that have been proposed to explain them. 

Why substances go into solution. We add a little sugar to a glass of water. The sugar molecules separate, diffuse throughout the water, and in time are found distributed equally throughout all parts of it. What causes them to do this?

Our present knowledge makes it seem probable that there is a tendency on the part of the sugar molecules to move apart. Some solids, like ice, mothballs, and gum camphor, evaporate on exposure to the air, and it is thought that other solids have this same tendency for the molecules to separate. It is believed, however, that the chief reason for a substance going into solution is that an attractive force exists between the molecules of the solvent and of the solute which causes them to intermingle and to come into as close contact as possible. We should think, therefore, not only of the molecules of the sugar as diffusing out through the water, but also of the water molecules as crowding in among those of the sugar and pushing them apart.

Not only solids, but also gases and other liquids dissolve in liquids. Further, gases, liquids, and solids may all be dissolved in solids, forming what are called " solid solutions." As we have seen (page 70), gases go into solution in certain metals, certain 160

rocks hold water in solution, and brass, solder, and other alloys are solutions of metals dissolved in each other. Our studies will, however, be confined to solutions in liquids. 

The kinetic theory of solution. In a gas the molecules are all supposed to be in motion, dancing and darting about, and the pressure exerted by a gas on the walls of a container is due to the gas molecules striking against it (page 332). Each molecule under given conditions, independently of its weight, is supposed to strike with the same force. It is the number of molecules and not the weight of them that is significant. 

Similarly, in a solution the molecules of both the solute and the solvent are supposed to be in motion, and the osmotic pressure exerted by solutions is due to this fact (page 434). As in gases, the pressure depends on the number and not the kinds of molecules, each molecule, independently of its size and mass, producing the same pressure. The kinetic theory of solution should be held in mind as our study of this section proceeds. 

Conducting power of aqueous solutions. In connection with the electrolysis of water (page 35) we saw that an aqueous solution of sulfuric acid readily permitted the passage of a current of electricity. We shall now try to find if all other substances when in solution permit the passage of the current. 

Experiment 36

This light cuts down the current, and at the same time the intensity of the light indicates how good a conductor the solution is. 

Take solutions of equal molar concentration (see page 436) of the following substances: hydrochloric acid, sugar, sodium hydroxide, sodium chloride, nitric acid, glycerine, sodium sulfate, alcohol, and copper sulfate. It may be convenient to fasten the stopper carrying

Aqueous solutions of what three classes of substances conduct the electric current?

Substances which in solution conduct the electric current are called electrolytes. Those which do not conduct the electric current are called non-electrolytes. 

Ionization. Acids, bases, and salts are supposed to conduct the electric current, because when they go into solution, a part of their molecules dissociate, or separate into ions. These are atoms or groups of atoms (radicals), negatively or positively charged with electricity. Turn back to page 67, to the diagram of the sodium chloride molecule. In ionization the two atoms composing the molecule separate. The dissociation of a few common compounds is shown below. HCI

H+ + Cl- Na2SO4 2Na+

SO4¯¯ CaSO4 NH₁(OH) Ca+++SO4¯¯ NH4+ + OH- The arrows in the equations are shown pointing in both directions because ionization is supposed to be a reversible reaction, some of the oppositely charged ions coming together and forming molecules, while some of the molecules are dissociating into ions. 

The plus and minus signs to the right of the symbols represent the charges the ions are carrying. 

When the reaction reaches a stage (which it does practically instantly) where the molecules are being formed as fast as they dissociate, the solution is in a state of chemical equilibrium. 

How the ionic theory explains electrolysis. Suppose HCl is added to a vessel of water. A part of the molecules dissociate into H+ and Cl ions. Now suppose that two electrodes that are connected to an electric battery are immersed in the solution. 

Oppositely charged bodies attract each other; so the positively charged hydrogen ions (called cations) move toward the negative pole, or cathode. As soon as they reach this pole each receives an electron from it, and then they are electrically neutral and

Introductory Chemistry are no longer ions but atoms. They then unite in pairs to form hydrogen molecules, which bubble off from the solution as a gas. 

In the meantime the negatively charged chlorine ions (called anions) have been passing to the anode, where each gives up an electron and is thus neutralized. Then, like the hydrogen ions, the chlorine ions first become atomic and then combine into molecules. The free chlorine to some extent goes into solution in the water, but escapes as a gas from the solution if the action is continued.

The removal of hydrogen and chlorine ions from the solution in this way causes more of the HCl to dissociate (since a certain percentage of the HCl present in water dissociates), and the ions thus formed are likewise removed. Thus, if the flow of the electric current continues for a sufficient time, the entire amount of HCl in the solution is decomposed. 

At the same time, water is also being decomposed, for it ionizes slightly into H+ and OH-. The hydrogen goes to the cathode as explained, and the OH to the anode, where the negative charge is neutralized and water and oxygen are formed: 4OH- 2H2O + O2 Thus, at the anode, chlorine mixed with a lesser amount of oxygen appears.

If a solution of a salt as, for example, CuCl₂ is used, the action will be as with HCl, except that the metal ions will be deposited as a coating on the cathode. By using an object such as a spoon or a piece of jewelry as the cathode, it can be given a coating of a metal like zinc, copper, silver, or gold of any desired thickness. The process is called electroplating, and is extensively employed.

If H2SO4 is used as the electrolyte, the reaction is somewhat more complicated. The hydrogen ions pass to the cathode as before and form the gas hydrogen. The SO-ions, each carrying a double negative charge, go to the anode, but the OH- ions

from the water, which are also present at the anode, lose their charges more easily than the SO4. Hence it is the OH- and not the SO- ions that are discharged. Or if by a strong current the SO₁-- ions are discharged, they react with each other, thus:

SO4+ SO₁¯ 2SO3 + 2H2O 2SO3 + O2 → 2H2SO4 The oxygen atoms thus released unite to form oxygen molecules, which are given off at the anode as we have seen (page 31). 

The H2SO4 is again ionized and the charges removed from the ions as before.

It will be noted that the end result of this reaction is to reduce the amount of water and not the amount of H2SO4 in the solution. 

Difference between atom and ion. The difference between an atom and an ion is that the ion carries an electrical charge. One important fact to be noted is that the electrical charge apparently has a marked influence on the properties of the atom. The most striking difference is in its chemical activity. In Experi- * ment 8 we found that free sodium decomposed water at once. 

According to the theory of ionization, dissociation of NaCl in water gives sodium ions, but no decomposition of water results. 

A second fact that should not be lost sight of is that the electrical charges on the ion do not come from the current, but are on the ions as soon as the solution is formed. In electrolysis the charges are either added to or removed from the ions. The positive ions are drawn to one pole and the negative ions' to the other and there neutralized, but it is not the electric current which causes the molecules to dissociate or gives the ions their electric charges. 

Degree of ionization. If ions are the carriers of the electric current through a solution, the more ions present the better the solution should conduct the current. Accepting this idea as correct, turn back and review Experiment 36. Do different

Experiments on the conductivity of solutions show that up to a certain point the more a solution is diluted, the greater the percentage of ionization of the solute molecules. 

Dissociation and chemical activity. The ionogens, or ion formers, are as a class exceedingly active chemically. Is this activity connected with their dissociative tendencies? One way to answer this question would be to determine whether those substances which dissociate most are chemically more active than those dissociating to a lesser degree. Another way would be to determine the activity of solutions of different concentrations of the same substance to see whether the solutions containing the highest concentrations of ions are most active.

Our work has now shown that the activity of an acid depends, not on the concentration of the solution, but on the amount of dissociation, or on the ion concentration. Acids and bases that ionize to a high degree are spoken of as strong, and those that ionize only slightly as weak. Most salts ionize to a high degree, and so there is no marked difference in their activity. 

Definitions of acid, base, and salt. From our studies it is now evident that the acids, bases, and salts are a distinct group of compounds, differing in their behavior in solutions from all other substances. In terms of the dissociation theory these compounds may be defined as follows:

Introductory Chemistry 

An acid is a hydrogen compound which ionizes, giving hydrogen ions and no other positive ions. A strong acid is one that ionizes to a high degree. 

A base is a hydrogen compound which ionizes, giving hydroxyl ions and no other negative ions. 

A salt is a compound which in solution furnishes positive ions other than H+ and negative ions other than OH-. Other evidences of ionization. According to the kinetic theory of solutions, the osmotic pressures exerted by solutions of non-electrolytes are proportional to the number of molecules in the solution (page 442). Electrolytes, however, give higher pressures than the concentrations of the solutions would lead us to expect, and the more active one of these substances is and the better it conducts the electric current, the higher is its osmotic pressure. These facts are explained by assuming that acids, bases, and salts dissociate and that each ion produces the same pressure effect as a whole molecule. 

Moreover, putting a substance into solution affects the boiling and freezing points of the solvent, and the greater the concentration of the solution, the greater is the effect produced. But, again, a given number of molecules of an electrolyte has a greater effect than has an equal number of molecules of a non-electrolyte. 

These results are likewise explained by the assumption that some of the molecules of the electrolyte dissociate and that each ion thus produced has the same effect on the boiling and freezing points as a whole molecule.

Thus not only the electrical conductivity of acids, bases, and salts, but also other properties possessed by them can be explained on the assumption that in solutions of these substances dissociation takes place. 

Dissociation in solvents other than water. Water is not the only solvent in which dissociation takes place, but it ranks as one of the highest from this point of view. Benzene is one of the poorest. Methyl alcohol falls in between the two, and in other

solvents various amounts of dissociation are found. Oppositely charged ions must attract each other, and they would come together or never separate if some solvents did not have greater power than others to overcome this attraction and force the ions apart. Among such solvents water holds a high rank.

SECTION THREE SPEED OF REACTIONS; CHEMICAL EQUILIBRIUM; NEUTRALIZA- TION; HYDROLYSIS 

IRON rusts more rapidly in a moist climate than in a dry one. 

Many dyes that retain their colors for long periods in weak light fade rapidly when exposed to sunshine. Fruits ripen more rapidly in a warm atmosphere than when kept in cold storage. The addition of manganese dioxide to sodium chlorate causes oxygen to be given off more rapidly (page 51). Such chemical reactions vary according to the conditions under which they take place.

In industrial chemistry the velocity of reaction is often important because the amount of the product that can be secured in a unit of time depends on this. Furthermore, in many cases the reactions are reversible, and the amount of product secured is determined by the point at which the reaction stops, or, stating this fact differently, the point at which chemical equilibrium is reached. It is therefore well to understand the methods by which reactions can be accelerated and the conditions under which they go on to completion. 

Methods of increasing velocity of reaction. The methods usually employed for accelerating chemical reactions are raising the temperature, the introduction of catalyzers, and increasing the amount of contact between the reacting substances. 

1. Raising the temperature. The use of heat to cause or hasten chemical reactions is so familiar that it requires no comment. 

Mention at least six chemical processes with which you have become familiar, either in practical life or in the laboratory, in which heat is applied to initiate or to increase the velocity of the reaction. 

The effect of increase of temperature on the velocity of chemical reactions is very great, roughly, a doubling for each 10°. 

How great an effect would an increase of 100° have? of 200°? 170

2. Use of catalyzers. We have seen that manganese dioxide accelerates the release of oxygen from potassium chlorate; and the catalytic effects of water, of turpentine in paints, and of metals in the Haber process of synthesizing ammonia have been mentioned. Catalytic agents are used to speed up numerous other chemical processes. The exact way in which a catalyzer works is still undetermined, but much research is being done in this field, and in the near future we shall doubtless understand catalysis better and also be able to employ catalytic agents more effectively in chemical work. The idea of being able to set catalyzers to work for us is appealing, and the possibilities in this method are very great. 

3. Increasing contact between reagents. The effect of this method can be shown by an experiment.

Introductory Chemistry 

Equilibrium and ionization. Suppose that we add enough sodium chloride to water to make a one-tenth normal solution. 

The molecules go into solution and immediately begin to dissociate. The dissociation continues, but the reaction is reversible and molecules are also built up by the combination of ions. At first the molecules are abundant and the ions relatively few; so the molecules are dissociated much more rapidly than they are built. But as the ions become more abundant and the molecules less abundant, the rate of building gradually overtakes the rate of dissociation, until finally a point is reached where the molecules are being built and ionized at exactly the same rate. 

When this point has been reached equilibrium has been established, and thereafter the solution will always show the same amount of dissociation. The establishment of such an equilibrium does not mean that the reaction has ceased, but that it is proceeding in both directions at equal velocity. In a one-tenth normal solution of sodium chloride, the equilibrium is established when 84 per cent of the molecules have been ionized. In a sulfuric-acid solution of the same concentration, the equilibrium point is reached when the acid is 61 per cent ionized. 

When two or more electrolytes are mixed in the same solution, the process by which equilibrium is reached is much more complicated, for any positive ion may unite with any negative one. 

For example, if we should mix a solution of potassium chloride with a solution of sodium nitrate, there would be eight substances in solution, with a possible equilibrium as represented below: (15%) NaNO3 (84%) (83%) 

Na+ + NO (85%) (14%) KCl Cl− +K+ (86%) 14 Y1 NaCl KNO3 (16%) (17%)

Conditions under which chemical reactions go to completion. 

In the reaction represented in the last equation the sodium nitrate and potassium chloride would never all be transformed, for they are constantly being reformed. But if the sodium chloride or potassium nitrate could have been removed from the solution, the reaction would have gone on to completion. Some examples of reactions in which the products are removed from the field of action will be found in our next experiment.

If in the above cases we should choose reacting substances whose products are less soluble, less volatile, or are more highly ionized, the reactions would be less nearly complete. That is, the nearness to completion to which a reaction goes depends on how completely one of the reacting substances is removed from the field. Water is slightly ionized, and the last reaction does not go quite to completion.

Explanation of neutralization. The properties characteristic of all acids may be explained as being due to the hydrogen ions formed when they dissociate, and the characteristic properties of bases to the hydroxyl ions that they give. Neutralization is the union of the hydrogen and OH ions to form water. This reaction is so important in chemistry that the explanation of it will be set forth in full.

The condition of the hydrochloric acid in a one-tenth normal solution may be expressed thus: (8%) HCl H++ Cl− (92%)

Similarly, the condition of a one-tenth normal solution of 

NaOH may be represented as follows: 

(9%) NaOH 

Na+ + OH (91%)

If the acid and the base are now poured together in just sufficient quantities to neutralize each other, the reaction indicated below results: (84%) (8%) HCl H+ + Cl (92%) + + (9%) NaOH 

OH+Na+ (91%) 14 H₂O NaCl (16%)

When an H and an OH ion meet, they form a molecule of water. 

Water is practically undissociated (page 166); so this removes these ions from the solution. Another HCl and another NaOH molecule then dissociate, for always a certain percentage of the acid and of the base are in ionized form. This process of removal of the H and OH ions continues with further dissociation until the acid and the base have been exhausted. Then the solution is neither acid nor basic, but neutral.

Some of the Na ions of the base and the Cl ions of the acid unite with each other, but most of them remain in the ionic state because in a one-tenth normal solution NaCl is 84 per cent dissociated.

What would happen if the water of the solution were evaporated?

When an acid and a base are neutralized, the heat released is known as the heat of neutralization. This heat can come only from the union of the OH ion with the H ion, as this is the only chemical change in neutralization.

Hydrolysis. It 

It was implied above that the reaction HCl + NaOH - NaCl + H2O goes to completion because one of the products, water, does not ionize. As a matter of

Introductory Chemistry fact, however, water is ionized to some extent, and to just this extent the above reaction is reversible. Moreover, in the solutions with which chemists ordinarily work there are many more water molecules present than there are molecules of the substance dissolved in the water, 555 molecules of water to 1 of the solute in a .1 M solution. Hence, in dilute solutions the ions formed from water are sufficiently numerous to cause an appreciable reversal of reactions like the one given above, and the equation should properly be written: 

HCl + NaOH 

NaCl + H2O

This reversed reaction is called hydrolysis. It is exactly the opposite of neutralization, and results in the formation of an acid and a base. But because the HCl and NaOH are ionized to practically the same degree, the number of H+ and OH- ions are approximately equal, and the solution is neutral. 

Hydrolysis (hydro, water + lysis, splitting water-splitting) is the decomposition of a salt by water to form an acid and a base. It always results in the production of an acid and a base. If the acid and base ionize equally, the solution is neutral. 

If the acid ionizes more highly than the base, the solution is acid. 

If the base is stronger than the acid, the OH- ions will be more numerous than the H+ and the solution will be alkaline.

SECTION ONE OCCURRENCE AND ALLOTROPIC FORMS OF SULFUR 

THE peoples about the Mediterranean Sea knew sulfur in connection with the active volcanoes of Italy and Sicily. Hence, it is not strange that these ancients believed that beneath the crust of the earth there existed an infernal region filled with fire and brimstone (sulfur). The word "brimstone" itself means burning stone, and because of the inflammable nature of sulfur, it was regarded by the alchemists as the principle of combustion.

Occurrence of sulfur. Native sulfur occurs in many parts of the world, although the most abundant deposits are found either in the regions of volcanoes or in connection with beds of gypsum, CaSO4, and limestone. Small deposits of sulfur may also be found incrusted with other mineral deposits in the vicinity of sulfur springs or geysers. The sulfur that is found about volcanoes is probably formed from the interaction of hydrogen sulfide, H₂S, and sulfur dioxide, SO2, under the influence of volcanic heat. According to one theory the deposits associated with gypsum are due to "sulfur " bacteria that decompose the gypsum into sulfur and calcium carbonate. According to another theory, petroleum has reacted with the gypsum to produce the sulfur and limestone. 

In combination with other elements, sulfur is widely distributed and is found in many common substances. Several minerals contain sulfur as one of their elements. Galena (a lead ore) and pyrite (often called "fool's gold ") are examples of common minerals in which sulfur is one of the chief constituents. In one form or another it is present in all soils and is one of the ten elements necessary for the growth of plants. It is a constituent of proteins and is present in larger or smaller amounts in different foods. It is estimated that there are between 4 and 5 ounces of it in the human body. 180

Occurrence and Allotropic Forms of Sulfur 181 

Industrial production. The volcanic regions of Sicily and southern Europe have been the principal source of the world's supply of sulfur, but at present we are no longer dependent upon Italy for our supply. In recent years we have been able to produce large quantities of sulfur from deposits found in connection with gypsum and limestone in the coastal plains of Louisiana and Texas. These remarkable sulfur beds lie several hundred feet beneath the surface of the earth. Some of them are 500 feet in thickness, and single beds contain as much as ten million tons.

The methods employed for the extraction and refining of sulfur differ greatly in the two principal regions where it is produced for commercial purposes. 

1. Sicilian deposits. The mines in Sicily cover an area about 

55 miles wide and 100 miles long. The deposits vary in thickness from 150 to 650 feet. In these deposits there is so much sedimentary rock, in the form of marly slate, that the proportion of sulfur is rarely more than 30 per cent of the total material quarried.

The sulfur in these beds is separated from the associated materials by fusion and liquidation. The heat necessary for the fusion is produced by burning part of the sulfur. In the old process the material is placed in a heap and set on fire. In this way about 

40 per cent of the sulfur is consumed in the heating process, and the remaining 60 per cent runs off and is saved. This sulfur contains a small percentage of impurities, and some of it is refined by distillation. A good share of it, however, is used in making sulfuric acid and in other processes that do not require the pure product. 

2. Louisiana deposits. Many attempts were made by ordinary shaft mining to bring the Louisiana sulfur from its depth under the overlying beds of sand and gravel, but these met with total or partial failure, owing to the difficulty of resisting the lateral pressure of the sand and the inrush of water from the

Introductory Chemistry 

Texas Gulf Sulphur Co. 

FIG. 42. A view in the sulfur fields of Louisiana. The practically pure molten sulfur is run into temporary bins for hardening. Then the solid material is broken up for shipment. 

The illustration shows the solid blocks of sulfur after the walls of the bins have been removed. sand. Finally the Frasch process for bringing up the sulfur was perfected, and in consequence the Louisiana-Texas region is now the chief source of the world's sulfur supply. 

The apparatus used in the Frasch process consists essentially of three concentric pipes that extend down through the overlying beds of sand to the sulfur beds below. Through the outer pipe, water heated to about 175° is forced down under a pressure of 250 pounds to the square inch. Through the inner pipe, which is longer than the outer one, air is forced down at a pressure of 400 pounds to the square inch. The superheated water melts the sulfur (its melting point is 115°), and the molten sulfur is forced upward through the middle pipe by the pressure of the air.

As fast as the molten sulfur comes to the surface it is conducted into bins, where it solidifies. This solid mass is subsequently broken up and shipped to the various industrial centers which utilize sulfur. The sulfur obtained in these fields runs as high as 99.5 per cent pure. A single well yields as high as 2000 tons

Occurrence and Allotropic Forms of Sulfur 183 a day, and sulfur from this field can be produced in practically any amounts desired.

Forms of sulfur. We have seen that oxygen has two forms, and in a later section we shall find that carbon exists as diamond, graphite, and charcoal. It will not be so surprising to us, then, to learn that sulfur has two solid, two liquid, and three gaseous forms. In the next experiment we shall study some of these forms and determine which is the most stable. Experiment 41 OBJECT. To prepare different forms of sulfur.

We have found that two solid forms, rhombic and monoclinic, differ as to their solubility in carbon disulfide. We have also learned that they have other physical properties which are different. It was observed that only the stable at ordinary temperature. At 96° it changes into the form, which is the stable form of sulfur between that temperature and 115°. is

The experiment showed further that there were at least two liquid forms. For the sake of convenience, we shall call the one of these produced at the lower temperature S, and the second S. 

It has been determined that when S, changes into a vapor at 445° its composition is S8, and if the temperature is raised still higher, the molecules of S. split up and form S, and finally S2. 

In other words, gaseous molecules containing eight, six, or two atoms may be formed.

The property of existing in different forms is called allotropism, and the different forms of the substance are called allotropes. 

Thus ozone and oxygen are allotropes, and the diamond, graphite, and charcoal are allotropic forms of carbon.

Our studies have shown that sulfur unites directly with metals, sometimes so rapidly that combustion takes place. The class of compounds thus formed is called sulfides. Sulfur also unites directly with many non-metals, as hydrogen, oxygen, carbon, and chlorine. The class of compounds thus formed are very important in everyday life.

Importance of sulfur in everyday life. Large amounts of sulfur and its compounds are being used constantly in the manufacture of widely different substances. In one form or another it is used in bleaching, in the manufacture of matches, rubber goods, disinfectants, medicines, and many other commercial articles. Sulfur is one of the constituents of black gunpowder. 

It is also one of the two ingredients that make up lime-sulfur spray, which is used on fruit trees in winter to kill the scale insects. It is one of the best fungicides known, and sulfur sprays and powdered sulfur are used more extensively each year on leaves and fruits during the growing season. Sulfur is being used as a fertilizer also in certain regions where the soil is deficient in it.

SECTION TWO SULFIDES 

THE sulfides most extensively occurring in nature are those of the metals, the sulfide ores of iron, copper, lead, and zinc being especially common. Of the other sulfides, hydrogen sulfide, H2S, is the most important. It occurs in the waters of many sulfur springs and is present in polluted waters also, since it is one of the products formed in the putrefaction of proteins. Since this compound is extensively used as a reagent in chemical laboratories, a somewhat extended study of it will be included in this section.

Preparation of sulfides. In our experiments in the preceding section we obtained a few metallic sulfides. These metallic sulfides were prepared in much the same way as the metallic oxides (page 54). Hydrogen oxide (water) may be formed by the action of an acid on the oxide of a metal, as is shown, for example, by the following equation : 

CuO + H2SO4 →CuSO4 + H2O We might expect a similar reaction between an acid and the sulfide of a metal. The hypothesis is easily subjected to the experimental test.

SECTION THREE SULFUR DIOXIDE AND SULFUROUS ACID 

WHEN sulfur is burned in the air, it unites with the oxygen of the air to form sulfur dioxide, SO2. This is a heavy gas, which when passed through water unites with the water to form sulfurous acid, H₂SO3. These two important sulfur compounds will be the object of our next studies. 

Preparation of sulfur dioxide. It might be supposed that the easiest way of preparing sulfur dioxide is to burn sulfur in air, but this is not practical, as the resulting product is likely to be mixed with the other gases of the air. In the next experiment we shall prepare sulfur dioxide by a process that is easily controlled and that will give us a fairly pure product.

Uses of sulfur dioxide and sulfurous acid. In one of our experiments we saw that sulfur dioxide caused a flower, a piece of calico, and an apple to lose their bright colors. This property of sulfur dioxide makes it valuable for bleaching straw hats, silk, wool, and fruits. Buildings and vessels are fumigated with it to rid them of rats and insects, and the spores of fungi in cellars and on fruits can be killed with it. It is also coming into extensive use as a substitute for ammonia in refrigerating systems, since it liquefies at only 8° and can be shipped and used at low pressures. The importance of sulfur dioxide and of sulfurous acid in the manufacture of sulfuric acid will be appreciated after a study of the next section.

SECTION FOUR SULFUR TRIOXIDE AND SULFURIC ACID 

SULFUR trioxide bears about the same relation to sulfuric acid as sulfur dioxide bears to sulfurous acid. In each case, when the oxide is brought into contact with water it unites with the water and forms an acid. Sulfur dioxide is the anhydride of sulfurous acid, and sulfur trioxide, SO3, is the anhydride of sulfuric acid. 

Sulfur trioxide, like water, is easily converted into a liquid, a solid, or a gas.

Sulfur trioxide is intimately connected with the manufacture of sulfuric acid, which is of course by far the most important of all the sulfur compounds. We shall therefore devote this section to a detailed study of sulfuric acid, considering sulfur trioxide only in its relation to the acid.

Importance of sulfuric acid. Sulfuric acid has been described as "the most important of all chemicals." It played a leading rôle in the work of the old alchemists, who flourished about a thousand years ago, and in modern chemistry its use has increased until both in the laboratory and in the industrial field it holds first place among chemical reagents. About 2,500,000 tons of it are used yearly in the fertilizer industry alone; in 1918, 2,700,000 tons were employed in the manufacture of explosives. The oil refiners use over 650,000 tons a year, and more than an equal quantity is required for making chemicals and drugs; large amounts (as high as 700,000 tons) are consumed each year in steel pickling (cleansing of the surface) and galvanizing; the dye industry is a heavy consumer of it; and smaller quantities are used in making fabrics, glue, and paint. These figures apply to the United States alone.

Preparation of sulfuric acid. At present the two principal methods of preparing sulfuric acid are the contact process and the lead-chamber process. The chamber process is the older, and 200

Sulfur Trioxide and Sulfuric Acid 

201 both processes are now in use. Only the contact process, which is the easier to understand, will be explained here. 

The first step in this process (as also in the chamber process) is to oxidize SO2 (generally obtained by burning sulfur or roasting sulfide ores) into SO3 2SO₂+ O2 2SO3 The conversion, however, is not so easy as it appears from the above equation, since the reaction takes place only in the presence of a catalyzer. 

In the contact process a mixture of sulfur dioxide and oxygen is brought into contact with finely divided platinum or ferric oxide to form sulfur trioxide. The sulfur dioxide and oxygen used must be freed from dust or other impurities, for these “poison the catalyzer." The temperature must be kept at 400°, for if it falls below this point the reaction slows down, and if it rises above it the reaction reverses, according to the equation: 2SO32SO2 + O2 As fast as the sulfur trioxide is formed it is passed into and dissolved in concentrated sulfuric acid, which by the addition of this gas becomes what is known as fuming sulfuric acid. When this solution is diluted, the sulfur trioxide unites with the water of dilution to form sulfuric acid. The sulfur trioxide unites much more easily with water that is mixed with concentrated sulfuric acid than it does with pure water alone.

We have become familiar with some of the properties of sulfuric acid that give it such wide usefulness in chemistry and in industrial life. Its action upon metals makes it useful in pickling metals, in galvanizing, and in electroplating. Because of its high boiling point, it is used in preparing other acids, and its great dehydrating power makes it useful as a drying agent. It has many uses that have not been mentioned (e.g., in storage batteries), and some idea of the material progress of a country can be gained from an estimate of the amount of sulfuric acid it consumes.

UNIT SIX CHLORINE 

CHLORINE belongs to the halogen family, which consists of four members: fluorine, chlorine, bromine, and iodine (page 393).

At ordinary temperatures fluorine is an intensely active lightyellow gas, chlorine a greenish-yellow gas, bromine a reddishbrown liquid, and iodine a purplish-black solid. If heated, bromine is changed to a reddish vapor and iodine to a vapor of brilliant violet hue. 

The name halogen means the "salt formers" (Greek: halos, salt, and gennan, to produce or form), and was applied to the family because its members unite with metals to form the salts most abundant in sea water. Collectively, these salts are known as halides. Of the halides, NaCl is the most abundant and most familiar.

All the halogens have a usual negative valence of one, and all unite with hydrogen to form hydrogen halides. These are colorless gases, all of which dissolve in water and form strong acids. 

With one of these, HCl, we are already familiar. 

All the halogens are so active chemically that they are found in nature only as compounds. The order of their chemical activity, however, is the reverse of that of their atomic numbers and their depth in color, fluorine showing the greatest activity and iodine the least.

Fluorine is found chiefly in certain rocks, the other halogens mainly in salts dissolved in sea water or in salt deposits formed by the evaporation of sea water. This means that the salts are easily soluble in water and are leached from the earth and carried down to the sea.

In this unit only chlorine, which is the most abundant and most familiar member of its family, will be studied. 205

SECTION ONE CHLORINE 

CHLORINE is a very active element and therefore occurs in nature only in the combined state. It is most commonly found in combination with the alkaline metals (sodium, potassium, calcium), sodium chloride being the most abundant of the compounds containing it. It was isolated by Scheele, a Swedish chemist, in 1774, but was not recognized as an element until some years later.

Preparation. The principal method of preparing chlorine commercially is by electrolysis of sodium chloride solution. A reaction takes place like that described on page 163, the sodium going to the cathode and the chlorine to the anode. 

Hydrogen ions, due to the slight ionization of water, are present in the solution, and these are more easily discharged than the sodium ions. Hence, hydrogen passes off at the cathode, leaving the sodium ions and OH ions in the water to form NaOH.

If a higher voltage current is used, not only the hydrogen ions but some of the sodium ions also are discharged. In this case the atomic sodium at once reacts with the water, displacing and releasing hydrogen. Thus the final products of the electrolysis are always hydrogen, chlorine, and sodium hydroxide. 

Experiment 52 OBJECT. To prepare chlorine from sodium chloride. 

CAUTION. Chlorine is intensely irritating to the respiratory passages.

Experiment 56 OBJECT. To determine the action of chlorine with water. 

Fill a test tube with water saturated with chlorine. Invert it in water as shown in Figure 45. Leave exposed to the sunlight until some change is noted. Result?

Write the equation showing the final products of the reaction of chlorine and water:

As a matter of fact, the chemical changes involved in this reaction are more complicated than the above equation would suggest. In the first reaction HCl and HCIO (hypochlorous acid) are both formed.

H2O + Cl2 · HCl + HClO 

FIG. 45. 

Then the HCIO decomposes into HCl and O, the reaction going on slowly in the dark and being hastened by light. Write the equation for the decomposition of HCIO: 

If chlorine water were left in sunlight, what solution would result?

When chlorine is dissolved in water, a certain per cent of it forms HCl and HCIO, and the balance remains in solution in the water.

Hence, chlorine water contains dissolved chlorine and the two acids. The reaction is a reversible one, and a movable equilibrium is established at a certain point, depending on the concentration of the solution. If a given volume of the gas is dissolved in an equal volume of water, the point of equilibrium is reached when one third of the chlorine has reacted with the water. 

Write an equation to represent this equilibrium:

Uses of chlorine. Thousands of tons of chlorine are used each year in the bleaching of cotton fabrics, linen, and paper. Large quantities are used in the disinfection of sewage and the purification of water. It has many uses in the preparation of drugs and dyes and in the extraction of metals from ores, and chlorine or its compounds are used in many industrial processes.

217 Will all substances containing oxygen liberate chlorine from hydrogen chloride?

The direct oxidation of hydrogen chloride is a second method of preparing chlorine. The oxygen of the air may be used as an oxidizing agent provided a catalyst — as, for example, copper chloride is used. A commercial process for obtaining chlorine known as Deacon's process is based on this reaction. 

Use of hydrogen chloride. Formerly large amounts of hydrogen chloride were used in the preparation of chlorine, but now most chlorine is produced by the electrolysis of sodium chloride. Hydrogen chloride is also employed in cleaning metals, in the preparation of the chlorides of different metals, in dyeing, as a laboratory reagent, and for various other purposes.

UNIT SEVEN CARBON 

CARBON belongs to a family the other members of which are silicon, germanium, tin, and lead (page 457). In accordance with their position in the periodic table, all of them have a valence of which may be either positive or negative, but all of them except silicon are bivalent in some compounds. Only carbon will be considered at this time. 4,

Carbon holds a unique position in chemistry in that it is the basal element in the composition of living protoplasm. Further, all food materials (not including minerals and water) are carbon compounds, as are all fuels and products of living organisms in general. Meat, starch, fat, sugar, illuminating gases, kerosene and other petroleum products, turpentine, alcohol, chloroform, and ether, all contain carbon as a constituent. More than 

200,000 carbon compounds are known, and the study of them constitutes in itself a great branch of chemistry, which is known as organic chemistry.

In preparing complex carbon compounds in laboratories and factories, certain substances obtained from petroleum and by distilling wood, coal, and bones are much used as starting materials, and these are often referred to as the sources of carbon compounds. All these so-called source materials, however, have their source in the products of a single process, photosynthesis, which goes on in green plants, and which we shall study later. How important is the rôle of carbon compounds through the whole organic world will be better appreciated after our study of this element. 219

SECTION ONE CARBON AS AN ELEMENT 

CARBON is one of the few elements that occur in nature in both the elementary and the combined state. The great number of carbon compounds and their importance in the organic world have already been noted. As an element, carbon is found in pure form as diamond and graphite, while coal is composed in part of carbon in the free state and in part of carbon compounds. 

Allotropic forms of carbon. We have found that sulfur exists in several different allotropic forms, but rhombic sulfur is apparently the only stable form. Carbon is unlike sulfur in that all its forms are stable. The diamond and graphite are carbon in crystalline condition. Charcoal, coke, boneblack, and soot are carbon in an amorphous form. It is difficult to think of the brilliant diamond as being the same material as lampblack or charcoal. Yet when they are burned the only product from any of them is CO2. (6 

Diamond and graphite. Diamonds are crystals of carbon.

They are found principally in South Africa, South America, and the East Indies. Generally in their natural condition they are very common-looking stones, and it is not until they have been cut and polished that they take on their brilliancy. There are many brown and even black diamonds that cannot be used as gems. 

Moissan was the first to produce diamonds artificially. He dissolved carbon in molten iron, and then suddenly cooled the iron by plunging it into water. Under these conditions the outside of the mass of iron cools first, and this causes a high pressure on the carbon near the center. This high pressure, together with the cooling, produces the artificial diamond. Most artificial diamonds have been very small. How diamonds are produced in nature is not known, but they are supposed to be formed from carbon compounds that have been subjected to high heat and cooled very slowly under great pressure within the earth. 220

Carbon as an Element 221 The diamond is the hardest substance known. The black or brown diamonds find use in glass cutters, points for drills, and other tools used in cutting hard substances. 

Graphite is a second form of crystalline carbon. It finds use in lubricants because of the greasy nature of its fine crystalline scales, and because within the crystals there is a slipping between the layers of atoms. Unlike amorphous forms of carbon, graphite oxidizes only with great difficulty, and mixtures of graphite and fire clay are used in foundries for lining furnaces and making crucibles. It conducts electricity, which makes possible its use in the carbon arc lamp and in electric batteries. Graphite is also used in stove polishes, in paints, and in many other ways. A mixture of graphite and clay is used in making "lead" pencils. 

Natural deposits of graphite are found in different countries, the finest quality being in Ceylon. Artificially it is made on a large scale by heating hard coal in an electric furnace with only a limited supply of air. The electric furnace produces temperature sufficiently high to vaporize the carbon, which condenses as graphite.

Amorphous carbon. It is customary to list under the heading of amorphous carbon (1) coal, (2) coke, (3) charcoal, (4) boneblack, (5) gas carbon, and (6) lampblack. Each of these will now be considered.

Coal. Coal has been formed from the vegetation growing in and carried down into the swamps and bogs of former geologic times. This vegetation has been transformed into coal by being buried and subjected to the pressure and heat of the earth. 

The amount of pressure and heating that the vegetation has undergone determines whether we have hard coal (anthracite), cannel coal, soft coal (bituminous), or lignite. Anthracite has been subjected to the greatest pressure and heating, and lignite to the least. Most of the volatile matter has been driven out of the anthracite, and it contains about 95 per cent pure carbon. 

The other forms of coal have a decreasing amount of carbon in

Introductory Chemistry the order in which they are named above. Bituminous coal has about 65 per cent of free carbon.

Coke. Coke is made by heating coal in the absence of air, which drives off the volatile products and leaves the carbon behind. From the next experiment it can easily be understood how coke and illuminating gas can be made on a commercial scale from coal.

Coke finds use as a fuel, but its greatest use is as a reducing agent in the extraction of iron. Formerly the gases from coke ovens were allowed to go to waste, but improved apparatus, by which the volatile products are saved, is rapidly coming into use. 

Charcoal. Charcoal is made from wood by a process similar in principle to that used in the manufacture of coke. 

Experiment 62 

OBJECT. To obtain charcoal by the destructive distillation of wood.

The tarry mixture in the bottle is known as pyroligneous acid. It contains acetic acid, alcohol, acetone, and other substances.

As in the manufacture of coke, the apparatus for the commercial production of charcoal has changed from an old form where all volatile matter was lost to a type where these are saved. 

Charcoal finds use in making high grades of iron and steel. 

Animal charcoal, or boneblack. If bones are subjected to destructive distillation, a very finely divided carbon results, known as animal charcoal or boneblack. Its use will become apparent through our work in Experiment 63.

Gas carbon. Gas carbon collects in the upper part of the retorts in the manufacture of coal gas. It is partly crystallized, for it

Introductory Chemistry conducts electricity. A mixture of gas carbon and coal tar is used for making carbons for arc lights. 

Lampblack. Lampblack is another variety of amorphous carbon. It results from an incomplete combustion of carbonaceous fuels, such as oils and gases. Lampblack is obtained by a process analogous to that which takes place when a kerosene lamp smokes. It is used in making paint and printer's ink. 

Physical properties of carbon. The usual physical properties of carbon in its different forms, such as color, odor, solubility, and density, are familiar to all. But carbon has an additional property - the power of adsorption - more or less of a physical nature, which gives it special value for many purposes. The adsorption is supposed to be effected by the molecules of the adsorbed substance being gathered and held on the surfaces of the adsorbing particles. It is doubtless an electrical phenomenon, but no entirely satisfactory explanation of it can be given. 

Experiment 63 OBJECT. To test the adsorptive properties of carbon. 

A. Adsorption of gases. Place a small piece of ferrous sulfide in a test tube and add 2 to 3 cc. of dilute hydrochloric acid. 

Write the equation for the reaction: Observe the odor of the gas which is issuing from the tube and then introduce 2 to 3 grams of powdered charcoal. Shake, and again test the odor. Result? 

Give a reason for the result noted.

B. Adsorption from liquids. Prepare a filter paper and place it in the funnel. Fill the cone to a half-inch depth with powdered charcoal, hollowing it out somewhat in the middle. Take about one third of a test tube of sugar. Melt it very carefully, and then heat it until it becomes dark brown. Pour it into about 25 cc. of water, and warm until the sugar dissolves. Pour this through the prepared filter.

What difference between these results and those in B and C? 

Suggest a reason for the results.

The result in B and C would suggest the use of carbon in the refining of sugar. In the manufacture of sugar, the sugar solutions are evaporated to the crystallization point in a partial vacuum which lowers the boiling point. Nevertheless, there is some charring, and carbon is used to clarify the product. When a substance is in an ionic state, adsorption seems to be less marked, as is shown in D above. 

Many substances besides charcoal have the power of adsorption, but charcoal is one of the most effective and on this account is used in gas masks (Fig. 48). The effectiveness of charcoal as an adsorbent is due largely to its porosity. Charcoal-from the shells of nuts (e.g., coconut) is peculiarly effective because in these the cells are fine and furnish large areas of adsorbing surfaces. Fine-grained, dense wood (e.g., boxwood) also gives charcoal of a superior grade.

Chemical properties of carbon. There are certain chemical properties of carbon that are familiar. We know that when the temperature has been sufficiently raised carbon is oxidized with the evolution of light and heat. The compound formed during this oxidation depends on the supply of oxygen. With a limited supply, carbon monoxide is formed. 

Write the equation for the reaction. When there is sufficient oxygen, carbon dioxide is formed. Write the equation for the reaction.

Introductory Chemistry 

Carbon has so great an affinity for oxygen that it unites readily not only with free oxygen, but also with oxygen that is in combination with other elements. This property of carbon causes millions of tons of it to be used annually to free metals from their ores. The process is easily demonstrated by an experiment.

Carbon compounds formed in the electric furnace. At the high temperature of the electric furnace, carbon will react with many elements with which it does not ordinarily combine. The heat of the furnace is produced by placing a substance of high

Carbon as an Element 227 resistance between the ends of the electrodes in the furnace. 

This substance is generally a mixture of the ores among which the reactions are taking place, or air may be the resisting substance where the current arcs cross from one electrode to the other. The following are three important compounds that are prepared in the electric furnace :

Carbon disulfide. If sulfur vapor is allowed to come in contact with carbon at the heat of the electric furnace, CS2 is formed. 

Write the equation for the reaction. To what compound of carbon and oxygen does carbon disulfide correspond?

Carbon disulfide is an inflammable liquid that volatilizes easily. It is used for destroying weevil in grain and other seeds and for killing burrowing animals in their holes. 

Calcium carbide. If a mixture of lime, CaO, and carbon are heated in the electric furnace, calcium carbide, CaC2, results. 

Write the equation for the reaction. This calcium carbide will react with water to produce acetylene, C2H2, and calcium hydroxide. Acetylene is used for both heating and lighting. Mixed with oxygen, it makes one of our hottest flames.

Carborundum. Silica, SiO2, reacts with coke at the high temperature of the electric furnace. If a limited amount of coke is used, free silicon and CO are produced. If more coke is used, SiC, silicon carbide, and CO are formed. 

Write the two equations for the reactions.

Introductory Chemistry 

Silicon carbide is known commercially as carborundum. It is one of the hardest substances known. Its extreme hardness makes it useful for grinding and for polishing metals. The carborundum crystals may be transparent or may have a color varying in depth from emerald green to a deep black. 

Importance of carbon. Some elements are important because they are useful in their elementary state; others, because of compounds which they form. Undoubtedly it has already been realized that carbon as an element is very interesting and important interesting because of its great variety of forms, and important because each of these forms has a commercial value. 

Ewing Galloway

FIG. 51. Scene in the plant of the Carborundum Company at Niagara Falls, New York. 

Grinding wheels and other articles are made by mixing the pulverized carborundum with powdered porcelain and iron, molding it into the desired forms, and firing it in a kiln.

Carbon as an Element 229 Some of the uses of coke, charcoal, boneblack, lampblack, diamond, and graphite have been mentioned. The most important of all these is the deoxidation of iron ores by coke, for on this process we are dependent for our iron and steel supply. 

With the use of coal as a source of heat and power we are all familiar — perhaps too familiar to realize its great importance or to appreciate that of all the hundreds of chemical processes man can carry on, he depends chiefly on one, the oxidation of carbon, for his energy supply. The use of carbon for this purpose is possible, because of the deoxidation of carbon by green plants and the building by them of compounds that yield energy when they are oxidized.

In our next studies the importance of carbon as a former of compounds will become apparent.

SECTION TWO CARBON DIOXIDE AND CARBONIC ACID 

CARBON dioxide is present in the air, as we have learned (page 109), but it is estimated that there is forty times as much in the waters of the sea as in the atmosphere, and vast amounts of it are tied up in the carbonate rocks (e.g., chalk, limestone, dolomite) of the earth. It often escapes from volcanoes in considerable amounts and is dissolved in some natural spring waters, such as those at Saratoga and Vichy.

Preparation. Carbon dioxide is a product of the oxidation of carbon. Hence in respiration, fermentation, the decay of organic matter, and the burning of carbonaceous fuels it is constantly being formed. It may also be obtained through the decomposition of carbonates (except the alkali carbonates) by heat.

CaCO3 → CaO + CO, MgCO3 MgO + CO₂ The easiest way to prepare carbon dioxide in the laboratory is to treat a carbonate with an acid.

Experiment 65 OBJECT. To prepare carbon dioxide. 

Arrange a generator similar to the one used for the preparation of hydrogen. Fill the generator with marble chips (largely calcium carbonate) to the depth of about 3 cm. Let the thistle tube touch the bottom of the bottle. Pour in enough water to cover the bottom. 

Then add a 6 N solution of HCl a few cc. at a time to generate an even evolution of the gas. Hold a bottle beneath the generating tube and then pour the contents of the bottle over a lighted candle. 

Collect four bottles of the carbon dioxide. Place them upright on the desk, covering them with glass plates. Save these for testing the properties of the gas.

Do not take down the apparatus, for it will be needed later. 231
