Uses of carbon dioxide. Many of the properties of carbon dioxide which we have studied would suggest uses for this gas. 

Some of the more important of these uses are as follows: 

1. In refrigeration. The boiling point of CO₂ given on page 

232 would suggest that it might be used like ammonia and sulfur dioxide in refrigeration work. With a mixture of carbon dioxide and ether, a temperature as low as - 78.5° C. may be attained. 

2. In fire extinguishers. Experiment 66 showed that it does not support combustion; hence if a fire is enveloped in an atmosphere of CO2, the flames are extinguished. 

The carbon-dioxide fire extinguisher contains a vessel of

Carbon Dioxide and Carbonic Acid 237 sulfuric acid and a concentrated solution of baking soda, NaHCO3, with an arrangement for bringing the solutions together when desired. Write the equation for the reaction by which CO2 is formed in it. + + The pressure caused by the evolution of the carbon dioxide forces the solution from the apparatus, and hence the flames are soon enveloped in an atmosphere of carbon dioxide. The density of the gas aids greatly in keeping it in contact with the burning material. 

3. In effervescent beverages. The effervescence of soda water and similar beverages is caused by the carbon dioxide which is forced into the water under pressure. 

Since carbon dioxide is a result of fermentation, there will also be an effervescence when any beverage in which there has been fermentation after bottling is opened. 

4. In baking. The rising of bread and cake is caused in the main by carbon dioxide passing up through the dough and expanding under the influence of the heat. This CO2 may be generated by fermentation of sugar by yeast, or it may result from the reaction of an acid with a carbonate. The latter is generally sodium bicarbonate (baking soda) and the former is either the lactic acid found in sour milk or potassium acid tartrate, KHC4H4O6, which is one of the ingredients of cream of tartar. 

5. In medicine. Carbon dioxide stimulates the respiratory centers, and the increased amount of carbon dioxide in the blood is the chief cause of the deeper and more rapid respiration after physical exercise is taken. Sometimes after anæsthetics have been used and in cases of poisoning with certain gases, carbon dioxide is introduced into the air used by the patient to cause deeper breathing and greater ventilation of the blood 6. Industrial uses. The chief industrial uses of carbon dioxide are in making sodium carbonate (page 279), "white lead" (page 

510), and "dry ice."

Introductory Chemistry 

7. As "dry ice." Carbon dioxide solidifies at − 79° and solid carbon dioxide is finding increasing use for cooling purposes. It has two great advantages over ice. The first is that it passes directly from the solid to the gaseous state, thus eliminating the troublesome liquid state. The second advantage is that in the change of state a very great amount of heat is absorbed. On the other hand, the accumulation of its vapors may make it objectionable for certain uses. 

In transporting ice cream, 200 pounds of solid carbon dioxide replaces 3000 pounds of ice and 600 pounds of salt, and in the transportation of fish 1200 pounds of it proved as effective as 17,000 pounds of ice and 1700 pounds of salt. Long-distance shipments of perishable goods that formerly required frequent re-icing can now be sent through with the initial supply of carbon dioxide. Furthermore, this dry ice can be included in small and light packages of butter, eggs, vegetables, or other foods. If desired, very low temperatures can be secured through its use.

SECTION THREE CARBON MONOXIDE; GASEOUS FUELS 

As its name suggests, carbon monoxide is a compound of carbon with one atom of oxygen. Since we know that the valence of carbon is 4 and that it ordinarily unites with two atoms of oxygen, we might infer that a limited supply of oxygen would be favorable for the formation of carbon monoxide. This idea is correct. 

The equation for the formation of this compound is : C+ O2(limited) — (1) In Experiment 66 it was seen that carbon dioxide could be reduced to carbon by the activity of metals high in the electromotive series. Metals less active bring about only a partial reduction. For example, when zinc is heated in an atmosphere of CO2, the latter is reduced to CO and ZnO is formed.

CO2 + Zn Carbon will bring about a similar reduction.

CO₂+ C (2) This reaction takes place in a coal fire. Carbon dioxide is first formed, but as this passes up through the hot carbon, the reaction represented by Equation 3 takes place, and then the carbon monoxide burns with a blue flame at the top surface of the coal.

Carbon monoxide is the most important of all the reducing agents used in industry. It will be seen later how it is employed in the preparation of iron.

Physiological effects of carbon monoxide. Carbon monoxide forms a very stable compound with the hæmoglobin of the blood

Carbon Monoxide; Gaseous Fuels 

243 and so ties up the hæmoglobin and removes its oxygen-carrying power. So much of it escapes from the exhausts of gasoline engines that the ventilation of tunnels through which many automobiles pass is very difficult, and even in open city streets where the traffic is dense it collects in quantities that are believed to be harmful. Many fatalities are due to carbon monoxide poisoning. Usually the gas comes from automobile engines in garages or other gasoline engines with the exhaust discharging indoors, from gas water heaters, and from leaky gas hose and gas fixtures. The treatment for carbon monoxide poisoning is artificial respiration. 

GASEOUS FUELS 

Besides being used as a reducing agent, carbon monoxide is the principal constituent of most gaseous fuels. Of these fuels the four main ones are coal gas, natural gas, water gas, and producer gas. The chemistry of the subject will be better understood by the student if he will at this time read the discussion of the hydrocarbons, beginning on page 459. 

Coal gas. Experiment 61 should have given an idea of how coal gas may be made by the destructive distillation of coal. 

A ton of coal of average composition yields about 12,000 cubic feet of gas and from 1200 to 1400 pounds of coke. The manufacture of coal gas is simple, and because of the lower percentage of carbon monoxide in it coal gas is safer than water gas or producer gas. Most of the carbon of the coal, however, remains in the retorts as coke. 

Natural gas.

Natural gas is found in connection with petroleum and has its origin in the same source (page 463). It is not a chemical compound, but a mixture of gases, as shown on page 244. The percentages of the various gases in this mixture vary in different localities and during the lives of individual wells. 

Water gas. This gas is made by passing steam over red-hot coal, when the following reaction takes place :

C+H2O−H,+ CO

Introductory Chemistry 

A certain temperature is required for the above reaction to take place. Hence it is necessary at frequent intervals to turn off the steam and pass heated air over the coal to reheat it to the reacting temperature.

Since this gas consists of CO and H2, it burns with a non-luminous flame. If the water gas is to be used for lighting purposes, it is enriched with petroleum oils consisting of hydrocarbons which burn with a highly luminous flame.

Producer gas. This is produced by passing heated air over heated coal. When the air first strikes the hot coal, carbon dioxide is formed. As this passes up through the hot coal, it is reduced to carbon monoxide. The carbon monoxide and the nitrogen of the air are the principal constituents of producer gas, as is shown in the following table. Gas engines are run with this gas and give much more power than is secured by the use of the same amount of coal under steam boilers.

In this connection it should be stated that not all luminosity is due to heated solid particles, since burning hydrogen gives some light and the ammonia flame is luminous. However, incandescent solids add greatly to the amount of light given off by a flame, as is shown by the use of mantles on gas lights, lanterns, and lamps. These mantles are composed of about 99 per cent thorium oxide and 1 per cent cerium oxide, and become incandescent at a comparatively low temperature.

SECTION FOUR OTHER COMMON CARBON COMPOUNDS 

THE fundamental process in organic chemistry is photosynthesis; for in this process the energy used by plants and animals is stored and in it the source material of all the thousands of carbon compounds found in living organisms is formed. This is a process carried on in the green parts of plants, and by it carbon dioxide and water are united to form formaldehyde. CO2 + H2O CH₂O+ O2 — → This is a deoxidizing process, and energy is required to carry it out. This energy is supplied by the light. Chlorophyll (the green pigment of the plant) is also necessary. It seems to act as a catalyzer which functions only in the presence of light. 

After the formaldehyde has been manufactured, its molecules quickly combine with each other to form simple sugars, C6H12O6. 

The process of combining molecules of the same kind to form larger molecules of the same percentage composition is called condensation, or polymerization. The complex substances thus formed are polymers of the simple substance out of which they are built. Formaldehyde has been polymerized into glucose synthetically by irradiation with ultra-violet light, and since carbon dioxide and water have been caused to unite in the same way, photosynthesis (and consequently food production) is now possible without plants.

The single sugars. The single sugars all have the simple formula C6H12O6. As in many other compounds in the field of organic chemistry, their characteristic properties are not due to any difference in the number and kinds of atoms in their molecules, but to differences in the arrangement of the atoms within the molecule. The two most common of these are glucose and fructose (fruit sugar). The structural formulas (diagrams of the way the atoms in the molecules are joined together) of glucose and of fructose are: 249

Introductory Chemistry 

Arthur, Webster, and Gutherie

FRUCTOSE 

Glucose can be made from starch. It is used extensively in candies and in syrups, but is less sweet than ordinary sugar.

Other Common Carbon Compounds 

251 The double sugars. The double sugars have the formula 

C12H22O11. The most common of these are saccharose, or common table sugar; maltose, or malt sugar; and lactose, or milk sugar. Of these, saccharose is much the sweetest, and sugar cane and the sugar beet are extensively grown because they yield it in large amounts. 

By the enzymes in the digestive juices or by boiling with acids, the double sugars can be broken into single sugars. Saccharose, for example, takes up a molecule of water and yields glucose and fructose.

C12H22O11+ H2O →C6H12O6 + C6H12O6 (Glucose) (Fructose) (Saccharose)

Maltose when similarly treated yields two molecules of glucose, and lactose yields one molecule of glucose and one of another single sugar called galactose. Because the double sugars can be so easily broken into single sugars, it is generally assumed that they are formed by uniting two single sugar molecules, an H2O being dropped out in the process. 

Starches. Starches are polymeres of sugar with the general formula (C6H1005). When they are broken up, they yield malt sugar, but the size of the starch molecule is unknown. It is probable, however, that it is very large, and n in the above formula may easily be 10, 20, or more. 

Starch is a reserve food supply in the plant. In most plants, as soon as the sugar reaches a certain concentration it is transformed into insoluble starch. In many plants the sugar is transported from the leaves to special parts (e.g., the tubers of potatoes and the seeds of grains) and is there converted into starch for storage. When this food supply is needed by the plant or its offspring, it is reconverted into sugar by enzymes, and is thus made soluble so that it can be transported to the parts where it is required. The starch is stored in small grains that are surrounded by a thin layer of cellulose. When heated in water, the

Introductory Chemistry grains swell and burst and starch paste is formed. Sugar cane and the sugar beet retain their excess carbohydrates as saccharose, and sweet corn carries less starch and more sugar than ordinary corn. In the digestive processes of animals, starch is converted into maltose by the saliva, and the maltose into glucose by the pancreatic juice.

Dextrin. By heating, starch can be converted (or split up) into dextrin. This is a sweetish gummy substance of the same general formula, (C6H10O5)n, as starch, but of course the molecules are smaller. It is formed in the crust of bread and gives it its characteristic flavor. Mucilage is made by dissolving dextrin in water.

Corn syrup is made by heating corn starch with acid and splitting the starch into a mixture of dextrin, maltose, and glucose. 

S. D. Warren Paper Co. 

FIG. 57. The beater room in a paper mill. The first step in paper making is the pulping of the wood. Then the pulp is bleached and passed through the beaters, where the fibers are reduced to suitable lengths, and various chemicals for coloring, loading, or sizing the paper are added. The pulp is next strained, and then converted into the finished product by rolling into sheets, drying, and surfacing. Paper is made from cellulose, which has a composition similar to that of starch, and like starch is built up from the products of photosynthesis.

Other Common Carbon Compounds 

253 Ethanol, or ethyl alcohol. Ethanol is ordinary grain alcohol. 

Its formula is C2H5OH, and it is made by the fermentation of a simple sugar, usually glucose. The sugar from which it is made is derived from the starch of grain or potatoes, from the juice of palms or other plants, or from the juices of fruits. The sugar is converted into alcohol by zymase, one of the enzymes of the yeast plant.

C6H12O6+ Zymase 2C2H5OH + 2CO₂ The alcohol in the fermented material may be concentrated by distillation. Water, as well as alcohol, passes over in the distilling process, and to secure a product that contains a high percentage of alcohol, the process must be repeated several times. 

When absolute alcohol (alcohol free from water) is desired, calcium oxide is introduced into the distilling apparatus. This unites with the water and thereby aids in securing a water-free product. 

Alcohol burns with a colorless flame and possesses a high heat value. Because of this, and because of its many other properties, alcohol has a number of important industrial and commercial uses. 

Acetic acid. Acetic acid is one of many organic acids (page 

471). It is produced through the fermentation of ethyl alcohol by a species of bacteria. In the reaction, oxygen from the air unites with alcohol to produce acetic acid and water. Complete the equation: 

→C2H4O2 + (Acetic acid) 

Acetic acid is a colorless liquid which freezes at 16.7°. A 

30 per cent solution in water is the concentration which is used commercially. By repeated freezing, the water content of this solution may be reduced to about 1 per cent. In this degree of purity acetic acid is known as "glacial acetic acid." 

Acetic acid is used extensively as a solvent in the manufacture of dyes, in the making of white lead by the Dutch process, and in many other industrial processes.

Introductory Chemistry 

Carbon is remarkable among the elements because of the great number of the compounds which it forms and the size and complexity of the molecules of many of these compounds. The valence of the element is 4, and it can satisfy this valence by holding to another carbon atom with one or two valence bonds and uniting with other atoms and radicals with the other valence bonds. In this way rings and long chains of carbon atoms are joined together, each one with other atoms or radicals attached to it, as is shown in the structural formulas for glucose and fructose on page 250. Both commercially and in all the biological sciences the chemistry of carbon is more important than the chemistry of any other element.

UNIT EIGHT THE METALLIC ELEMENTS 

A METAL is an element that has three or less than three valence electrons. A non-metal is an element that has four or more valence electrons. In chemical reactions metals are losers of electrons and non-metals are gainers of electrons. 

The above statements hold in a general way, and they set forth distinctions that it is well to have in mind. They are, however, too sweeping to be strictly applied, for many of the elements with valences of three and four may either gain or lose electrons, according to their environment, and some elements of variable valence (e.g., chromium, manganese) may react either as a metal or a non-metal, according to the number of electrons in the valence shell. In reality, metals and non-metals are but the two parts of one continuous larger group, and no hard and fast chemical distinctions between them can be made. 

All metals are crystallizable. All of them possess luster. 

Most of them exhibit the properties of malleability, tenacity, and hardness to a considerable degree, which accounts for their value to mankind. All of them except mercury (gallium and cæsium melt at 30° and 28.5°, respectively) are solids at ordinary room temperature. They are relatively good conductors of electricity and heat, which means that if they are brought into contact with either of these forms of energy, the energy is rapidly and without great resistance transmitted throughout the mass. 

The lightest metal is lithium, which has a specific gravity a little more than half that of water (0.59), and the heaviest is osmium, which is 22.5 times as heavy as water. Those metals having a specific gravity of less than 5 are spoken of as the light metals, and those of higher specific gravity, as the heavy metals. 

This description of the physical properties of metals would seem to indicate that they are set off more clearly from the non- 257

Introductory Chemistry metals physically than chemically. This is not necessarily the case, for non-metals, such as carbon and selenium, may be good electrical conductors, and the non-metal tellurium even shows metallic luster. Therefore, from the physical point of view it is plain that the line of demarcation between metals and nonmetals cannot be sharply drawn. The explanation of many of the physical properties of the metals doubtless lies in atomic structure, for in the table of the elements the physical periodicity may be even more marked than the chemical. 

All but about twenty of the elements are metals. Uses have been found for about half of them. Doubtless others will become of great value as we come to understand their properties better and learn how they may be employed.

SECTION ONE ORES AND METALLURGY 

SOME metals are found in the native, or free, state. Most of them occur only as compounds. The compounds found in nature that contain useful metals in such form that they can be profitably extracted are called ores.

Introductory Chemistry 

Metallurgy, in its narrower and original sense, is defined as the science of extracting metals from their ores. In the wider sense in which the term is used, it includes the preparation of alloys and all chemical work with metal compounds. 

Methods employed in the preparation of metals. Some classes of compounds - as, for example, the sulfates, phosphates, and silicates require the employment of special methods for the extraction of the metals in them. There are, however, certain methods that have wide application in the separation of different metals from their ores, and some of the processes based on these principles will be briefly discussed here. 

1. Heating oxides with an electronizing agent. In the table on page 259 it will be noted that many useful metals occur as oxides. 

In our earlier work we have seen that the copper of copper oxide can be electronized to metallic copper by the use of hydrogen or carbon.

EXERCISE 

Write the equations representing the reactions when copper is displaced from copper oxide with hydrogen and with carbon, respectively. 

Iron and other metals may be freed from their oxides in the same way. Complete the equation showing the reaction when ferric oxide of iron is heated with carbon: Fe2O3 + Where carbon fails to free a metal from its oxide, aluminum, which is a more powerful electronizing agent, is employed. It would be, of course, too expensive to use aluminum for the production of the cheaper metals like iron and zinc, but some of the more valuable metals are liberated from their ores by heating with aluminum. Chromium is an example of a metal that is freed from its oxide in this way. Cr₂O3 + 2A1 - 2Cr + Al2O3 Does aluminum or chromium have the greater affinity for electrons?

Does a "powerful electronizing agent" gain or lose electrons easily?

Electronization with aluminum has the advantage that it gives a purer metal without the possibility of the formation of carbides, which frequently occurs when carbon is used as the electronizing agent. Recently a mixture of the rare earth metals ("misch metal") has been employed in the freeing of metals from their oxides. 

2. Converting carbonates into oxides by roasting. If a carbonate of a metal is heated, CO2 is driven off and the oxide of the metal formed. Then by electronization of the metal it is freed from the oxide, as has been described above. Write equations showing the reactions in the production of free zinc from ZnCO3: 

3. Conversion of sulfides into oxides. By reference to the electromotive series it will be seen that oxygen lies below sulfur (page 387). Therefore you would expect that oxygen would have a greater attraction for the valence electrons of metals than sulfur. That this is the case is shown when a metallic sulfide is heated in oxygen or air. Complete the equation:

ZnS + O2 After the oxide is obtained, the metal is freed, as has been indicated above. 

4. Electrolysis. We have seen that the metal ion in a salt solution is positively charged and that if an electric current be passed through such a solution, the metal is carried to the cathode. If the metal is one that does not react with water or with other substances in solution, it will be deposited at the cathode in elementary form. Solvents other than water are sometimes used, as in the preparation of aluminum (page 554). 

Recently the electrolytic method has been extensively employed in the preparation of metals. In some cases the current is

Introductory Chemistry passed through a dry salt of the metal rather than a solution. 

Because of the poor conductivity of the dry salt a large amount of the electrical energy is converted into heat, which fuses the salt. 

The molten compound then decomposes, and the metal is carried to the cathode and there deposited. The electrolysis of fused chlorides and hydroxides is one of the most economical methods of obtaining metals, especially those like sodium and potassium, which cannot be prepared from solution because of their reaction with water.

Preparation of metal compounds. Many metal compounds are desired because they are themselves valuable; others because the free metal or some other useful compound may be obtained from them. In the manufacture of these compounds, as in the extraction of metals from their ores, a few methods of general application and many special methods are employed. Some of the methods having a wide application have already been used in our work, and these will now be briefly reviewed. 

1. By direct union of a free metal with another element. We have seen how metal oxides, sulfides, and chlorides can be prepared by this method. Write equations representing such reactions. 

2. By decomposition of a compound with heat. Preceding experiments have shown that compounds may be produced by decomposing other compounds by heat.  

Pb(NO3)2 + heat

CaCO3 + heat Fe(OH)3 + heat 3. By methods based on equilibrium in solutions. By reference to the section on chemical equilibrium, write equations to illustrate how a compound of a metal may be prepared under each of the three different conditions indicated below:

When a reaction is carried to completion because one product is a gas.

When a reaction goes to completion because of an insoluble product.

As we shall see in our analytical work, a great variety of compounds may be prepared and isolated by the proper selection of methods based on the principle of removing from the field of action one of the reacting substances. 

4. By methods based on fusion. A good example of a reaction that can be brought about by the fusion method is the following: Na2CO3 + BaSO4 BaCO3 + Na2SO4 –

Barium sulfate is insoluble in water and in most acids, and it is not decomposed by heat; but when it is heated with sodium carbonate, the latter melts and forms a medium in which the barium sulfate dissolves. A reaction then takes place in which insoluble BaCO3 is formed. When the cooled solution is placed in water, the sodium sulfate and any excess sodium carbonate dissolve and in this way are separated from the insoluble barium carbonate.

Solubility of compounds. In chemical industry many of the manufacturing methods are based upon the differences in the solubility of compounds, and in analytical chemistry we are able to separate substances largely because of these differences. To know the solubility of compounds in water and other reagents is, therefore, very useful. Some general ideas as to the solubility of compounds can be gained from an examination of the table on the next page. It should be understood, however, that there is no such thing as an insoluble substance, if we mean by insoluble that none of the substance goes into solution. 

For example, barium sulfate and barium chromate are considered insoluble; yet experiments have shown that 100 cc. of water will dissolve .00024 gram of the former and .0001 gram of the latter. In other words, you would have to put these respective amounts into 100 cc. of water before any would be precipitated. When the solubility of a compound is of the order of the solubilities of barium sulfate and barium chromate, the compound is considered insoluble.

All hydroxides are insoluble except Alloys. Many metals combine with other elements (usually metals) to form metal-like substances that are called "alloys."

When one of the constituents is mercury, the alloy is known as

an “amalgam.” So great is the importance of alloys that without them our industrial and manufacturing life could not be carried on.

Vanadium steel, nickel steel, titanium steel, manganese steel, molybdenum steel, tungsten steel, and chrome steel are some of the widely used iron alloys. Brass, bronze, and copper tin-plate are copper alloys. Aluminum alloys, the most important of which are magnalium and duralumin, are also fast coming into use.

Recent studies have shown that there are at least three general classes of alloys; namely: (1) heterogeneous mixtures; (2) homogeneous mixtures or solid solutions; and (3) compounds. 

1. Heterogeneous mixtures. When a molten mixture of certain substances with different melting points is allowed to cool, the one first separating out is the one whose addition will raise the melting point. The temperature which is sufficiently low to cause all the substances to begin to separate out is known as the eutectic point. 

In alloys formed from materials of this type, crystals of one substance are found embedded in the other substance or substances. This heterogeneous mixture gives the properties required for certain uses. Babbitt metal, an alloy of lead and antimony, is an example of an alloy of this type. 

2. Homogeneous mixtures or solid solutions. In solid solutions each crystal settling out contains a certain portion of all the constituents of the alloy. This constant composition is not obtained unless we have carefully controlled conditions. For example, the cooling must take place very slowly if such results are to be obtained. An alloy composed of gold and platinum is an example of an alloy of this kind. 

3. Compounds. From our study of the structure of matter, it should be no more surprising to learn that a metal may unite with other metals than it was to find that such non-metals as oxygen and sulfur unite with each other. 

In some of the alloys (e.g., sodium or potassium amalgam) one metal is supposed to unite with another metal, forming a true

Introductory Chemistry chemical compound. Although such compounding is theoretically possible and experimentally realized, yet experimental results are somewhat puzzling because of the unique valence shown by some of the metals. For example, in NaZn₁2 and 

NaCd, sodium shows a remarkably high valence. A further fact of interest is that chemists have shown that a metal may even unite with itself. For example, it is believed that PbPb and PbPb₂ have been prepared. Our present conception of the structure of matter is not inconsistent with the formation of such compounds.

Properties of alloys. Certain elements have been found to impart specific properties in general to the alloys in which they are found. Cadmium and bismuth increase fusibility; tin and lead, both soft metals, impart hardness and tenacity; and arsenic and antimony produce brittleness. Generally an alloy is more easily fusible than any of the metals composing it. These properties of alloys in some cases can be traced to a simple mixing of the crystals of the respective components. In other cases the properties of the alloy suggest that the compounds in it act on one another in lowering the vapor pressure, and hence in changing the melting point. In other cases there is such an entire change of properties that the difference can only be accounted for in terms of chemical reaction.

A study of the properties of alloys has led to the classification as given above; yet this classification does not seem to account for all the properties of all the alloys that we know. It is difficult to see how such small quantities of one element as are sometimes introduced can cause the great alterations in the properties of the other that we sometimes find, and in cases where many facts indicate that a compound has been formed, the alloys may retain many of the physical properties of one or all of the components. 

It has been suggested that this last phenomenon may be attributed to the fact that in these alloys we have compounds in which there is a less complete transference of electrons than in most

cases of chemical reaction. That is, in powerfulness the forces exerted in alloy compound formation are intermediate between the forces which act in solutions and those which act in ordinary compound formation. It is quite generally believed that the forces operating in all these processes are fundamentally connected with the structure of matter and hence are electrical in nature. 

The importance of the metal alloys lies in the fact that often the peculiar properties needed in a material required for a particular purpose cannot be found in one metal but can be found in a combination of metals. Thus tungsten steel, consisting of two metals, has the hardness necessary in the machine tools used in metal working, and duralumin possesses the combined lightness and strength desired for airplane and airship construction. Since a practically limitless number of alloys can be made by combining the different metals in different proportions, it is evident that the field has possibilities as yet realized only to a slight extent. More will be said about alloys under the treatment of the respective metals.

SECTION TWO THE ALKALI METALS 

THE metals of the alkali group are lithium, sodium, potassium, rubidium, and cæsium. They are known as the alkali metals because the hydroxides and carbonates of the two most common of them, sodium and potassium, have been used for centuries as alkalies. Of these metals, sodium is the most abundant. Potassium is next in abundance, and lithium third, while rubidium and cæsium are rare.

The metals of this group when freshly cut are silvery white and extremely soft. They are all oxidized (deëlectronized) easily on exposure to air. It will be recalled how those with which we have worked were kept under kerosene or oil in order to prevent contact with air.

The outstanding feature of the members of the alkali group is the similarity of their properties, both physical and chemical. 

Where there is variation of properties, it is only one of degree, and only sodium and its compounds will be studied in detail. 

The following table brings out the gradation of certain physical properties:

Spectra of elements. Had the flames in the above experiment been examined with a spectroscope, it would have been found that each element had a characteristic spectrum. For example, if the yellow flame of sodium had been examined, fine lines very

Introductory Chemistry close together would have been seen in the yellow region of the spectrum.* In the spectrum of potassium, red and violet lines appear. Also, the alkali-earth family calcium, barium, and strontium can be identified by the flame test, and therefore they likewise might be expected to have characteristic lines in the spectra, which is the case. In fact, all metals have characteristic spectra, but the spectrum test is not a very feasible one for some of the metals low in the electromotive series because of the high temperatures at which they volatilize. 

Solubility of salts of the alkali metals. Practically all the salts of the alkali metals are soluble in water. This property gives these compounds a very exceptional importance in chemistry. 

Many compounds which are insoluble in water are valueless until they can be brought into solution in this medium. To effect this, the general method of procedure is to convert the substance into an alkali salt, which will in all probability be soluble in water. 

Thus "mercuro-chrome" is made soluble by producing its sodium salt, which carries into solution the complex radical of this compound.

SECTION THREE SODIUM AND ITS COMPOUNDS 

SODIUM is not the most active of the alkali metals; yet its activity is far too great for it ever to be found free in nature. 

Large quantities of it are found in South America in the form of sodium nitrate. Another important compound of sodium is cryolite, which is a double fluoride of sodium and aluminum, Na AlF6. This is found in largest amounts in Greenland and is used in the electrolytic preparation of aluminum (page 554). 

Sodium is found in greatest abundance, however, as sodium chloride. This compound is very widely distributed, being found in sea water, in inland lakes which have no outlet, as rock salt, in salt wells, and in small quantities in practically all rocks and soils. In addition to its wide domestic use, sodium chloride is the basic compound in the preparation of hundreds of other compounds.

Preparation. Sodium may be obtained by heating its carbonates with carbon in a retort and allowing the escaping vapors of the metal to pass under kerosene for condensation. The reaction is according to the following equation :

Na2CO3+2C3CO + 2Na In the above reaction the heat first decomposes the sodium carbonate into sodium oxide and carbon dioxide. Na2CO3 Na2O + CO2 Then in the presence of the heated carbon the CO2 is electronized to CO and the Na2O to the free metal. The products formed are volatile and hence leave the field of action, so the reaction goes on to completion. 

A more convenient method of preparing sodium is by the electrolysis of the fused hydroxide. In this process hydrogen and the metal are liberated at the cathode and oxygen is liberated 273

Introductory Chemistry at the anode. This method was discovered in 1808 by Sir Humphry Davy, who observed that when an electric current was passed through a piece of potash, there appeared at the cathode "small globules, having a high metallic luster and being precisely similar, in visible characters, to quicksilver." Soda behaved in a similar manner. 

Commercially, sodium is prepared by the use of a Castner

Cell. This consists essentially of a large iron vat in which have been introduced two iron anodes, and at the base of the vat an elevated iron bar which serves as a cathode. The hydroxide is placed in the vat and heated sufficiently to melt it. Solid sodium hydroxide Sodium Gauze Anode Cathode Melted sodium hydroxide

The alkali metal is deposited FIG. 58. Diagram of apparatus for the at the cathode, and because of preparation of sodium. its lightness it quickly rises to the surface, where it can be scraped off and placed under kerosene for preservation. Electronically, the reaction may be represented by the following equations:

NaOH Na+ + OH-

EXERCISES 

I. What is the appearance of a freshly cut piece of sodium? 

2. What is the result of exposing this freshly cut surface to the air? 

3. Write an equation representing the reaction of sodium with water: + When sodium is exposed to air and moisture, the coating formed is sodium hydroxide. The oxygen of the air first forms the oxide or peroxide. Then the moisture reacts with the oxide or peroxide, giving a film of sodium hydroxide. 

Explanation of reaction of sodium with water. Water ionizes to a small degree, giving a slight concentration of hydrogen ions. Sodium stands above hydrogen in the displacement series. Hence, when sodium is brought into contact with water, the sodium atoms lose their valence electrons to the hydrogen ions. This converts the hydrogen ions into atomic hydrogen, which escapes from the solution in the form of hydrogen molecules (page 164).

The removal of these hydrogen ions displaces the ionization equilibrium of water, producing more hydrogen ions, which in turn draw electrons from the atoms of sodium. Thus the reaction between sodium and water goes on to completion, forming sodium hydroxide and hydrogen as the new products.

Write out the chemical reactions, including the electronic exchanges involved:

To learn exactly how this method is employed commercially, consult a textbook on industrial chemistry. 

The details of the second method will be learned from the next experiment, but before beginning the experiment, attention will be called to some of the conditions that must be met to bring the manufacturing process to a successful outcome. Similar reasoning may be offered in preparing other compounds. 

If sodium hydroxide is to be prepared by double decomposition, two conditions must be met: (1) One of the compounds must furnish the sodium and the other the hydroxyl radical. (2) In order that the reaction may go to completion, one of the compounds formed must be removed from the field of action by being volatile, insoluble, or only slightly ionized. Many compounds could be chosen that would furnish the sodium and the hydroxyl ions, but the second condition greatly limits our choice of materials.

There are three important varieties of this salt: (1) The crystallized variety, which crystallizes with ten molecules of water of hydration, and which is efflorescent; (2) the anhydrous variety, which is deliquescent; (3) the monohydrated variety, which is stable under ordinary atmospheric conditions.

Sodium carbonate is hydrolyzed by water to some extent, and its aqueous solutions show a decided alkaline reaction to litmus. 

The reaction is: 

Na2CO3 + H2O —— NaOH + NaHCO3 

Account for the alkaline reaction.

Preparation of sodium acid carbonate and sodium carbonate. 

Sodium carbonate can be prepared by two distinct commercial methods the Le Blanc method and the Solvay or Ammonia process. Only the latter method will be taken up. 

The Solvay process depends upon the double decomposition of sodium chloride and ammonium bicarbonate. The sodium bicarbonate formed in the reaction, on account of its lesser solubility, separates from the solution. The bicarbonate when

Introductory Chemistry heated readily decomposes into sodium carbonate, water, and carbon dioxide.

Complete the equations expressing the principal reactions of the Solvay process 

NaCl + NH4HCO3 

2NaHCO3 + heat

The Solvay process is usually carried out commercially by treating a saturated solution of sodium chloride with carbon dioxide and ammonia under pressure. When, as mentioned above, sodium bicarbonate crystallizes out, the resulting ammonium chloride is treated with calcium oxide so that the ammonia may be regenerated for further use. Likewise the carbon dioxide escaping when the bicarbonate is converted into normal salt is used to make the process a continuous one. 

Uses of sodium carbonate. Because sodium carbonate is the only carbonate that is both cheap and soluble, it has a number of uses. One of these is in the preparation of other chemical compounds, as we have seen in Experiment 76. Its common name, washing soda, indicates its use in laundries. Its action as a grease remover is due to the formation of the hydroxyl ion, for when the salt is placed in water it hydrolyzes. Write an equation showing the products formed in the hydrolysis of sodium carbonate:

Acids spilled on clothes may be neutralized by the application of sodium carbonate, while any strong base used in neutralizing the acid might do as much harm as the acid itself.

Sodium bicarbonate. Sodium bicarbonate, or sodium hydrogen carbonate, is prepared as an intermediate product in the Solvay process for the preparation of sodium carbonate. Sodium bicarbonate is far less soluble in water than is the normal salt. 

Boiling water converts the acid salt into the normal salt with the liberation of carbon dioxide. An aqueous solution of sodium bicarbonate is neutral or very slightly alkaline to litmus, the alkalinity usually being due to the presence of some carbonate. 

The ionization of sodium bicarbonate is expressed by the following equation:

NaHCO3 Na+ + HCO3- Write the reaction of HCl with both the normal carbonate and the acid carbonate: What type of acids would not liberate carbon dioxide from the carbonates?

Carbon dioxide is liberated from carbonates in several different ways in baking. When sour milk (lactic acid) is added to dough containing baking soda (sodium bicarbonate), the carbon dioxide is slowly liberated during the heating. When it is not desirable to use sour milk, a baking powder is used, which contains some added acid substance in addition to the sodium bicarbonate. 

This acid material is usually alum, acid tartrate, or acid phosphate. These baking powders must be kept dry during storage. Why?

In baking powders a rather insoluble acid is used, so that no great reaction will take place until the mixing is thoroughly done.

Alloys of sodium. Sodium amalgam is much used as a reducing agent. The sodium when united with the mercury as an amalgam is less active than when it is free; yet it is loosely combined with the mercury and (although it acts much less rapidly) it will reduce almost any substance that free sodium will reduce. The amalgam can be easily prepared by mixing the sodium in the mercury, when they unite with the liberation of heat. Or it can be prepared by making mercury the cathode in the electrolysis of a sodium salt. Sodium will alloy with potassium to make a liquid solution at ordinary temperature. This is used to remove the last traces of water or oxygen from gases.

SECTION FOUR CALCIUM AND ITS COMPOUNDS 

CALCIUM is the fifth most abundant element in nature.

From its position in the electromotive series would you expect it to occur in a free state?

Calcium carbonate occurs as limestone and chalk, and as marble, which is carbonate rock that has been heated in the earth and crystallized. Calcium chloride is found in many mineral waters and the phosphate in practically all phosphate beds. 

Gypsum, or calcium sulfate, occurs abundantly, while fluor spar, or calcium fluoride, exists in certain localities. Dolomite, a rock of which whole mountain ridges are composed, is a double carbonate of calcium and magnesium. 

Preparation and properties. Calcium may be prepared by fusing calcium chloride with metallic sodium: 

CaCl2 + 2Na

Explain why the reaction takes place. 

It may also be prepared by electrolyzing the molten chloride or bromide. The calcium ions of the fused salt pass to the cathode, and each receives two electrons, thus becoming elementary or atomic calcium. What will be given off at the anode? 

Calcium is a silvery white metallic substance. It decomposes water at ordinary temperatures and readily reacts with dilute acids. Upon exposure to air it becomes tarnished, due to the formation of calcium nitride. It has a great affinity for nitrogen and is frequently used to remove nitrogen from the atmosphere in the preparation of argon.

Calcium combines also with hydrogen to form calcium hydride, CaH2.

Great quantities of lime are used for correcting the acidity of soils. Calcium oxide, calcium hydroxide, and pulverized limestone are all employed for this purpose. 

Calcium sulfate, or gypsum. This salt is found in nature combined with two molecules of water, CaSO4.2H2O. When heated to 115° C., it loses three fourths of this water of hydration and forms "plaster of Paris." Thus 2 (CaSO4) · 2H2O + heat (115°) — ― (CaSO4)2 H2O + 3H₂O This partially dehydrated salt, (CaSO4)2 H₂O, on exposure to the air takes up moisture; i.e., the reaction reverses on drying and the material hardens by the formation of a mass of interlacing, needle-shaped crystals. Plaster of Paris is used in dentistry and surgery in making casts, for when it takes up water to go back into gypsum, it swells and fills all the details of the mold into which the wet plaster of Paris has been poured. If, however, the gypsum be heated above 115° C. and made completely anhydrous, the compound combines with water very slowly.

Tricalcium phosphate, Cas (PO4)2. This salt is found in bones, in teeth, and in rock beds formed of animal remains. It is a white amorphous powder almost completely insoluble in water, but soluble in dilute hydrochloric or nitric acids.

The oxygen loses its captured electrons when the chlorine changes from positive to negative, and is released in this condition. It is this atomic oxygen, with its uncompleted octet, that causes the bleaching which treatment with bleaching powder brings about.

Portland cement. Material which is used commercially as cement today is essentially a mixture. One analysis of a typical sample showed the following composition: 

Calcium oxide, 64 per cent 

Silica, 22 per cent

Aluminum oxide, 7 per cent 

Iron oxide, 4 per cent 

Sulfur trioxide, 1.6 per cent 

Magnesium oxide, .8 per cent 

Sodium and potassium oxide, about .6 per cent 

When this material is mixed with water or with water and sand and allowed to stand, it sets into a hard mass. Within a few hours a considerable degree of hardening takes place, but the hardening process continues over a period of months or even years. In the setting of cement a great deal of the silica and lime unite to form calcium silicate, and also some of the lime unites with the aluminum oxide, forming calcium aluminate.

SECTION FIVE IRON 

IRON is closely associated with cobalt and nickel. These elements occur mostly as sulfides and oxides, and iron is by far the most abundant of the three. Complete the table below : 

COMPOUND ELEMENT SYMBOL ATOMIC WEIGHT VALENCE MELTING BOILING POINT POINT DENSITY 

Oxide Chloride Iron 2, 3 1550 2430 7.85 Cobalt 2, 3 1478 8.72 Nickel 2, 3 1452 8.90

About 4 per cent of the earth's crust is iron, and it ranks next to aluminum as the most abundant metal. The large supplies of it available, the ease with which it can be extracted from its ores, and its many desirable properties have given it the widest use of all the metals. In the United States alone 30 million tons of it are produced annually.

Occurrence. The principal iron compounds occurring in nature are magnetic oxide, or magnetite, Fe3O4; hematite, Fe2O3 ; iron pyrites, or pyrite, FeS2; and siderite, FeCO3. Iron is the cementing substance in a number of rocks, and it is present in nearly all sand and clay (the red color of soils is due to iron. oxide). Iron is a constituent of the hæmoglobin in the blood of animals and is present in all living matter; so it is not only a very abundant element, but also one of very wide distribution. 

It is found in the elementary form in meteorites, and lumps of metallic iron are found in Greenland. It is supposed that these are either of meteoric origin or that they have resulted from the burning of coal deposits and the consequent freeing of iron from its ore. 

Preparation. Iron is prepared from its oxide by electronizing the iron with carbon (coke). If the ore is a carbonate, it is first 290

Iron converted into an oxide by heating, and then the metal of the oxide is electronized. The sulfide (iron pyrites) occurs in very large quantities, but it is not mined for the commercial production of iron. Only ores containing 30 or 40 per cent of iron can be profitably worked in the face of the competition that now exists.

The electronization of the iron is carried out in blast furnaces made of iron and lined with fire brick. Coke, limestone, and iron ore are introduced at the top of the furnace, and hot air, under pressure, from below. The principal reactions in the process are represented by the following equations: 2C + O2 C+02 →2CO → CO₂ CO₂+ C— → 2CO Fe2O3 + CO— 2FeO + CO2 Fe2O3 + 3CO Fe2O3 + 3C CaCO3 CaO+SiO2 

→3CO2 + 2Fe 3CO2Fe → CaO + CO CaSiO3 (slag) At the temperature of the furnace (1000° to 1500° C.) the limestone decomposes into calcium oxide and carbon dioxide. 

The latter escapes along with the carbon monoxide, and the former unites with the siliceous impurities present to form a slag consisting essentially of calcium metasilicate, CaSiO3. This slag rises in the molten iron and floats on its surface and is drawn off through an opening above where the molten iron escapes. 

The reaction in the blast furnace is continuous. Alternate charges of coke, ore, and limestone are added at the top, and the slag and molten metal are withdrawn at intervals below. The molten iron is drawn off into "converters" and made at once into steel, or it is run into sand or steel molds and hardened into pigs." This iron contains carbon and silicon, in addition to small amounts of manganese, phosphorus, and sulfur. The fur- (6

Introductory Chemistry nace gas contains enough carbon monoxide to make it profitable to save it for fuel.

Forms in which iron is used. The properties of iron are affected both by the treatment that the metal receives and by other substances that may be present in it in small amounts. 

Hence it can be prepared so that it has many different properties that fit it for different uses. It is used chiefly as pure iron, cast iron, wrought iron, and as iron alloys, or steels, that contain small percentages of carbon and sometimes of various metals besides iron.

This pure iron is silvery white and very hard and brittle, but if it is annealed for two hours. at 900°, it becomes extremely tough. It is used for the manufacture of very thin-walled tubing, and on account of its high magnetic qualities, in the construction of transformers and other electrical instruments.

Cast "pigs" and cast scrap iron are melted in foundries, and such articles as stoves, window weights, lamp posts, fire plugs, sewer pipe, and manholes and their covers are made from iron of this kind. Because of the presence of the impurities in the metal, cast iron expands as it solidifies, and this expansion helps to fill out the mold and give a perfect shape to the article that is being cast.

Introductory Chemistry 

In cast iron that has been cooled rapidly the carbon is in the form of iron carbide, FeC. Such iron is very hard, and is called chilled cast iron.

If the molten iron is cooled slowly, part of the carbon is in the form of graphite. This gives the iron a gray color, and it is called gray cast iron. It is much softer than the chilled cast iron. 

If the cooling is intermediate in its rapidity between the two extremes mentioned above, an intermediate product, white cast iron, is secured.

Wrought iron. Wrought iron is made by burning. the impurities out of pig iron and hammering or rolling the metal as it cools. The pig iron is melted in a shallow, flat-bottomed puddling furnace, the bottom and walls of which are lined with iron oxide (either FeO or Fe2O3), which furnishes oxygen for the removal of the impurities. The molten metal is stirred through an opening in the side of the furnace to bring it into contact with the air and with the furnace lining, and oxidation takes place very rapidly. The carbon in the iron is oxidized to carbon monoxide, which escapes. The other oxides combine with the furnace lining and form a slag, which rises to the surface and is removed. As the impurities are removed the melting point of the iron is raised, and hence it becomes a pasty mass, its constituency telling the experienced workman when most of the impurities have disappeared. When the reaction has progressed sufficiently far, the viscous iron is removed from the furnace by rolling it, on the ends of iron rods, into balls weighing about 

100 pounds. These balls are at once put under heavy hammers or passed between rolls. The hammering or rolling forces out most of the slag remaining in the iron. The remaining slag and some Fe2O3 that may be present is pressed into long, thin scales within the iron, giving to it a fibrous structure. 

Iron that is prepared in this way may be as much as 99.9 per cent pure. It has a higher melting point than cast iron, is tougher, and possesses much more tensile strength than pig iron.

Iron 

LINING MOLTEN IRON FAIR BLOWN IN AIR THROUGH PERFORATED BOTTOM THE BLOW POURING Scientific American 

FIG. 63. A converter for the manufacture of Bessemer steel. 

It can be forged easily, and because of this property and its high tensile strength it is used in making such articles as bolts and chains. So-called "mild steel" is prepared by the introduction of a fractional percentage of carbon. This increases the tensile strength of the iron, and steel of this kind is now replacing wrought iron for many purposes. Wrought iron is more resistant chemically than steel, however, and on this account it is preferred to steel as material for pipes that must resist corrosion. The resistance of wrought iron to chemical changes is due to the scales of slag which enclose and protect the fibers of iron. 

Steel. Ordinary steel is iron containing from .16 per cent to 

2 per cent of carbon. Part of the carbon is free, and part is combined as iron carbide. Special steels contain silicon, nickel, chromium, vanadium, tungsten, molybdenum, or other components in addition to the carbon. By varying the composition and the treatment to which it is subjected, a steel having almost any property desired can be produced.

Introductory Chemistry 

Processes employed in steel making. Steel is made by the 

Bessemer process, acid and basic; by the open-hearth process, acid and basic; and by the crucible process. The process employed depends on the impurities in the iron and on the kind of steel desired.

The Bessemer process. The Bessemer acid process is used with ores that are free from sulfur and phosphorus. In it the molten iron direct from the blast furnace is poured into a huge pear-shaped iron vessel (the "converter "), which is lined with silica and clay. Air is forced through the molten iron, and the oxygen of this air converts the silicon, manganese, and carbon to their oxides. A part of the iron is likewise deëlectronized to ferrous oxide, but this is reconverted into iron by the introduction of an alloy of manganese, carbon, and iron. The manganese and carbon electronize the iron of the ferrous oxide to metallic iron, and the excess of these elements dissolves in the iron and converts it into steel.

In the Bessemer process a "blow" takes only about ten minutes, and the metal is kept molten only by the oxidation of the impurities. Hence there must be prompt action to get the process completed and the steel out of the converter before it hardens. The oxidation of the carbon is accompanied by a flame from the mouth of the converter, and an experienced workman can tell by watching the flame when the carbon is practically all consumed. When this stage is reached, the converter is turned down to a horizontal position and coke or a carbon- and manganese-bearing alloy (e.g., spiegeleisen, ferromanganese) is introduced, the converter again turned upright, and air blown through the metal for a short time. Then the converter is tilted and the steel poured into ladles, by which it is carried and poured into steel molds. Aluminum is now added to remove any air trapped in the pouring and thus to prevent bubbles in the steel. 

The ingots from these molds are reheated to whiteness, and then rolled into rails, girders, or other similar objects.

Iron Practically the only Bessemer process used in our country is the acid one. It does not remove sulfur or phosphorus, and when Flame

FIG. 64. Diagram of an open-hearth furnace for the manufacture of steel. these are present in the iron, as in Germany, the basic process must be used. In this process the converter is lined with limestone or dolomite, which are basic and which will unite with the phosphorus and sulfur oxides and remove them as slag. 

Open-hearth process. The basic open-hearth process is carried out in a large but shallow furnace which is lined with limestone or other basic material. The heat is furnished by gas or sprayedoil flames, which are reflected from the roof of the furnace on to the charge. The impurities are converted to oxides, as in the 

Bessemer process, but here the basic lining of the furnace gives a product especially free from phosphorus and sulfur, which render the steel brittle. It requires about eight hours to complete the purifying process in this furnace, and hence the open-hearth process has the great advantage of being more under control. Chemical tests are made to determine the amount of carbon in the charge, and by adding more carbon or allowing the heating to proceed, the carbon content can be brought to the percentage desired.

More steel is made by the basic open-hearth process in the United States than by any other method. Generally speaking, the steel made by this process is more uniform and of better

Introductory Chemistry 

Ewing Galloway

FIG. 65. An electric furnace for the manufacture of tool steel. Because of the high temperatures and the more complete control of the process that it gives, the electric furnace is being increasingly used in the manufacture of crucible steel. quality than Bessemer steel. In the acid open-hearth process the furnace is lined with a siliceous instead of a basic material. 

This process is little used in the United States. 

The crucible process. Crucible steel is made by melting very pure iron (wrought iron, Swedish iron, or electrolytic iron) or open-hearth steel in covered clay or clay and graphite crucibles and adding exact percentages of various ingredients according to the kind of steel desired. The highest grade of special steels used in tools, cutlery, and machinery is crucible steel. The crucibles are heated in furnaces, and the electric furnace is used very successfully in the manufacture of these steels, as the higher temperatures it furnishes remove the impurities more completely.

Iron Tempering of steel. When allowed to cool very slowly, ordinary steel is soft and resembles wrought iron in its properties. 

The slow cooling allows the carbon and iron carbide, Fe¿C, to separate out, and thus the steel is a mechanical mixture of iron, graphite, and iron carbide. When steel is cooled rapidly, it becomes very hard and brittle, the degree of hardness depending to some extent upon the amount of carbon present. The rapid cooling does not allow the carbon to settle out, and there results a solution of carbon and of iron carbide in iron which gives the hardness and brittleness to the steel. This process by which the hardness of steel is regulated is known as tempering. 

Ferrous and ferric iron. Iron is of variable valence and gives rise to two separate and distinct classes of compounds; namely, ferrous and ferric compounds. In the first the iron is divalent, and in the second it is trivalent. This variation in the valence of iron is very nicely demonstrated by electrolysis. A definite quantity of electricity will liberate one-and-one-half times as much iron from a ferrous solution as the same quantity will liberate from a ferric solution, showing that in the ferrous compounds the iron has lost two electrons and in the ferric compounds three electrons.

Iron is found in all living matter, and recent studies indicate that the rate of respiration in different tissues varies in proportion to the amount of iron they contain. Because of this and other facts, it has been suggested that respiration the process from which the life energy is obtained—is the changing of iron from the ferrous to the ferric state.

UNIT ONE SOME FUNDAMENTALS OF CHEMISTRY 

THE essence of the scientific method - "the only method by which the human mind reaches truth" is the verification of hypotheses. In all fields of human interest and study men formulate theories, but in the realm of nature it is possible to set up experiments or make long series of observations on uniformly reacting materials to find whether guesses that have been made are correct. Hence, science is constantly ridding itself of false explanations. The pathway along which it has advanced is strewn with theories that have been tried and have been proved by greater knowledge of facts to be incorrect or inadequate. 

But while most scientific hypotheses are of short duration, an occasional one is put forth that for a long time is consistent with the new as well as the old facts of the science. Such a hypothesis is the atomic theory of the structure of matter formulated by John Dalton. In this unit we shall become familiar with some of the facts on which this theory is based and with some of the fundamental conceptions of matter that are explained in terms of it. 307

A generalization from results like these gives us what is known as Dalton's law of multi ple proportions. This law is stated as follows: 

A fixed weight of one substance combines in such a way with different weights of a second substance that the ratio of these different weights may be expressed by small whole numbers (1, 2, 3, etc.). 

The last column of the table above shows these ratios.

The law of multiple proportions is easily explained by the atomic hypothesis. Suppose that 1, 2, 3, and 4 atoms of oxygen unite with potassium chloride to form potassium hypochlorite, potassium chlorite, potassium chlorate, and potassium perchlorate, respectively. On the assumption that all atoms of the same element have the same weight, it is clear that the ratios between the weights of the oxygen in the different compounds would be 1, 2, 3, and 4, according to the law of multiple proportions. The fact that the ratios between the weights of a substance that is formed in varying amounts in a series of compounds can always be expressed in whole numbers leads to the conclusion that there are no half atoms in chemical reactions. 

If the atoms were broken up, we should expect fractions to appear in the ratios.

Law of combining weights. We have seen that 8 parts of oxygen combine with 1.008 parts of hydrogen to form water. Were we to experiment with the weight of other substances with which 8 parts of oxygen would combine, we should obtain results similar to those given in the table below.

66 107.88 66 magnesium silver Since 23 parts of sodium and 35.46 parts of chlorine each unite with the same number of parts (8) of oxygen, you might expect from a geometrical axiom (things equal to the same thing are equal to each other) that 35.46 parts of chlorine would unite with 23 parts of sodium. Experiment proves this to be true,

Introductory Chemistry and in general the combining capacity that a substance shows when it unites with oxygen is a measure of its combining capacity when it forms compounds with other substances. 

How many parts of chlorine will combine with 31.78 parts of copper?

How many parts of chlorine will combine with 107.88 parts of silver?

The combining weight of oxygen is taken as a standard in determining the combining weights of other substances. This standard is given a numerical value of 8, and the combining weights of other elements are assigned corresponding numerical values. Three parts of carbon, 23 parts of sodium, and 35.46 parts of chlorine unite with 8 parts of oxygen; so the combining weights of these elements are placed at 3, 23, and 35.46, respectively.

Oxygen is used as the standard in establishing these weights because it is found in combination with more substances than any other one element. Its combining weight is given a value of 

8 because approximately 8 parts of oxygen combine with 1 part of hydrogen, which is the element with the lowest combining weight. 

The law of combining weights may be stated as follows: A given weight of one element or some multiple of that weight will always combine with a fixed weight of another element. 

Explain the law of combining weights in terms of the atomic theory.

Dalton's atomic hypothesis. John Dalton was an English schoolmaster who became greatly interested in physics and chemistry. Another great chemist of his time, Sir Humphry 

Davy, described him as "a very coarse experimenter" and as "trusting to his head rather than his hands" to do his work. 

There is room for both headwork and handwork in science, however, and Dalton used his head on the facts that came to his knowledge to such good effect that among chemists his name endures as that of one of the truly great.

Dalton published many very important papers on the behavior of gases and formulated the law of multiple proportions, but his great contribution to science was the framing of the atomic hypothesis of the structure of matter. Long before his time some scientists and philosophers had held that matter is composed of small particles which they called 66 atoms," but they thought of these as physical atoms (corresponding to our molecules) rather than as the atoms of the chemist. It was Dalton who conceived of atoms in the sense in which the chemist thinks of them (of there being as many different kinds of atoms as there are elements), and who in general gave such extension and definiteness to the atomic theory that the chief facts of chemistry can be accounted for in terms of it. His principal assumptions were the following:

FIG. 66. John Dalton (1766-1844). His great scientific hypotheses of all time. atomic hypothesis is accounted one of the 1. All matter is composed of indivisible particles known as atoms. 

2. Atoms of the same element have the same weight, but atoms of different elements have different weights. 

3. These ultimate units of matter have the power of attracting each other and thereby effecting chemical union. 

Dalton's hypothesis did not attempt to explain why chemical reactions take place or the energy changes accompanying them. 

Moreover, we now believe that Dalton was wrong in his idea that the atom is indivisible and the ultimate unit in matter, and wrong also in believing that all atoms of the same kind have ex-

Introductory Chemistry actly the same weight. Yet the formulation of the atomic theory brought system and order into chemical thinking, and the thousands of facts of chemistry that have been discovered since it was announced have confirmed Dalton's supposition that matter is composed of atoms of different kinds which unite to form molecules and that these atoms pass through ordinary chemical reactions unchanged. Recent discoveries have caused the theory to be extended rather than discarded, and in its main features it is so universally accepted that in the study of chemistry and in chemical investigations it is customary to proceed as though atoms and molecules were known actually to exist. Dalton's atomic hypothesis must, therefore, be ranked as one of the great scientific hypotheses of all time.

SECTION TWO PROPERTIES OF GASES 

In a solid the molecules have fixed positions. In a liquid they move freely over one another, but there is an attraction between them that causes them to remain together in the mass. 

In a gas the molecules move freely in any direction, and instead of being held together as in solids and liquids, they tend to fly apart. The fundamental difference in these three states of matter is the relative ease with which the molecules can move about among one another. 

Because of the interrelations of the molecules composing them, solids, liquids, and gases differ in many ways. A solid has a definite shape and a definite volume. A liquid has a definite volume, but its shape is determined by any vessel that may happen to contain it. A gas has neither definite shape nor definite volume. It always occupies the entire space left at its disposal. As might be expected, heat and pressure have comparatively little effect on the volumes of solids and liquids but have a great effect on the volume of a gas. 

Effect of temperature on the volume of a gas. In the next experiment we shall find how heat affects the volume of a gas.

When the pressure remains constant, the volume of a gas is found to vary directly as the absolute temperature.

Gas pressure may be expressed in pounds per square inch, in atmospheres, or in centimeters or millimeters of mercury. Usually the pressure is measured by the height of the column of mercury which it will balance. The next experiment will show the effect of pressure on the volume of a gas and at the same time demonstrate the method by which the pressure is measured. 

Experiment 88 

OBJECT. To determine the effect of pressure on the volume of a gas. 

The experiment is to be carried out at constant temperature. The room temperature is sufficiently constant, but the tube containing the air column must not be touched with the hands, for gases are very sensitive to changes in temperature, as we learned in the preceding experiment.

Pour mercury into the long arm of a J tube, imprisoning a column of air in the closed end of the tube. Continue adding mercury until it stands at about the same level in both arms. 

When the mercury stands at the same level in the two tubes, the pressure exerted on this imprisoned column of air is evidently the at-la mospheric pressure.

When the mercury in the longer (or open) tube is lower than that in the closed tube, the entrapped air is not under a full atmosphere of pressure. The pressure is then equal to the barometric pressure minus the difference in level of the mercury in the long arm and the short arm.

Boyle's Law. Summing up the facts learned in the preceding experiment, we may say that the volume of a gas varies inversely as the pressure. When the pressure is increased, the volume becomes smaller. If the pressure is doubled, the volume is decreased one half. If the pressure is three times as great, the volume is one third the original volume. Conversely, if the pressure is decreased, the volume increases in a proportional degree. The generalization of this statement is known as Boyle's 

Law and may be briefly expressed thus: 

The volume of a gas varies inversely as the pressure, provided the temperature remains the same. 

Application of Boyle's Law. By the use of Boyle's Law, it is easy to calculate the volume of a gas at any pressure.

Problem. What would the volume be at a pressure of 700 mm. of a quantity of oxygen which occupies 105 cc. at a pressure of 800 mm.? 

From facts learned above there will be a proportionate increase in volume as the pressure decreases from 800 mm. to 700 mm. Hence, the new volume will be 80 of the original volume. Thus: 800

For the same reason that a standard temperature was selected, a standard pressure has been agreed upon. It has been found that the average reading of the barometer at sea level is 760 mm., and this value has been taken as standard. When the volume which the gas would have at this particular pressure is calculated, the operation is called correcting the gas to standard pressure.

In discussions of the behavior of gases and in problems relating to gases it is assumed, unless otherwise stated, that the gases are under standard pressure and at standard temperature. 

Vapor pressure. Suppose that we have a liquid (e.g., water) in an inclosed vessel. The molecules begin to pass off at once into the space above the liquid. These molecules are in rapid motion, which means that some of them will return to strike against the surface of the liquid. 

A certain number of these will stick, so there is both a movement of the molecules away from the liquid and a return to it. At first, the number that escape in a unit of time is greater than the number that return to its surface, but as the concentration of the vapor above the liquid increases the rates at which the molecules leave the liquid and return to it become more nearly equal. Finally, the two rates become equal, and we have an equilibrium. If we allow one of the rates to be represented by V₁ and the other by V2, we may represent the condition of equilibrium mathematically thus: V₁ V₂ When equilibrium has been established, the pressure will thereafter be constant, because the number of molecules above will no longer increase.

The pressure exerted by the vapor above 

FIG. 69.

the liquid is called the vapor pressure of the liquid, and is a measure of the tendency of the molecules to leave the liquid and go into the gaseous state. Volatile liquids have, of course, higher vapor pressures than those that evaporate less easily. 

The vapor pressure of a liquid is constant at any given temperature, but if the temperature is raised the vapor pressure is at once increased. Both V₁ and V2 are increased, and there is a greater concentration of the molecules in the space above the liquid. 

The vapor pressure of water (or of other liquids) may be measured with the apparatus shown in Figure 69. One barometer tube is entirely filled with mercury and inverted in a vessel of mercury. The other is almost filled with mercury and the filling completed with a few drops of water. The tube is then inverted, when the water at once rises to the top of the tube. 

Here a part of it evaporates, and its vapor pressure is shown by the difference in level of the mercury in the two tubes. 

Corrections for pressure of water vapor. Gases are usually collected in the laboratory over water or mercury. When the former is used, some of the water evaporates and mixes with the gas. Hence the total pressure is not due to the gas itself, but in part to the water vapor. If the pressure of the water vapor were known, the pressure of the gas itself could be found at once by subtracting the pressure of the water vapor from the total pressure. The amount of water vapor in the gas will depend upon the temperature, and because of this fact the pressure due to water vapor is always the same for the same temperature, if the gas is saturated with the water vapor. Tables for these values have been carefully worked out and one may be found on page 600 of this volume. This correction is called the correction for water-vapor pressure, or more briefly, correction for vapor pressure.

X X Partial pressures in gases. The water-vapor pressure in gases collected over water is an example of what is called partial pressure in gases. When a mixture of gases is confined, the molecules of each gas move about independently and each exerts its own pressure without reference to the pressure exerted by the others. Thus in a distended automobile tire, part of the pressure is exerted by the nitrogen of the air, part by the oxygen, a lesser amount by the argon, and the remainder by the water vapor, carbon dioxide, and the rarer gases of the atmosphere. Each component of the air exerts its pressure exactly as it would if the others were not present, and the sum of all their pressures makes up the total of the pressure within the tire. 

This conduct of gases is explained by the fact that in comparison with the diameter of the molecules the distances between them are very great, so that the molecules of one gas move in between those of another as though they were in vacant space. 

It is because one gas can enter into and exert its influence in a field already occupied by another gas, that corrections for watervapor pressure are necessary in gases collected over water. 

Correction for difference in level. When the volumes of gases are measured in bottles or tubes over liquids, the levels of the liquid inside and outside the vessel are supposed to be

the same. Usually the inner and outer liquids can be brought to the same level by raising or lowering the vessel containing the gas, but if such adjustment is not possible, a correction must be made for such a difference of levels. 

When the inside level is the higher, it is evident that the column of liquid within the vessel helps the gas to balance the atmospheric pressure. Hence the pressure of the gas must be equal to the atmospheric pressure minus the height of this column of liquid.

When the gas stands over mercury, the difference in levels measured in millimeters is subtracted from the atmospheric pressure. For water, only one thirteenth of the value used for mercury is required, because water has only one thirteenth the weight of mercury.

If the level of the inside liquid is the lower, correction for the difference in levels must be made by addition.

FIG. 70. Joseph Louis Gay-Lussac (1778This simple relation which exists between reacting volumes of gases was first pointed out in 1808 by Gay-Lussac.

His discovery led to the following law, known as the Law of Gay-Lussac:

Gases combine in volumes which bear a simple ratio to each other and to the product formed, provided the product is in a gaseous condition. 

By "simple ratio " is meant 1850). He formulated the law of combining a ratio which is made up of volumes and was one of the great teachers of small whole numbers. For his generation. example: : the volume of hydrogen
