The volume of oxygen 

The volume of oxygen : the volume of water vapor :: 1:2 The volume of hydrogen: the volume of water vapor :: 2:2 Avogadro's Hypothesis. In 1811 Avogadro, an Italian physi cist, proposed a hypothesis to account for the behavior of gases.

It is stated as follows: 

Equal volumes of gases when subjected to the same conditions of temperature and pressure contain an equal number of molecules; that is, a liter of gas, whether it be oxygen, hydrogen, nitrogen, carbon dioxide, or a mixture of gases like air, will always contain the same number of molecules under the same conditions of temperature and pressure. A different statement of Avogadro's 

Hypothesis is:

Under the same conditions of temperature and pressure all gases have equal molecular concentrations. 

On this assumption Avogadro was able to give a satisfactory explanation of why gases follow the laws of Charles, Boyle, and

Gay-Lussac; but it was not until about fifty years after the theory was put forth that scientific men realized its value in explaining the many experimental facts that had been established. Since 1860 the value of the hypothesis has been recognized, and it is now looked upon as one of the fundamental theories of physical science. 

Application of Avogadro's Hypothesis. We have learned that I volume of chlorine combines with 1 volume of hydrogen to form 2 volumes of hydrogen chloride gas, HCl. Let us suppose that I volume of each gas contains one hundred molecules. 

Then, according to Avogadro's Hypothesis, the following relation would exist: 100 molecules chlorine 100 molecules hydrogen 

200 molecules hydrogen chloride gas

Now, every molecule of hydrogen chloride gas contains at least one atom of hydrogen. Hence there must be at least two hundred atoms of hydrogen in the two hundred molecules of hydrogen chloride.

These two hundred atoms of hydrogen have come from the one hundred molecules of hydrogen; therefore, each molecule of hydrogen must have broken into at least two atoms. Of course, the molecule of hydrogen might have broken into any even number of atoms, but there is no case where the hydrogen molecule is known to be broken into more than two parts, and therefore for the present we must assume that the molecule contains two atoms. 

EXERCISES 

1. Show that a molecule of chlorine contains at least two atoms. 

2. Using the volume relation existing between oxygen, hydrogen, and water vapor, prove that a molecule of oxygen contains at least two atoms.

Introductory Chemistry 

3. Prove that a molecule of water vapor contains at least one atom of oxygen and two atoms of hydrogen. 

4. Prove that a molecule of ammonia gas contains at least one atom of nitrogen and three atoms of hydrogen. 

5. Show that a molecule of nitrogen contains at least two atoms. 

Elements such as oxygen and hydrogen, which are gaseous at ordinary temperatures, usually have two atoms in a molecule. 

This is not true for all the elements. Experiments show that phosphorus has four atoms to the molecule, while the number of atoms in the sulfur molecule varies from two to eight, according to the temperature. Further, as we have noted, the molecules of the inert gases and of metals consist of single atoms.

The kinetic theory of gases. The kinetic theory of gases is a hypothesis offered in explanation of the behavior of gases under different conditions. According to this theory all gases are composed of molecules which are moving at high speed. Each molecule is supposed to move in a straight line until it strikes a neighboring molecule or the containing vessel. Then the molecule rebounds with perfect elasticity, taking a direction according to the angle at which it struck and traveling again in a straight course with velocity unchanged until a second impact again changes its direction.

A gas imprisoned in a container is thus thought of as consisting of a swarm of molecules, darting about and striking against each other and the walls of the container. When the molecules strike the sides of the container, they exert pressure on it - tend to drive it outward as an inconceivably rapid bombardment from a multitude of small hammers would do. Under changes of temperature and pressure the molecules are not supposed to break up; so the number of molecules within the retainer remains constant. Any increase in the pressure which the gas exerts on the container must, therefore, be due either to more molecules striking a unit of surface in a unit of time or

to a speeding up of the molecules so that they strike both more frequently and with greater force. 

As the temperature increases, the speed of the molecules increases and they strike more frequently and at higher speeds. 

Hence, they exert more force against the wall of the container, and in Experiment 87 we saw the movable wall of water forced downward with an increase of heat. 

When pressure is exerted on a gas, the molecules are crowded into a space such that the force of the molecules striking outward is just equal to the pressure. If more pressure is added, the volume of the gas is still further decreased, and this smaller volume is accompanied by a corresponding decrease of surface against which the molecules strike. Hence, more of them strike against a unit of surface in a unit of time, and the heavier pressure is thus balanced. On the other hand, if the pressure is lessened, the volume of the gas expands until only a sufficient number of molecules are striking the surface to balance the decreased pressure.

Since equal volumes of all gases under similar conditions exert the same pressure and since by Avogadro's Hypothesis equal volumes of all gases under similar conditions contain the same number of molecules, it follows that the molecules of all gases have equal striking force. This means that the molecules of the lighter gases must move with higher velocities than those of heavier ones, since the momentum of a moving body is equal to its mass multiplied by its velocity. That the molecules of the lighter gases do have the more rapid motion is shown by the higher diffusibility of these gases and by other evidence which cannot be given here. 

Our studies in this section show that the behavior of gases can be explained in terms of atoms and molecules. In the next section we shall see how, from a study of gases, certain inferences concerning atoms and molecules may be drawn.

SECTION THREE MOLECULAR AND ATOMIC WEIGHTS 

CHEMISTS Speak of atomic and molecular weights. Do they know the actual weight of an atom? Are molecules large enough to be weighed? Do all atoms have the same weight, or do different kinds differ in weight? And do all molecules weigh the same, or do they vary in weight according to the number and kinds of atoms that are in them? 

Through recent experiments in the field of radioactivity it has been rather conclusively shown that there are at least 

27,000,000,000,000,000,000 (2.7 X 1019) molecules in a cubic centimeter of gas under standard conditions; so by weighing a measured volume of gas and dividing its weight by the number of molecules it contains, the actual weight of a single molecule can be obtained. The atomic and molecular weights the chemist uses, however, are relative and not actual weights. By rather simple experiments it is possible to prove that atoms and molecules have different weights and to determine the weight of one atom or molecule as compared with that of another. How this is done will be made clear in this section. 

The standard in determining atomic and molecular weights. 

The hydrogen atom is the lightest atom known. Formerly this was used as a standard in determining atomic weights and was given a value of 1. But because oxygen combines with so many more substances than hydrogen, it has been thought best to use the atomic weight of oxygen as a standard. This is assigned a value of 16, twice the combining weight of oxygen (page 313).

By this standard the atomic weight of sulfur is 32, by which we mean, not that an atom of sulfur has any particular weight, but simply that it is twice as heavy as an atom of oxygen. The weight of a molecule of oxygen is 32, since, as we have seen, it consists of two atoms of oxygen, each with a weight of 16. 

The weight of the hydrogen atom is slightly more than 1 when 334

measured in terms of the oxygen standard, as we shall see later (page 337).

How does the standard taken for the weight of an atom of oxygen compare with the number of protons in the nucleus of the oxygen atom (page 39)?

Determination of molecular weights of gases. By Avogadro's 

Hypothesis, equal volumes of different gases under the same conditions of temperature and pressure contain the same number of molecules. Hence, to determine the relative weights of the molecules of different gases it is necessary only to weigh equal volumes of the gases under standard conditions. Since the weights of all other elements are compared with that of oxygen, the first step in determining molecular weights is to find the weight of a measured volume of oxygen.

Determining molecular weights. The weight of a liter of oxygen will have the same ratio to the weight of a liter of hydrogen as the weight of one molecule of oxygen will have to one molecule of hydrogen. Hence, to calculate the molecular weight of hydrogen it is necessary to have only one additional fact, the weight of a liter of hydrogen. By experiment the weight of a liter of hydrogen is easily found to be 0.09 gram. Having now the weights of a liter of oxygen and of a liter of hydrogen under standard conditions, and having assigned 32 as the weight of a molecule of oxygen, the molecular weight of hydrogen may be worked out by proportion: 

1.43 0.09:32: x x = 2.014 (molecular weight of hydrogen)

It has been proved that the molecule of hydrogen contains two atoms; hence the atomic weight of hydrogen is 1.007. More careful experimental work has fixed 1.008 as the atomic weight of hydrogen.

How does the atomic weight of hydrogen compare with the number of protons it contains (page 39)?

Perhaps it is well to call attention again to the fact that these weights assigned to oxygen and hydrogen are relative and not actual weights. We found, however, that the entire number of molecules in the liter of oxygen weighs almost sixteen times as much as the same number of hydrogen molecules. So if we allow 

32 to represent the weight of a molecule of oxygen, a molecule of hydrogen by the same standard will weigh approximately one sixteenth as much.

Gram-molecular weight. Suppose that you could weigh one molecule each of oxygen, chlorine, and carbon dioxide. You would find their relative weights to be 32, 71, and 44. Suppose now that you should run into and imprison in containers 32 grams of oxygen, 71 grams of chlorine, and 44 grams of carbon dioxide.

By gram-molecular weight (also called "mole") is meant the number of grams of a substance that is equal to its molecular weight. 

The number of molecules in the gram-molecular weights of all substances is the same.

Gram-molecular volume. By experiment it is found that under standard conditions the weight in grams of 22.4 liters of any gas equals the gram-molecular weight of the gas. Thus under standard conditions 22.4 liters of chlorine weigh 71 grams and 22.4 liters of carbon dioxide 44 grams.

This volume of any gas (22.4 liters) is known as its grammolecular volume (G.M.V.). It is the volume of the gram-molec-

ular weight of the gas under standard conditions, and for all gases it is the same, 22.4 liters.

An easy method of finding the molecular weight of a gas is based on the above facts. For if we determine the weight in grams of 22.4 liters of the gas under standard conditions, we have at the same time the relative weight of the molecule. For example, the molecular weight of oxygen is determined thus: 

1.43 grams (weight of 1 liter) X 22.4 = 32.03

Of course, in determining molecular weights different methods are used and the results checked against each other. 

Determination of molecular weights of substances that cannot be vaporized without decomposition. The methods of determining molecular weights discussed above can be applied only to gases or to liquids or solids that can be vaporized without breaking up the molecules. For other substances other methods must be used which will not be described here. If the substance goes into solution without decomposition, its molecular weight can be found from its effect on the boiling point and the freezing point of the solution. Molecular weights may also be determined by calculations made from the results secured by accurate analyses of compounds made up in part of substances of known atomic and molecular weights.

Introductory Chemistry 

Determination of atomic weights. A very accurate method of determining the atomic weights of elements that enter into gaseous compounds is outlined in the following steps: 

1. Determine the molecular weight of a great many gaseous compounds which are known to contain the element in question. 

2. By careful analysis determine the percentage by weight of the given element in the various compounds selected. 

3. Multiply the percentage by the molecular weight of the compound containing the element. The smallest product is taken as the atomic weight of the element. 

A concrete example will help to make this method plain. Six gaseous compounds of sulfur are listed below, with their molecular weights; the percentages of sulfur in them, obtained by careful analyses; and the products obtained by multiplying the percentages by the molecular weights. The figures in the last column give the weight of the sulfur in the molecule. No compound has been found which has less than 32 parts in a molecule. According to the atomic hypothesis the atom is the smallest part that shares in a chemical reaction, and therefore is the smallest part that could be found in a molecule. Hence, the atom of sulfur must have a weight of 32.

The specific-heat method. A second method of determining atomic weights is that of Dulong and Petit. These experimenters found that the atomic weight of an element in the solid condition, multiplied by its specific heat, equals a constant, 6.4. atomic weight X specific heat = 6.4 6.4 atomic weight specific heat

By finding the specific heat of an element the atomic weight can be approximately calculated. The method is less exact than the one given above, but it eliminates any danger of the wrong multiple of the atomic weight being used. This might be done if no compound containing only a single atom of the element were used in making determinations by the method first given. 

By the specific heat of a substance is meant the number of calories required to raise the temperature of 1 gram of the substance 1 degree. The specific heat of water is 1; of all other substances, less than 1.

Other methods of determining atomic weights. There are other methods of determining atomic weights, but they will not be given, since in themselves they are no more accurate than the one first given. It will be noted from the table above that it would be impossible to say whether the atomic weight of sulfur is 32.07 or 32.06. The determination of the exact atomic weight of an element is a difficult and important task - dif-

His exceedingly accurate determinations of atomic weights and his work on the compressibilities of the elements, together with his other fundamental researches, won for him the 1914 Nobel Prize in Chemistry and gave him a high rank among the chemists of our time. ficult because many principles influence the final selection of the number adopted as the atomic weight; important because all chemical analysis and much of the most refined work in chemistry depend upon their exact value.

He tried the ratio by many different methods, and his results were so concordant that his ratio, 1: 15.879, has been generally accepted. Richards worked on a large number of metals, and his determinations are ranked among the very best which have ever been made. He improved old methods, devised new ones, and applied them all with wonderful skill and exactness.

UNIT TWO CLASSIFICATION OF THE ELEMENTS 

EIGHTY-NINE elements are known to chemists, all differing in their atomic weights and in their properties. To carry as a blank memory load all the known facts concerning these elements is impossible, and if these facts are to serve as a guide for chemical studies and investigations they must be arranged in some way that will give them meaning. Fortunately it is now possible in large measure to arrange our chemical data in a very significant way; for certain great laws in accordance with which the facts of chemistry can be aligned have been developed, and hypotheses that are of great value in explaining these laws have been formulated.

We have seen how the molecular and atomic hypotheses explain elements and compounds, and how the ionic theory gives a probable explanation of the difference in behavior of electrolytes and non-electrolytes. We have learned also that the elements can be roughly divided into metals and non-metals, and that the elements can be divided into families the members of which more or less closely resemble one another. 

Since protons and electrons are the ultimate stones of which atoms are built, the most fundamental explanations of chemical facts and phenomena must be in terms of these. We shall, therefore, in the next sections take up some of the discoveries that have been made concerning the structure of the atom and some of the explanations of the problems of chemistry that these discoveries have suggested. 343

SECTION ONE ATOMIC WEIGHT AND ATOMIC NUMBERS 

ATOMS are built of protons and electrons, and a proton has a mass 1845 times as great as the mass of an electron. It follows, then, that the weight of the electrons in an atom may be considered as negligible, and we can think of differences in atomic weights in terms of protons only. Hydrogen is the lightest element, because its nucleus consists of only one proton; uranium is the heaviest, because it carries in its nucleus the greatest number of protons.

Helium nuclei in heavier atoms. It is believed that helium nuclei (see page 40) are used intact as building stones in the nuclei of heavier atoms. Along with other reasons for this belief is the fact that in the disintegration of the radium atom, helium nuclei are shot off.

The helium nuclei leave the radium atom at a speed of about 19,000 kilometers a second, and on account of their mass and great velocity they fly straight through the atoms of other substances placed close to the radium. In doing so they shatter off electrons from these atoms, and protons (hydrogen nuclei) have been knocked out of nitrogen, fluorine, sodium, and other elements by bombarding them with helium nuclei from radium. 

Hydrogen can be secured only from certain elements; so it is assumed that the nuclei of some atoms are built entirely of helium nuclei and others of helium nuclei and protons. 

The weight of the proton. The weight of the hydrogen atom is given as 1.008. In most other atoms the weight of a proton is considered as 1, but in some atoms it is more than 1. This variation in the weight of the proton is supposed to be due to a difference in the amount of compression it is under to the extent to which the positive electric charges are packed together. Physicists state that the mass of the electron varies with its velocity, and the weight of the proton is supposed to decrease as it is forced 344

together into a smaller volume. 

As we shall see later, this assumption explains certain facts relating to the atomic weights of argon and a few other elements that have long been a puzzle to the chemists who have studied them. 

Isotopes. If we look over the table of atomic weights (see the inside of the back cover of this book), we find that many of the weights are not whole numbers. How can this large percentage of fractional weights be explained if the nuclei of these atoms are built up of helium nuclei each of which has an even weight of 4, and of protons with a usual weight of 1? For example, how can we build a nucleus for chlorine of helium and protons that will have a weight of 35.46?

International 

FIG. 74. Travis William Aston, of Cambridge 

University, whose investigations have notably increased our knowledge of the atomic nucleus and of isotopes.

The explanation in this case is that there are two kinds of chlorine, one with a weight of 35 and one with a weight of 37, and that the ordinary chlorine with which we deal is a mixture of the two. Each of these forms of chlorine has exactly the same number and arrangement of the electrons outside the nucleus (and hence has the same chemical properties), but one has in its nucleus two more protons and two more electrons than the other. 

These different forms of the same element, varying only in their atomic weights, are called isotopes. By reference to the table on the inside of the back cover it will be seen that isotopes are very common and that most fractional atomic weights can

Introductory Chemistry be accounted for by them. Fractional weights not thus accounted for are believed to be due to different degrees of compression of the proton, which causes a variation in its weight.

ATOMIC NUMBERS 

By the use of different chemicals, fireworks and stage lights are given various colors. Furthermore, when elements are heated to incandescence, the spectroscope shows that each has its own characteristic spectrum. The differences in the light from different elements is in the wave length, and recent experiments have shown that there is a difference in the length of the X-ray ec el FIG. 75. Diagram of an X-ray tube, showing how X-ray spectra like those shown in Fig. 

78 are obtained. The impact of the stream of electrons (b) on the target (d) causes Xrays (e) to be given off. These are reflected from the face of a crystal (f) to a photographic plate (g). It is found that the elements can be arranged in a series in which the length of the X-ray waves become shorter in a perfectly regular manner. The number of an element in this series is called its atomic number.

慶​優 ​FIG. 76. Diagrams showing the crystal lattices in unit cubes of sodium chloride and of calcium fluoride. The arrangements of the atoms in the crystals are worked out from Xray spectra similar to that shown on the next page. The metal ions are the ones shown in black. waves given off by different elements when they are used as targets in the X-ray tube. These differences run in a regular manner, and the elements have been arranged and numbered from I to 92 in the order of their X-ray wave length. 

This work has given us some exceedingly important information relating to the elements, and a classification of them according to their atomic numbers removes certain difficulties that have always been encountered when they are classified according to atomic weights. Since crystals are used in measuring the length of X-ray waves, a discussion of their structure will be introduced at this point.

The structure of crystals. When a crystal forms from a solution, it begins with a small crystal with the shape characteristic of a crystal of the substance, and grows by successive layers of the materials being deposited on this. When it goes into solution, the material comes off in layers, leaving the undissolved part always with the form of the original larger crystal. If a crystal is broken, the fractures run along lines separating the layers of the materials. 

If a piece of glass is ruled with very fine lines (the distances between the lines must be approximately the wave length of

FIG. 77. A spectrum of sodium chloride made by passing X-rays through powdered sodiumchloride crystals. At first, large and as complete crystals as possible were used in securing spectra of this kind, but it has been found that they can be more conveniently made by passing the X-rays through a small amount of the powdered crystalline material. Each line of the spectrum represents a layer of atoms in the crystal. From these spectra not only the distances between the planes, but between the atoms also, can be calculated, since "in a given crystal, the atomic population of a plane is directly proportional to the inter planar distance." 

FIG. 78. 

Spectograms of the "K series" of X-rays for elements Nos. 72, hafnium (bottom), 74, tungsten (middle), and 78, platinum (top). The spectograms were made by passing waves from an X-ray tube through a crystal of calcite (CaCO3) and allowing them to fall on a photographic plate. The amount the waves are refracted depends on their length, and this varies in a regular manner in accordance with the position in the atomic number series. (Redrawn from photographs furnished by Dr. J. M. Cork of the University of Michigan.)

American Museum of Natural History 

FIG. 79. Crystals of halite. The modern conception of a crystal is that it is not an aggregation of molecules, as was formerly supposed, but is composed of "crystal units," each of which is built up of atoms (or rather ions) arranged in a perfectly definite way. Halite crystals are always cubical, and other crystals take definite geometrical forms. light), light passed through it or reflected from it is separated into its colors and the lines seen in the spectrum formed by a glass prism appear. If X-rays are passed through or reflected from a crystal, the layers in the crystal act like the lines on the glass and an X-ray spectrum is formed. By causing this spectrum to fall on a photographic plate, a record of it can be made, and from these records many facts concerning both crystals and other substances have been learned. 

In the first place, it has been found that crystals are built up of atoms (not molecules) arranged in a perfectly regular manner. 

By methods and calculations of an advanced kind, the patterns according to which the atoms are arranged in different crystals, and even the distances between the atoms, have been determined. (See Figure 76.) The distances between the atoms in a crystal are from 10 to 20 millionths of an inch.

Underwood Press Service

FIG. 80. H. G. J. Moseley, who arranged the elements according to their atomic numbers.

Moseley's atomic numbers. 

In an X-ray tube the X-rays are produced by bombarding a "target" with a stream of electrons from the cathode.

The X-rays are given off by the target under the impact of the electrons (Fig. 75). 

After it had been discovered that X-ray spectra could be formed by the use of crystals, Moseley, a young English physicist, used different elements for the target in the X-ray tube, and thus secured and photographed the X-ray spectra of most of the elements from aluminum to gold. He found that the X-ray waves from these elements became shorter in a regular manner, but at certain places the shortening was twice as great as was to be expected. At these places it was believed that five undiscovered elements come in, and three of these have been found. Moseley numbered the elements from 

I to 92 in the order indicated by their X-ray spectra, assigning numbers to those elements that could not be used as targets in the X-ray tube.

It is believed that these differences in the X-ray spectra of the elements is due to the difference in the number of unbalanced protons in the nuclei of the atoms. Hydrogen has one such unbalanced proton, helium two, and each succeeding element an additional one until we reach uranium, which has 92. The atomic number of each element, therefore, indicates the number of unbalanced protons in the nucleus. It likewise tells us how many electrons are outside the nucleus of the atom, since there

must be enough of these electrons to balance the excess protons within the nucleus. In the first 20 elements, the atomic number is approximately one-half of the atomic weight. If the nucleus were made entirely of helium nuclei, why should this be true? 

Importance of atomic numbers. Moseley died fighting at 

Gallipoli, when he was only twenty-six years of age, but all mankind is indebted to him for his brilliant investigations. The atomic numbers of elements have proved more fundamental than the atomic weights. Both the physical and the chemical properties of the elements seem to depend more on the number of excess protons in the nucleus and the corresponding external electrons which balance these than on the total mass of the atom. 

In the next sections we shall study further the structure of the atom and the differences in the atoms of the different elements. 

In connection with these studies, the following quotations may prove of interest: 66 Suppose that an inch-square piece of graphite is enlarged until it covers the entire orbit of the earth, 190,000,000 miles from side to side. The nuclei of the carbon atoms would be, thus, about 1 miles apart. Each nucleus would be perhaps a foot in diameter (the exact size of the nucleus is in doubt) and each planetary electron would be about 1 inches in diameter. If you stood on one of the 12-inch globes corresponding to a nucleus, the two nearest electrons would be revolving around you at an average distance of about 350 feet, while the four outermost electrons would be at an average distance of about mile." SECTION TWO THE PERIODIC CLASSIFICATION 

SUPPOSE that we had samples of all the elements and wished to separate them into groups according to their common properties. 

On what basis could we arrange them? 

First of all, suppose that we lay them out in the order of their atomic numbers, which in general is the same as the order of their atomic weights. The first pair are the two light gases, hydrogen and helium. We shall place them side by side, with their atomic numbers above them and their atomic weights below:

2 Hydrogen

Helium 4 As we know, hydrogen has a positive valence of 1 and enters into many compounds, while helium is so inert that it has no compounds and even its molecules are monatomic. Hence, although these elements stand next to each other in atomic numbers, we must conclude that chemically they are far apart. 

After helium we have four solids - lithium, beryllium (also called glucinum), boron, and carbon. Then come four gases nitrogen, oxygen, fluorine, and neon. The atomic numbers and atomic weights of these elements are: 3 5 ΙΟ 

Lithium Beryllium Boron Carbon Nitrogen Oxygen Fluorine Neon 6.94 10.82 12 14 16 19 20.2 

As we look over this group, we find that with neon we have come to an inert gas like helium. Lithium, at the other end, is a light alkali metal that reacts with water like sodium and potassium. Beryllium is a silvery white metal less active than lithium; and boron, which we know in its compounds, borax and boric acid, is a dark brown powder. None of these elements is much like hydrogen, but lithium has a positive valence of 1 and in 353

Introductory Chemistry solutions gives an ion with a single positive charge. Suppose that for the present we spread hydrogen and helium apart so that the one will stand over lithium and the other over neon, thus: He

Li Be B C N O F Ne 

The next element is Na, which is much like lithium, and then come magnesium, aluminum, silicon, phosphorus, sulfur, chlorine, and argon. These fit in order right along under our last row, each one showing the same valence and many others of the same properties as the element above. Our table now stands: 

Li Be B C 

Na Mg Al Si He Ne

N O F P S Cl A In this arrangement the metals are on the left and the nonmetals on the right. Further, there is a change of valence across the table. The first, second, and third columns have positive valences of 1, 2, and 3; carbon and silicon show a valence of 4, either positive or negative; nitrogen and phosphorus are 5 positive or 3 negative. The most intensely active elements (not counting the inert gases) are at the ends, and those forming the greatest variety of compounds are in the center. Thus far our elements seem to run in cycles, or periods, which begin with an element having a positive valence of 1 and which end with an inert gas. In the elements following these we find two more such cycles, but each of these is 18 numbers long instead of 8 or 2.

If we list the elements through these cycles, we have:

Introductory Chemistry gradually change their properties and valences from left to right until we come to copper and silver. Then the valence begins again at 1 and continues as in the periods above. After these two periods of eighteen we have another which may be considered as of the same length, but there is trouble at one point. Fifteen elements (the rare earths), all with a usual valence of 3 and all much alike, seem to belong in the third place of the period. For the present it seems best to class them as one element and to complete the table as it is on page 355. 

The final period starts as though it intended to run through another cycle of 18, and then breaks off with uranium. It is believed that there are no elements heavier than uranium; for all those in the final period, as well as radon in the period above, are radioactive, which means that their atoms disintegrate and break down into elements of lower atomic weight. 

After we have become personally acquainted with a greater number of the elements the table will mean more to us, but from the position of an element in the table it is possible to know its general properties. Base-forming properties decrease from left to right, and acid-forming from right to left. Metallic properties lie toward the bottom and left, the non-metallic toward the top and right. The two blanks indicate either undiscovered elements or elements that have never been formed, but because of the positions of these elements their properties are already rather accurately known.

M. Courtines' castle of the elements. An interesting method of showing the periodic relationships of the elements has been worked out by M. Courtines, of the College of France. This arrangement is shown in Figures 82 and 83, and a castle similar to the one there shown may be made by laying off a piece of cardboard as indicated in Figure 81, cutting it on the line below Al and Si and folding and pasting it as the illustrations indicate.

Hydrogen is pasted to the back of helium, which (rightly perhaps) leaves it standing alone in the classification scheme, and the strip bearing the symbols of the rare earths is folded accordionlike and fastened in the opening under yttrium. In the discussion of his castle, M. Courtines calls attention to the fact that the chemical conduct and valences of the elements in the tower are quite regular, but that, in the annex, valences are often variable and periodicity in physical properties is often much more marked than in chemical properties. For example, Cu, Ag, and Au are the column of high electrical conductivity, while Cr, Mo, W, and U are the family of high melting points. 

The classification of M. Courtines is, of course, based on atomic numbers, and in the original castle the squares contained not only the symbols for the elements, but also the atomic numbers, atomic weights and weights of isotopes, and the number of electrons in the successive layers outside the nucleus. For the sake of simplicity, this detail has been omitted here. 

History of the periodic classification of the elements. In the earlier days of chemistry, the elements were classed as metals and non-metals, largely on the basis of their physical properties. 

Resemblances to each other of elements within these groups were soon noticed, however, and for more than a century attempts at finding a more detailed and scientific classification have been made. The more important steps in the growth of the classification scheme up to the Periodic Atomic Number Table were all based on atomic weights and are as follows: 

1. Döbereiner's triads. Between 1816 and 1829 the German chemist, Döbereiner, called attention to the fact that similar elements occur in groups of three. Iron, cobalt, and nickel are one such trio. Chlorine, bromine, and iodine, and calcium, strontium, and barium, are others. 

In some of these groups (e.g., iron, cobalt, nickel) the atomic weights are nearly the same. In other groups the weight of the middle element is practically the mean of the weights of the other

The Periodic Classification 

359 two. Add together the atomic weights of chlorine and iodine, divide the sum by two, and see if the result approximates the weight of bromine. 

2. Newlands' octaves. The English chemist, Newlands, in 

1863 attempted to classify the elements, beginning with lithium, according to their atomic weights in periods of seven: Li Na Gl

Mg Al Si P S Cl At that time the inert gases had not been discovered, and Newlands found every eighth element was "a kind of repetition of the first." This striking resemblance to the notes in music caused Newlands to formulate "the law of octaves." Wrongly determined atomic weights threw Newlands' classifications out of line and prevented his work receiving the recognition it deserved. 

3. The Mendeléeff classification. About 1869 Mendeléeff, a 

Russian, and Meyer, a German, independently of each other and apparently without knowledge of Newlands' work, devised a table of elements practically as it appears on page 380. This is commonly known as the Mendeléeff Table. It has received a few modifications, as atomic weights have been corrected and new elements discovered, and the family of inert gases has been added. Mendeléeff, however, showed great boldness by questioning atomic weights where these prevented him from fitting all the known elements into his table and in predicting that new elements having certain properties and approximate atomic weights would be discovered where there appeared to be blanks in the table, and since his prophecies have been largely fulfilled, his table was remarkably complete. Its chief disadvantage lay in the fact that in most cases two families (a tower and an annex family of M. Courtines' castle) having the same valence but otherwise quite unrelated were brought together in one vertical column. In the row of spaces across the top of the table, “R” stands for any element in the column below, and the positive and

SECTION THREE ELECTRONS AND CHEMICAL REACTIONS 

IF matter is composed of electricity, all the properties of matter must ultimately be accounted for in terms of electricity. For example, whether a substance takes the solid, liquid, or gaseous form; its hardness, flexibility, and density; its adhesive, cohesive, and magnetic properties; its color, odor, taste; and its boiling and freezing points International

FIG. 84. Sir Ernest Rutherford, of Cam bridge University, who formulated our modern conception of the atom. After Thomson had discovered the electron, Rutherford gave us our picture of an atom with a positively charged center and electrons moving like planets about it. must be due to the number, arrangement, and movements of the protons and electrons in the atoms and molecules. 

It is quite beyond the power of modern science to explain all the properties of matter in electrical terms, and it is beyond the scope of this text to follow out the studies that have been made in this field. 

Yet it should be stated that a surprising knowledge of the structure of the atom has been gained, and many of the properties of matter can be explained in the light of this knowledge.

We shall begin our work in this section by building models of the atoms in the first period of eight. As has been explained, the elements in this period consist of a nucleus and pair of electrons, as is found in helium, and about these a second shell which, when filled, contains eight electrons. 362

FIG. 87. The completed cardboard model.

It should be understood that because electrons repel each other, they will shift their positions as each successive one is added, to get as far away as possible from each other. In showing the electrons as held in one place, our models do not correctly represent the conditions that exist in a real atom. 

The dynamic and static atom. In the theories which the physicists have built up concerning the structure of the atom, the electrons are supposed to revolve about the nucleus in orbits, and not to stand poised in space as our model would indicate. Doubtless these theories are substantially correct; for according to all known physical laws, a stationary electron would fall into the nucleus, just as a stationary planet would fall into the sun. It has been very difficult, however, to conceive of chemical reactions and phenomena in terms of revolving electrons (although a beginning in the explanation of chemical facts in these terms has been made), and studies in this field involve difficult reasoning and mathematical calculations that are very complex. It is simplest, therefore, in chemical work to think of a static atom, with the electrons fixed somewhat as in our model. This may seem like an unreasonable thing to do, but it must be remembered that as the electrons speed about the nucleus in their orbits (the hydrogen electron has been calculated to make 1,000,000,000,000,000 revolutions per second) the attracting and repelling electrical and magnetic forces influencing them varies as their distances from the positively charged nucleus and from the other electrons. Hence there may be certain places in the orbits where the electrons exert their greatest influence on other atoms, and we may think of our static electrons as occupying these points.

Pairs and octets of electrons. Work with radium and the facts of chemistry indicate that for some entirely unknown reason electrons tend to go in pairs. Of more than 200,000 known compounds the molecules of only a very few (called "odd molecules") have an odd number of valence electrons. Carbon has four valence (or outer shell) electrons and oxygen six. 

Hence in a molecule of CO2 there is a total of sixteen of these electrons. The NO molecule has eleven valence electrons. 

Hence, it belongs to the quite exceptional class of odd molecules. That electrons go by twos is simply one of the facts of nature that we must accept, as we do the fact that a material CL

FIG. 88. Diagram of a chlorine atom. 

body is attracted by the earth. Another fact is that beyond the first period (hydrogen and helium) the valence electrons tend to go in eights. No element has a valence of more than eight, and the valence of an element is explained by its tendency either to lose enough electrons so that it will not have any of an eight, or to take on enough to complete the eight. It is to be noted that when atoms unite and share their electrons, these shared electrons do double duty by filling out the octets of both atoms. The facts stated above will perhaps be better appreciated after our studies are a little more advanced.

Introductory Chemistry Experiment 91 

OBJECT. To prepare models of certain molecules. Working with some of your neighbors, set side by side the models of a fluorine and of a lithium atom, as indicated in Figure 89. Transfer the bead from the lithium to the fluorine. Which atom is now negatively and which positively charged?

To illustrate the formation of an oxygen molecule, bring together two oxygen atom models. Do not transfer electrons from one atom to the other, but allow the atoms to share two pairs of electrons so as to fill out both octets. 

Illustrate the formation of a carbon dioxide molecule by the sharing of electrons between the carbon and oxygen. 

Polar and non-polar molecules. It is generally believed that electrons act in two ways in chemical reactions. In electrolytes, an electron passes entirely from one atom and takes its place in an orbit about another atom. Because one atom is then positively and the other negatively charged, they are held together by the electrical forces that always operate between such bodies. 

Such a molecule is called a polar molecule, because the electrical charge is different at the two ends, and compounds of this kind are said to have polar valence. 

In the other class of chemical combination, the atoms are supposed to be held together by a binding pair of valence electrons. 

According to the theory, this pair of electrons takes a position between the two atoms (or rather, they exert their binding in- HON FIG. 89. Diagram of arrangement of atoms for the formation of a molecule of carbon dioxide.

fluence at a point between the atoms), and by their attraction for the atoms hold them together.¹ Molecules formed in this way LOOD CO₂ 

FIG. 90. Diagram of a molecule of carbon dioxide. 

are called non-polar, and this kind of valence is called non-polar valence. Do we find both kinds of valence in the same molecule? 

According to another theory, the valence bond is always a pair of electrons between the atoms. This pair not only attracts the atoms but is attracted by them. If the attraction from both atoms is equal (as we might expect, for example, in a hydrogen molecule), the binding pair of electrons will be midway between the atoms. If one atom pulls harder than the other, the pair will move closer to the one exerting the greater force. The position of the valence pair in H2 and HCl may be illustrated thus: H: H 

Since only atoms of the same kind will exert an exactly equal influence on the pair of binding electrons, the theory implies that it is only in the molecules of elements that these electrons will be exactly midway between the atoms. In all other molecules (or, rather, in all joined pairs of atoms in molecules) the binding electrons will be displaced toward one atom. The 

¹ It would seem that the two electrons of the binding pair should repel each other, since both are negative. However, according to the theories of the new wave mechanics, it is possible for the electrons to attract each other if we assume that in addition to their movements in orbits about the atoms they have certain spinning motions, comparable to the rotation of the earth.

Introductory Chemistry electrolytes are substances in which a weak-pulling and a strongpulling atom are matched, with the result that the electron pair FIG. 91. Irving Langmuir in his research labo ratory at the General Electric Company. Dr. 

Langmuir has made many important applications of science in industry, and he has greatly enriched pure science both by his scientific discoveries and his theory of the structure of the atom. is drawn far to one side. The non-electrolytic unions are those in which the contestants are of more nearly equal power. It will be noted that according to this theory electrons pass entirely from one atom to another only when molecules are ionized, and that the molecules of all compounds are to some extent polar.

Electronegativity. All atoms attract electrons, but some attract them more strongly than others. Metals do not cast off their electrons, but are robbed of them by the harder-pulling non-metals. 

The power of an atom to at- tract electrons is called its electronegativity. The appropriateness of the term is appreciated when it is remembered that electrons are particles of negative electricity. The most highly electronegative element of all is fluorine. The weakest in this property is cæsium.

The elements spoken of as having negative valence are those of sufficiently strong electronegativity to gain electrons in most chemical reactions. The elements with positive valence are those of weaker electronegativity which usually lose electrons. 

It is possible, however, for an atom either to lose the electrons in an uncompleted shell or to gain enough to complete it. 

Hence, all elements are capable of exerting either a positive or

a negative valence.

The sum of the positive and negative valences of an element (after hydrogen and helium) is eight.

Let us now turn back to our periodic table and review it in the light of the new facts at our command.

ELECTRONS AND THE PERIODIC TABLE FIG. 92. Gilbert N. Lewis, professor of chem istry in the University of California. He has distinguished himself in the field of thermochemistry, and his theories of valence and of electron arrangements have aided in the explanation of chemical phenomena. The atomic models shown in this section are based primarily on his conceptions.

In our present discussion it will be simpler to speak of atoms as gaining or losing electrons, even though we understand that in many reactions (possibly all of them) the action is not a transference of electrons bodily from atom to atom, but a sharing of a valence pair. We shall also, following the old idea of considering elements as positive and negative, speak of the atoms that lose electrons as getting rid of them, even though we understand that these atoms are robbed by their more powerful electronegative associates. In our review of the elements, it is important to bear in mind that the fewer the electrons in the shell, the more easily they are lost, and that the more nearly the shell is filled, the greater is the tendency to complete it. It should also be remembered that the atoms of the inert gases have filled shells, and that the atoms of all other elements tend to become like them. 

The period of two. The hydrogen atom is unstable both because its electron is unpaired and because its shell is unfilled. 

It can be made to combine with one of the alkali metals and

Introductory Chemistry ionize so that it gains an electron and is a negative ion, H-. 

We may, therefore, think of it as being like the halogens in requiring only one electron to fill its shell and as reacting like the halogens to gain this. It could, therefore, be placed over the halogens in the periodic table and is sometimes so placed. In ordinary chemical reactions, however, hydrogen loses its electron, and for this reason it is simplest to think of it as belonging chemically with the metals of the first column. 

Helium, in contradistinction to hydrogen, already has its pair of electrons and its shell filled. It is, therefore, so inert chemically that even its molecules are monatomic. The alchemists called gold, platinum, and other metals that are resistant to acids and to oxygen the "noble" metals. Following this nomenclature, chemists call helium, neon, and the other inert gases the noble gases.

The first period of eight. Lithium has only one electron and therefore loses it easily. Beryllium does not lose its two quite so easily and is therefore less active. Boron has still more of a tendency to hold its electrons. It unites with oxygen (B2O3) and other negative elements, but its compounds do not ionize. 

Carbon holds its four still more tightly, and its compounds are non-polar. It can be stripped by the more powerful electronegative oxygen or chlorine to form compounds like CO2 and CCl4, but in turn it can rob hydrogen (CH4) and the metals to complete its own eight. Nitrogen has its shell more than half filled and tends to go on and add three to complete the eight (NH3), but a more powerful element can take the five (N2O5). 

Oxygen is near the completion of its shell and its pull is so powerful that it can gather in electrons from almost any source. Fluorine is the most highly electronegative of all the elements and is the most active of any in the list. Neon has its shell complete and, like helium, is inert. We may think of its eight electrons as forming a complete negative electrical field about the atom, so that any electron coming near it is repelled.

The second period of eight. Sodium begins a series that has the same two shells as neon (2 and 8) and outside of these a third N₂Os FIG. 93. Diagram illustrating a possible structure for the molecule of N₂O. shell. The nucleus of the sodium atom contains one more unbalanced proton than that of neon, and hence the atom has one more external electron. This can find no place in the completed shell of the previous series and so is the first member of a third. shell. Then as we go across the period the outer electrons are farther from the nucleus than in the period above and have one more layer of repelling electrons between them and the nucleus. 

These factors cause the valence electrons to be more loosely held, and in general elements that lose electrons in reactions are more active than those in the same column in the period above, while those that gain them are less active. Thus potassium stands ahead of sodium in the displacement series. Fluorine,

Introductory Chemistry on the other hand, is more active than chlorine and can displace chlorine in many compounds. Even oxygen, which is one electron farther than chlorine from a completed shell, can rob chlorine to form the highly explosive compound, Cl₂O7. 

Assuming that a metal is a substance that loses electrons, account for the fact that as we go down in the periodic table the line of separation between the metals and non-metals moves farther to the right.

The longer periods. Thus far the arrangement of the elements in our table has been quite satisfactorily accounted for, but when we come to the longer periods, explanations are not so easy. By turning back to page 355 we find that after the third period there are eighteen elements in a cycle, and if we count the rare earths, the sixth period contains thirty-two elements. Furthermore, in these periods the valence begins at one and runs up to an actual or theoretical valence of eight; then it begins again at one and again runs up to eight. As a still further difficulty, we have in these periods many elements of variable valences - iron, for example, having a valence of either two or three and manganese showing valences varying from two to seven. How can these facts be harmonized with the theory of an outer valence octet? 

The best suggestion that can now be offered as to the electronic arrangement in these heavier elements is briefly as follows: 

As the nucleus of the atom grows larger, the central positive charge finally becomes sufficiently powerful to hold more electrons in the inner layers, and as electrons are added, they may either remain on the outside of the atom or drop into its interior. The following quotation *will give a working concept of the theory and of its application to some of the elements of varying valence in 

Period IV: 

"The number of electrons which can be held in stable equilibrium in a single layer by a positive charge increases 

*From Sir J. J. Thomson's The Electron in Chemistry. Copyright 1923 by the 

Franklin Institute. Used by permission of the publishers.

FIG. 94. William D. Harkins of the University of Chicago. Professor Harkins' investiga tions into the nature of matter are noteworthy, and "Harkins' Helix" is a scheme for classifying the elements that is familiar to all chemists. The apparatus shown in the illustration is used to photograph the paths of helium nuclei as they shoot out from radioactive substances at a speed of 12,000 miles per second. with the charge. Thus, though an inner layer of eight might be as many as the positive charge possessed by the lighter elements could stabilize, yet the heavier elements with their large positive charges might be able to stabilize more than this number. We should thus expect that at some stage in the list of elements the number of electrons in the inner layers would increase, that while when we pass from one element to the next and the number of electrons in the atom increases, the additional electron stays in the inner layer instead of going to the one on the outside of

When this process begins, the change from atom to atom will not be the addition of an electron to the outer layer, but a reorganization of the distribution of electrons in the interior of the atom." 

To illustrate the applications of this new idea of added electrons sometimes dropping to an interior layer, let us turn to the table on page 380 and examine the valences of the elements of the fourth period.

Potassium, calcium, and scandium. These, the first three elements of the period, are regular in their chemical behavior. 

We can account for their properties by supposing that, as in the elements of the preceding periods, the added electrons stay in the surface layer. We can indicate the number of electrons in each layer of the potassium atom, starting from the inside, by the figures 2, 8, 8, 1. For calcium the electron distribution is repre- 

2, and for scandium by 2, 8, 8, 3. sented by 2, 8, 8,

Titanium. In titanium we come to the first element of

irregular behavior. If the electrons had what Professor Thomson has called the "normal" arrangement, their distribution would be represented by 2, 8, 8, 4, and the valence of the element would be 4. The existence of titanium tetrachloride, TiCl4, shows that the electrons may be arranged in this way, but titanium chlorides of the composition TiCl, and TiCl₂ are formed. 

The conclusion is drawn, therefore, that sometimes I and sometimes 2 of the electrons drop to a layer beneath the surface, the distribution of the electrons in these forms of the titanium atom being represented by 2, 8, 9, 3 and 2, 8, 10, 2. 

Vanadium. The pentafluoride, VF5, and the chlorides, VC14,

VCl3, and VCl2, occur. The electron distribution in these forms is represented by 2, 8, 8, 5; 2, 8, 9, 4; 2, 8, 10, 3; and 2, 8, 11, 2, respectively.

Chromium. The fluoride has the formula CrF6. Sometimes, therefore, there are six electrons in the valence (surface) layer of the atom and the electron distribution is represented by 2, 8, 8, 6. 

The chlorides of chromium are CrCl, and CrCl2, indicating also atoms in which the distribution is 2, 8, 11, 3 and 2, 8, 12, 2.

Manganese. In manganese, if none of the electrons dropped within, the distribution would be 2, 8, 8, 7. The fluoride, MnF7, shows that this type exists. Other fluorides, and compounds with elements other than fluorine, show valences of 6, 4, 3, and 2, indicating atoms in which the electron distribution is respectively 

2, 8, 9, 6; 2, 8, 11, 4; 2, 8, 12, 3; and 2, 8, 13, 2.

Iron, nickel, and cobalt. All these elements show positive valences of 2 and 3, their chlorides being of the type FeCl2 and FeCl3. 

From this it is assumed that in their outer electron layers they are identical, and that the differences in these elements is in the nuclei and in the number of electrons in the layer below the surface one.

Introductory Chemistry 

Copper. The chlorides of copper are CuCl and CuCl2, indicating atoms with either one or two electrons in the outer layer. 

The electron distribution in these atoms is represented by 2, 8, 18, 1 and 2, 8, 17, 2.

After passing copper, the valence of the elements to the end of the period changes regularly with the addition of each electron. 

Quoting Professor Thomson again : "The normal process by which, when we pass from one element to the next in order of atomic weight, the new electron goes to the outer layer seems to be resumed after passing copper; thus we have zinc with two electrons in the outer layers; gallium with three; germanium with four; arsenic with five; selenium with six; bromine with seven; and krypton with eight. "Thus we see that as we proceed up the list of elements we may expect to meet with a batch of elements in whose atoms the electrons change from one tautomeric distribution to another with but little expenditure of energy. In this batch the ordinary progress of valency with atomic weight is interrupted, and the valences are variable. On passing through the batch the regular sequence is resumed, the series goes on and ends with eight electrons on the outer layer, while the next series begins with one in that layer." 

Experiment 92 

OBJECT. To build models of the atoms of certain elements of variable valence.

A. Construct a model of the manganese atom with the electrons in what Thomson calls the normal arrangement. Shift the electrons to give the other arrangements indicated by the valence of manganese. 

B. On the same model show the suggested structures of the iron, nickel, and cobalt atoms. 

At this time we shall not go further into the arrangement of the electrons in the heavier elements, but as we study the different families, their valences and some of their other properties will be considered from an electronic point of view.

ELECTRONS AND CHEMICAL EQUATIONS 

Positive valence is due to loss of electrons. Negative valence is due to a gain of electrons. In chemical reactions electrons do not disappear, because what one atom or molecule loses, another must gain. By holding these facts in mind and thinking in terms of them, the balancing of chemical equations is greatly simplified. 

The total positive valence in a molecule of a compound is equal to the total negative valence. This can best be shown by a few examples:

Electronic equations. In addition to the facts shown by an ordinary chemical equation, an electronic equation indicates the electron transfer in the reaction. To write such an equation, first express the skeleton equation as usual and mark the valence of each element. For example, the reaction between carbon and oxygen would be: 

C° + O₂º

C4+O₂² 

The carbon atom loses four electrons, and these are gained by the oxygen, so the carbon can be written (C 4€) and the oxygen (O2 + 4). The electronic equation, with arrows to show the source and destiny of the electrons, is expressed thus:

Introductory Chemistry 

(C-4)+(O₂+46) CO₂ Since the number of electrons lost by one molecule must be equal to those gained by the other, it is easy to balance an equation by placing the proper coefficients before the parentheses. 

In the above equation no adjustment of the coefficients is necessary, but a few other examples, in which the equations must be balanced, will be given :

Cu°O₂º Cu²+0²- 

2(Cu 2€)+(O2 + 4€)—2 CuO 

Since each atom or molecule of copper loses only two electrons, while each molecule of oxygen gains four, two molecules (or atoms) of the copper are necessary to furnish the required number of electrons for the copper. After the left-hand side of the equation is balanced, the right-hand side can be balanced at once. 

In the above case, as soon as two atoms of copper were used, a "2" must be placed before the copper oxide.

The change of valence is placed on the right of the vertical arrows and numbers are placed on the left of the arrows so that the products of the numbers on the two sides of any of the two vertical arrows will be equal to the product of the numbers on the two sides of the other arrow. The numbers on the left of the arrows are the coefficients of the molecules which undergo electronic changes.

The discovery of protons and electrons has given us explanations of many chemical facts and phenomena which were not explainable on the basis of an indivisible atom. Investigations in this field are highly technical, and the research workers in it are as yet comparatively few, but the studies of these workers are showing us why and under what conditions chemical reactions take place. This knowledge will of course aid us in bringing about desired chemical reactions and in inhibiting those that we do not wish to take place.

SECTION FOUR THE ELECTROMOTIVE SERIES 

In our earlier work in chemistry we have learned that the metals can be arranged in a displacement, or electromotive series, and that the place of an element in this series is determined by its electronegativity, or the force with which it holds its electrons. 

Obviously this property, from the point of view of the chemist, is a most fundamental one; for the way an element reacts chemically depends on whether it gains or loses electrons under the varying conditions to which it is subjected, and this in turn depends on the tightness with which the electrons are held. 

Factors determining the place of a metal in the displacement series. In the displacement series the metals are arranged in the reverse order of their electronegativity. Any complete explanation of electronegativity is, in the present state of our knowledge, of course impossible, but three of the factors involved in it are listed below. In the discussion the statements made apply only to the factor under consideration, and no account is taken of the effects of other factors that may be operative at the same time. 

1. Number of electrons in the valence layer. The attracting power of an atom increases with the nearness to completion of its valence layer. In terms of this factor, explain why the elements on the left of the Periodic Table are metals. 

2. Number of layers of electrons outside the nucleus. The farther out from the nucleus the valence electrons are and the more layers of electrons there are between them and the nucleus, the more loosely the outer electrons are held. In terms of this factor, explain why carbon and nitrogen are non-metals and lead and bismuth are metals. 

3. Mass of the atom. If only factors (1) and (2) were operative, hydrogen should be the most highly electronegative of all the elements and should stand in the column over fluorine. Explain why the above statement is true. 382

The mass of the hydrogen nucleus is so small, however, that it cannot hold its electron securely. Hence in chemistry it is placed with the metals. In the same way the lightness of lithium explains its place above rubidium, potassium, and sodium in the electromotive series, while the high electronegativity of heavy metals, such as lead and gold, is accounted for in large part by their relatively large nuclear mass. 

What we call the electronegativity of an atom - its power to attract electrons is the resultant of the operation of all these (and doubtless other) factors. We cannot determine the effect of each factor individually, but their relative combined effects in different elements can be determined. 

Experiment 93 

OBJECT. To determine the relative electronegativity of certain metals. 

In separate test tubes put 2 cc. normal solutions of Zn(NO3)2, FeCl3, Cu(NO3)2, AgNO3, Hg(NO3)2, and Pb(NO3)2.

Drop a strip of zinc in each tube and let stand for five minutes. 

In a similar manner insert strips of pure iron wire in another set of test tubes containing the same solutions. 

With other sets of test tubes repeat the experiment, using lead, copper, and mercury.

After about five minutes remove and examine first the zinc strips, rinsing if necessary. Then remove the others in order. Determine if there are any deposits on the metals in the different tubes and the nature of these deposits. Record results in the table on the opposite page.

Describe any other evidence of change in any of the solutions. 

Was any element used in the experiment replaced by an element above it in the electromotive series (page 387)?

Experiments of this nature helped to give the order of this series. The chemical changes involved are simple displacements, and hence the series has become known also as the displacement series.

The explanation of why the reactions take place is that each element in the series holds its electrons less firmly than the one which follows it. Make a diagram similar to that on page 67, showing in terms of electrons exactly what happens when a piece of zinc is placed in copper nitrate. 

The relative force with which metals hold electrons may be measured electrically, as is shown in the next experiment. 

Experiment 94

OBJECT. To measure the difference in the force with which zinc and copper hold their electrons. (Instructor.)

Take a N solution of zinc sulfate in a beaker (A) and a N solution of copper sulfate in a beaker (B). For one electrode place a strip of copper in the copper solution, and a strip of zinc in the zinc solution for the other electrode. Connect the zinc electrode to the negative pole of a voltmeter by means of copper wire, but leave the wire from the copper electrode disconnected until all apparatus is set up. Connect the two beakers by a U tube which has been filled with one of the solutions. When all is in readiness, touch the loose end of the wire to the plus pole of the voltmeter and note the amount of its deflection. 

Record result.

The deflection of the voltmeter has shown that a current has passed through the wire; i.e., a stream of electrons has passed from the zinc to the copper. The copper ions bathing the copper electrode have a stronger attraction for the electrons than have

the zinc ions at the zinc electrode, and hence the copper ions pull the electrons in their direction. Since the current is due to the higher electronegativity of the copper ions, the voltmeter can be used to measure how much greater pull for electrons the copper ion has than has the zinc ion. 

The electrons pulled over are taken up by the copper ions in the solution which bathes the copper electrode, and this causes a deposition of metallic copper on the copper electrode, leaving the negative ion, SO, in solution. 

The zinc electrode, on the other hand, has been robbed of some of its electrons, and therefore some zinc is left in ionic condition. 

These excess positive zinc ions pass into solution, where they unite with the excess sulfate ions which have been left by the deposition of the copper. The sulfate and zinc ions reach each other by means of the U tube. 

Electromotive force. The apparatus arranged in Experiment 

94 is an electrical cell, or battery, and the force which it displays is called its electromotive force (E. M. F.). This depends on (1) the relative electronegativity of the two elements and (2) the concentrations of the solutions. In our experiment, however, the two solutions were of equal ionic concentration, so in this case the electromotive force developed was due to and was a direct measure of the difference in the force with which the zinc ions and copper ions attract their electrons. Thus by having the concentrations of the solutions equal, it is possible to have the electromotive force of a cell the same as the difference in the electronegativity of the two elements used as electrodes. 

Electrical potential. By keeping one of the above metals, say zinc, as one electrode and using solutions of equal ionic concentrations, the electromotive force developed by other elements paired with the zinc could be measured. In other words, zinc could be made the standard, and the relative negativity of the other elements could be determined with respect to zinc.

The significance of the electromotive series in chemistry. 

In the electromotive series we have (1) the elements lined up in the order of their electronegativity and (2) the electromotive force developed between some of the elements and hydrogen measured. The elements high in the series lose electrons most easily, and those at the lower end attract electrons most strongly.

A complete electromotive scries would include all the elements,

Introductory Chemistry but methods for making some of them into electrodes and keeping them bathed in solutions of constant concentrations have not as yet been worked out. 

Our present knowledge of the relative electronegativity of the elements is most useful and explains many of the facts of chemistry that we meet from day to day. 

EXERCISES 

1. Explain why the metals high in the electromotive series react with water at ordinary temperatures, while metals low in the series do not. 

2. The power of metals to act as electronizing agents is indicated by their order in the electromotive series. Explain why. 

3. Which metals oxidize most easily? Why? 

4. Mercuric oxide can be decomposed, by heating, into mercury and oxygen. The oxides of metals high in the electromotive series are more stable and cannot be decomposed by ordinary heating. Explain why. 

The hydrogen electrode. It is of interest to see how a gaseous element like hydrogen may be made into an electrode. This is done by using a piece of platinum and allowing it to be bathed by a solution of HCl, which furnishes hydrogen ions in standard concentration, while gaseous hydrogen is bubbled through the solution. The hydrogen gas is adsorbed by the platinum until the platinum is covered with hydrogen, and the stream of hydrogen run into the solution keeps the platinum saturated. When the electrode thus prepared is connected with any other electrode, it acts as though it were a strip of hydrogen, and if a voltmeter is introduced into the circuit, it measures the strength of the current passing between the hydrogen and the element composing the other electrode. For instance, if the hydrogen were connected with a zinc electrode as is shown in Figure 97, we would measure the strength of the current between the hydrogen and zinc, and by using other elements in place of zinc, all of them could be compared in terms of hydrogen. Hence it is possible to use the hydrogen electrode in measuring relative negativities,

and it can also be used in measuring the hydrogen ion concentration of solutions, as we shall now see. — эг SO Zinc

FIG. 97. Diagram showing how the difference in electronegativity of zinc and hydrogen is measured.

Measuring hydrogen ion concentration. The ionic concentration of the solutions bathing the electrodes is a factor in determining the electromotive force of a cell (page 385). Just why this is true we shall not try to explain. That it is true the simplest experiment will show, and because it is true the hydrogen electrode can be used for determining hydrogen ion concentration.

In making the measurement the hydrogen electrode, which is now the variable, is dipped into the unknown solution and measured against a mercury electrode bathed in saturated mercurous chloride (" calomel electrode "), which now becomes the standard. The current passes from the hydrogen to the

Introductory Chemistry mercury, and its strength varies as the concentration of the hydrogen ions in the solution. 

The hydrogen electrode is used by scientists in many fields for determining hydrogen ion concentration, and its introduction has been followed by distinct scientific advancement in many lines. In medical practice it is employed to determine the hydrogen ion concentration of the blood and other body fluids; in agriculture, to determine the acidity of soils; in bacteriology, to measure the hydrogen ion concentration of culture media. 

Chemical reactions (e.g., hydrolysis) in which hydrogen and hydroxyl ions are released can be followed through with it, and in scientific study and in industry almost countless opportunities for the use of the hydrogen electrode in determining hydrogen ion concentration have been found. 

The boundary zone between metals and non-metals. Turn back to the Atomic Number Periodic Table. Draw a line on it that will have just to its right or just above it B, Si, P, Se, and Te. 

Only non-metals are above or to the right of this line. 

Now draw a second line, which will have just to its right or just above it Zn, Ga, Sn, and Sb. Only metals lie below or to the left of this line. The elements between the two lines are amphoteric. In general they resemble the metals rather than the non-metals, but their hydroxides ionize to give either hydrogen or hydroxyl ions, according to the environment. 

Aluminum is a typical element of this group, and the reaction of its hydroxide is given in acid and in basic media respectively, in equations (1) and (2) below:

Al(OH)3 Al(OH)3 Al³+ + 3OH¬ H++ (AlO2)¯ + H₂O (1) (2) Write equations showing the reactions of aluminum hydroxide with an acid and with a base:

If we draw the graphic formula of Al(OH)3, exactly what happens becomes more evident:

H-O-A1-0-H In an acid medium the OH radicals separate from the aluminum. In an alkaline medium the two of them break between the H and the O, one of the released hydrogens uniting with an OH to form water and the other remaining free in ionic form. 

It must always be remembered, however, that the chemical conduct of an element depends on its environment and that no classification of the elements on the basis of their reactions can be held too rigidly. Practically all the elements lying between the alkali metals and alkaline earths on the left side of the table and the halogens on the right can be forced to act amphoterically. Especially is this true of a number of the elements in columns 3 and 4 at the right of the table.

SECTION ONE FLUORINE 

FLUORINE does not have as many uses as the other members of the group, and discussion of it will therefore be more limited. It is found largely as calcium fluoride or fluor spar, CaF2, as sodium aluminum fluoride or cryolite, Na,AlF6, and in a mineral called apatite, which has a composition CaF2 · Ca3(PO4)2. It is prepared by the electrolysis of potassium fluoride, KF, dissolved in liquid hydrogen fluoride, H₂F2. Moisson, a French chemist who achieved popular fame by producing diamonds artificially, prepared fluorine in the free state for the first time in 1886. He found that copper could be used because the copper fluoride formed on the surface of the copper acted as a protective coating. 

Physical properties. The activity of fluorine prevents our making any experimental study of its properties. Its physical properties are given in the following table:

Chemical properties. Fluorine is so extremely active that it reacts with most substances. With the lighter metals it reacts instantly and violently, but only slowly with gold and platinum. 

With copper it unites at once, but a layer of copper fluoride forms on the surface of the copper and prevents further reaction. In its ordinary reactions it shows a valence of one. HYDROGEN FLUORIDE 

The most important compound of fluorine, from the chemist's point of view, is hydrogen fluoride, which is a gas. Hydrofluoric acid is a solution of this gas in water. The formula for hydrogen fluoride is usually written H2F2, but by varying the temperature, HF, H₂F2, or HзF3 may be produced.

Common glass is composed mostly of sodium and calcium silicates, and when hydrogen fluoride comes in contact with it, the metals (sodium and calcium) in the glass are converted into fluorides, NaF and CaF2, and the silicon into silicon tetrafluoride, SiF4. The surface thus attacked is left roughened, and this gives the glass an opaque appearance.
