From your experimental work, what kind of bottles would you recommend as containers for hydrofluoric acid? 

Hydrofluoric acid has the characteristic properties of an acid. 

What are they?

In spite of its activity in attacking glass, hydrofluoric acid as an acid is rather weak. It dissociates much less completely than 

HCl or H2SO4, and so furnishes a weaker concentration of hydrogen ions than these acids. The presence of both the hydrogen and fluorine ions, however, gives hydrofluoric acid an exceedingly wide range of combining powers. 

Uses of hydrogen fluoride. Hydrogen fluoride is used principally for etching glass. The marking of laboratory glassware, such as cylinders, burettes, and thermometers, is done by this method. In finishing cut glass, the superficial designs are often etched either with the gas itself or with its aqueous solution. The gas gives a dull finish; the acid gives a more sparkling, glossy appearance. 

Sodium fluoride, NaF, is used by poultrymen on their fowls as an insecticide. It is applied either by dusting a small amount among the feathers or by dipping the fowls in a solution of it.

SECTION TWO BROMINE 

BROMINE was given its name because of its odor (Greek: bromos, a stench). It occurs chiefly as sodium bromide and magnesium bromide. Like sodium chloride, it is found in sea water and in deposits where bodies of water have been evaporated. The large salt beds of Michigan, West Virginia, Ohio, Kentucky, and especially the Stassfurt deposits in Germany are rich in bromides. The striking resemblance of this element to chlorine will be noted as our study proceeds. 

Preparation. When water containing a mixture of bromides and of relatively large amounts of sodium chloride are evaporated, much of the sodium chloride crystallizes out first, leaving a "mother liquor " much richer in bromine than the original solution. From this liquor, bromine can be liberated by electrolysis. 

Write the equation illustrating the reaction when sodium bromide is electrolyzed:

Chlorine is also set free in the electrolytic process, but since this is more active than bromine chemically, it reacts with the bromides in the solution and releases the bromine in them: 

2NaBr + Cl2 2NaCl + Br2

It is therefore not necessary to remove the salt from the water to secure the bromine in it. 

The laboratory method of preparing bromine is similar to the method employed in the preparation of chlorine.

Relative solubility of bromine in water and in carbon disulfide. 

Carbon disulfide is not soluble in water. Hence, if these two substances are placed together in a test tube, they remain separate. Now if a third substance, soluble in both carbon disulfide and water, is introduced into the test tube, it divides itself between them in accordance with its solubility in each. If this third substance gives a color to the solution, it is possible to make a qualitative comparison of its solubility in the carbon disulfide and in the water.

SECTION THREE 

IODINE IODINE is present on the earth in relatively very small quantities; but it is one of the elements necessary to the life of the human body, and it is highly toxic to bacteria as compared with its toxicity for animal tissues. These properties make it best known to the general public as a wound disinfectant and in connection with goiter and other deranged conditions of the thyroid gland. It.occurs chiefly as sodium and potassium salts, which are soluble in water and therefore tend to accumulate in the sea. It is found in the form of sodium iodate, NaIO3, in connection with the Chile nitrate deposits, and is accumulated by seaweeds from the water in which they grow. It was first isolated from the ashes of these weeds. 

Preparation. The chief source of supply of iodine is the Chile nitrate beds; but some is derived from seaweeds, and much more could be secured from this source if a profitable market for it could be found. Commercially the iodine is prepared by chemical treatment of the iodine-containing substances and by electrolysis.

Uses of iodine. Iodine is used in photography and the dye industry, but, as has been noted, its most familiar uses are as a disinfectant and in medicine. Recent investigations have shown that the waters of the Great Lakes area and certain other parts of our country are notably deficient in iodine and that, generally speaking, sea foods are two hundred times as rich in iodine as are the foodstuffs produced in these regions. In these areas of iodine deficiency, goiter is common among both men and animals, and in many places the best means of providing an artificial iodine supply is receiving careful consideration. A few cities are adding it to their water supplies; in Switzerland it is added to the salt sold, or only salt naturally containing iodine is allowed to be marketed; in a number of places chocolate tablets containing iodine are being distributed to the school children as medicine. 

Fortunately the human body is able to draw its iodine supply from inorganic compounds like sodium or potassium iodide, and the problem is one capable of easy solution.

Introductory Chemistry 

Energy changes accompanying chemical reactions. When hydrogen and chlorine are mixed, they unite with an explosion (page 209), and when potassium is brought into contact with water, so much heat is liberated by the ensuing reaction that the hydrogen evolved is ignited (page 45). Where such chemical changes take place, the substances formed always contain less energy than the original substances. That is, if two substances combine without the application of heat, electricity, or other form of energy, there is always a loss of energy accompanying the reaction. The energy liberated is usually in the form of heat, and the amount of it is generally measured and expressed in calories. 

On the other hand, many chemical reactions take place only under the application of energy. An example of a reaction of this kind is the union of calcium with carbon to form calcium carbide, CaC2, the product used in the production of acetylene for lighting. The union is brought about by the intense heat of the electric furnace, and the amount of energy absorbed is very great. This energy is regained when the acetylene secured by treating the carbide with water is burned. Photosynthesis (page 

249) is another example of a chemical reaction in which energy is absorbed.

Heat of formation and decomposition. The heat liberated or absorbed when a substance is formed is called the heat of formation. That liberated or absorbed when a substance is decomposed is called the heat of decomposition. The heat of decomposition is always equal to the heat of formation, in accordance with the law of the conservation of energy. From the statements made above it is evident that in calculations relating to the energy changes accompanying chemical reactions, either the heat of formation or the heat of decomposition may be a minus quantity. 

Thermal equations. When 1.008 grams of hydrogen react with 35.46 grams of chlorine to form 36.468 grams of hydrogen chloride, 22,000 calories of heat are given up. The entire process would be represented thus:

Iodine 409 H + Cl →→→→→→ HCl + 22,000 calories Since the gases involved in the above reaction each have two atoms to the molecule, it is customary to write the equation : H2 + Cl2 →→→→→ 2HCl + 2 X 22,000 calories Such an equation is known as a thermal equation. 

The heat of formation of hydrogen bromide is 8,400 calories and of hydrogen iodide — 6,040.

Write the thermal equations by which these two compounds are formed: In the reaction through which hydrogen iodide is formed, is heat absorbed or evolved?

Do reactions in which heat is evolved or those in which it is absorbed take place more readily?

Mark a long arrow above the following equation to indicate which way the reaction would go according to the preceding answer: 

2HBr + Cl2 = 2HCl + Br2

Is the direction of the arrow consistent with the replacement work done with chlorine and bromine? 

How could knowledge of the heat of formation help in predicting the way in which a reaction would go?

Practical importance of the energy changes in chemical reactions. Ordinarily in chemistry we are concerned with the material substances entering into and resulting from the reactions, but in many of the chemical processes in practical life the chief importance is attached to the energy liberated or absorbed. Fuel is burned for the heat it gives off and not for the ashes and the gases that go up the chimney; the oxy-acetylene flame is for cutting steel and not for the production of CO2 and water; the radio pack" on the addition of water gives warmth, and this "C

Introductory Chemistry is all that the user desires of it. In the branch of the science called "thermochemistry," energy changes are the important thing, and, as in many chemical processes in practical life, the material substances resulting are practically by-products. 

Salts of the halogens (halides). The halogen acids, like all other acids, have their salts. We have already become familiar with the calcium, magnesium, sodium, and potassium halides. 

In the preparation of the halogen salts the usual method of obtaining a salt is applicable; that is, by the action of an acid upon a hydroxide or of an oxide on a metal. The best-known fluoride is that of calcium. Sodium chloride is the most important chloride. Potassium bromide and potassium iodide are used in medicine. The silver salts of bromine and iodine find application in photography.

From your experience in the preparation of the halogen acids, how could you distinguish between a fluoride, a chloride, a bromide, and an iodide?

PH3, ASH3, and SbH, are called respectively phosphine, arsine, and stibine. They are all poisonous gases.

The acid character of these elements decreases with the increase of the atomic weights. Bismuth - the one of the greatest density forms no acid, but forms bases instead. It has the 

413

Introductory Chemistry properties of a metal rather than of a non-metal, since a metal has been defined as an element whose oxide combines with water to form a base. Antimony and arsenic are both acid- and baseforming elements. Such elements are said to be amphoteric. 

They are on the border line between metals and non-metals. 

With the increase in atomic weights, the different members of the group also show an increase in the power of conducting heat and electricity, which are metallic properties. 

Nitrogen and some of its compounds have been discussed in some detail in Part One; we shall now turn our attention to the oxides of nitrogen, and then to the other elements of the family.

SECTION ONE OXIDES OF NITROGEN 

BECAUSE of its wide occurrence, its chemical activity, and its unusual chemical properties, oxygen combines with more substances than does any other element. Hence, when we study the compounds of any element, we are likely to find oxygen compounds prominent among them. In this section we shall study the compounds which nitrogen forms with oxygen alone. 

The five oxides of nitrogen. Nitrogen combines with oxygen in five different proportions, as given below. This series of compounds affords a striking illustration of the law of multiple proportions, and in these compounds we have an example of an element exerting a valence of from one to five. 

Nitrous oxide 

Nitric oxide. N20 NO

Nitrogen trioxide (nitrous anhydride) 

Nitrogen peroxide

Nitric pentoxide (nitric anhydride). N203 NO2 or N2O4 N2O5 We shall study experimentally only three of these oxides nitrous oxide, N₂O, nitric oxide, NO, and nitrogen peroxide, NO2. 

NITROUS OXIDE 

Nitrous oxide is a gas used as an anæsthetic in dentistry and for minor surgical operations. The hysterical symptoms which accompany its use have caused it to receive the name of "laughing gas."

Nitrous oxide was discovered in 1776 by the distinguished English chemist, Priestley, who two years earlier discovered oxygen. Its physiological effects were first noted by Sir Humphry 

Davy, who after inhaling it wrote: "The gas raised my pulse upwards of 20 strokes and made me dance about the laboratory as a madman." It was the first of the substances having anaesthetic properties with which chemists became familiar. 415

Introductory Chemistry 

Experiment 106 

OBJECT. To prepare nitrous oxide. 

Set up the apparatus as shown in Figure 99. 

Put about 7 grams of ammonium nitrate, NH4NO3, in the large test tube. A thin-walled flask may be used instead of the test tube. Heat slowly. 

CAUTION. Brown fumes or very great foaming indicate that decomposition is taking place too vigorously. Remove the flame immediately on indication of these; otherwise an explosion may result. 

FIG. 99.

Uses of nitric oxide. The fact that nitric oxide will take up oxygen from the air and easily release it again makes it possible to use it as a catalytic agent in the "chamber process " for the manufacture of sulfuric acid (page 201), and for converting the nitrogen of the air into nitric acid. (See page 141.) These uses of nitric oxide have given it an importance possessed by few chemical substances, and in future it will probably become even more important than it is at the present time.

We have already become slightly acquainted with this heavy brown oxide of nitrogen. It is a very poisonous gas; even slight amounts, if inhaled, cause headache and nausea. Great care should therefore be used in working with it.

NITROUS TRIOXIDE AND NITROGEN PENTOXIDE 

Nitrogen trioxide, N2O3, and nitrogen pentoxide, N2O5, are very unstable oxides and of little importance. They unite with water, forming nitrous acid and nitric acid. 

H2O + N2O3

H2O + N2O5 → 2HNO2 2HNO3

The above reactions show that these two oxides of nitrogen belong to what class of chemical substances (page 47)?

The oxides of nitrogen furnish striking examples of compounds that differ in their physical and chemical qualities from the parent substances of which they are composed. Nitrous oxide, for example, which when inhaled quickly produces unconsciousness, is in its physiological effects vastly different from the inert

Introductory Chemistry nitrogen or the life-supporting oxygen that enter into its composition. The oxides of nitrogen also show how different compounds formed by the combination of the same elements in different proportions may be different from each other in their chemical and physical properties.

SECTION TWO PHOSPHORUS 

PHOSPHORUS Compounds are found in almost all rocks and soils. The most widely distributed of these compounds is apatite, a mineral which contains calcium and fluorine or chlorine combined with oxygen and phosphorus. These phosphorus compounds are dissolved with difficulty, but carbonic acid and organic acids bring some of them into solution and make them available for growing plants. Animals in turn secure the phosphorus from plants, and the mineral matter of bones and teeth is calcium phosphate, Ca3(PO4)2. Proteins containing phosphorus help make up the tissues of the brain, nerves, and muscles also. 

Among foods, egg yolk, beans, peas, nuts, and the outer layer of the wheat grain are especially rich in phosphorus. 

Preparation. In South Carolina, Tennessee, Georgia, and 

Florida, and in Idaho, Utah, and Wyoming, are great beds of phosphate rock, Ca3(PO4)2. These beds were formed by the phosphates from the bones and teeth of animals (fish) being dissolved in water and redeposited. Phosphorus is made chiefly from this rock, although bone can be used. The phosphate is continuously fed into an electric furnace, together with coke and silica. The heat of the furnace decomposes the calcium phosphate thus:

Ca3(PO4)2 →→→→ 3CaO + P₂O5 (1) The silica (SiO2) reacts with the calcium oxide, as given on page 496, to form calcium silicate. 

Write the equation for the reaction (2) The P₂O, is reduced by the coke to phosphorus, carbon monoxide being formed. Complete the equation for the reduction process. 423

+ P₁ (3) Add Equations (1), (2), and (3) according to directions given on page 125. The resulting equation is The calcium silicate is drawn off from time to time in the form of slag, while the phosphorus passes out as a gas and is condensed under water. It is then molded into sticks like those bought for laboratory use. 

Properties. The next experiment will show the principal properties of phosphorus.

Experiment 110 OBJECT. To discover some of the properties of red phosphorus and of white phosphorus. (Instructor.)

Red phosphorus is converted into the white phosphorus by heating it to about 290° C. and suddenly cooling the resulting vapors. This process may be carried out in the following manner : 

Drop into a hard glass test tube a half gram of the red phosphorus and fix the test tube in a horizontal position, having the phosphorus at the closed end. Be careful not to point the open end toward your neighbor. Keep a small flame under the phosphorus, heating it slowly but steadily while keeping the open end of the tube cool. When the greater portion of the red phosphorus has disappeared, let the tube cool as suddenly as possible. Take some of the deposit on the end of a long splinter of wood, and bring it near a flame. Result?

Ignite some red phosphorus. Which kind of phosphorus seems to ignite at the lower temperature? 

B. White phosphorus.

CAUTION. White phosphorus is very poisonous. Positively do not touch with the hands. Use care, for it takes fire easily. Do not put any residues in waste receptacles; burn them under a hood.

FIG. 101. A view in a match factory. The second man from the left is feeding match sticks into a machine which forces the ends of them into holes in an endless belt, or conveyer.

What compound is formed when red phosphorus burns in oxygen?

The same compound is formed when white phosphorus burns in oxygen or air. Since no other products are formed in either case, it is quite evident that both forms must consist of nothing but phosphorus,

Introductory Chemistry and hence phosphorus exists in allotropic forms. What is meant by allotropic forms?

Matches. Since 1827, when friction matches were introduced, the chief use of phosphorus has been in the manufacture of matches. The two common types of matches are the ordinary friction match and the safety match. The latter lights only when brought into contact with a prepared rubbing surface or when rubbed quickly over some smooth non-conducting surface like glass or slate.

Until quite recently the head of the friction match contained white phosphorus, an oxidizing agent (e.g., lead dioxide, manganese dioxide, or potassium chlorate), and glue. The wooden splints are dipped into melted paraffine and then into the paste of white phosphorus and the oxidizing agent. When dry, the heads are dipped into varnish to exclude air and moisture. On striking, the phosphorus is set on fire and readily ignites the paraffine, which in turn sets fire to the wood. 

Constant exposure to the fumes of phosphorus produces diseases of the bones, so that the use of white phosphorus in the manufacture of matches has been prohibited by law in most countries, and a compound of phosphorus and sulfur (P4S3) is now used in place of white phosphorus. 

The head of the safety match contains no phosphorus, but consists of a difficultly combustible material, antimony trisulfide (Sb2S3), and an oxidizing agent, which may be potassium dichromate, red lead, or one of those mentioned above. The striking surface on the box contains a mixture of powdered glass, red phosphorus, and glue.

Compounds of phosphorus. From a study of the table on page 413, it is apparent that phosphorus unites with hydrogen, chlorine, and oxygen. It forms phosphorous acid, H¿PO3, phosphoric acid, H3PO4, and four other acids that are less common. 

Phosphorus trioxide, P₂O3, and phosphorus pentoxide, P2O5, are the acid anhydrides of phosphorous acid and phosphoric acid.

Phosphorus 427 Write the equation for the formation of each acid from its anhydride. What three possible sodium salts might we obtain from phosphoric acid?

Fertilizers. Nitrogen has already been mentioned as a necessary constituent of plants. Furthermore, it was seen that it was most available for the plant when it was in the form of a nitrate. Phosphorus is a second element that plays a very important rôle in plant growth. This element is most available for the plant when it is in the form of superphosphate, CaH4 (PO4)2, because this compound is freely soluble in water and the phosphate, Ca3(PO4)2, is practically insoluble. Superphosphate is made from calcium phosphate found in the beds already mentioned, by treating it with sulfuric acid. Write the equation for the reaction: CaSO4 + The phosphate fertilizer consists of a mixture of the calcium sulfate and the calcium superphosphate. The other element for which there is the greatest need in most soils besides the nitrogen and phosphorus is potassium. A complete fertilizer contains all three of these elements, but the ratio of them in the mixture is changed, according to the crop and the needs of the soil. 

No other country in the world has such rich phosphate deposits as the United States, and as much as 3,000,000 tons are mined in a year. Most of the supply has come from Florida, but vast deposits are in reserve in the West.

SECTION THREE ARSENIC, ANTIMONY, BISMUTH 

ARSENIC, antimony, and bismuth have not found as extensive uses as phosphorus and hence will be discussed in less detail. 

Bismuth is a rather rare element and exists both in combined and uncombined states. It is found in several countries, but 

Bolivia furnishes the chief supply. 

Arsenic and antimony occur both as the oxides and sulfides. 

The sulfides can be easily changed to the oxides by roasting. 

The oxides are reduced with carbon.

All can be converted into the oxide by heating in the air.

The chief use of arsenic is in spray or dust materials for combating insects. Paris green and arsenate of lead are widely used by gardeners, truckers, and orchardists; dusting with calcium arsenate against the cotton-boll weevil is extensively practiced; and dusting the breeding places with Paris green or calcium arsenate is proving an effective method of deştroying the malaria mosquito. The poisonous qualities of arsenic give it a use in medicine also. It is employed in conjunction with quinine in refractory cases of malaria, and salvarsan and neoarsphenamin are arsenic compounds.

FIG. 103. An interior view of an arsenic oven, showing the condensed arsenic on the walls. 

Uses of antimony and bismuth. Antimony, like water, has the unusual property of expanding when it solidifies. Because of this property it is used in type metal (70 per cent Pb, 20 per cent Sn, and 10 per cent Sb), since the expansion forces the metal tightly into the molds and gives sharp edges and corners to the type. It also imparts hardness to the type. It is used in the form of antimony black on the surface of plaster statues to give them a metallic appearance. Sulfides of antimony are used in paints and are extensively employed in vulcanizing rubber. 

Physiologically, bismuth is quite inert, and it is given to make different parts of the alimentary tract visible in X-ray work. 

Its principal use is in making alloys. Just what rôle it plays in alloys may be seen from the following experiment.

Arsenic, Antimony, Bismuth 431 Experiment 111 

OBJECT. To prepare the alloy known as Rose's metal.

Measure out 10 grams of bismuth and 5 grams each of tin and lead. Reduce each ingredient to as fine a condition as possible, and then mix the three together thoroughly. 

Heat the mixture in a crucible with a Bunsen flame at full heat, at the same time stirring the mixture with a wire nail. When it has all become molten and the mixture uniform, pour it out into a sand mold. 

When it has cooled, break off a piece and place it in boiling water. Result?

Bismuth melts at 268° C., lead at 327° C., and tin at 232° C. 

How does the melting point of the alloy compare with that of the respective metals?

Wood metal is another alloy of bismuth. It is made of the same components as Rose's metal, with the addition of cadmium. 

It has even a lower melting point (65° C.) than Rose's metal. 

This low melting point makes both alloys useful in making such articles as electric fuses, automatic fire alarms and sprinklers, and safety plugs in boilers.

Compounds of both antimony and bismuth, like those of arsenic, are proving effective as internal germicides. As yet, finding effective remedies of this kind is to a considerable extent a matter of chance, but among the compounds of the heavier members of the nitrogen family seems to be an unusually promising place to search for them.

UNIT FIVE A FURTHER STUDY OF SOLUTIONS 

Look over the experiments you have performed up to this time. In nearly all of them the reactions have taken place in solutions, and what we call general chemistry is mostly a study of aqueous solutions.

This fact alone is sufficient reason for a more extended discussion of solutions, and a second reason is the fact that the theories formulated and the conclusions reached concerning solutions have wide applications in sciences other than chemistry, especially in the biological sciences. In their study we shall include a discussion of chemical equilibrium and of mass action, because an understanding of these has come to us from investigations of solutions and because they are most easily presented in connection with the other subject matter of this unit. 433

SECTION ONE SOLUTIONS 

It has already been explained how in a saturated solution the molecules of the solute are not supposed to lie at rest, but to be going into and out of solution at an equal rate (page 24), and it is believed that in all solutions the molecules of both the solvent and the solute are in motion as are the molecules of a gas. 

Some easily performed experiments will show some of the facts on which this theory of solutions rests. 

Experiment 112 ОВЈЕСТ. 

OBJECT. To demonstrate osmotic pressure. 

(To be performed by the instructor) Make up a very concentrated solution of sugar in water. 

Prepare a thistle tube as shown in Figure 104. Over the end of the thistle tube tie securely a piece of parchment paper or pig's bladder. Then fill the thistle-tube bulb and an inch or so of the stem with the concentrated sugar solution. Adjust the thistle tube so that the top of the solution in it is just level with the surface of the water in the vessel, support it in this position, and allow to stand. Results?

Osmosis. The action of the solution in the thistle tube may be explained briefly, as follows: 

FIG. 104. 

The molecules of both the water and the sugar are in motion and are striking against the parchment. 

The latter is, however, differentially permeable: it is practically impervious to the sugar and keeps it within the bulb, while the water passes freely through it. More water passes in than passes out, however, because: 

1. On account of the presence of the sugar molecules inside, 434

Solutions 435 the water is diluted and fewer water molecules are in contact with a unit area of the membrane; and 

2. The sugar attracts and holds the water molecules within and prevents them from passing out as they otherwise would do. 

Stated more scientifically, the sugar lowers the vapor pressure of the solution and thus decreases the tendency of the molecules to diffuse from the sugar side.

For these reasons water enters the bulb more rapidly than it passes out, and in consequence the liquid rises in the bulb stem.

The diffusion of water through a differentially permeable membrane as in our experiment is called osmosis. A general law of diffusion is that the balance of movement is always from the place of greater to the place of less concentration. Hence, in osmosis the movement of the water is toward the side where it is diluted by the solute. 

One theory to account for the differential permeability of the membrane is as follows: The water molecules are more highly adsorbed by the membrane than are those of the sugar, and therefore the surface of the parchment becomes covered with a layer of water molecules. These dissolve, as it were, in the parchment and pass through to the other side, while the sugar is prevented from coming into contact with the membrane by the adsorbed layer of water molecules.

Osmotic pressure. The passage of the water into the bulb of course increases the volume of the solution, and if the solution is confined, it exerts pressure on the walls of the container. This pressure is called osmotic pressure. In our experiment the height of the column in the tube is a measure of the osmotic pressure developed. Theoretically, if the membrane were strong enough, the solution would expand until a pressure was produced that would equal the osmotic pressure that the solution is capable of exerting. In technical language:

Introductory Chemistry 

The hydrostatic pressure which is necessary to bring the solution into equilibrium with the pure solvent, when the two are separated by a differentially permeable membrane, is a measure of what is called the osmotic pressure of the solution.

State briefly how the osmotic pressure varies with concentration.

535 508 554 521 513 From the facts in the above table it is plain that the osmotic pressure varies directly with the concentration; that is, the greater the number of sugar molecules present, the greater the osmotic pressure.

Molar solutions. In chemistry the standard solutions used are the molar and the normal. A molar solution contains in I liter of the solution a number of grams of the solute equal to its molecular weight. For example, the molecular weight of sodium chloride is 58.5, and a molar solution of this salt has 58.5 grams of the sodium chloride dissolved in a liter of the solution. 

The molecular weight of HCl is 36.5, and a molar solution of

Solutions 437 

Charles W. Foulk

FIG. 105. Jacobus Henricus van't Hoff (1852-1911) and Wilhelm Friedrich Ostwald, two investigators of the properties of solutions. Van't Hoff possessed constructive imagination to a high degree and was regarded as perhaps the leading physical chemist of his generation. Ostwald has had a prominent part in clearing up the theories of physical chemistry and in advancing this science to the position which it now holds. this contains 36.5 grams of the acid in a liter of the solution. In like manner, 1 liter of a molar solution of sodium hydroxide contains 40 grams of sodium hydroxide. 

Since the molecular weight of a compound expressed in grams is known as the gram-molecular weight, it can be said that a molar solution contains one gram-molecular weight of the solute per liter of the solution. The same number of molecules of the solute are found in a given quantity of a molar solution, no matter what solute is used. 

Since a molar solution contains a given amount of the solute in 1 liter of the solution (not 1 liter of the solvent), it is made up by dissolving the required amount of the solute in less than a liter of water and then diluting this solution to a volume of 

I liter.

It should be noted that a liter of a molar solution of an acid which has one replaceable atom of hydrogen to the molecule (e.g., HCl, HNO3) contains 1 gram of hydrogen, while a liter of a molar solution of an acid like H2SO4 contains 2 grams of hydrogen. Likewise, a molar solution of an acid like H3PO4 contains 3 grams of hydrogen to the liter and has a corresponding capacity to neutralize a base. 

Similarly, molar solutions of bases and salts differ according to the number of hydroxyl groups or the number of metal atoms and acid radicals contained in the molecule. 

Normal solutions. In analytical chemistry, solutions of acids, bases, and salts of a known concentration are much used. The standard of concentration used in making up these solutions is that of the molar solution of an acid with one replaceable hydrogen atom, or of a base or a salt solution of equivalent concentration. Such a solution is called a normal solution. It may be defined as an acid solution which contains 1 gram of replaceable hydrogen per liter, or a base or salt solution of equivalent concentration.

The normal solution of HCl is the same as the molar solution, since each molecule of the HCl contains one atom of replaceable hydrogen. The molar solutions of H2SO4 and H3PO4 must be diluted to two and three times their respective volumes to give normal solutions, because the molecules contain two and three atoms of hydrogen. Similarly, the molar solutions of bases and salts that furnish more than one atom or radical of the distin-

Solutions 439 guishing substance from each molecule must be diluted to bring them to normal concentration. From the above it will be seen that all normal solutions, whether of acids, bases, or salts, have equal concentrations of the distinguishing substances in them.

How many grams of NaOH are there in a liter of it? 

Do molar solutions develop equal osmotic pressures? According to the kinetic theory of gases, a molecule of one gas is as effective as another in developing pressure—it is the number and not the kind of molecules that is significant. Are different kinds of molecules equally effective in developing osmotic pressure? This question can be answered experimentally by the use of molar solutions in all of which the molecular concentration is the same.

When the solutions are tested, it is found that alcohol and some other substances give the same pressure as the same concentration of sugar solution, but that acids, bases, and salts develop higher pressures than equal concentrations of nonelectrolytes.

The higher pressures produced by the electrolytes are explained on the assumption that a part of their molecules dissociate into ions and that each ion produces the same pressure effect as a whole molecule.

The boiling point of solutions of solids. It is easily demonstrated by experiment that the boiling point of a solution of a solid in water is higher than the boiling point of pure water. 

Experiment 115 

OBJECT. To find the effect of dissolved solids on the boiling point of aqueous solutions.

A. Put 10 cc. of water into a test tube and clamp the test tube in a vertical position upon the ring stand. Make a mark where the water comes in the test tube. Suspend a thermometer so that the bulb is just immersed in the water. It must not touch the side of

Solutions 443 the test tube. Using a small flame, boil the water and record the temperature at which it boils. A few small pieces of broken glass added to the liquid will cause it to boil more uniformly. 

B. Replace the water that has evaporated. Add 1 gram of sodium chloride, and heat again to boiling. When solution is complete, record the boiling temperature in the table. 

C. Repeat the work until 3 grams have been added, recording the boiling point after the addition of each gram and being careful to keep the volume constant.

How does the increasing concentration of NaCl affect the boiling point of the solution?

Freezing point of solutions. Experiments show that when a solid is dissolved in water, the solution does not freeze at oº, but at some temperature below this point. This explains why bodies of salt water do not freeze as easily as do those of fresh water. It also explains why salt is used in the freezing of ice cream. The added salt lowers the freezing point of the water; hence the ice is caused to melt. Each gram of ice that melts takes up 80 calories of heat, and this heat comes from the cream. 

The temperature of the cream is thus lowered until its freezing point is reached. 

Different effects of electrolytes and non-electrolytes on boiling and freezing points. Careful experiments have shown that solutions of different non-electrolytes of the same molar strength all have exactly the same effect in lowering the freezing point. 

A solution of 46 grams of alcohol, C₂H¿O (atomic weight, 12 X 2

Introductory Chemistry 

+6 X 1 + 16 X 1 = 46), in water has the same freezing point as a solution containing 342 grams of sugar, C12H22O11, at the same dilution.

On the other hand, a solution of an electrolyte (acid, base, or salt) of equal molar strength has a much greater effect than the non-electrolytes in lowering the freezing point. For example, 74.6 grams (gram-molecular weight) of potassium chloride lowers the freezing point twice as much as 342 grams (gram-molecular weight) of sugar. Similarly, solutions of all the electrolytes have lower freezing points than solutions of non-electrolytes at the same concentration. Correspondingly they raise the boiling point more than the same concentration of non-electrolytes. 

Freezing and boiling points and the theory of dissociation. 

The dissociation theory explains the different effects of electrolytes and non-electrolytes on boiling and freezing points. With non-electrolytes the effect is proportionate to the number of molecules in the solution. Electrolytes produce a greater effect for the same molecular concentration. This is explained by assuming that some of the molecules of the electrolyte dissociate into two or more ions and that each ion has the same effect on the boiling or the freezing point as a whole molecule. If we accept the theory as true, it is obvious that the amount of dissociation in a solution of known concentration can be determined by measuring its freezing and boiling points. It further becomes evident that in solutions, as in gases, each particle has the same effect irrespective of its weight. 

We thus see that experiments on the osmotic pressures and on the freezing and boiling points of solutions divide substances into the same two classes as did the experiment on the conductivity of solutions. Acids, bases, and salts are set off as a class differing from other substances. These facts were before the chemical world in 1888, when Arrhenius, a Swedish chemist, offered the dissociation theory in explanation of them. As this theory is so important, we shall in the next section examine it in more detail.

SECTION TWO THEORY OF IONIZATION 

THE gift which is known as constructive imagination is required for the formation of scientific hypotheses. It is the power to start with a series of facts. and then picture a way things might be to give an explanation of these facts. 

Many persons have imaginations that are active under unrestricted conditions. But the combination of fertile imagination, mental capacity, and persistent interest required to formulate a working scheme of things under definitely restricted conditions is rare indeed, and in the scientific world those in whom it is found are held in high regard. Dalton and Darwin are outstanding examples of scientists who had this power. 

Svante August Arrhenius possesses the gift of constructive imagination in a high degree. When he was twenty-eight years of age, he put forth a theory that accounts for certain facts about solutions so satisfactorily that it is generally accepted. 

Principal assumptions of Arrhenius' theory. The main as sumptions in Arrhenius' theory of electrolytic dissociation, or ionization, are the following: 

1. When acids, bases, and salts are dissolved in water, some of the molecules immediately dissociate into smaller parts. These parts are called ions (Greek: ion, going), and compounds which form them are called ionogens (ion formers). Only acids, bases, and salts are ionogens. 

An ion consists either of an atom or a group of atoms (a radical). 

When HCl dissociates, the hydrogen and chlorine atoms are the ions; when H2SO4 dissociates, two hydrogen ions and one ion which consists of the SO, radical are formed. 

2. Ions are charged with electricity. The ion or ions formed from one part of the molecule are charged with positive electricity, while those from the other part of the molecule are charged with negative electricity. An ion may therefore be defined as an 445

Introductory Chemistry atom or a radical charged with electricity. It would be well at this time to examine again Figures 20 and 21. 

The electrical charges on ions vary in strength, and the variation is by definite units. Thus an ion carries one, two, three, or four unit charges, according to whether the valence of the atom or radical is one, two, three, or four. A unit negative charge is equal in quantity to a unit positive charge. 

3. Ionization or dissociation is a reversible reaction; that is to say, the ions can come together again and form a molecule of the substance. In ordinary solutions only part of the molecules are ionized, but theoretically, if solutions were infinitely diluted, all the molecules would be ionized. 

According to the above assumptions, hydrogen chloride dissociates in water into hydrogen and chlorine ions, and its condition in solution may be expressed by the following equation:

HCI H+ + Cl− The dissociation is represented by the reversed arrows, because some of the hydrogen and chlorine ions are constantly recombining to form molecules of HCl while other molecules of HCl are dissociating. All ionization equations will hereafter be represented by reversed arrows.

Explanation of the electrical charges carried by ions. In most chemical reactions some atoms lose electrons and others gain them (page 67). For example, when hydrogen unites with chlorine there is an actual passage of an electron from the hydrogen atom to the chlorine atom. When by the ionization of 

HCl these atoms are separated, the chlorine keeps the electron which it had gained, and so has an excess negative charge, while the hydrogen ion is positively charged because it failed to regain its electron before separation from the chlorine. HCI H+ + Cl− Ions have physical and chemical properties quite different from corresponding atoms, and these properties are accounted

Theory of Ionization 

447 

FIG. 107. Sir Humphry Davy (1778-1829) and Michael Faraday (1791-1867), two of the earliest and greatest workers in electrochemistry. Davy advanced the idea that chemical forces are electrical in nature and introduced the method of electrolytic decomposition now so widely employed. The even greater Faraday built the first electric generator, and thus provided electric current for the chemists who followed him. The illustration shows them in the laboratory of the Royal Institution of Great Britain when Faraday was a young assistant to the older and already famous Sir Humphry. (From a lithograph in the Chemists' 

Club of New York.) for by their electrical charges. When, for example, sodium chloride dissociates in water, the sodium ions remain inactive in contact with the water, whereas sodium atoms and water immediately react when brought together. This shows that the chemical properties of substances are due to the number and arrangement of the outer electrons in the atoms and molecules. 

It should be noted also that when a molecule dissociates, the sum of the charges on the negative ion or ions is always equal to the sum of the charges on the positive ion or ions. For

Introductory Chemistry example, when HCl dissociates, the positive charge on the hydrogen is equal to the negative charge on the chlorine. When 

H2SO4 dissociates, the positive charges on the two hydrogen ions are equal to the negative charge on the SO4 ion. Because the negative and positive charges in a solution exactly equal each other, the whole solution is electrically neutral. 

Determination of hydrogen ion concentration. Often in medicine, agriculture, manufacturing processes, and laboratories it is important to know the hydrogen ion concentration of a solution. This may be determined by measuring its electrical conductivity. For determining the exact hydrogen ion content of weak solutions, a special instrument is used and the concentration is expressed in terms of what is called a pH scale. Neutrality is at the point where pH = 7. A pH lower than 7 indicates acidity, and one higher than 7 alkalinity. 

The advantages in the use of the pH scale will be better appreciated if it is remembered that in chemistry practically all the work in solutions and the discussion of them relates to aqueous solutions, and that since water ionizes to some extent, both hydroxyl and hydrogen ions are always present in these solutions, whether they are neutral, acid, or alkaline. Neutrality is the point where the H and OH ions are equal in number. In the direction of acidity the hydrogen ions outnumber the hydroxyls, and on the alkaline side the latter are more numerous. 

The pH 

scale provides for the expression of the hydrogen ion concentration not only in acid solutions where these ions outnumber the hydroxyls, but in neutral and alkaline solutions where they are relatively less numerous. In determining hydrogen ion concentration, we are in reality finding the extent to which these ions outnumber or are outnumbered by the OH ions. 

Indicators. Certain substances which change their colors under the influence of hydrogen and hydroxyl ions are used as "indicators" of hydrogen ion concentration. Litmus changes color when pH = 7, and phenolphthalein takes on or loses its

Theory of Ionization 

449 pink color when pH 8.7. Other indicators change colors at other points of concentration, and by their use the acidity or alkalinity of solutions within a certain range can be quite accurately determined.

Hydrogen content versus hydrogen ion concentration. At the beginning of Experiment 114 (page 440), only a part of the hydrogen of the acid was ionic, the balance being in the undissociated molecules. As the experiment proceeded, the hydrogen ions were removed by reaction with the base and others were supplied by the dissociation of molecules of the acid, until finally the entire hydrogen supply was exhausted. In this way we found the total hydrogen content of the acid. 

By hydrogen ion concentration is meant something quite different from the total hydrogen content. It is determined by the amount of hydrogen that is in the form of ions and hence is a measure of the activity, or strength, of the acid solution. It is evident that a dilute solution of an acid may have a high hydrogen ion concentration and at the same time a small total hydrogen content, while a more concentrated solution might have a lower hydrogen ion concentration and a much greater total hydrogen content. Weak acids (e.g., acetic) may have a high total hydrogen content and a very low hydrogen ion concentration.

What has just been said of acids applies to bases also. The supply of potential hydroxyl ions in the blood is spoken of as its "alkali reserve."

SECTION THREE MASS ACTION AND CHEMICAL EQUILIBRIUM 

THEORETICALLY all chemical reactions are reversible, and we have found many of them to be so. Nitrogen and oxygen unite to form NO, and the NO can be decomposed into nitrogen and oxygen. A dilute solution of an electrolyte ionizes, and when the solution is concentrated by evaporation, the ions again unite to form molecules. Ammonia and water form ammonium hydroxide, and when heat is applied to this, the reaction reverses and it separates into ammonia and water. 

NH3+ H2O NH4OH

During the study of this section the reversibility of chemical reactions should be kept in mind. 

Mass action. Two chemical substances, A and B, cannot react unless they come in contact, and the rate of the reaction depends on the number of molecules (using the term molecule to include all reacting chemical units) of A coming in contact with the molecules of B in each unit of time. Or, stating this fact differently, the more molecules are brought together in a unit of time the faster will be the reaction.

The number of contacts per unit of time may be increased by increasing the concentration of A or of B, or of both. If the concentration of A is doubled, the probabilities of molecules of A striking molecules of B is doubled and the rate of reaction is doubled. If the concentration of B is doubled or tripled, this likewise doubles or triples the rate of reaction. If the concentration of A is doubled and that of B tripled, the reaction rate is increased sixfold. The speed of a chemical action is proportional to the product of the molecular concentrations of the reacting substances. This is called the law of mass action. Heat, by increasing the speed of the molecules, increases the number of contacts 450

Mass Action and Chemical Equilibrium 

451 between reacting substances. It also "activates " the reacting substances, thus making the contacts more effective. 

Dynamic, or mobile equilibrium. Since the speed of a reaction depends upon the concentration of the reacting substances, it is evident that the speed of the reaction will slow down as the reaction proceeds; for the supply of molecules of the reacting substances is being constantly depleted. For example, if A and 

B react to form C and D, thus: 

A B C + D, the molecules of A and B are being gradually used up to form C and D. If C and D remain in contact, they begin to react to form A and B. Since the concentration of C and D is at first very small, the rate at which A and B are first formed is very small. We might represent this reversible reaction by arrows, whose lengths represent the respective speed of the reactions, thus: 

A+ BC + D

Since the speed of the reactions depends on the concentration of the reacting substances, the speed with which A and B reacted to form C and D would be decreased, while the speed with which C and D reacted to form A and B would be increased. Finally the reaction would be going as fast in one direction as in the other.

This condition represents a reversible reaction in a state of dynamic, or mobile, equilibrium. 

A+ BC + D

The arrows have the same length, denoting that the reaction is proceeding as fast in one direction as in the other. 

Criteria of equilibrium. A state of equilibrium is characterized as follows: 

1. At equilibrium there are two opposing processes in full operation and these processes are neutralizing each other.

Introductory Chemistry 

2. A change in conditions favors or retards one of the opposing activities and hence displaces the equilibrium to a new point.

This last statement will be better appreciated after further work.

Effect of concentration on equilibrium. It is interesting to show experimentally the effect of a change in concentration on equilibrium. 

Experiment 116 

OBJECT. To observe the effect of changing the concentration of one of the reacting substances. 

Into each of four test tubes put 5 cc. of ferric chloride solution, so dilute that only a faint color appears. Add 1 cc. of 0.5 N potassium sulfocyanate, K(CNS), to each test tube. Set one test tube aside for reference. Write the equation for the reaction: The color must be due to the formation of what compound? 

The addition of more of either one of the substances on the left side of the equation will increase the intensity of this color. 

To one test tube add saturated FeCl solution; to the other, add solid KCl. Results?

Has the equilibrium been shifted to the right or to the left? 

Which substance would you add to decrease the color? 

Using the third test tube, test the correctness of your answer.

Which way has the equilibrium been shifted in this case? 

This experiment shows how the point of equilibrium may be shifted by a change in concentration.

Ionization constant. The application of the equilibrium equation gives us a second method of determining the degree of ionization. 

Applying the equation in the case of a solution of HCl, we have or ionization constant. [H+][CI] [HCI] K, We know the total concentration of the solution, and if we now find experimentally the hydrogen ion concentration, we can determine all the values in the equation, including that of K. 

The chlorine ion concentration is equal to the hydrogen ion concentration, since the HCl gives equal numbers of each on ionization. 

The total concentration minus the hydrogen ion concentration gives the concentration of the undissociated HCl. 

This gives all the values on the right side of the equation, and the value of K is then easily obtained.

Effect of temperature on equilibrium. Increase of temperature would increase both the rate in which A and B reacted to form

Mass Action and Chemical Equilibrium 

455 C and D, and vice versa. Yet a higher temperature is generally more favorable to one of the opposing reactions than it is to the other. Hence increase of temperature shifts the point of equilibrium to the side less favored, which is the side of the reaction that absorbs heat. This leads to the law of van't Hoff, which is: Heating a system which is in equilibrium displaces the equilibrium in the direction that uses up heat. From this it follows that a reaction is made more nearly complete by heat when the reaction absorbs heat and less complete if it gives out heat. Van't 

Hoff's principle holds only if equilibrium is already established when the change of temperature is applied. 

In what direction does an increase of temperature shift each of the following equilibriums?

C + O2 N2 + O2 

CO2 + 97,000 calories 2NO - 43,200 calories Effect of pressure on equilibrium. If, after equilibrium has been established, the pressure is increased, the reaction will move faster in that direction which will decrease the pressure. For example, when an equilibrium has been established between nitrogen and hydrogen,

N₂+ 3H22NH3 N2 An increase of pressure would tend to push the equilibrium to the right. By so doing the pressure would be decreased, since the gases on the left occupy twice the volume they would on the right. 

Indicate whether you would increase or decrease the pressure in order to shift the equilibriums in the following equations to the right: 2H2 + O2 2H₂O (gas) N2 + O2 2NO 2C+02 2CO C + O2 CO2 N2O4 2NO2 CO2 + H2 CO + H,O

Introductory Chemistry 

Law of Le Chatelier. The ideas of mobile equilibrium are summed up in the principle of Le Chatelier: When a system in equilibrium is subjected to a change (e.g., a change of temperature, concentration, or pressure), there is a shift in the point of equilibrium which tends to restore the original condition, or to relieve the strain.

Van't Hoff's law is a special case under the more general law of Le Chatelier. 

At this point two facts should perhaps be pointed out. The first is that chemical reactions are electrical in nature and take place with all the speed of electricity. Hence, when materials are brought into contact, equilibria are established almost instantly. If you have used indicators in determining the acidity or alkalinity of solutions, this statement will be appreciated. 

The other fact is that most of our laboratory experiments show reactions that go to an end, rather than reversible reactions, because one product leaves the field of reaction. These reactions are chosen in large part because they do go to an end and leave visible end products, but it should not be forgotten that they are exceptional; that the usual reactions are the reversible ones that go on undiscovered by us in our reagent bottles and in nature all about us. Unthinkingly we regard reacting materials standing in dynamic equilibrium as being in a static condition, and do not consider that a reaction is occurring unless an end product appears.

Do these variations differ from those in the families already studied?

The members of the family have four valence electrons, and they gain or lose electrons in pairs. Hence they have valences of two or four.

Carbon may function either positively or negatively, as in the compounds CCl4 and CH4, but the tendency to gain electrons decreases as the atomic weights increase, and therefore we rarely find tin and lead exerting a negative valence. Since basic properties accompany a loss of electrons, we should expect to have the basic properties increase with an increase of atomic weight. 

This is in accord with facts. Carbon and silicon furnish weak acids, germanium and tin are amphoteric, while lead is basic. 457

SECTION ONE THE HYDROCARBONS 

COMPOUNDS of carbon and hydrogen are called hydrocarbons. 

Many hundreds of these are known, but the study of them is much simplified by the fact that they run in series in which the composition of each member differs from its neighbor by one atom of carbon and two of hydrogen (CH2). The two most important hydrocarbon series are the methane and the benzene, and our study will be devoted chiefly to these, with only a mention of some of the other series. Since the molecules of many of the carbon compounds we shall study are quite large and lie in three dimensions in space, it would be quite difficult for us to show their structure by building them up of cubical atoms as we did some of the simpler molecules. We shall, therefore, in this study indicate the structure of the molecules by simple diagrams in the plane of the page. 

Methane. Methane, CH4, is known as " marsh gas," because it is formed when organic matter decays. It is a product of the distillation of coal and wood and is given off in nature from coal and petroleum, forming the explosive "fire damp" of coal mines and a large part of natural gas. The following account by 

Thomas Paine of an experiment carried out over 140 years ago is of interest at this time: "In the fall of the year that New York was evacuated (1783), General Washington had his headquarters at Mrs. Berrian's, at Rocky Hill, in Jersey, and I was there. 

The Congress then sat at Princeton. We had several times been told that the river or creek that runs near the bottom of Rocky Hill, and over which there is a mill, might be set on fire, for that was the term the country people used, and as General Washington had a mind to try the experiment, General Lincoln, who was also there, undertook to make preparation for it against the next evening, Nov. 5th. This was to be done, as we were told, by disturbing the mud at the bottom of the river, and holding something in a blaze, as paper or straw, a little above the surface of the water. "A scow had been stationed in the mill-dam, and Gen. Washington, Gen. Lincoln, and myself, and I believe Col. Cob (for Col. Humphries was sick), and 3 or 4 soldiers with poles, were put on board the scow. Gen. Washington placed himself at one end of the scow, and I at the other. Each of us had a roll of cartridge paper, which 459

Introductory Chemistry we lighted and held over the water, about 2 or 3 inches from the surface, when the soldiers began disturbing the bottom of the river with the poles. "As Gen. Washington sat at one end of the scow, and I at the other, I could see better anything that might happen from his light, than I could from my own, over which I was nearly perpendicular. When the mud at the bottom was disturbed by the poles, the air bubbles rose fast, and I saw the fire take from Gen. Washington's light, and descend from thence to the surface of the water, in a similar manner as when a lighted candle is held so as to touch the smoke of a candle just blown out, the smoke will take fire, and the fire will descend and light up the candle. This was demonstrative evidence, that what was called setting the river on fire, was setting the inflammable air on fire that arose out of the mud."

Isomerism. There is only one methane, one ethane, and one propane, but there are two butanes, each with the formula C4H10. 

One of these boils at 1°, and the other at -17°. The first is called normal butane and the second isobutane. It is assumed that the difference in them is in the way the atoms are built together in the molecule, and the accepted formulas for the two compounds are:

FIG. 108. Ira Remsen, formerly professor of chemistry at Johns Hopkins University and president of that institution. He was noted as a writer of textbooks, as an investigator of the carbon compounds, and as a leader who made his university a center of chemical research. 

Substances of the same simple formula but differing in their properties are called isomers of each other. There are never more isomers of a compound than there are possible ways of arranging the atoms, which is evidence for the theory that the difference in isomers is in their graphic formulas. Isobutane is re garded as a propane in which one of the hydrogen atoms of the middle carbon is replaced by a methane group. Petroleum.

Petroleum comes from plant and animal remains imprisoned in the rocks. It is a mixture of members of the methane series, from methane up to compounds that have as many as sixty carbon atoms in the molecule. 

By fractional distillation petroleum is separated into the lighter liquids like petroleum ether, gasoline, naphtha, benzine, and kerosene; the heavier liquids which are used as lubricating oils; semi-solids like vaseline; and solids like paraffin and asphalt. 

Between kerosene and the lubricating oils is a middle fraction (gas oils) which is made into gasoline by " cracking." The cracking is done by bringing the oils to a high heat under pressure under which treatment the molecules break up into smaller ones. 

Methane is a gas, and it is this which comes off in gas wells and forces the petroleum out of gushers. 

Ethylene and acetylene. Ethylene, C2H4, and acetylene,

C2H2, are the first members of two homologous series that are

Ethylene has recently come into use as an anesthetic and is preferable to chloroform and ether because the patients recover more quickly and are less nauseated. It has the disadvantage of being highly explosive. 

Acetylene is formed from calcium carbide by the action of water (CaC₂+ 2H2O Ca(OH)2 + C₂H₂) and is employed for lighting and for the production of the oxy-acetylene flame. All the members of the ethylene and acetylene series have the unsaturated structure shown by these two substances. 

Benzene and the benzene series. The carbon compounds we have considered are straight chain compounds. The formula for

The Hydrocarbons 

465 benzene, C6H6, and its derivatives are best accounted for on the assumption that the six carbon atoms are placed in a ring, or closed chain. Hence they are called ring compounds, or closed chain compounds. The graphic formula for benzene is: H-C C-H H-C C-H The successive members of the series are built up by displacing the hydrogen atoms of the molecule and adding in their places methyl groups. If one of the hydrogen atoms is replaced by 

H—C—, toluene, СÅН¿(CH³), is formed, and the other members

of the series are formed by substituting additional methyl groups for other hydrogen atoms. The benzene ring itself is quite stable, and although more than one half of all known carbon compounds are benzene derivatives, the closed chain of six carbon atoms remains intact in all of them. The modifications are made by variations in the atoms and radicals attached to the ring, and also in the more complex compounds by the joining together of more than one ring.

Benzene is sometimes called benzol. It is obtained by the distillation of coal, and should not be confused with benzine, which is obtained from petroleum and is a near relative of kero-

Introductory Chemistry sene, naphtha, and gasolene in the methane series. Benzene is a colorless liquid with a density of 0.8784, and is a good solvent of fats and oils. Its chief use is as a starting substance from which other compounds are prepared.

SECTION TWO SOME HYDROCARBON DERIVATIVES 

• MANY kinds of atoms and radicals can displace the hydrogen in hydrocarbon molecules, or can be used in the first place instead of hydrogen in building molecules. In this way an almost infinite number of carbon compounds are formed, but all of them have as a molecular skeleton the chain or the ring of carbon atoms we have found in the hydrocarbons, and it is customary to think of them as derived from the hydrocarbons. Concrete examples will give the best understanding of the structure of these substances and of how they are formed. 

Halogen derivatives of the hydrocarbons. If methane is mixed with chlorine and exposed to diffuse light (direct sunlight causes an explosion with the formation of carbon and HCI), the chlorine displaces the hydrogen atoms of the methane molecule. The different products that can be formed are shown below. Cl Cl Cl H-C-CI H-C-Cl Cl-C-Cl 

Cl 

MONOCHLORO- METHANE 

OR 

METHYL CHLORIDE DICHLORO-METHANE TRICHLORO-METHANE TETRACHLORO- METHANE 

OR 

METHYLENE CHLORIDE 

OR OR 

CHLOROFORM 

CARBON

TETRACHLORIDE 

The other halogens, except iodine, react with methane, forming compounds analogous to the compounds of chlorine. 

The hydrogen atoms in other members of the methane series can likewise be displaced by atoms of the halogens. How many chlorine compounds could be formed with ethane? 

Important halogen derivatives of the methane series. Of the halogen derivatives of the methane series, the three most important will be briefly discussed. 467

Introductory Chemistry 

Chloroform. Chloroform, CHCl3, may be prepared by the direct chlorination of methane as indicated above, but it is usually manufactured by distilling acetone or alcohol with solution of bleaching powder to furnish the chlorine. It is a colorless liquid with a pleasant odor and taste, and a density of 1.498. It vaporizes easily, and its greatest uses are as a solvent and as an anesthetic. 

Carbon tetrachloride. Carbon tetrachloride is in appearance very much like chloroform. One of the best ways to prepare it is to pass dry chlorine mixed with the vapors of carbon disulfide over antimony pentasulfide in a heated tube. It has the high density of 1.58, but has a low boiling point. It is non-inflammable, which causes it to be used in fire extinguishers; and because it is a good solvent, we find it as one of the principal constituents of the commercial cleansing preparation called carbona. It is used in medicine as a vermifuge. 

Iodoform. Iodoform is CHI,. It is a yellowish, crystalline solid, with a pungent penetrating odor. Formerly it was used as a disinfectant and antiseptic, and hence the odor of iodoform was associated with hospitals. Much more powerful germicides have now come into use. 

Oxygen derivatives of the hydrocarbons. Oxygen enters into the hydrocarbon molecule in a number of different ways, and in so doing forms a number of groups of compounds. In synthetic chemistry the oxygen is sometimes inserted into the molecule by the attachment of radicals of which the oxygen is already a part, but since in all the reactions oxygen unites with the carbon atoms we may consider that in all of them the hydrocarbons are oxidized. The details of how all the different processes can be brought about are given in courses in organic chemistry. Here we shall concern ourselves briefly with the more important classes of compounds that are formed by these reactions. 

The alcohols. If methyl chloride is treated with sodium hydroxide, the reaction indicated below takes place.

H H-C-CI+ NaOH H-C-OH + NaCl CH3OH is methyl, or wood, alcohol. It has the OH group which is characteristic of a base, but alcohols do not release this group by ionization. Similarly an alcohol is formed from each of the other members of the methane series by the insertion of an OH. 

If one of the hydrogen atoms of benzene is displaced by an OH, phenol, or carbolic acid, is the product. This is like an alcohol in having one OH group attached to a carbon atom, although of course the remainder of the molecule is quite different. The most important members of the alcohol series are: 

1. Methyl alcohol. Methyl alcohol may be produced from methane. (See page 461 and above.) But the principal source of methyl alcohol is from the destructive distillation of wood; hence its commercial name "wood alcohol." Methyl alcohol has a low boiling point (64.6°). It burns with a smokeless flame, and hence is a convenient fuel for the production of small quantities of heat. Its largest use is perhaps as a solvent for paints and varnishes. It is poisonous and when taken internally produces blindness.

Methyl alcohol can be produced synthetically at a very low cost from hydrogen and carbon monoxide in the presence of a suitable catalyst. 

2. Ethyl alcohol. This is common (or grain) alcohol. Although theoretically it can be prepared from ethane, the most important method of producing ethyl alcohol is by the fermentation of certain sugars by yeast. Glucose, C6H12O6, is the sugar generally used in the fermentation. Zymase, one of the enzymes of the yeast plant, brings about the following reaction : C6H12O6 2C2H5OH + 2CO₂

Introductory Chemistry 

After the fermentation is complete, the fermented liquid is subjected to distillation and the ethyl alcohol is thus separated from the water.

Ethyl alcohol has many properties in common with methyl alcohol. It is the intoxicating constituent in alcoholic beverages. 

It is often "denatured" by adding wood alcohol or some other poisonous substance. 

3. Glycerine. The simple formula of glycerine is C3H¿(OH)3. 

Since it has three carbon atoms it may be derived from propane. 

Glycerine is a by-product of soap manufacture. It is hygroscopic (holds water and therefore does not dry up). Besides having numerous uses of its own, it is employed extensively in the manufacture of nitroglycerine. 

The aldehydes. If methyl alcohol vapor and air are driven through an electrically heated gauze woven of fine copper or platinum wire, the following reaction takes place :

2H-C-OH + O2 —— 2H-C-O + 2H₂O The product is formaldehyde, which is the simplest of the aldehydes. It is a gas with a sharp, stinging odor, and is used as a disinfectant and preservative. A 40 per cent solution of formaldehyde in water is called formalin. The characteristic atomic group in the aldehydes is -C=0. 

The group is univalent, and by uniting it with the radicals of the hydrocarbons of the paraffin series the higher aldehydes are formed. Acetaldehyde and propionic aldehyde are formed by the union of the methyl and ethyl radicals with the aldehyde group, and have the following formulas:

H H H-C- 

ACETALDEHYDE

PROPIONIC ALDEHYDE 

Organic acids. An acid is a compound that gives hydrogen ions in solution, and many of the oxygen derivatives of the hydrocarbons will do this. The characteristic atom group, or radical, in all of these is -C-O-H. This group yields hydrogen ions, which can be replaced by a metal. 

Acetic acid, CH3COOH, is one of the most common of the organic acids and one of the most valuable commercially. Its principal source is the distillation of wood, but it is formed by a species of bacteria that converts the ethyl alcohol of hard cider to vinegar. The equation for this reaction is:

C2H5OH + O2 CH3COOH + H₂O Acetic acid is prepared from acetylene and may be obtained by the oxidation of acetaldehyde. но но 

Acetic acid is used as a solvent, in dyeing, and in the manufacture of white lead by the Dutch process. It is a colorless liquid which solidifies at 16.7°. Commercial acetic acid is a 

30 per cent solution of the acid in water. "Glacial acetic acid " often contains not more than 1 per cent of water.

Some other organic acids. Oxalic acid gives the sour taste to rhubarb and is present in certain other plants. It consists of

Introductory Chemistry two COOH groups and hence has two hydrogen atoms that can be replaced. Write its graphic formula. 

Oxalic acid finds use as a mild reducing agent, and in the cleaning of hats and the removal of stains from clothing. It is also used in dyeing, tanning, and printing, and is an important reagent in the chemical laboratory. Even in very small amounts it is poisonous.

Malic, citric, and tartaric acid are found in apples, strawberries, gooseberries, oranges, and other fruits and give the sour taste to these fruits. Are they more abundant in the ripe or unripe fruits? Both tartaric acid and the salts of tartaric acid are found in grapes and other fruits. The acid potassium salt, KH(CH4O6), is known as cream of tartar. Butyric acid gives the undesirable flavor to rancid butter. 

Esters. We have seen that an alcohol contains an OH group. 

We might therefore expect that an alcohol would react with an acid to give a salt and water, just as NaOH or KOH would. This supposition is correct, the reaction taking place according to the following equations:

KOH + HNO3 C2H5OH + HNO3 

(Ethyl Alcohol) 

KNO3 + H2O →C2H5(NO3) + H₂O (Ethyl Nitrate) (1) (2) In reaction I above the metal and in reaction 2 the ethyl radical (C2H5) displace the hydrogen of the acid and the reaction results in the formation of a nitrate and water. Obviously in its mode of formation the ethyl nitrate is comparable to a salt, and such products of acids and the alcohols are often called ethereal salts. 

In chemistry they are more commonly referred to as esters. 

When an alcohol reacts with an acid, water and an ester are formed. 

If an organic acid is substituted for the inorganic acid, the reaction proceeds as indicated above but is slower. In the next experiment an ester will be prepared.

Experiment 118 

OBJECT. To prepare ethyl acetate, CH3COO(C2H5). 

Place 25 cc. of concentrated sulfuric acid in a 200 cc. dry distilling flask, add 25 cc. of absolute ethyl alcohol, and then add 30 cc. of glacial acetic acid.

Distill until about 5 cc. of the distillate have been obtained. Pour this back into the flask and redistill it, determining the temperature at which the greater part of it comes off. This may be done by passing a thermometer through the cork so that its bulb will be opposite the exit of the delivery tube. The temperature found will be the approximate boiling point of the ethyl acetate. Place the figure in the table below.

Complete the equation for the reaction. The sulfuric acid acts only as a catalytic agent, and need not be considered in the equation.
