It should be understood that, although esters are formed in the same manner as salts, they do not resemble salts in any way in their fundamental properties, since they do not ionize and do not conduct an electric current. Many esters are liquids; many of them are strongly odorous; and some of them volatilize easily. 

Many perfumes and flavors are esters or are mixtures of esters. 

Fats. Fats are the esters formed when glycerine reacts with long-chain organic acids. They are often referred to as the glycerol esters. The acids uniting with glycerine to form the fats most familiar to us are oleic, palmitic, and stearic. The fats

Introductory Chemistry that these form are called olein, palmitin, and stearin, or glyceryl oleate, palmitate, and stearate. These fat molecules are quite large and complex and to give an appreciation of this fact the following formula for stearin is given:

H-C- -O-C18H350 H-C-O-C18H350 H-C-O-C18H350 The fatty acids are attached to the glycerine molecule in place of the H of the OH group, and the difference in different fats lies in the composition of the radicals. Of the three fats we have mentioned, olein is a liquid and palmitin and stearin are solids. 

Most plant and animal fats as we know and use them are not a single chemical compound, but a mixture of several of them. 

Olive and cottonseed oil are chiefly olein and hence are liquid. 

Beef tallow contains all three of the fats mentioned above, but is a solid because it has a smaller proportion of olein. Coconut oil is chiefly an ester of lauric acid, and like olein this fat is a liquid. The fatty acids from which the liquid fats are formed are unsaturated (page 464), and in the presence of a catalyzer these acids will unite with hydrogen and become solid. "Crisco" is manufactured in this way from cottonseed oil. Two atoms of hydrogen are added to the large oil molecule and then the product becomes a solid at the temperature at which it is used. 

Saponification of fats. If a fat is treated with a strong alkali, such as sodium or potassium hydroxide, glycerine and a soap are formed. Sodium forms solid (hard) soaps and potassium semiliquid (soft) soaps. In the next experiment a sodium soap will be prepared.

Experiment 119 

OBJECT. To prepare hard soap. 

Place an evaporating dish on the wire gauze in readiness for heating. 

Then drop about a teaspoonful of beef tallow or about 10 cc. of cottonseed oil into the evaporating dish. Add 5 cc. of a concentrated solution of sodium hydroxide and an equal volume of alcohol. (The alcohol acts only as a solvent and is used merely to save time.) 

Gently heat the mixture, stirring constantly, until all the alcohol is evaporated and a pasty mass is left. Dissolve this in water, add salt, and let cool. The solid material which forms on the top is hard soap. The glycerine is in the liquid in the bottom of the dish. 

Potassium hydroxide gives soft soap.

The reaction that has taken place can be understood by reference to the formula for stearin on page 474. The fatty acid radicals are detached, and each is united with an atom of sodium. The OH groups of the hydroxide take the place of the acid radicals and complete the formation of the glycerine. Write the equation showing the reaction between stearin and sodium hydroxide. Chemically the soap which we have made is a mixture of sodium stearate, sodium oleate, and sodium palmitate. 

Acetone. Acetone is the simplest and most important of a large class of substances called the ketones. The characteristic atom group of the ketones is C=O. The simple formula of acetone is (CH3)2CO, and the graphic formula is нон d d d H-C- -C-C-H Acetone is a colorless liquid. It is present in normal urine in small amounts, and in diabetes the quantities of it are considerably increased. It is one of the products formed by the

Introductory Chemistry destructive distillation of wood and is secured by the distillation of calcium acetate.

Ca(CH3COO)2- (CH3)2CO + CaCO3 Acetone is used in the manufacture of chloroform, as a solvent for fats, and in the manufacture of varnishes and explosives. 

During the war large quantities of alcohol were converted to acetic acid and this in turn to calcium acetate, which was then distilled for acetone.

The ethers. An ether is composed of two radicals joined by an atom of oxygen. The graphic formulas for the ethers formed from the methyl and ethyl radicals are: H- -C- -0- -C-H H H H H H-C-C 

COCCH 

H H H H 

DIMETHYL ETHER DIETHYL ETHER 

These ethers are called dimethyl and diethyl because there are two radicals in the molecule. They are in reality oxides of the methane and ethane radicals, and are called also ethyl and methyl oxides. Ethers of the higher members of the methane series are also formed, and mixed ethers can be prepared by joining to the oxygen atom two radicals of different kinds. The ethers may be regarded as derivatives of water formed by the substitution of various radicals for the hydrogen of the water. 

Write the graphic formulas for methyl ethyl ether and for ethyl propyl ether.

The ether that is used as an anesthetic is diethyl ether. It is a liquid that volatilizes easily and is highly inflammable. It is much used as a solvent. Dimethyl ether is a gas. Note that it has in its molecule the same number of carbon, hydrogen, and oxygen molecules as ethyl alcohol. To what is the difference in their properties due? What are such compounds called?

Carbon compounds with sulfur. Sulfur belongs in the same family with oxygen and in its chemical conduct it resembles oxygen. It has been natural, therefore, for chemists to try to substitute sulfur for oxygen in carbon compounds, and in these attempts they have been highly successful both from a scientific and a practical point of view. It is easy to make sulfur compounds homologous to the ethers and alcohols, such as (C2H5)S and C₂H¿SH. The latter compounds are ill-smelling substances and are called "mercaptans" because they react readily with mercury compounds in the manner shown in the following equation : 2C₂H¿SH + HgO → (C₂H¿S) ₂Hg + H₂O Perhaps the most valuable practical results obtained by the substitution of sulfur for oxygen in carbon compounds has been in the manufacture of dyes. Many new dyes have been made in this way, and the variety of colors among the sulfur dyes is quite as great as among the oxygen dyes. 66

SECTION THREE SOME OTHER CARBON COMPOUNDS 

MORE than 200,000 carbon compounds are known, and it is manifest that in a text of this kind there must be whole groups or series of them that cannot even be mentioned. Just which of them should be selected for discussion in a brief study it is difficult to decide.

It would be interesting to spend all our available time on some one group, such as that of the dyes. Great strides have been made in this field within the short space of one decade. Before the World War the Americans made only a small percentage of their dyes, but now the tables are turned and America manufactures most of its dyes and imports only a small percentage. This was made possible by persistent and systematic research. Such research was very costly and undoubtedly would not have been undertaken had it not been for a group of far-sighted business men who supplied the capital. The work was accomplished only by the expenditure of two million dollars, or even more, per year. 

This research is still continuing, and it is difficult to anticipate the vast number of new compounds which may be discovered as a result of the work. The driving force in this field has been, and still is, the slogan, "Better dyes at less cost." 

In this closing section on carbon it will be possible only to mention the work accomplished in a few of these special research fields. But even a brief survey of the products obtained from this research work will help us to realize the economic importance of these products and their intimate association with our everyday affairs; and it will also help us to understand how the "creative chemist" goes about his work. 478

Cellulose. Cellulose is a complex carbohydrate polymer and has the same general formula as starch. In the plant it is used chiefly as a building material, being the principal constituent of the cell walls. Cellulose is a stable compound, but by treatment with acid it can be converted into glucose. Cotton fibers are practically pure cellulose. In wood the cellulose is mixed with a substance called lignin, which in chemical composition lies between the hydrocarbons and the carbohydrates. Paper is made from cellulose, and the lignin and resins must be extracted from wood pulp before it can be used in paper making. Filter paper is practically pure cellulose. 

The nitro compounds. If benzene is treated with nitric acid, the following reaction takes place :

C6H6 + HNO3 (Benzene) → C6H¿NO2 + H2O (Nitro-benzene) This process is called nitration, and by it the nitro group, NO2, is introduced into the benzene molecule in place of one of the hydrogen atoms. The graphic formula is: H-C C-H H-C C-H In similar ways it is possible to introduce the nitro group into other carbon compounds. When only one or two of these groups is inserted, the compounds are relatively stable, but by the use of concentrated reagents and higher temperatures a larger number

Introductory Chemistry of nitro groups may be attached to one molecule. These compounds are unstable and explosive. 

We have spoken of the nitro compounds as though the nitro group were introduced into the molecule of the other substance, but we might think of the large organic molecule as replacing the hydrogen of nitrous acid, HNO2. Viewed in this way, it becomes evident that the nitro compounds are nitrites and that they may be regarded as organic salts. 

Explosives. Old-fashioned black gunpowder is a mixture of potassium nitrate, charcoal, and sulfur. The structural formula of potassium nitrate we might write thus: K—O—N In the above chemical arrangement the nitrogen has lost its electrons to the oxygen has had the five electrons in its outer shell stripped off instead of being allowed to gain the three to complete the shell as it desires. Hence the arrangement is an unstable one, and one that can be broken up with great rapidity. 

In the explosion of the powder the nitrogen atoms unite with each other and the oxygen with the carbon and sulfur. In this manner a large quantity of gas is formed, which is greatly expanded by the heat of the reaction. The explosive effect of the powder is due to the pressure exerted by the gas. 

The higher explosives are made by nitrating complex carbon compounds, and they carry within their molecules all the materials needed to form the gas. Nitroglycerine and nitro-cellulose (guncotton) contain the nitrate group. Tri-nitro-toluene (T.N.T.) and picric acid contain the nitro group. Dynamite is nitroglycerine absorbed in sawdust, infusorial earth, or other absorbent material. Guncotton is nitrated cotton, and looks

much like ordinary cotton. It is dissolved in acetone or some other solvent into a semi-solid material and made into "grains" of smokeless powder. Cordite is guncotton dissolved in nitroglycerine, and has the explosive force of both the substances of which it is made. Picric acid is made by the nitration of phenol (carbolic acid). The manufacture of all these explosives requires nitric acid, which accounts for the great demand for nitrogen compounds in time of war. 

Some important cellulose products. Gentle nitration of cellulose causes it to take up some nitrate groups, but not enough to make it explosive. It is then called "soluble cellulose" or pyroxylin. This is dissolved in alcohol and ether to form collodion. It is prepared in other solvents for use as lacquers. Mixed with camphor and heated under pressure it becomes celluloid, from which almost countless articles are made among them toilet articles, photographic films, windows for automobiles, and cel-o-glass. If cellulose acetate instead of cellulose nitrate is used, the products are much less combustible. 

Cellulose acetate dissolved in acetone and other solvents is used as a lacquer which dries as a transparent waterproof film. 

Artificial leather is made by coating canvas with celluloid or with celluloid and a solution of cellulose acetate. By one process rayon is made by forcing collodion through fine holes into water, which dissolves out the ether and alcohol and leaves the pyroxylin in long threads, or the threads are exposed to warm air and the ether and alcohol are allowed to evaporate. The threads are then treated with ammonium or calcium sulfite to take out the nitrate groups. By other methods used in the manufacture of rayon, the cellulose is brought into solution in various reagents and after being formed into threads is by appropriate chemical treatment given the composition found in the finished product.

Other synthetic plastics. Formaldehyde acts on phenol and other members of the benzene series to form resinous products

Introductory Chemistry 

Edwin Levick

FIG. 109. Leo Hendrick Baekeland. He has worked in the fields of photochem istry, electrochemistry, and plastics, and is known to the public as the inventor of the first successful developing-out photographic paper, Velox, and of Bakelite. 

which can be molded into any desired form and then hardened. 

These substances are waterproof, are not attacked by acids or alkalis, are non-inflammable, and are non-conductors of electricity. The best known of these materials is "bakelite." Pipe stems, fountain pens, and many other common objects are made of bakelite, but its greatest field of usefulness is in the electrical

industry. Formaldehyde forms compounds somewhat similar to bakelite with protein substances (e.g., blood, milk casein, and soy-bean products) that are used for phonograph records, buttons, soles of shoes, and many other purposes. The Edison phonograph records are made of " condensite," which is a chlorinated naphthalene. Synthetic rubber is made by the polymerization of the liquid isoprene, CH,, but as yet the manufactured article has been unable to compete with the plant product in price. 

The amino compounds. If tin is treated with hydrochloric acid in the presence of nitro-benzene, the nascent hydrogen liberated will displace the oxygen of the nitro group. C6H5NO₂+6H — (Nitro-benzene)

CH¿NH2 + 2H2O (Aniline)

The NH2 group thus introduced into the molecule is called the amino group. Aniline, the product in the above reaction, is an oil from which many dyes are prepared. 

If the amino group is introduced into an organic acid (page 471), an amino acid is formed. For example, glycine, the simplest of the amino acids, is acetic acid with one hydrogen atom displaced by an amino group. Others of these acids are much more complex. 

Nineteen amino acids are known, and they are used as building stones in the formation of protein molecules. 

A given protein may yield on analysis only certain of the amino acids, and different proteins yield the different ones in different percentages. The protein molecule is not well understood, but it is believed to be a polymer of the anhydrides of amino acids and very complex. True proteins include a small percentage of sulfur, and some proteins (especially those of the brain) contain phosphorus also. The amino acids are crystalline substances that dissolve in water. 

Cyanogen and the cyanides. Carbon unites with nitrogen to form the cyanide radical, N=C—. This, as the formula indicates, has a valence of one.

Introductory Chemistry 

Cyanogen, C2N2, an extremely poisonous gas, is formed by the union of two cyanide radicals. Write its graphic formula. 

Hydrogen cyanide, HCN, is formed by the union of the hydrogen ion with the CN ion. This is a deadly gas which dissolves in water to form hydrocyanic, or prussic, acid. 

The metal cyanides are formed by the displacement of the hydrogen of HCN by a metal (e.g., Na, K, Ca, Hg). Like all the other CN compounds, they are intensely poisonous. They are used in metallurgy, and sodium cyanide has come into extensive use for the killing of insects on crop plants and for the extermination of burrowing animals. The sodium cyanide is dusted over the plants or a small amount placed in the burrow of the animal, and by its reaction with the moisture of the air or earth, HCN is evolved. No prolonged application is necessary, for the HCN is so deadly that a single inhalation is fatal. 

Phosgene. The war gas, phosgene, is COCl2. It can be made by exposing CO and Cl2 to sunlight, or by passing them through hot charcoal. We can think of phosgene as having the same graphic formula as carbonic acid, except for the substitution of Cl for each of the OH groups. H-O- C=O Cl-C-O 

CARBONIC ACID PHOSGENE 

Dyes. The dye industry is important nationally chiefly because in time of need it is easy to turn from the making of dyes to the making of explosives and other chemicals required in war. 

Modern dyes are made chiefly from coal tar and are derivatives

B 16 

Ewing Galloway 

FIG. 110. A view in a dye factory. of the benzene series. Aniline (page 483) is important as a base in their manufacture. Over five thousand dyes have been made, and nine hundred are in use. The formulas of many of them are quite complex, and sulfur and metals enter into the composition of many of them. Most of the dyes that were formerly secured from plants and animals can now be made more cheaply from coal tar.

Drugs. Throughout the history of medicine physicians have been testing the various chemicals that were available to them for their physiological and therapeutic effects. Some of the substances that they found to exert a great influence on the body are metals like mercury and arsenic. Most such substances are carbon compounds, and their effects are not due to the elements in them, but to the way in which the atoms are grouped and attached in the molecule. Thus the deadly hydrocyanic (prussic) acid is merely HCN. Strychnine, which is a powerful stimulant

Introductory Chemistry 

Kadel and Herbert FIG. 111. Testing the purity of a dye. 

as well as a strong poison, is C21H22N2O2. Morphine, which is likewise a poison but a narcotic instead of a stimulant, is C17H19NO3. Digitalis, the extracts of the ductless glands (in as far as they are known), and many other powerful drugs are also carbon compounds. Ether, chloroform, ethylene, and the alcohols are examples of such compounds that we have studied, and if we knew where to search, we should doubtless be able to find among the carbon compounds that have been formed, or might be formed, substances with practically any physiological effects desired. It is quite possible that some chemist of the future will prepare complete substitutes for sleep and rest. 

Among compounds with marked physiological effects, none have received more attention than those that are markedly toxic to microorganisms. Substances that can be used for disinfectants outside the body (e.g., formaldehyde, phenol) are comparatively easy to find, but those that can be used to kill germs

within the body are more difficult to discover. Such substances must be differential poisons -more poisonous to the microorganisms that are to be killed than to the cells of the host. 

Such substances do exist, and quinine, arsphenamine, mercurochrome, gentian violet, and hexyl-resorcinol are some of the substances that are now being used to combat germs that have established themselves within the body. Most of the substances now being used for this purpose are complex carbon compounds with a metal introduced into the molecule. 

No field of chemistry at the present time offers more fascinating opportunities for discoveries than this. Copper and silver solutions in concentrations of one part in 100 million will kill Spirogyra in one minute; sulfur and copper are included in sprays against mildews and rusts because they are particularly deadly to fungi; tetanus toxin in incredibly small doses will kill mammals but is harmless to the alligator; one drug may affect chiefly one tissue or organ of the body and another drug another tissue or organ; and we know that some chemicals in concentrations that the body can bear are destructive to certain germs. 

It is, therefore, not unreasonable to attempt the discovery of substances that will have special toxic effects against particular germs, and there is every reason to hope that new medicines of great value will soon be in our hands.

Carbon compounds formed in living organisms. The student is now in a better position to appreciate the statements made on page 249 as to the ultimate source of carbon compounds. It is possible for chemists to begin with carbon as an element, unite it with hydrogen, and build it on up into more highly organized compounds, but they do not do this. Instead of doing this they distill, or break up in other ways, such substances as coal, wood, bones, tar, and petroleum, and from them secure complex compounds of many different kinds. Then by modification of these compounds - polymerizations, detaching or adding atoms or radicals, substitutions, and similar changes — they produce the 

---

Introductory Chemistry 

compounds that they desire. The materials from which these base compounds are secured are called the " sources of carbon compounds, but in reality they are secondary sources. The real primary sources are the plants and the animals from which the wood, coal, bones, tar, and petroleum come. 

The original carbon chemists are the green plants, which take in carbon dioxide, water, and mineral compounds from the soil and transform them into the sugars, starches, celluloses, oils, gums, perfumes, coloring materials, proteins, and almost countless other substances. The most fundamental of the processes carried on by plants is photosynthesis, in which the power of the sunlight is used to lift the carbon to an energy level from which, as it descends, it furnishes the energy for the building of many compounds and for the carrying on of life. Animals have much less power of synthesis, but they derive from the plants single sugars, amino acids, and fatty acids and glycerine, and recombine these in various forms. The study of the compounds formed in living organisms and the chemical processes that go on in them is called physiological chemistry. 

It has been said that carbon compounds are so abundant because the carbon atom has four hands and desires that each of these hands be held. While with one hand it holds to another carbon atom, it stretches out its three other hands to any atoms or radicals that may be close by. By placing different kinds of atoms and radicals near the hands that reach out from carbon chains and rings, an almost infinite number of compounds can be formed.

SECTION FOUR SILICON 

SILICON, like carbon, has a valence of four. We have seen the similarity in composition of some of the silicon compounds to those of carbon (page 457), and many other analogous compounds are formed by the two elements. Like carbon atoms, those of silicon unite with each other, but no chains longer than six occur and the total number of known natural and artificial silicon compounds is less than a thousand. 

Silicon does not occur free like carbon, but in the combined form it is more than one hundred times as abundant as carbon. 

It forms about one-fourth of the earth's crust and, next to oxygen, is the most abundant of all the elements in the part of the earth that we know. Practically all rocks except limestone and dolomite are in part composed of silicon compounds, and the main substance of sand and sandstone is silicon dioxide, SiO2. 

Preparation. On page 233 we learned that carbon dioxide may be deoxidized by the metals high in the electromotive series. 

Since silicon is in the same family as carbon, silicon dioxide might be expected to be deoxidized in a similar manner.

In crystalline form it consists of slender black hexagonal needles, of such a hardness that they easily scratch glass. Its density is slightly greater than that of carbon and about one third that of iron. In time of war, hydrogen for filling balloons is made by the reaction of silicon with hot sodium hydroxide solution.

Silica. At 400° silicon combines with oxygen to form silicon dioxide, or silica. This is a very common substance. Sand is

FIG. 114. Quartz glass and its makers. This glass of so many remarkable properties is one of the long series of valuable new substances that the electric furnace is giving us. chiefly composed of it, sandstone is silica grains bound together with a cementing substance, and quartz is silica in fairly pure form. 

In crystalline form it is beautifully clear and is known as rock crystal. Smoky quartz, rose quartz, and amethyst are silica crystals colored by impurities. Agate, jaspar, onyx, opal, carnelian, and other precious and semi-precious stones are amorphous silica colored by impurities. The infusorial earth which is used for adsorption work (page 224), as well as for insulation and as a polishing powder, is composed of the siliceous shells of diatoms, which are very minute plants. Deposits of these shells, marking the sites of former lakes, are found in Virginia, California, Maryland, and other states. Because of their beauty and the extraordinary variety of forms among them, diatoms are favorite objects for microscopic study.

Silicon 495 Quartz glass. Silica has a very high melting point (1600°), is resistant to most chemical reagents, and has the lowest coefficient of expansion of any substance known. Because of these qualities glassware made from it can be substituted for platinum for many purposes in chemical laboratories and can be plunged red-hot into cold water without breaking. When melted, silicon remains a thick, viscous substance, and it is difficult to free it from air bubbles; but recently a method of manufacturing clear silica glass in the electric furnace has been perfected. Besides other unusual and valuable properties this glass has great transparency, and unlike ordinary glass permits the passage of ultra-violet waves. Because of this latter quality, it is used in the quartz mercury vapor lamps that are used in sterilizing water and in the treatment of rickets and surgical tuberculosis. In the children's ward of one of the most famous hospitals of the country a window of silica glass has been installed, and but for the expense of manufacture, ordinary glass would be in large part replaced by glass of this kind.

Chemical properties of silica. Physically, silica and CO2 are quite in contrast; but they correspond in composition, and in their chemical conduct there are many striking similarities.

Glass. Glass is manufactured by fusing silica with various other substances, according to the kind of glass made. Window glass, or bottle glass, is made from sand, a sodium compound, and limestone. In the manufacture of Bohemian glass, or hard glass, a potassium compound is substituted for the sodium. Flint glass is made from silica, a potassium compound, and lead oxide instead of the calcium carbonate. 

Pyrex glass is a sodium glass containing borax and alumina in addition to the ingredients of ordinary window glass, and

Silicon 499 borax is used in the manufacture of a number of other glasses made for special purposes.

The sodium and potassium compounds are usually used in the form of carbonates, but the sulfates and especially the nitrates may be used in case it is desirable to oxidize the ferrous compounds, which may be in the manufacturing materials as impurities, over to the less noticeable ferric compounds. The particles of silica must be of even size and not too large to prevent rapid reaction with the other material and not too fine to cause too rapid fusion and excessive foaming. The lead used in the manufacture of flint glass is in the form of red lead, or litharge. 

This gives to the glass a very high refractive power, besides giving it a greater density and brilliancy. As high as 50 per cent of lead is sometimes used.

There is no definite composition for glass. It consists of a complex mixture of silica and of silicates, according to the material used. The following equation might represent an approximation to the composition of ordinary window glass: . - Na2CO3 + CaCO3 + 6SiO2 →→→ 2CO2 + Na2O · CaO · 6SiO2 The furnaces for melting up the mixtures are of different types, but all are made from the very highest type of fire clay. A good form for use in making lead glass is known as the tank furnace, in which the raw materials are continuously fed in at one end and the molten glass removed from the other end. 

The required amounts of the various materials are carefully weighed out and thoroughly mixed. This mixture is shoveled into the furnace, and as soon as it melts down more is added until the furnace is full of the molten glass. This charge is at first opaque, and gas bubbles of carbon dioxide (largely) will be seen coming up through the charge. As the gas bubbles clear up, the glow becomes transparent, and then the dirt and other impurities which have collected on the top are taken off. 

In no manufacturing process is the purity of the material of

Introductory Chemistry 

APA I KO 

FIG. 117. Blowing radium tubes. A practical knowledge of the glass blower's art is sometimes of great assistance to the chemist in his work. more importance than in the making of glass, and especially of lead glass, for this must be free from all colors. Copper and iron are common impurities. The former in the lower oxide gives a red color and in the higher oxide a blue color, while the latter in a ferrous condition produces a green and in the ferric condition a yellow. Cobalt oxide gives a blue color, copper or gold oxide a rich red, chromium oxide a green, and when colored glass is desired these or other metal oxides are added in small amounts. 

Some color effects can be neutralized by adding a compound that produces a complementary color. For example, manganese dioxide (MnO2) in small quantities produces a pink which will kill the green color of the ferrous iron. 

The defects in glass may be listed as follows: (1) "Sooty

Silicon 501 glass," in which the glass contains small bubbles of unexpelled gas, caused as a rule by too low heat; (2) "Stony glass," which contains undissolved portions of the original materials from the furnace; (3) "Cordy or wavy glass," with threads or cords running through it; (4) "High color" glass, containing an excess of manganese; (5) "Low color" glass, with an excess of iron.

Plate glass is made by pouring the molten mass on a table and rolling it out to the desired thickness with a hot iron roller. The thickness is governed by the height of the flanges along the edges of the table which support the roller. 

Window glass is made by a skilled workman or a mechanical device blowing the glass into a large cylinder. The ends are then cut off, and the cylinder cut lengthwise and heated while it spreads out into a flat plate. 

Bottles are blown in molds of the desired shape and size. In one operation the entire bottle is made, with the exception of finishing up the neck. This has to be reheated and molded into the desired shape. The bottles are then annealed (slowly cooled) so that they will not be brittle. 

Cut glass is a glass containing lead which is blown or molded into the required shape. The glass is then etched or cut, according to the use to which it is to be put. In etching, all the parts which are not to be etched are covered with paraffin and the exposed design is treated with hydrofluoric acid until etched to the desired depth. In cutting glass, the design is first cut out with sand and an iron wheel. Then finer-grained stones are used, and it is finally polished on a wooden wheel with putty and pumice. In the manufacture of decorative glass, high-grade table ware, lamp bulbs, and optical glass, a glass containing lead is used.

SECTION FIVE TIN AND ITS COMPOUNDS 

Ar ordinary temperatures tin is a silvery-white, lustrous, crystalline metal. In nature it occurs chiefly as its oxide, SnO2, which is called "tinstone." Bolivia and the Malay States produce it in largest amounts. The East Indies, Siam, China, Cornwall, and Australia are less important sources. The amount produced in the United States is so small as to be negligible. 

Preparation. The metal can be obtained from the oxide by electronizing it with carbon. Complete the equation expressing this reaction:

SnO2 + Explain this reaction in terms of electrons.

Tin often contains iron as an impurity. Suggest a method by which they might be separated. Look up the melting points of the two elements.

Properties and uses. Tin does not react with the air at ordinary temperatures, nor with weak acids and alkalies. It reacts with hydrochloric acid to give hydrogen and stannous chloride, SnCl₂; with concentrated sulfuric acid to give stannous sulfate, water, and sulfur dioxide; while when treated with concentrated nitric acid it forms metastannic acid (H2SnO3) 5, nitric oxide, and water. The equations for the action of these three acids on the metal are: The fact that tin does not tarnish when exposed to air or to weak acids and alkalies causes it to be used for making " tin plate," which is manufactured by dipping sheets of iron in melted tin. This process is used in making "tin" cans, cups, pans, pipes, and other articles. 503

Introductory Chemistry 

Ewing Galloway

FIG. 118. A tin mine in the Federated Malay States. 

Tin is alloyed with lead to make solder and pewter (page 508), and bronze is a copper, lead, zinc, and tin alloy.

Compounds of Tin 

Tin has valences of two and four, and two sets of compounds, stannous and stannic, are formed. Some of the more important of these will be mentioned.

Stannous oxide and hydroxide. If stannous chloride is treated with sodium hydroxide, stannous oxide, SnO, results. If the chloride is treated with sodium carbonate, stannous hydroxide, Sn(OH)2, is formed, but this hydroxide is so unstable that it has not been isolated. The stannous hydroxide, like aluminum and zinc hydroxides, is amphoteric. Complete the equation for the

Tin and Its Compounds 

505 reaction of stannous hydroxide with hydrochloric acid and sodium hydroxide, respectively: + SnCl2 + Na2SnO2 (sodium stannite) Acids. It has just been seen in the preceding paragraph how stannous hydroxide, Sn(OH)2, reacts as if it were stannous acid, H2SnO2. When a stannic salt, such as stannic chloride, SnCl4, is treated with sodium hydroxide, a-stannic acid, H₂SnO3, results. 

One would expect H4SnO4 to be formed, and it is thought that it is formed and then immediately transformed to H2SnO3 by losing one molecule of water. If an excess of sodium hydroxide is used, sodium stannate, Na2SnO3, results. Stannic hydroxide will also dissolve in dilute mineral acids, and hence it acts similarly to the stannous hydroxide.

The reason we call H2SnO3 a-stannic acid is that there is another stannic acid, formed by a different reaction, which has different properties and is known as ẞ-stannic acid, or metastannic acid. The ẞ-stannic acid is formed by treating tin with concentrated nitric acid. The formula for this acid is written (H2SnO3) 5. It is almost insoluble in dilute mineral acids and an excess of sodium hydroxide, and therefore differs quite clearly from the a-stannic acid. Nevertheless it may be converted into sodium stannate, Na2SnO3, by fusing it with sodium hydroxide. 

The ẞ form of the acid is the more stable, and hence the a form gradually changes into the B. 

Chlorides. There are two chlorides, the stannous chloride, SnCl2, and the stannic chloride, SnCl4. The former is prepared by treating tin with hydrochloric acid. The equation for the reaction would be: + SnCl2 The crystals have two molecules of water, SnCl2 2H2O. This salt gives an acid reaction in water, due to hydrolysis, and

Introductory Chemistry because of this hydrolytic property, it is used as a mordant in dyeing. It is easily converted to the stannic chloride, and therefore it is often used as an electronizing agent. The following equations represent its action on mercuric salts: 2HgCl2 + SnCl2 →→→→ 2HgCl + SnCl4 2HgCl + SnCl2 →2Hg SnCl4 The stannic chloride may be produced by deëlectronizing the metal of the stannous chloride with chlorine, thus: 

SnCl2 + Cl2 SnCl4

If the anhydrous salt is desired, it may be made by passing chlorine over hot tin. Chlorine will act on tin more readily than it will on iron, and therefore this chemical reaction is utilized to recover the tin from "tin" cans and other tinned ware. The anhydrous form is a colorless liquid, while the pentahydrate, SnCl4 5H2O, is a solid. This is called oxymuriate of tin and is used in dyeing.

SECTION SIX LEAD AND ITS COMPOUNDS 

LEAD is a very widely distributed metal, contrasting sharply with tin in this respect. The United States dominates the world in the lead industry. Missouri, Idaho, Utah, and Oklahoma are the leading states in its production. Australia and 

Spain also produce large quantities. The total production of the world is more than a half-million tons each year. 

Lead does not occur in native form, but it can be extracted from its ores with comparative ease, and because of this fact it was known and used by the ancients. Various articles made of lead have been found in some of the oldest of the Egyptian tombs. The early Romans were familiar with it and used lead pipes extensively for conveying water. The principal lead ore is galena, which is the sulfide, PbS. The sulfate, PbSO4, and the carbonate, PbCO3, are also found. 

Lead is the cheapest of all the metals except iron. Chemically it is more active than its use for pipes, acid chambers, and coatings for roofs and fences would indicate; for the metal itself quickly tarnishes and it is the coating of oxide or carbonate that forms on it which gives to lead products their resistant qualities. It is the heaviest of the metals that are in common daily use, its specific gravity being 11.34 as compared with 2.7 for aluminum, 7.19 for zinc, 7.85 for iron, and 10.5 for silver.

On the other hand, it is lighter than mercury (sp. g., 13.595), gold (sp. g., 19.32), and platinum (sp. g., 21.37).

Preparation. When lead ore is roasted, part of it is converted into the oxide and part of it is oxidized to the sulfate. 

2PbS + 302 →2PbO2SO2 2PbS + 302 — → PbSO4 + SO2 Then a mixture of this lead oxide and sulfate, together with some raw sulfide, is heated in a blast furnace with limestone and coke and air are blown through it. 507

Introductory Chemistry 

In the reactions the oxygen and sulfur in the ore are removed by the formation of CO2 and SO2, and the lead left free. The calcium of the limestone unites with silica, which is often present in the lead ore, and forms calcium silicate, which is removed as slag. 

Gold, silver, copper, tin, antimony, arsenic, bismuth, and iron are often present in lead ores, and as the lead comes from the blast furnace it is far from pure. The lighter and more easily deelectronized elements are removed by heating the lead for several hours in open pots, when most of the impurities, except copper, silver, and gold, rise to the surface and are skimmed off. Silver can be removed by the addition of zinc (page 524). Copper and gold can be separated from lead by electrolysis. Since silver can likewise be recovered by this process and other impurities removed by it, much of the lead now produced is purified in this way. 

Properties and uses of lead. Lead is easily rolled into sheets and has small tensile strength. It melts at 327°, and just below this point it becomes plastic, and accordingly it is easily formed under pressure into pipes which are used in cable and plumbing work. It forms many alloys with such elements as tin, arsenic, and antimony. Tin and lead (half and half) form ordinary solder. 

Arsenic is used with lead to make shot, while type metal contains lead and antimony. Lead reacts with the gases of the air very slowly at ordinary temperatures, but after long exposure forms the suboxide Pb₂O, or basic carbonate, Pb(OH)2.2PbCO3. 

The latter is the "white lead " used in paints.

These compounds form a coating over metallic lead, protecting it against any further action of the air. Hard water forms a carbonate and sulfate over the lead, both of which are insoluble. 

Soft water with air in it forms Pb(OH)2, which is somewhat soluble. Hence soft drinking water flowing through lead pipes may result in lead poisoning. Hydrochloric acid reacts very slowly with lead, and lead is so resistant to sulfuric acid that it is used for making chambers where sulfuric acid is prepared.

Lead and Its Compounds

Nitric acid attacks lead, giving lead nitrate and oxides of nitrogen. 

Like carbon, lead has valences of two and four.

Lead oxides. The four valence electrons of lead may be removed in pairs, as is shown by the composition of the five oxides of lead.

The suboxide has a few uses. It is easily formed by gently heating lead oxalate. 2PbC204 Pb20 + 3CO2 + CO The monoxide PbO is formed by heating lead in a current of air. If the heating is continued below 545°, part of the lead loses four electrons and passes over into the dioxide, PbO2. If, however, the lead is heated at higher temperatures, the compound reverts to PbO. The chief use of lead dioxide is in making storage batteries.

Red lead, or minium, may be considered a lead salt of plumbic acid. Its formula would be written thus: Pb2(PbO4). 

One reason we believe that red lead is such a salt is because when nitric acid is allowed to react with red lead, plumbic acid is first formed. This breaks up at once, giving water and lead dioxide, thus:

Pb2PbO4 + 4HNO3 → 2Pb(NO3)2 + H₁PbO H₁PbO4 PbO2 + 2H2O Lead monoxide is used in the manufacture of lead salts, glass, and lead plaster. The dioxide is used as an oxidizing agent, while the red lead is used in paints and glass.

Lead compounds in paint. By double decomposition between a soluble lead salt and ammonium carbonate solution a finely divided precipitate of normal lead carbonate, PbCO3, is formed. 

This compound becomes basic upon long contact with water, forming 2PbCO3 Pb(OH)2, or white lead. The plastic white lead used as a pigment in the paint industry is this material ground with linseed oil.

White lead is produced also by the old Dutch process, in which metallic lead is subjected to the influence of carbon dioxide, steam, and acetic acid vapor for a prolonged period of time. 

An electrolytic process is now being used which has very great promise for the future production of white lead.

The storage battery. In a cell of the lead storage battery two sets of lead plates are immersed in sulfuric acid, Sp. Gr. 1.2.

In the lead oxide plate are atoms of lead that have lost four electrons, while in the other plate the lead atoms still have their full quota of electrons. Naturally the Pb¹+ lead has the strongest pull for electrons, and when the two plates are connected by a wire or other metallic conductor, a stream of electrons passes from the metallic lead plate to the lead oxide plate. The plate which loses electrons is the negative plate. The plate which receives electrons is the positive one.

To build up the energy in a run-down cell, or to charge it, a current is run into it in the reverse direction of the current produced by the cell - that is, electrons are pumped out of the lead oxide plate and back into the metallic lead plate. 

At the positive plate this deëlectronizes the lead in the PbSO of the sulfate coating from Pb++ to Pb++, which causes it to take on another sulfate radical and form lead disulfate, Pb (SO4)2. (PbSO4)2++ SO,²— - → Pb(SO4)2 This compound hydrolyzes with water, thus:

Pb(SO4)2 + 2H2O – PbO2 + 2H2SO4 The PbO2 is deposited on the plate, which tends to restore both the positive plate and the solution to their original conditions.

Introductory Chemistry 

The negative plate receives electrons in the charging. This changes the Pb++ lead of the lead sulfate coating into Pbº lead, which no longer holds the SO, radicals in combination. The SO ions thus released unite with hydrogen ions to form H2SO4, so at this plate also the reaction tends to restore both the plate and the solution to their original conditions. The amount of charge in the battery can be ascertained by measuring the density of the sulfuric acid solution with a hydrometer. 

An electric cell arranged as described above develops an electromotive force of about two volts, which is the difference in potential of Pbº and Pb+. A storage battery may consist of one of these cells or of any number of them connected together. 

Edison storage battery. The Edison storage cell is filled with potassium hydroxide. One plate is iron and the other nickelic oxide, Ni₂O3. The reaction of the cell is shown by the following equation:

Fe + 3H2O + Ni2O3 Discharge →Fe(OH)2 + 2Ni(OH)2 Charge

The Edison cell is lighter than the lead cell and less easily injured. It has a higher internal resistance than the lead cell, and an Edison battery is not suitable for use where (as in an automobile starter) a heavy current is needed for a short period of time.

UNIT SEVEN THE COPPER FAMILY 

THE Copper family consists of copper, silver, and gold. Each of these shows a valence of one, and in addition copper has a valence of two and gold a valence of three. These variations can be explained only as similar variations in other elements are explained, by the passing out and in of electrons of the valence layer. The symbols for the elements are derived from their Latin names, cuprum, argentum, and aurum.

Complete the table: 

CONSTANTS AND COMPOUNDS OF THE COPPER FAMILY COMPOUND ELEMENT SYMBOL ATOMIC WEIGHT DENSITY MELTING BOILING POINT POINT VALENCES 

Oxides Chlorides Copper Silver Gold 8.9 1083 2310 I, 2 10.5 

955 1955

19.3 1062 2536 

I, 3

Usually with increase of atomic weight there is a rise in the boiling and freezing points, but there is no regularity in the variation in these properties in this family. In the usual manner, as the atomic weights increase, the stability of the compounds decreases. Thus of Cu2O, Ag2O, and Au₂O the cuprous oxide is the most stable and the aurous oxide the least, and the hydroxides and chlorides rank in stability in the same order. 

The copper family is the family of high electrical conductivity. 

By the standard and in the units in which conductivity is measured, that of copper is 64, of silver 67, and of gold 45.5. The next highest metals are aluminum and chromium, with conductivities of 39 and 38.5. Nickel is 15, iron 10, platinum 9, tin 7.7, and cadmium 1.3. The above figures explain why copper is so extensively employed for electrical transmission. 515

Introductory Chemistry 

Chemically the family is rather inert, and all of its members are found in the native state. Because of this fact copper, silver, and gold were known to the peoples of ancient times, and the copper age followed the age of stone. A characteristic feature of the chemical conduct of the members of the family is their tendency to form large, complex negative ions (" complexes ") with ammonia and cyanogen. The cyanide process for the extraction of gold is based on the tendency of gold to react in this way.

SECTION ONE COPPER AND ITS COMPOUNDS 

THE world's annual production of copper is about one million tons. It is very widely distributed, but the chief sources of supply are Africa and the great mountain system that stretches from Alaska to Cape Horn. Of the world's total output the 

United States supplies about 70 per cent. After the United 

States, Chile, Japan, and Mexico rank as producers in the order named.

The best-known native mines for copper in the United States are in Michigan, near and along the southern shore of Lake Superior. These mines were even worked before the discovery of America by Columbus. Great quantities of native copper have been obtained from them. Some of the masses of ore have weighed as much as 500 tons. In spite of the continuous working of the mines they are still in operation. 

Occurrence. Copper occurs as the native metal and as oxides, sulfides and ferrisulfide, and as the basic carbonate. The sulfides are the most widely distributed, occurring often with either the free metal or the oxide. The form in which copper occurs is often distinctive of a locality, as is illustrated by its forms in two of the important copper fields of the United States.

EXERCISE 

Write equations to show how the oxide, hydroxide, and carbonate could have been formed.

The Lake Superior copper field is unique in that in it large nuggets of native copper (some of them weighing four hundred tons) sometimes occur. 517

Introductory Chemistry 

Metallurgy. The process used in the preparation of copper depends on the form in which it occurs. The following methods are commonly employed: 

1. Extraction of native copper. When the free metal occurs in rock, it is only necessary to grind and heat the mixture. Under this treatment the copper melts and drains off. If the copper is found in sand or gravel, the grinding can be dispensed with. 

2. Treatment of oxygen-bearing ores. Oxides and carbonates that are relatively free from sulfides are mixed with coke and limestone and then heated in a furnace. The copper is electronized to the free metal by the coke. The limestone unites with the non-volatile impurities to form a slag. 

CuO+C- Cu(OH)2CuCO3 + C — 3. Treatment of the sulfur ores. Freeing the copper from the sulfur ores is a somewhat more complicated procedure. In the 

Montana ore, which contains iron as well as sulfur, the process is as follows: 

First, the ore is roasted (but not melted), which changes the greater part of the sulfide into the oxide. After the roasting process is started no more fuel is needed, as the oxidation of the displaced sulfur gives sufficient heat. 

The ore is next heated in very large reverberatory furnaces (20 by 100 feet) lined with silica brick and floored with magnesite, MgCO3. Coke and limestone are mixed with it. In the heating, the copper in the ore is electronized to the free metal. Much of the iron and other impurities go into slag. The molten material drawn off from the bottom is called "matte" and is a mixture of free copper and cuprous and ferrous sulfides. It contains also any arsenic, silver, or gold that may have been in the ore.

The molten matte is now run into a converter with a siliceous (quartz and clay) lining and a blast of air forced through it.

Copper and Its Compounds 

519 

Ewing Galloway

FIG. 122. Copper bars ready for shipment from Calumet, Michigan. The copper age followed the age of stone, and with the increased use of electricity we are tending toward a copper age again.

Sulfur, iron, and other impurities are oxidized, the SO2 passing off and the iron going into the slag. The molten and somewhat impure copper (" blister copper ") is run off into molds and later refined. 

The flotation process of concentrating copper ores. Copper ores are concentrated by crushing them and mixing the crushed material with water which contains less than 1 per cent of some cheap oil. The mixture is stirred vigorously and allowed to flow into a large tank. The waste rock is more easily wet by the water, and the copper is more easily wet by the oil. Hence the rock stays with the water, while the copper floats with the oil. 

Ores as low in copper as 2 per cent are utilized by this method, which is one of the most important metallurgical processes introduced in recent years.

Introductory Chemistry 

Electrolytic refining of copper. Much copper is electrolytically purified. The principle of the method by which this is done can be understood from Figure 123. Thin sheets of pure copper and heavy slabs of impure copper from the smelter are placed in a copper sulfate bath made acid with sulfuric acid. The slabs are made the anode and the sheets of pure copper the cathode of an electric circuit and the current turned on, when the copper from the impure slabs passes over and deposits on the pure sheets. 

In this way a heavy slab of pure metal is built up at the cathode, while the impure slab at the anode disintegrates. Only a very slight potential difference is used (less than 0.5 volt). This voltage is not sufficient to carry over the zinc, lead, silver, gold, or other impurities. Therefore the zinc remains in solution and the noble metals drop to the bottom of the bath and form a "mud" on the bottom of the tank. 

Mere traces of some impurities greatly decrease the conductivity of copper, and when the metal is used for electrical transmission, it is electrolytically refined. For example, .03 per cent arsenic decreases the conductivity 14 per cent.

Properties and uses. 1. Because of its high electrical conductivity it is used in generators, motors, transmission lines, ocean cables, other electrical machinery and apparatus, and in almost countless other ways. 

2. Roofing, spouting, and laboratory ware are made of it, and ships' bottoms are covered with it because of its resistance to corrosion. This resistance is due in great part to the fact that when copper is exposed to water and air it reacts to form a thin, adherent layer of basic carbonate, which protects it against further corrosion. 

3. Because of its wearing qualities and its resistance to corrosion, copper is used in making the electrotypes and halftones that are used in printing. An electrotype is made by making a wax impression of a page from a temporary set of type. This wax impression is brushed with graphite to make it a conductor

The current is turned on and the deposition of the copper in the wax mold continued until a thin copper plate is formed. This is now removed from the wax and the back filled with lead to give it strength. The finished plate is the electrotype or electroplate used in the printing of a book. Recently experiments have been made in plating these electrotypes with chromium to protect them from corrosion and wear.

Copper forms two sets of compounds, cuprous and cupric. The cuprous ion, Cu+, is practically colorless. The cupric ion, Cu++, is an intense blue.

Cupric compounds. The principal cupric compounds are CuO, Cu(OH)2, CuSO4, Cu(NO3)2, CuS, and cupric ammonia and cyanide complexes. These compounds will now be studied.

SECTION TWO SILVER AND ITS COMPOUNDS 

SILVER, like the other noble metals, is found free in nature. It is also found in nature alloyed with copper, gold, platinum, and mercury, and in ores, such as argentite, Ag₂S, proustite, AgASS3, and horn silver, AgCl. Galena, or lead sulfide, PbS, often contains considerable quantities of silver sulfide, and copper ores often contain silver also. 

Silver is mined at scattered points all over the world, but the chief silver-producing mines are located in Mexico, the United States, Canada, and Australia. South America produces a considerable amount, and it has been mined in Europe for many centuries. The average yearly value of the silver produced in the United States is about $45,000,000. 

Preparation. Silver is prepared as a by-product in the electrolytic refining of copper (page 520) from the anode mud in the bottom of the tanks used in the process. Another important method used in the preparation of silver is known as the Parkes process. It is used where the silver is found in association with the lead ore, galena, PbS. The ore is smelted and the lead and silver recovered as an alloy, which is known as "base bullion."

This bullion is placed in large kettles and melted and is kept in this condition until most of the impurities are oxidized. These impurities rise to the surface like cream and are skimmed off. 

The zinc which rises and floats as a surface layer on the molten metal is added in such amounts that its ratio to the lead is about I to 100. The zinc is practically insoluble in the lead, but the silver is more soluble in the zinc than in the lead. Accordingly, as the zinc rises, it brings up most of the silver. The ratio of the concentration of silver in the two metals is the ratio of its respective solubility in each. 524

Silver and Its Compounds 

525 The zinc layer is now removed and reheated to a temperature sufficiently high to distill off the zinc, leaving the silver and a certain amount of lead behind. This residue is then heated in a cupellation furnace, which' oxidizes any lead to PbO. This is absorbed by the bone ash which lines the furnace, and the silver is thus separated from the lead.

If any gold is present in the original bullion, it will still remain with the silver. The two metals are separated by treating the mixture with sulfuric acid. Silver sulfate, which is soluble in water, is formed, while the gold is not affected by the acid and remains in metallic form. The silver is displaced from the silver sulfate with copper, and thus secured in the metallic form.

There are some other methods used in the production of silver. 

When it occurs in a native state, the amalgamation and cyanide processes are two that are extensively used (page 531).

Properties and uses. Silver is the whitest of all the metals and is capable of taking a high luster. Its high luster causes it to be used for coating mirrors. It is the best conductor of heat and electricity of any of the metals, but its cost makes its use very limited for such purposes. It is very malleable, and is so ductile that an ounce of it has been drawn into a wire a mile in length. It is very inert chemically, which makes it suitable for use as money and for tableware and ornaments. Silver coins in the United States contain about 10 per cent of copper, while those in England are only 7.5 per cent copper. Sterling silver has the same composition as the English silver coins. 

The dilute acids, except nitric, do not affect silver, and hot concentrated caustic alkalies have no effect on it. Therefore silver dishes are often used when working with caustic alkalies, since platinum cannot be used. Hydrogen sulfide forms a coating of silver sulfide over the metal, liberating hydrogen. Write the equation for the reaction:

Introductory Chemistry 

Halides of silver are the light-sensitive substances used in photography. Silver compounds are also employed medicinally. 

Compounds of Silver 

The most familiar and one of the most important compounds of silver is silver nitrate. pure silver in nitric acid. 3Ag + 4HNO3 - This salt is prepared by dissolving The equation for the reaction is: 3AgNO3 + NO + 2H2O The excess of acid is expelled by heat and the solution evaporated to the crystallizing point. Silver nitrate occurs in colorless, transparent crystals. These are very soluble in water, and the solution is neutral to litmus. When pure, the nitrate is not affected by light, but in the presence of some electronizing agent the silver is converted to the free metal, giving a black deposit. 

This is what causes the stains when silver nitrate gets on the skin.

Silver nitrate is used as an eye disinfectant and in the treatment of infected throats. When mixed with a little hydrochloric acid and fused, it is used as lunar caustic, or caustic pencils. 

It is a very common laboratory reagent for the detection and estimation of the halogens and as the starting point in the formation of other silver compounds.

Like the oxide, all other silver salts, except the iodide and the sulfide, are soluble in ammonium hydroxide, forming silver ammonia complexes. When the chloride dissolves in ammonium hydroxide, the following compound is formed: Ag(NH3)2Cl. 

The compound dissociates principally in this manner : Ag(NH3)2ClAg(NH3)2+ + Cl- The positive ion undergoes a secondary ionization and produces a small amount of Ag+ ions, but not enough to be precipitated by any reagent except a sulfide or an iodide. 

Photography. A photographic plate or film is made by coating a glass plate or a cellulose film with gelatin in which silver bromide is suspended. When such plates or films are treated with electronizing agents (called "developers" in photography) — "in

Introductory Chemistry as, for example, hydroquinone or pyrogallol- the silver of the AgBr is electronized and darkens the gelatin coating by settling in it as particles of metallic silver. Exposure of silver bromide to light causes it to react much more rapidly with the electronizing agent; so when a plate or film that has been exposed in a camera is treated with a developer, the parts that have been most affected by the light become the darkest. When the development of a plate has proceeded far enough to bring out to the best advantage the image on it, the electronizing process is stopped by removing the plate from the developer and immersing it in the "fixer," which is a solution of sodium thiosulfate. 

This fixes the image by dissolving out and removing any remaining silver bromide from the gelatin coating, while leaving the metallic silver, which gives the contrasting shades of light and dark. 

After the fixing process is complete, the sodium thiosulfate is removed by washing in water, and the plate or film is then dried. 

A plate or film thus prepared is called a negative," because it is dark where the object that was photographed was light and light where the object was dark.

Photographic prints are made by allowing the light to shine on photographic paper through the negative. These papers are of two kinds, "printing-out" papers and "developing" papers. 

The printing-out papers are coated with a thin gelatin film which contains silver chloride. The developing papers, like plates and films, are sensitized with silver bromide. 

The printing-out papers are much less sensitive to light than are the developing papers, and the image becomes visible as the light reaction (or "printing" process) goes on. When this has proceeded far enough, the print is "toned" by immersing it in a solution of sodium chloraurate, NaAuCl, or of potassium chlorplatinite, K2PtCl. In the toning the silver in the parts affected by the light replaces the gold or platinum, leaving the gold or platinum in a metallic condition on the paper. The gold gives a reddish tone and the platinum a dark effect to the print.

Silver and Its Compounds 

529 After toning, the prints are fixed, like plates, in sodium thiosulfate to remove the remaining silver chloride, and then washed and dried. Silver chloride papers are used by photographers in the making of "proofs," but these are not fixed or toned. 

Developing or bromide papers are much more sensitive to light than are the chloride papers, and, as on plates or films, the images on them are invisible until they have been developed in the same way as plates or films. After development they are toned and fixed by the process that has been described above.

SECTION THREE GOLD AND ITS COMPOUNDS 

GOLD in nature occurs frequently in association with copper, silver, and platinum. It is found in a finely divided condition in rocks (usually quartz) and, in the sand and gravel formed from the disintegration of these rocks, the particles of the metal are found as placer gold. A few large nuggets have been found, one from California weighing 190 pounds. Some gold exists free; most of it is alloyed with its associated metals; and small amounts occur as gold selenide, AuSe2, and telluride, AuTe₂ and (AuAg) Te2. 

It is found in minute amounts in rocks and soils everywhere, and each ton of sea water contains about .2 gram of gold. 

Preparation. There has been a great deal of work done in trying to produce gold from mercury. It is theoretically possible, but it is still an open question. The experiments which have been reported favorable have been shown to be in error. These experiments have resulted in producing some very delicate tests for gold. One experimenter suddenly found traces of gold in a material which he was using, and on investigation found that it had resulted from handling his gold spectacles before handling the material in question.

In placer mining the gold-bearing sand or gravel is simply washed in pans or in sluice boxes. The particles of gold settle in the bottom, and the lighter materials are washed away. 

This does not secure all the gold, and placer mining is profitable only where the metal is plentiful. In "hydraulic mining a stream of water from a high-pressure source is used to wash the gold-bearing sand and gravel into the sluice boxes. 99 Gold is separated from its ores by smelting, by the amalgamation process, and by the cyanide process. 

1. Smelting process. This process is used with gold-bearing ores that contain lead and silver. The method of separating the associated metals from each other has been described (page 524). 530

Gold and Its Compounds 

531 2. Amalgamation process. When gold occurs in rock in a finely divided state, the rock is crushed in stamp mills, and this finely divided material in the form of a slime is run over copper plates which are coated with mercury. The mercury amalgamates with the gold, while the non-metallic materials pass on. 

The amalgam is scraped from the copper plates and the mercury distilled off, leaving the free gold. 

3. Leaching, or cyanide process. In the cyanide process the gold-containing materials are left in contact with a dilute solution of potassium or sodium cyanide in the presence of atmospheric oxygen. In the United States sodium cyanide is exclusively used in the process. The gold reacts to form complex ions, from which it is displaced by zinc. The following set of equations give the principal reactions: 4Au + 302 NaCN+HOH Au2O3 + 6HCN 2NaOH+2Au(CN)3 2NaAu(CN)2 + Zn — → 2Аu2O3 →NaOH + HCN → 2Au(CN)3 + 3H₂O → 2NaAu(CN)2 + 2HCN + O2 → 2Au + Na₂Zn(CN)Ą The cyanide process was introduced in 1890 and has made possible the working of low-grade ores, thus greatly increasing the world's gold supply.

The value of the gold produced in the United States for 1925 was approximately $50,000,000, while for 1915 it was about $100,000,000. It is probable that the production will continue to decrease, unless new deposits of the metal or new processes of extraction are discovered.

Properties and uses. In accordance with its position in the electromotive series, gold is very inactive chemically. Its noncorrosive property, together with its rarity and beautiful lustrous yellow color, makes it much used in jewelry and ornaments and for money. Because of its softness it is not used for these purposes in a pure condition, but is alloyed with other metals like silver

Introductory Chemistry or copper. The purity of gold used is expressed in carats: 24 carats means 100 per cent pure; 22 carats means 22 parts of gold and 2 parts of other metals. The standard alloys recognized by law are 22, 18, 15, 12, and 9 carats gold. The coinage in the 

United States is 90 per cent gold and 10 per cent copper, while 

English coinage is 91.66 per cent gold and 8.33 per cent copper.

The latter is sometimes called "sterling gold." 

Gold is attacked chemically by powerful deëlectronizing agents. Reagents of this nature are selenic acid, “aqua regia,' fused hydroxides, and fused nitrates. The reaction with aqua regia is given by the following equation:

Au + [HNO3 + 3HC1] (aqua regia)

AuCl3 + NO + 2H2O AuCl3 + HCl →→→→→→ HAuCl The gold is transformed into chlorauric acid, HAuCl, where the gold is fixed in the complex ion, AuCl¯. 

Compounds of Gold 

Gold is capable of forming two series of compounds, as has already been mentioned (page 517) the aurous, with a valence of one, and the auric, with a valence of three. More attention has been given in the past to gold alloys than to its compounds. 

The organic compounds are receiving considerable attention, with the hope that they will prove useful in the treatment of infections, especially tuberculosis. The other usual compounds of gold are the hydroxides and oxides, halogen compounds, sulfides, and gold complexes. 

1. Hydroxides and oxides. When auric and aurous solutions are treated with a soluble hydroxide, auric hydroxide, Au(OH)3, and aurous hydroxide, Au(OH), are formed. On heating, these hydroxides go over into auric oxide, Au2O3, and aurous oxide, Au₂O. On further heating, these oxides are decomposed, giving the metallic gold.

Gold and Its Compounds 

533 2. Compounds with halogens. Gold combines with the halogens to form halides, as AuCl; and AuBrз. These halides combine directly with the halogen acids or their salts to form compounds in which the gold functions as a non-metal by going into the negative ion. Examples of these compounds are: HAuCl, HAUBг, NaAuCl, and NaAuBr4. These compounds, like the oxides, are decomposed by heat, metallic gold and the free halogen being released in the decomposition. As we have seen, chlorauric acid is formed when aqua regia acts on gold, and sodium chloraurate is used for toning in photography. 

3. Sulfides. Like the other members of the family, gold is precipitated from its solutions by hydrogen sulfide. It comes down as a mixture of aurous and auric sulfides, Au₂S and Au¿S3. 

These sulfides are soluble in solutions of alkaline sulfides (e.g., K2S and Na2S) in which complexes are formed. 

4. Complexes. Gold enters into complex negative ions, the · most important of which are aurocyanogen, Au(CN)2¯, and auricyanogen, Au(CN),¯. These ions have already been met with in the cyanides which were mentioned in connection with the preparation of gold (page 631). In gold plating a compound containing one of these complex ions is used as the plating solution.

The sodium salt, NaAu(CN)2, is generally used. A piece of gold is used for the anode, and the object to be plated is made the cathode. The salt undergoes two ionizations — a primary and a secondary the secondary ionization being slight as compared with the first.

NaAu (CN)2 Au(CN)2 Na++ Au(CN)2 (primary) Au++ 2(CN) (secondary) Aut€

Au⁰ The gold must not be driven on to the object too fast, or the coating laid down will not be smooth and tightly adherent. This

Introductory Chemistry 

FIG. 124. Diagram illustrating the method by which objects are plated with gold. A represents the anode and C the cathode. too rapid deposition is prevented by the low concentration of the gold ions in the solution, due to the slight secondary ionization of the gold complex.

The other most common complex ion of gold is formed when gold is treated with aqua regia, AuCl (page 532). As previously stated, this forms unstable halogen acids and salts. 

Detection of gold. Since gold does not enter into our analytical scheme, a brief discussion of the methods used in its detection will be given here. Its presence is determined by a fire-assaying process. A weighed quantity of the gold-bearing material is mixed with a relatively large quantity of lead and some borax. 

This mixture is heated in a forced-draft furnace, which oxidizes the impurities and causes them, along with most of the lead, to unite with the borax. A slag glass is thus formed, and a socalled "lead button," which contains the gold and any silver that may be present, is left behind. This is cupeled (treated by the Parkes process), to separate the gold and silver from the lead, and the gold is freed from the silver by the process already described (page 525).

Because iron pyrites, which has a yellow color, is often mistaken for gold, it has received the name of "fool's gold." It may be distinguished from gold by heating, when the iron pyrites is converted to iron oxide with an odor of burning sulfur, or if iron pyrites is hammered, it is found to be brittle and not malleable like gold.

Gold and Its Compounds 

535 

Gold alloys. Rhotanium and palau are two gold alloys that are very resistant chemically. Rhotanium is composed of gold, palladium, and a small percentage of rhodium. Palau consists of gold and palladium. These alloys are used for the manufacture of crucibles and other laboratory ware.

At ordinary temperature these elements show little tendency to deëlectronize when exposed to the air. At sufficiently high temperatures zinc and cadmium unite readily with oxygen, while mercury unites with oxygen very slowly and only through a certain range of temperature. It will be recalled how, in the preparation of oxygen, mercuric oxide decomposed at the higher temperature.

From the position of the members in the electromotive series, one would expect that cadmium and zinc would liberate hydrogen from dilute acids and that mercury would fail to do so. This is in accordance with facts. Mercury, however, does react with concentrated sulfuric and nitric acids, as will be seen when mercury is taken up in detail.

Zinc is decidedly amphoteric, and all the members of the family 537

Introductory Chemistry exhibit hydrolytic tendencies to a greater extent than is usual with elements which possess a positive valence of two. Their oxides and hydroxides are much more easily decomposed and less soluble than those of the alkaline earth metals, while their sulfates are more soluble. 

The analytical arrangement of the metals places cadmium and mercury in the next group. Hence only zinc will be discussed in detail in this section.

SECTION ONE ZINC AND ITS COMPOUNDS 

ZINC is too high in the electromotive series to be found free in nature. Its usual occurrence is in the form of the sulfide, ZnS; the oxide, ZnO; and the carbonate, ZnCO3. The discussion of the general methods employed in metallurgy (page 260) suggests at once how zinc can be freed from the elements with which it is combined.

Write the equations for the reaction or reactions in obtaining zinc from the ores named above: 

Look up the boiling point in the table on page 537 and suggest how the zinc would naturally separate itself from other materials after it has become electronized. 

Because pure zinc is much less active chemically, and hence is more durable than zinc which contains one or more other metals in it as impurities, much of the zinc used today is purified by electrolysis.

Properties and uses. The unique properties of zinc give it many uses. It acts in air at ordinary temperatures much like aluminum, by becoming coated with a transparent film of oxide which protects it from further deëlectronization. Because of this property it is used for roofing, gutters, sinks, and many other purposes, and for coating (" galvanizing ") iron and steel, which corrode when exposed to air. Within a definite range of tem- 539

Introductory Chemistry perature (100-150°) zinc can be rolled into sheets, and when allowed to cool to ordinary temperatures, it retains its softness and malleability. This property gives zinc a wide use in the form of sheet zinc. When melted and poured into water it forms thin, brittle flakes, which are known as "granulated" or 

66 mossy" zinc.

Reference has already been made to its power to release hydrogen from dilute acids. It should be said in passing that this does not take place readily unless some impurity, such as iron, is in the zinc. Sometimes some foreign substance, such as copper sulfate, is added to the acid solution in the preparation of zinc, in order to hasten the liberation of the hydrogen. Zinc liberates hydrogen from a strong hydroxide, such as NaOH, forming a zincate. 2NaOH + Zn Na₂ZnO2 + H2 This reaction is in accordance with the amphoteric nature of zinc. 

Compounds of zinc. The compounds of zinc which have proved of greatest practical value are zinc sulfate, zinc sulfide, zinc carbonate, zinc oxide, and zinc chloride. The first four of these compounds will be prepared in the next experiment.

Commercial zinc oxide is used extensively in the manufacture of white paint. It forms an excellent substitute for white lead in paints which are to be used where there are traces of hydrogen sulfide in the atmosphere, for the sulfide of zinc is white, and hence the paint is not discolored by this compound. Pure zinc oxide is used medicinally as an astringent and healing powder.

Zinc sulfide, mixed with barium sulfate, is also used as a paint pigment, under the name of "lithopone." The lithopone is prepared by precipitating the two insoluble salts together from a solution of barium sulfide and zinc sulfate.

Zinc chloride is a white crystalline powder, very deliquescent in the atmosphere and readily soluble in water. At a temperature exceeding 115° C. the salt volatilizes with partial decomposition. Its deliquescent and volatile nature is utilized in producing smoke screens. The shell which is to produce the smoke screen is filled with a mixture of hexachloroethane, zinc oxide, and zinc. 

This filling is ignited by means of an impact fuse and a suitable starting mixture, and the pressure produced by the gases throws off the cover of the shell. A dense smoke pours from the shell. for about ten minutes. These clouds produced by the reactions of the filling substances are essentially zinc chloride saturated with moisture.
