SECTION TWO MERCURY AND ITS COMPOUNDS 

MERCURY is found in Spain, Texas, California, Italy, and Austria. 

Small amounts of it are found free, but the chief source of mercury is a red mineral known as cinnabar, or mercuric sulfide (HgS). 

The ores seldom contain more than 1 per cent of the element. 

Mercury has the distinction of being the only metal that ordinarily exists in liquid form. 

Preparation. Most sulfides may be changed into oxides by heating in air, but mercuric oxide decomposes at 400-600°, and hence when cinnabar is roasted, sulfur dioxide and metallic mercury result. 

Write the equation for the reaction: 

FIG. 127. Apparatus for the refining of mercury.

The volatile products which include the mercury are led to a condensing chamber, where the mercury is obtained in the liquid form. 

Another method generally employed consists of roasting sulfide with lime and condensing the vapor of mercury which results. The following equation expresses the reaction: 2HgS+ 2CaO 2CaS+ 2Hg + O2 

Mercury is purified by distillation in a partial vacuum or by filtering it under pressure through chamois skin and allowing it 544

to drop through a column of dilute nitric acid, followed by a washing with distilled water. Frequently both of these processes are resorted to with the same sample of mercury if the metal is desired in an exceptionally pure state. 

Properties and uses. Mercury is the only metal which is liquid at ordinary temperatures, although cæsium and gallium melt at summer temperatures (page 227). When pure it is silvery white and does not oxidize in the atmosphere until the temperature reaches about 300°. For its boiling point, freezing point, and density, see the table on page 537. The low freezing point and low vapor pressure of mercury and its non-adhesive nature toward solids make this liquid indispensable in the preparation of thermometers, barometers, and other scientific instruments of measurement. When mercury vapor is heated to a sufficiently high temperature, it is a conductor of electricity, and hence it finds use in vapor lamps. 

Mercury is either univalent or divalent and forms mercurous and mercuric compounds. It readily reacts with concentrated nitric or sulfuric acid, although hydrochloric acid has little or no effect upon it. With other metals it easily forms amalgams, which have been previously discussed. The halogens and sulfur combine directly with it, forming salts of mercury. 

Mercury and its salts are finding increased use in medicine (pages 547-550). 

A great many vapor density determinations have shown that mercury vapor is monatomic, and hence the metal in its uncombined state is indicated by the symbol Hg. The other members of the zinc family also have low enough boiling points so that it has been possible to determine their molecular weights by the vapor-density method. They also are monatomic. 

On account of the high boiling points of most of the metals, vapor-density determinations are quite impossible. The molecules of these metals are supposed to be monatomic, but that they are monatomic has not been definitely proved.

Introductory Chemistry 

Isotopes of mercury. Mercury exists in six isotopic forms, with atomic weights of 198, 199, 200, 201, 202, and 204. These isotopes have been separated by Harkins and his students at the University of Chicago by the repeated fractional distillation of mercury. Very careful density determinations have been made, indicating beyond a doubt the existence of these isotopes. 

Compounds of mercury. The halides of univalent mercury are insoluble in water and are decomposed by light. The hydroxide Hg(OH)2 is unstable and breaks up at once into the corresponding oxide, HgO. The hydroxide Hg(OH) is unknown, but the corresponding oxide, Hg2O, can be prepared from the nitrate (page 549). These oxides are feeble bases. The reaction of mercury with ammonia is very characteristic, ammono-basic mercury compounds, such as HgNH2Cl, being formed (page 550).

The sublimation takes place in an apparatus so designed that the sublimed HgCl is collected in a large chamber. The mercurous chloride prepared by sublimation is not so finely divided as that prepared by precipitation, and because of this fact is less active medicinally. In medicine it is known as calomel.

Organic compounds of mercury. Elementary mercury and the inorganic compounds of mercury have long been used by mankind as remedial agents. The last three decades have witnessed the effort of the scientist to introduce mercury into organic molecules, in order to obtain compounds that will be less toxic to human protoplasm and yet retain the toxicity of the mercuric inorganic compounds for microorganisms pathogenic for man. 

The results of these efforts have been most encouraging. Welldefined methods by which mercury may be introduced into organic molecules have been worked out, and although hundreds of these organic mercury compounds are of no medicinal merit, some of them are proving highly useful in the treatment of certain infections. Of these new compounds, mercurochrome, derived from an organic dye, is best known.

Mercuric cyanide, Hg(CN)2, is so slightly ionized that the presence of the mercury ion cannot be shown by any reagent except hydrogen sulfide. Mercuric sulfide is the nearest insoluble and the least ionized of any mercury salt.

UNIT NINE ALUMINUM, MAGNESIUM, AND COMPOUNDS OF IRON 

THESE three elements belong in different chemical families, and it is only for convenience that they are grouped in this study. 

By reference to the Periodic Table it is seen that the family to which aluminum belongs consists of boron, aluminum, gallium, iridium, and thallium. The first of these elements is best known to us in its compound, boric acid. The last three are metals which are practically unknown outside the chemical laboratory. 

Aluminum, which is the most abundant metal in the earth's crust and the third most abundant of all the elements, is the important member of the family and the only one we shall study. 

It is replacing both steel and copper for certain uses and becomes increasingly more important in industrial life. 

Magnesium belongs with calcium in the alkaline earth family. 

As an element it does not have extensive uses, but asbestos is a magnesium compound and magnesium alloys of aluminum and of iron are very important. More than 2 per cent of the earth's crust consists of magnesium, so the supply of it is inexhaustible. 

Like all the other alkaline earths, magnesium has two electrons in the valence shell and hence has a positive valence of two. 

Like the other members of its family it is silvery white in color. 

It burns in the air with a brilliant white flame which causes it to be used in flashlight photography.

The metallurgy of iron, its uses, and a few of its compounds have been discussed in Part One, but because of the very great importance of this element the chemistry of it is here given in more detail. 553

SECTION ONE ALUMINUM 

ALUMINUM is not found free in nature, but in compounds it occurs in practically all rocks except limestones and sandstones. 

It is one of the constituents of the silicates in clay and in the feldspars, which are the most abundant of all the materials of the earth's crust. The feldspars are double silicates containing aluminum and sodium, or calcium, or potassium (e. g., KAlSiĝOg). 

In Greenland large deposits of cryolite, Na,AlF6, occur. 

The supply of aluminum in silicate form is inexhaustible, but no low-cost method of extracting it from these ores has been found. Hence, the present commercial source of aluminum is the oxide, Al2O3 H2O, deposits of which are found in a number of our Southern states. Ruby and sapphire are crystalline aluminum oxide colored with impurities. The valence of aluminum is three and the formula for its oxide is Al2O3. 

Preparation. Aluminum is singular in its production in that it is the only common metal whose ores must be carefully purified by a refining treatment in order to produce the metal in a pure condition. Furthermore, great care must be used in the purification of all materials used in the process. The entire process is one of careful chemical control. This is in great contrast to the earlier production of iron, copper, gold, silver, and lead by the rule-of-thumb process. It might be said that the process of purification of aluminum is a production of modern science. 

Oersted, a Danish chemist and physicist, produced aluminum as early as 1825, as a chemical curiosity. Wöhler and Deville were successful in obtaining it by the reduction of aluminum fluoride and cryolite with metallic sodium. Castner made an attempt to cheapen the method of preparation by working out a process for the production of sodium. He was successful (page 554

Aluminum 555 274), and until the year 1886 all the aluminum used was prepared by the decomposition of aluminum chloride with metallic -Aluminum oxide dissolved in fused cryolite 

Tron Carbon

Aluminum 

FIG. 128. Diagram of apparatus for preparation of aluminum. 

sodium. The following equation represents the reaction: 

AlCl3 +3Na 3NaCl + Al What other metals could be used to displace aluminum in this reaction?

The use of metallic sodium in the process made aluminum very expensive and consequently limited its use, but in 1886 Charles Martin Hall, an American, and Paul Heroult, a Frenchman, independently discovered a less expensive method of preparing it. 

The process depends upon the electrolyzing of aluminum oxide in molten cryolite (AlF3.3NaF).

The electrolysis is carried out in a carbon-lined cell. This carbon lining acts as a cathode, and a series of carbon bars is used as the anode. The cryolite, because of its poor conductivity, transforms a sufficient quantity of electrical energy into heat to raise the temperature to 1000° and cause it to melt. Aluminum oxide is dissolved in the molten fluoride and is decomposed into aluminum and oxygen. The oxygen escapes or combines with the carbon of the anode, forming carbon monoxide. The molten aluminum, because of its greater density, sinks to the bottom of the cell, from which it is drawn off at intervals. In the process the cryolite is not decomposed.

Introductory Chemistry 

Properties.and uses. The position of aluminum in the electromotive series indicates a high order of activity, and it might be expected to oxidize with great rapidity when exposed to oxygen or to air. This actually happens, but the oxide forms a thin, transparent coating over the surface, which effectively protects the metal and stops the action. This property of the oxide, together with the fact that it is not affected by weak acids, makes aluminum especially useful for the manufacture of cooking utensils and paint. Weight for weight, aluminum conducts heat and electricity better than copper and is being used to replace copper in some kinds of electrical work. Because of their resistance to corrosion and their combined lightness and strength, aluminum alloys are extensively used. In steel making, aluminum is employed in large quantities because it unites with the gases in the molten metal and thus frees it from air bubbles. Aluminum is attacked, however, by hydrochloric acid. Write the equation for the reaction: It is also attacked by the hydroxides of the alkali metals. Could an aluminum kettle be used for making soap?

The thermite melting process. The high position of aluminum in the electromotive series means that it does not hold its valence electrons very strongly, and this in turn means that it can displace the metals below it from their oxides with a great release of energy. For example, when powdered metallic aluminum is heated with ferric oxide in an open crucible, a violent reaction takes place, according to the following equation : 

2Al + Fe2O3 Al2O3 + 2Fe + 185,000 cal. At this white heat the iron is melted and protected from oxidation by a liquid slag of aluminum oxide. 

A mixture of powdered aluminum and a metal oxide is called thermite, and melting a metal in this way is called the thermite process.

Aluminum 557 

Aluminum Company of America 

FIG. 129. Aluminum is usually thought of as a material used in the manufacture of kitchenware, but it has many other uses. The illustration shows some articles of furniture made of it.

A method of welding iron castings is based on this reaction. 

The broken casting is placed in a mold of clay and sand, and the edges to be welded are raised to red heat with a blast or blow torch. Then white-hot iron, melted by the thermite process, is run from the bottom of the crucible into the space between the pieces of broken casting. This melts the edges of the casting and welds it perfectly upon cooling.

The more expensive metals are prepared by the electronization of the metal of their oxides with aluminum, as indicated above (page 556). The metal in this way can be obtained free from carbon, an impurity difficult to remove when the reduction is carried out with this element. Manganese, molybdenum, and chromium are among the metals that have been prepared by this process.

Compounds of aluminum. Some of the important compounds of aluminum will now be prepared and studied.

Electronegativity of aluminum. A metal has been defined as an element which has three or less valence electrons. In this classification aluminum is in the amphoteric zone and functions as either a metallic or non-metallic element, according to its environment (page 390). This behavior is in sharp contrast to the alkali and alkaline earth metals, which exhibit only basic properties.

Its position in the electromotive series shows that it possesses greater electronegativity than the alkali or alkaline earth metals, but, like the strongly basic metals, it can be used to electronize many useful metals that stand below it in the series. In its resistance to weak acids and in being attacked by alkalies, aluminum shows properties in striking contrast to those of the metals above it in the electromotive series.

SECTION TWO MAGNESIUM AND ITS COMPOUNDS 

MAGNESIUM Occurs in nature in the form of magnesite, which is the native carbonate of the metal. This magnesite, when mixed with wood fiber, is frequently used as magnesite stucco for exterior decorating. Dolomite, which is a double carbonate of magnesium and calcium, composes large mountains in some sections of our country. In Stassfurt, Germany, kieserite, which is magnesium sulfate, is found in great abundance. Talc and asbestos are native forms of the silicates of magnesium. 

Preparation and properties. Formerly, metallic magnesium was prepared almost entirely by the decomposition of magnesium chloride with metallic sodium, according to the following equation: 

MgCl2 + 2Na 2NaCl + Mg 

Explain this reaction from the electronic standpoint. 

Today magnesium is prepared by electrolyzing fused anhydrous carnallite. This is a double salt of magnesium and potassium chloride, having with its water of hydration present the formula MgCl2 KC1·6H₂O. This mixed compound has a lower melting point than the pure magnesium chloride. The voltage employed is sufficient to decompose the magnesium compound with the deposition of metallic magnesium, but not sufficient to decompose the potassium chloride present. A modification of the Hall method employed in the preparation of aluminum (page 555) is now used in the manufacture of metallic magnesium. This consists essentially of dissolving magnesium oxide in molten cryolite, which is a double fluoride of sodium and aluminum, and decomposing the magnesium oxide by the passage of an electric current. 562

Magnesium and Its Compounds 

563 Metallic magnesium is silvery white and very soft. It quickly tarnishes in moist air with the formation of the oxide, which shows its tendency to lose its two valence electrons. It decomposes hot water with the liberation of hydrogen and burns in the air with a brilliant luminosity, the light possessing a large proportion of ultra-violet rays. The residue which remains after magnesium is burned in air is a mixture of magnesium nitride and magnesium oxide.

Magnesium dissolves readily in most dilute acids. A very pure metallic magnesium, when exposed to oxygen, becomes coated with a layer of magnesium oxide and remains protected quite well by the newly formed film of oxide, so that further deelectronization (oxidation) does not occur. This has led manufacturers to use magnesium in making metallic implements in which lightness is highly desirable. An alloy of aluminum and magnesium, known as magnalium," has been prepared and sold commercially on account of its combination of lightness and high tensile strength. 66 The compounds of magnesium. Some of the more important compounds of this metal will now be prepared and studied.

This compound, magnesium sulfate or "Epsom salt,” has been known as one of the household laxatives for a period of two centuries. Magnesium silicates. There are many silicates of magnesium.

The most common are asbestos, talc (soapstone), and meerschaum.

Magnesium and Its Compounds 

565 Asbestos is a soft, fibrous material which is a non-conductor of heat and electricity. Because of these properties and because of its non-inflammability, it is used to cover boilers, furnaces, and water and steam pipes, and as an insulating covering for electric wires. Talc is used in making sinks and tops for laboratory tables where acids are used. When finely powdered, purified, and perfumed, talc is used as a talcum powder. Meerschaum is used for making pipe bowls and articles of like nature.

SECTION THREE COMPOUNDS OF IRON 

In our former study of iron we have seen that this element has two valences and forms two sets of compounds, ferrous and ferric. 

This fact must be kept in mind through our present study. ""

The sulfates and sulfides of iron. To many persons these are the most familiar iron salts. "Fool's gold" and " copperas are the two that are generally known. 

Ferrous sulfide, FeS, is found in nature as such or is prepared by heating iron and sulfur mixed in weights proportional to their atomic weights. Direct combination between the iron and sulfur takes place with the formation of a black brittle mass of ferrous sulfide. The formation of this compound results from the removal of two electrons from the iron atoms and their appropriation by the atoms of sulfur. Write the electronic equation for the reaction:

Ferrous disulfide, FeS2, is abundant in nature. It is called iron pyrites, or simply pyrite. It is referred to also as "fool's gold," because of its superficial resemblance to gold. Ferrous disulfide is used as a source of sulfur in the manufacture of S sulfuric acid. Its probable structural formula is Fe S

Ferric sulfide, Fe2S3, is comparatively rare in nature. It might be supposed that it could be formed by treating a solution of a ferric salt (e.g., FeCl3) with hydrogen sulfide, but when this is done the iron is reduced to the ferrous condition with the precipitation of free sulfur. 

2FeCl3 + H2S 

2FeCl2 + 2HCl + S 566

Compounds of Iron 

567 Ferric sulfide can be made, however, by passing hydrogen sulfide over ferric oxide at 100°.

Fe2O3 + 3H2S — ✈ Fe2S3 + 3H2O The water is driven out of the field of reaction by the heat, and the process goes to completion. 

Ferrous sulfate, FeSO4 7H2O, is prepared commercially by roasting the native ferrous sulfide in the air, when it is oxidized to ferrous sulfate. The mass is then extracted with hot water and evaporated to the crystallizing point. Ferrous sulfate crystallizes in bluish-green prisms with seven molecules of water of hydration. In the laboratory this salt is conveniently obtained by dissolving iron in dilute sulfuric acid and evaporating the resulting solution. It is efflorescent in air, and for this reason the green crystals are frequently coated with a white powder; i.e., the anhydrous salt. The salt is readily soluble in water, however, and almost completely insoluble in alcohol. Ferrous sulfate is commonly known as greenstone, green vitriol, and copperas. It is used in tanning, in the manufacture of dyes and inks, and in the clarification of water. 

When a solution of one part of ammonium sulfate and two parts of crystallized ferrous sulfate are mixed and concentrated, a double salt crystallizes from the solution, having the composition 

(NH4)2SO4∙FeSO4·6H2O. This is ammonium ferrous sulfate and is the only ferrous salt that does not oxidize readily to the ferric state in the air.

Ferric sulfate, Fe2(SO4)3, may be prepared from the ferrous salt by oxidation with nitric acid, as described under the preparation of ferric chloride.

Ferrous and ferric iodide. In the presence of water, iron and iodine react and form ferrous iodide. The salt is secured by evaporating the resulting solution to the crystallizing point. Ferrous iodide is prone to oxidize very quickly. Ferric iodide,

Introductory Chemistry however, immediately decomposes into ferrous iodide and free iodine.

The hydroxides and oxides of iron. The hydroxides and oxides of iron include ferrous hydroxide and ferric hydroxide; ferrous oxide and ferric oxide; and magnetic oxide, which is a complex compound formed by the union of ferrous and ferric oxides.

Compounds of Iron 

569 Ferric oxide is a weaker base than the lower oxide and is the source of trivalent iron salts. Rouge and Venetian Red are finely divided ferric oxide. The latter makes one of the most durable of all paints.

Magnetic oxide, Fe3O4, is composed of ferrous and ferric oxides, FeO Fe2O3, and is sometimes called magnetite, or lodestone, because of its marked magnetic properties. It has a metallic luster and iron-black color. It may be produced artificially by the combustion of iron in an atmosphere of oxygen. 

Blueprints. One of the most interesting facts connected with iron is its valence change, and the making of blueprints is a process that is possible because of this valence change. When certain organic ferric salts are exposed to the sunlight, the iron is electronized from the ferric to the ferrous condition. Ferric oxalate gives the following reaction: 

Fe2(C2O4)3 + light 2 FeC2O4 + 2CO2 If paper which is coated with ferric oxalate and potassium ferricyanide be exposed to the sun, it turns blue, due to a reaction. between the ferrous oxalate and the potassium ferricyanide. 3FeC2O4 + 2K3[Fe(CN)6] → Fe3[Fe(CN)6]2 + 3K2C2O4 Indicate the electronic change during the process. 

The ferrous ferricyanide which is formed is blue in color and is called Turnbull's blue. It is insoluble in water, and if a blueprint paper that has been exposed under a photographic negative or blueprint tracing is washed in water, the oxalate and potassium ferricyanide will dissolve out, leaving the paper white except where it has been exposed to light. In these parts it is blue, due to the deposition of the ferrous ferricyanide (Turnbull's blue). In making blueprint paper commercially, sodium ferric oxalate, NaFe(C2O4)2, or ammonium ferric oxalate is used.

UNIT TEN COLLOIDAL CHEMISTRY 

THUS far we have studied the chemistry of molecules, ions, and atoms; but there is a branch of the science which deals with larger particles — particles which are aggregates of molecules and are intermediate in size between the molecules and ions of solutions and the larger particles of suspensions which settle out on standing (page 22). This science is much less advanced than molecular chemistry and is called colloidal chemistry (Greek: kolla, glue). 

Some substances (e.g., glue, gum arabic, starch, gelatin) under conditions ordinarily occurring form colloids, and these were formerly called colloids in contrast to the crystalloids which form true solutions. Now chemists are able to form colloids from almost any substance (see page 580), and they speak of the colloidal and crystalloidal state, rather than of colloidal and crystalloidal substances. A substance is in the colloidal state when it is dispersed in particles larger than molecules but too fine to settle out of the dispersion medium. 

Some terms used in colloidal chemistry. In a colloid the substance which is dispersed is called the dispersed phase or internal phase. The medium in which the particles are dispersed is called the external phase, or the dispersion medium. An apparently liquid colloid is called a sol. A jelly-like one is called a gel. An emulsoid is a colloidal dispersion of a liquid in a liquid, although the term "emulsion" is often used interchangeably with "emulsoid." A suspensoid is a colloidal dispersion of a solid in a liquid. Emulsoids and suspensoids differ from emulsions and suspensions in that the particles in them are finer. 

Classification of colloids. According to whether the dispersed phase and the dispersion medium are gaseous, liquid, or solid, colloids may be classified as follows: 571

Introductory Chemistry 

1. Dispersion medium a gas, while dispersed phase is: a. A liquid. Examples: Cloud, fog, mist. 

b. A solid. Examples: Dust, smoke. 

All gases are soluble in each other, and hence there are no colloids with both phases gaseous. 

2. Dispersion medium a liquid, while the dispersed phase is: a. A gas. Examples: Foam, whipped cream. 

b. A liquid. Examples: Emulsions (or emulsoids), such as mayonnaise, and the milky juice of plants. c. A solid. Examples: Colloidal suspensions, such as mucilage, glue, oildag. 

3. Dispersion medium a solid, while the dispersed phase is: a. A gas. Examples: Bubbles in solids, as in bread and porous lava. b. A liquid. Examples: Liquid inclusions in minerals (e.g., water droplets in sulfur crystals); cheese, which is liquid butter fat dispersed in solid casein. c. A solid. Examples: Solid mixtures, such as alloys, cement, porcelain.

Size of colloid particles. Microscopists measure the diameters of the objects which they study in microns, a micron being .001 of a millimeter. A millimicron is .oor of a micron, or one millionth of a millimeter. The abbreviation for micron is μ and for millimicron, μμ. The following diagram gives the size of colloidal particles measured in these units: 

Approximate colloid field

Ο.Ι μμ Ι μμ 1 το μμ 100 μμ Ιμ 1 το μ 100 μ 

I mm.

Ultramicroscopic field Particles can be seen only by the light reflected from their surfaces.

Particles settle out but slightly on standing. 

Particles are in rapid vibration. 

Microscopic field

Real form of particles may be seen. 

Particles settle out on standing. 

Particles have but slight motion.

Colloidal Chemistry 573 If a colloidal dispersion is placed on ordinary filter paper, particles up to about o.1 μ pass through. This is about the smallest particle that can be seen with a microscope, and we may regard a filter paper as a rough instrument for separating colloidal particles from those of larger size. It should be understood that in a given colloid the particles are not all of one size. 

Characteristic properties of colloids. The following properties are characteristic of colloidal dispersions: 

1. In liquid colloidal dispersions the freezing and boiling points are practically the same as those of the dispersion medium. In our study of solutions we found that the more concentrated a solution, the lower the freezing point and the higher the boiling point. 

In colloids these effects are not found. Does this indicate that in a colloid the molecules are separate or in aggregates? 

2. Colloids diffuse slowly and pass through membranes with difficulty. In one of our experiments we shall see how colloids can be separated from crystalloids on the basis of this property. 

A colloidal dispersion in a liquid makes luminous the pathway of a beam of light through it. This will also be demonstrated by experiment. 3. 

4. Colloidal particles have a constant and rapid irregular dancing movement. This topic will be discussed in a later paragraph. 

Other general facts concerning colloids will be given as our study of them proceeds.

SECTION ONE THE PREPARATION OF COLLOIDS 

SUBSTANCES can be brought into the colloidal state, either by causing molecules to unite until a particle of colloidal size is built up, or by reducing it from the mass into sufficiently fine particles. The two methods are known as the condensation and dispersion methods, but both condensation and dispersion can be brought about in several different ways. 

DISPERSION METHODS 

The chief dispersion methods are (1) grinding, or mechanical disintegration, (2) peptization, and (3) emulsification. We shall now prepare colloids in each of these ways. 

Mechanical disintegration. If brittle materials are finely powdered, some of the particles will be of colloidal dimensions. 

Experiment 130 OBJECT. To prepare a colloid by grinding. 

Grind up dry clay or limestone and divide it into three differentsized particles by the use of 50-mesh (to inch), 100-mesh, and 200mesh sieves. Place about one-half gram of the material in each of the three respective test tubes. Fill each test tube one-half full of distilled water, and shake. Let stand; note time it takes each to settle, and record results in the table below.

574

In the manufacture of cement and of clay products, the material is finely pulverized to bring it into the colloidal state, and in pharmacy many of the preparations are made by grinding the dispersed substance to particles of colloidal size. 

Peptization. The father of colloidal chemistry was Thomas 

Graham, who was born in Glasgow in 1805 and died in London in 1869. He found that if boiled white of egg were put in water with pepsin and a trace of hydrochloric acid, the egg white was dispersed in particles of colloidal size. Since this is the process which takes place in the digestion of proteins, Graham called it peptonization." This term has been shortened by colloidal chemists to "peptization." 66

OBJECT. To prepare a colloid by peptization in the presence of an electrolyte.

A. Into each of three beakers of distilled water drop a small piece of coagulated egg white. To one beaker add pepsin only; to the second, a trace of hydrochloric acid only; and to the third, both pepsin and acid. Note, and record below, the reaction in each tube.

Many precipitates like ferric hydroxide may be peptized by the use of some electrolyte; for example, cobalt and nickel sulfide are easily peptized by hydrogen sulfide, a fact that has to be kept in mind in qualitative analysis. 

Experiment 132 

OBJECT. To prepare colloids by peptization without an electrolyte. 

A. Make up in hot water a 1 per cent solution of gelatin and a 

2 per cent solution of agar, and allow each to cool. These are known as gels, or, in common language, "jellies."

B. Heat 5 grams of Ivory soap in 20 cc. of water until colloidal dispersion takes place. Then allow it to cool. Result? 

What has happened to the water? 

C. To 100 cc. of water add 62 grams sugar, 5 grams tartaric acid, and I gram pectin. Heat to 105° and cool. This is ordinary jelly except for the flavor, which may be added just before cooling. 

D. Pour 1 cc. of a sodium silicate solution (Sp. Gr. 1.10) into an equal volume of N acetic acid. Allow to stand until definite change is noted. Result?

If this silica gel is allowed to stand for some time, it gradually loses water and becomes more and more like SiO2 (page 496). 

The above experiments show examples of substances that are peptized by water itself without the addition of any other substance. Glue, gum arabic, and dextrin are other substances that are peptized by water. Some substances which do not form colloids in water are peptized in other media. For example, guncotton, which is unaffected by water, is peptized by amyl acetate or a mixture of alcohol and ether. This shows that in the formation of colloids the dispersion medium is as important as the substance dispersed.

Emulsification. An emulsion is a liquid dispersed in another

liquid. As hydrochloric acid is necessary for the peptization of egg, so a third substance, called an "emulsifying agent," is necessary for the preparation of permanent emulsions. 

Experiment 133 

OBJECT. To prepare emulsions by the use of different emulsifying agents. 

A. Soap. Shake 5 cc. of some oil (e.g., kerosene) with water and allow to stand. Result? 

Now add 5 cc. of the same oil to 20 cc. of a 1 per cent soap solution. Result? 

Save the emulsion for a later experiment., B. Egg. Beat together a whole egg, and while still beating it add a few drops of cottonseed oil. Then add a few cubic centimeters of the oil at a time until 100 cc. have been added. Add 2 cc. of vinegar and gradually work in another 100 cc. of oil. The product is mayonnaise. A little more oil may be added if it is not thick enough. Five grams of salt are needed to fit it for table use. 

C. Acacia. Mix in a mortar 4 parts cottonseed oil, 2 parts powdered acacia, and 2 parts water. Grind them together until the mixture "creams." Then dilute with more oil or water while stirring constantly. At first add the oil or water very slowly. 

The following explanations of how emulsifying agents act have been offered: 

1. They are adsorbed, or held on the surface of the oil particles. 

A film is thus formed about each oil particle, and these films prevent the small particles from coming together into bigger ones. 

2. The emulsifying agent forms a hydrate with water, and this coats the oil particles. 

3. The "orientation theory" recently put forth by Harkins and Langmuir supposes that one end of the emulsifying molecule is soluble in oil and the other end in water, or, to state it differently, that one end is attracted by the oil and the other by the water. Hence the molecules of the emulsifying agent arrange themselves about the particles, each with one end in the oil and

Introductory Chemistry 

Emulsifying agent Oil

HO H₂O Emulsifying agent 

FIG. 133. Diagram illustrating the orientation theory of Harkins and Langmuir. If the smaller ends of the molecules of the emulsifying agent are attracted to the oil, droplets of oil are formed in the water. If the larger ends of the molecules turn to the oil, droplets of water form in the oil. the other in the water. If the smaller ends of the molecules are in the oil, oil droplets in the water will be formed. If the smaller ends of the molecules are in the water, water droplets will be formed in the oil.

In all the theories the emulsifying agent is thought of as collecting about the emulsified particles, and it is supposed that the surface tension of the oil or water, or of both, is lessened and their tendency to stay apart thus increased. 

CONDENSATION METHODS 

Colloids may be formed by condensation in the following ways: (1) heating and cooling, (2) the electric arc, (3) replacement of the solvent, (4) chemical reactions that release insoluble substances from solutions. One or more colloidal dispersions will be prepared in each of these ways.

Experiment 134 OBJECT. To prepare colloids by condensation. 

A. Heating and cooling. Take two separate portions of very dilute sulfuric acid. Heat one portion to boiling and add 10 cc. of a hot 

10 per cent solution of barium chloride, and allow to cool. 

To the cold portion of sulfuric acid add 10 cc. of cold 10 per cent barium chloride solution. Stir each and allow to stand. Which shows the greater colloidal tendency? This experiment shows why in quantitative analysis hot barium sulfate is precipitated. The explanation of the reaction is as follows:

When barium sulfate is precipitated cold, many particles of colloidal size are present among the larger particles. On heating, it is these smaller particles that go into solution, because the smaller ones are more soluble than the larger ones. Then on cooling, because the solution is now supersaturated, there is deposition, and this takes place about the larger particles, which did not lose their identity. Thus by the heating and cooling, the larger particles are built up at the expense of the smaller.

Another method of obtaining a colloid by cooling is to cool a saturated alcoholic solution of sulfur with liquid air. This gives a colloid in which the sulfur is highly dispersed. The cooling causes part of the sulfur to come out of solution, and this gathers into particles of colloidal size.

B. The electrical method. Using electrodes of platinum, copper, or silver, a voltage of 30-110, and an amperage of 5-10, form an arc under a .002 N NaOH solution by bringing the electrodes together and separating them. The metal is first vaporized by the heat of the arc and then condensed into particles of colloidal size. When the dispersions have become highly colored, filter into clean containers and save.

C. Replacement of solvent. Make a saturated solution of sulfur in alcohol and pour it into a beaker partly filled with water. Explain the result. 

D. Chemical reactions. 

1. Deëlectronization. Make a saturated aqueous solution of hy drogen sulfide and leave it exposed to the air. The sulfur is replaced by oxygen, and the sulfur molecules as they are formed gather into colloidal aggregates. Write the equation for the reaction: 

Which element has been electronized and which deëlectronized? 

Is all the sulfur of colloidal dimensions? 

Give reason for your answer. 

Make a test to see if your conclusion is correct. 

2. Electronization. To 100 cc. of distilled water add a few drops

Introductory Chemistry of a 0.1 per cent solution of gold chloride. Neutralize the gold chloride with a little dilute potassium carbonate solution. While heating slowly, add drop by drop a 0.1 per cent solution of tannin and heat until a cherry-red color appears. Save this colloidal dispersion for later use. The red color is due to colloidal gold. 

3. Hydrolysis. Bring about 200 cc. of distilled water to a boil and add a few cubic centimeters of a 30 per cent ferric chloride solution. 

The ferric chloride is hydrolyzed, and the resulting ferric hydroxide is colloidally dispersed. Write the equation for the reaction: 

Save the colloid for later work. 

4. Metathesis. Make a saturated solution of arsenious trioxide by boiling 1 gram of it in 100 cc. of water. Filter the solution and pass hydrogen sulfide into it. Then filter into a clean flask and save for a later experiment. Arsenious sulfide in colloidal dispersion has been formed. Write the equation for the reaction:

Our work has now shown that colloids through condensation can be formed in many different ways. The essential feature common to all these methods is that by them single molecules are brought together into colloidal aggregates. 

Von Weimarn's dictum. The Russian chemist, Von Weimarn, states: "The colloidal state is a universally possible state of matter." 

This investigator has carried out experiments which indicate that under the right conditions and with the proper method any substance can be brought into colloidal dispersion. He prepared, for example, barium sulfate in any condition from that of a clear molecular solution to a thick gel by mixing equal volumes of barium thiocyanate (Ba(CNS) 2.H₂O) and manganous sulfate (MnSO4-4H2O) solutions of equivalent concentration. The following table, taken from Taylor's Chemistry of Colloids (2d Ed.), shows the results secured by Von Weimarn when varying concentrations of the mixed salts were used:

It is noted that the greater the concentration of the barium sulfate, the greater the tendency to come down in a gel condition. 

This means that a gel results when the number of condensation points are large. When the condensation points are few we get a crystalline condition, and at certain intermediate points a sol condition prevails.

SECTION TWO A STUDY OF COLLOIDS 

In the preparation of many colloids, a second substance which is non-colloidal is also formed. For example, in the making of colloidal ferric hydroxide, hydrochloric acid was produced at the same time.

A method for separating these substances from the colloids with which they are mixed will be demonstrated in the next experiment. 

Experiment 135

OBJECT. To dialyze ferric hydroxide. 

Prepare and set up a dialyzer according to the directions of the instructor.

Then pour into it a portion of the colloidal ferric hydroxide prepared in the last experiment. From time to time test the water surrounding the beaker for hydrogen and chlorine ions. Also test it for the ferric ion. Account for your results. 

Save the material in the dialyzer for further experiments. 

Graham used the dialyzer to distinguish between colloids and crystalloids. He found that crystalline substances diffuse through such a membrane as parchment paper, while non-crystalline substances like gum and glue pass through only very slowly. 

Animal bladders, gold beater's skin, collodion membranes, and porous clay vessels covered with layers of gels are also used as diffusion membranes. The gel membranes are the most effective of all and are called ultra filters. Dialysis is much used in the purification" of toxins and antitoxins and of enzyme prepa- 66 rations.

Colloid particles diffuse very slowly, and in the past their failure to pass through membranes was supposed to be due to the slowness of their movement. Doubtless, however, a great part of the explanation lies in the fact that the colloid particles are too 582

large to pass through the fine-meshed membranes used as filters. 

Some gels are more porous than others, and the fineness of the mesh of collodion membranes varies with the way these membranes are hardened in alcohol. The more finely dispersed colloids will pass through the coarser membranes, but are held back by the finer ones; so filters can be used to determine the relative size of the particles in different colloids. Filters are also used to distinguish between true solutions and transparent colloids, for some colloidal dispersions are so fine that it is difficult to tell whether they are molecular or colloidal without a definite test.

The Tyndall effect. A sunbeam entering a darkened room through a small opening makes luminous dust particles in the air. The dust particles reflect the light waves and thus become visible. Colloidal particles are large enough to deflect a light wave, and if a beam of light is thrown through a colloidal dispersion, its path becomes luminous. (See Figure 8.)

Experiment 136 

OBJECT. To render colloidal particles visible (Tyndall effect). 

Project a powerful beam from a projective lantern or other strong source of light into the arsenic sulfide dispersion prepared in a previous experiment. Result?

Repeat the experiment with a solution of sodium chloride in distilled water. Result?

Single molecules are too small to deflect a light wave from its path, and a true solution does not show luminosity. 

In the ultramicroscope, light from the side is thrown into the solution being examined. As viewed from above, the field is dark except the particles that reflect the light upward. These are luminous, and because of the magnifying power of the microscope, each particle is easily visible as a luminous point. 

The Brownian movement. When small particles are observed under a microscope, they have an irregular but constant

Introductory Chemistry and rapid dancing motion-seemingly chiefly to and fro, but often resulting in a slow, zigzag movement across the microscopic field. This movement was first described by a Scotch botanist, Robert Brown, from his observations on pollen grains in water, and is known as the "Brownian movement." 

Experiment 137 OBJECT. To observe the Brownian movement. 

Observe, under a high-power microscope arranged by the instructor, the movement of finely dispersed particles. Select some one particle and observe its course for a time. Carmine or a little India ink in water will furnish a satisfactory preparation, or other finely dispersed material may be used.

The explanation usually given for this movement is that the particles in suspension in the liquid are bumped and driven about by the water molecules striking against them. However, it might be possible for the particles to have a movement of their own, just as individual molecules have, and if they had electric charges so that they would repel each other the movement could be accounted for. It is easy to find whether colloidal particles are charged, as will be shown by the next experiment. 

Experiment 138 

OBJECT. To find (1) if colloidal particles are electrically charged, and (2) if so, whether all colloids carry charges of the same sign. 

Place some of the dialyzed ferric hydroxide colloid in a U-tube. 

Place a platinum electrode in each arm of the tube. Connect with a direct current of 100-200 volts. Allow it to run until a change is noted at one of the electrodes. Describe what you have observed. 

Determine the direction of the current and decide whether the colloid is charged positively or negatively. Give a reason for your decision.

Find the charge carried by an arsenious sulfide dispersion. 

Do all colloids have charges of the same sign?

Precipitation of colloids by electrolytes. Ions carry charges of electricity. Would it be possible to remove the electricity from the particles of a colloid by treating it with an electrolyte ?

Treat about 5 cc. of each colloidal dispersion you have made and saved with an electrolyte.

Account for the deltas at the mouths of rivers flowing into the sea. 

Suppose colloidal materials in the waste water from a factory were polluting a stream and you were given the problem of eliminating them. What procedure would you suggest?

Valence of ions and their discharging power. Is the power of an ion to discharge a colloid proportional to its own charge? 

Or, stating the question differently, does a bivalent ion have twice the power and a trivalent ion three times the power of a univalent one? The problem can be attacked experimentally

Introductory Chemistry by using electrolytes that will give ions of different combining powers.

Can you now make any additions to your suggestions for the elimination of colloidal materials from factory wastes? 

Evidently the precipitating power of ions increases very rapidly with increase of valence. In this connection it should be stated that the hydrogen ion and the hydroxyl ion are exceptional in that their effects on colloids are much greater than their univalence would lead us to expect.

Adsorption of ions by colloids. Both positive and negative ions are present in a solution of an electrolyte. It would seem that these oppositely charged ions should neutralize the effects of each other on colloidal particles that if one is working in a manner to precipitate the colloid the other would be working in

a way to favor its dispersion. It is evident, however, that one of them can precipitate the colloid in the presence of the other. 

One theory in explanation of this is that a colloid particle adsorbs the ions of opposite sign and thus brings more of these than of the other ions into contact with its surface. We can determine by experiment that one ion of an electrolyte is more highly adsorbed than the other.

Experiment 141 

OBJECT. To find if colloids adsorb the precipitating ion. 

A. Place in one beaker a solution of neutral Na2SO4 and in another a solution of neutral K2SO4. Add neutral barium chloride to each. 

When the precipitate has settled, test the supernatant liquid for acidity and record results.

Explain the results.

B. Shake a dilute solution of KCl with soil or clay and test the supernatant liquid for acidity. Result? 

Is the precipitating ion carried down by the colloidal particles? 

Colloids adsorb not only ions but molecules as well. It has been shown that the three principal soil colloids — ferric oxide, aluminum oxide, and silica- take up by adsorption large quantities of the soluble salts of the soil, and hold them in such a way that they are not washed away by rains and yet are readily available to the plant. Hence these colloids serve as a storehouse of food materials for the plant. 

Gels in particular are noted for their adsorbent powers, and in industrial processes they are coming into wide use as adsorbents- silica gel (see page 498) being the one most

Introductory Chemistry commonly employed. When this gel is allowed to stand, it loses water and cracks into a fine granular, or honeycomb, condition, so that its particles offer a vast adsorptive surface (4 acres of surface in I cubic centimeter of the gel). It is used for such purposes as to remove water vapor from air, to adsorb oxides of nitrogen, to remove sulfur from petroleum, and to catch gasoline or other escaping vapors that it is desired to save. By heating, these can be driven off from the gel and if desired can be recovered, while the silica is made ready for use again. 

The cleansing action of soap. The cleansing power of soap is due to adsorption of dirt and grease by the soap colloid. The colloidal particles attract the dirt and thus remove it from the article that is being cleansed. As we have seen, soap emulsifies oils, and in washing emulsification brings the grease into a form that allows it to be adsorbed. 

The importance of colloidal chemistry. In practical life colloidal chemistry is much more important than our brief study of it would indicate. With the exception of the skeleton and a small amount of food not yet used, practically the whole animal body is in the colloidal state, and the living material of plants is likewise colloidal. 

Rocks and soils must be largely explained in terms of colloidal chemistry, and the astronomer believes that comets' tails and cosmic dust are particles of matter in the colloidal state. In dyeing, tanning, lubrication, and cement work; in the manufacture of rubber products, paper, glass, inks, soaps, paints, cellulose products, and porcelain and clay products; in cooking, washing, dairying; and in many other common activities, it is colloidal processes that are chiefly involved. For more detailed knowledge of colloidal chemistry and its applications, texts on that subject must be consulted or a course in this branch of chemistry must be taken.

The chemistry of proteins. Dispersions of proteins have been considered as colloidal in nature, but recent investiga-

tions assign to hæmoglobin a molecular weight of 66,800, and it is now believed that the particles of a dispersed protein are enormously large single molecules and not colloidal aggregates of molecules, as was formerly supposed. If we classify dispersions on the basis of size of particles, it is perhaps not so erroneous to think of a dispersed protein being in the colloidal state as at first thought might appear, and perhaps in future we may class them as colloids of a particular kind. Because living matter itself is a mixture of proteins, and the metabolism of a living organism consists largely of reactions involving proteins, any advance in our knowledge of protein chemistry is of the greatest importance. Few changes in matter are more spectacular than those that take place during the cooking of an egg.

UNIT ELEVEN RADIUM AND THE INERT GASES 

In this final unit we shall consider very briefly certain elements that are of interest chiefly from the scientific point of view. The first of these, radium, is distinguished from the other elements that we have studied by the fact that its atoms are breaking down. 

Helium nuclei and electrons shoot out from its nucleus. Just as when a fish leaps out of water it starts a circle of waves, so the electrons as they leave the atoms set up waves in the ether. 

These waves are similar to X-rays, but are shorter and more penetrating. Chemically, the reactions of radium are similar to those of the other alkaline earth metals. It is chiefly because of its radio-activity that this element is of interest to us. 

The other elements which we shall discuss are the members of the family of inert, or inactive, gases. These gases have a zero valence, which from the standpoint of chemical activity puts. them in a class by themselves. Not only do they form no compounds under any ordinary conditions, but their molecules consist of single atoms. This inactivity on the part of these gases is accounted for on the theory that their valence shells are already complete. 591

SECTION ONE RADIUM 

IN 1899 Becquerel, a French scientist, noted that uranium and its salts give off emanations that affect a photographic plate. 

Mme. Curie later discovered that certain ores containing uranium were far more radioactive than uranium itself. This observation led to the discovery of polonium, actinium, and also of radium, which is a million times more radioactive than uranium. Radium bromide was prepared by Mme. Curie in 1902, and radium was isolated by her in 1910.

Occurrence. Radium is found in Europe in a uranium-bearing ore called "pitchblende," and in Colorado and Utah in another uranium-containing ore called "carnotite." A radium-bearing ore has been discovered in the Belgian Congo. The ore of our

Western states is the chief source of radium, and 150 tons of it yield .2 gram of radium bromide. This compound is the most expensive substance in the world, being worth $100,000 per gram. 

Its high cost is, of course, due to the tedious and expensive process of extraction and the demand for it in medical work. 

Properties and compounds. Mme. Curie prepared metallic radium by the electrolysis of radium chloride, RaCl2. A mercury cathode was used, and after the electrolysis the mercury was distilled, when radium remained as a silvery white metal. It quickly tarnished in air, decomposed water at ordinary temperature, and melted at 700°.

The radioactivity of radium is not affected when it is in a compound, and when radium is spoken of, usually the bromide or chloride is referred to. These are self-luminous and maintain a temperature approximately 2° above that of the surrounding atmosphere. Measurements have shown that radium bromide produces enough heat hourly to convert its weight of ice into water. The scarcity of radium has prevented any extensive study of its compounds, but Mme. Curie produced a white radium 592

Radium 

593 

FIG. 134. Mme. Curie, the discoverer of radium. 

carbonate, RaCO3, similar to the carbonates of the other metals of the alkaline earth group. 

Disintegration of radioactive elements. Uranium and thorium are both radioactive, and they are believed to be mother substances that give rise to three independent series of radioactive compounds, each of which ends in one of the isotopes of lead. 

Two of the three series descend from uranium to lead through different routes; the other series through a third line of substances from thorium to lead. One of the uranium series is shown below. The helium and electrons indicated as being discharged are both from the nucleus of the atom. It will be noted that the same substance does not give off both a helium nucleus and an electron.

FIG. 135. Paths of helium nuclei 

(a-particles) shot out from a radioactive substance into cool, moist air. The helium nuclei penetrate the air to a distance as great as 7 cm. before being stopped, and in doing so each one tears through about 80,000 atoms, knocking one or more electrons from each atom for one half of the molecules of any given radioactive substance to decompose. Thus, of a gram of radium, one half will decompose in 1700 years (and one half of the remainder in another 1700 years), and one half of a gram of radon will decompose in 3.8 days. Naturally, the members of the series that disintegrate slowly will accumulate in greater amounts and those that disintegrate rapidly will be rare.

In the above series it should be noted that with radium we start with an alkaline earth (a solid) and that this by the loss of a helium nucleus is transformed into radon. as it passes through. Minute droplets of water condense on the free electrons, and a brilliant arc light focused on these makes the track of the helium nucleus visible. A change in the course of a particle is due to its collision with the nucleus of an atom of one of the gases of the air.

The rate of decomposition of radioactive substances is unaffected either by the temperature of liquid air or of the electric furnace. The amount of heat released by the decomposition of a gram of radium is equivalent to that produced by burning a ton of coal. In medical work radium is used chiefly for the effect of the ether waves generated by it.

Introductory Chemistry 

Knowing the products that are formed by the decomposition of an element and the rate at which the decomposition proceeds has given a new method for calculating the age of rocks. Thus calculations based on the relative amounts of uranium and lead in a piece of North Dakota rock and on the rate at which uranium is transformed into lead gave the age of the rock as a little over 1,500,000,000 years. This, of course, is the calculated time that has passed since the rock solidified and not the calculated the earth. age of

71 The most exceptional property shown by these gases is their zero valence and their chemical inactivity. A second characteristic of the family is the large number of isotopes found in it. 

Tin and xenon are the only elements which have as high as seven isotopes, and there are only three other elements namely, cadmium, selenium, and mercury-which have as high a number (6) of known isotopes as krypton.

KRYPTON AND XENON 

Krypton occurs in the air in about one part in 19,000,000, while only one part of xenon is found in 190,000,000 parts of air. 

These gases are found in company with argon and are best obtained from the residue after the fractional distillation of liquid argon. They occur in such small quantities and are so inert chemically that no use for them has as yet been found. 597

Introductory Chemistry RADON 

Radon is an emanation from radium (page 593), and no other source of it is known. Its important properties and uses are all in connection with its radioactivity. Its use in medicine has already been discussed (page 595). NEON Neon is found in the atmosphere to the extent of one part in 60,000, and traces of it have been found in certain rocks in company with helium. It may be obtained from the atmosphere by liquefaction of the air in a manner similar to that used in the isolation of helium. Because of its chemical inactivity, it is used in electric-light bulbs, and because an electric spark passes through it with great ease and creates a luminosity which makes its passing evident, neon-filled spark testers are made for the use of automobilists.

ARGON 

Argon is found in the atmosphere to the extent of one part in 104 parts of air, and also in certain minerals. A small amount has been found in gases from sea water and in certain springs. 

It has been reported that for some unknown reason argon accumulates in human blood. 

Argon is prepared from the atmosphere by running air over various reagents to remove other constituents. The water is removed by some dehydrating agent, such as calcium chloride or sulfuric acid; the carbon dioxide by passing the air through soda lime; the oxygen by copper; and the nitrogen by magnesium. 

This leaves the argon mixed with the other rare gases of the air. 

Another method is by passing an electric spark through a mixture of air and oxygen and removing the oxides as fast as they are formed.

Argon is used for filling electric-light bulbs and tungar rectifier bulbs.

The Inert Gases 

599 

HELIUM 

Since

The element helium was first discovered in the sun. that time it has been discovered in some of the planets, including the earth. Certain minerals and rocks have been found to contain an appreciable quantity of it, and it is found in certain springs and in volcanic gases. The atmosphere contains about one part in 185,000. The natural gases of Texas and Kansas are especially rich in helium. The commercial source of helium is natural gas; the obtaining of it from the atmosphere and some minerals is only of scientific interest. The process by which the helium is separated from the other constituents of the gas has already been described. 

The outstanding property of helium is its chemical inactivity, and it is because of this property and its low density that it is valuable in dirigibles. Its low freezing point makes it useful in producing low temperatures. The pre-war price of helium was $2500 per cubic foot and the post-war price 66 cents per cubic foot.

Recently it has been reported that helium has been synthesized from hydrogen by the use of a metal catalyst.
